--- Page 9 of 338 ---
preface
ix
 
 
PREFACE
Ancient India and modern South Asia are famous for the richness of 
their traditions of knowledge and culture, which, for around two millennia, were predominantly transmitted through manuscripts. Within 
this area, South India is remarkably rich in remnants and reminiscences of its former intensive participation in manuscript culture. Our 
access to ancient Indian texts, many of which have thus a history of 
over a millennium and some of over two millennia, is based on manuscripts whose lifespan is nevertheless rarely more than a few centuries. 
These manuscripts have been preserved in families of priests and traditional scholars, in monasteries, temples and libraries. Ancient texts 
in domains such as poetry, medicine, grammar etc. we study nowadays through some printed edition, but for a profound study of a 
selected text it is important to know the history and pedigree of the 
manuscripts on which a text is based. Not only the manuscripts themselves as text sources are to be studied, but also their mutual relationship, their production, distribution and collection, their participation 
in a wider oral or ritual culture. From the 19th century onwards, when 
print culture started to take over from manuscript culture, manuscripts were massively abandoned and efforts were started to collect, 
store and preserve them. In the second half of the 19th century the 
then Government of India supported these efforts and allocated funds 
for the search and collection of manuscripts, which were often privately owned in a family that had no successor for a learned father or 
grandfather. 
This volume is an outcome of the workshop “Production, distribution and collection of Sanskrit manuscripts in Ancient South India” 
which was organized at the International Institute for Asian Studies, 
Leiden, on 20-21 April 2007. The aim of the workshop was threefold: 
1. to study the production, distribution and collection of palm leaf 
manuscripts from early to modern times; 
2. to get a better picture of the ancient, pre-modern, and recent history of currently available manuscripts of the smaller and larger, 
public and private collections inside and outside India;

--- Page 10 of 338 ---
preface
x
3. to place the Johan van Manen collection of ca. 400 South Indian 
palm leaf manuscripts in a larger context. 
The current volume contains most of the papers of this workshop 
which have been updated in the light of the discussions, and an extensive introduction which places these papers in a larger context. The 
volume deals with aspects of manuscript culture in ancient and premodern South India, especially in connection with the production, 
distribution and collection of manuscripts mainly of Sanskrit texts, 
and taking into account the South Asian and Asian contexts. 
There are twelve contributions and an introductory essay. Most 
contributions have been presented, in a preliminary form, at the 
workshop, but two, the one by A. Esposito and the one by H. Moser, 
have been invited after the seminar. Also the introductory essay, by 
my husband Jan E.M. Houben (EPHE, Paris) and myself, has been 
written later in order to deal with larger theoretical and historical 
issues. The twelve contributions that follow the introductory essay 
deal mainly with concrete (South Indian) manuscript collections and 
concrete problems such as the script, the dating of manuscripts, the 
search for manuscripts in order to establish an unedited text, setting 
up an electronic data base with regard to a group of manuscripts, etc. 
The editor expresses her thanks to the institutions and persons that 
have supported the organisation of the workshop which was at the 
basis of this book, the J. Gonda foundation (KNAW, Amsterdam), the 
Leids Universiteitsfonds (LUF, Leiden), the School of Asian, African 
and Amerindian Studies (CNWS, Leiden), and, last but not least, the 
International Institute for Asian Studies (IIAS, Leiden). Special thanks 
are due to the ever helpful staff of the IIAS and its successive Directors, 
Prof. Dr. Wim Stokhof, Prof. Dr. Max Sparreboom, and Dr. Philippe 
Peycam, as well as to Deputy Director Dr. Manon Osseweijer and Drs. 
Anna Yeadell who supported not only the workshop but also the elaboration of its papers into a volume on manuscript culture in South 
India. She expresses her thanks to Professor Chistopher Minkowski, 
Boden Professor of Sanskrit, University of Oxford, for giving a 
thought-provoking introductory lecture on The lives of Manuscripts 
and the defects of Scribes. The opening recitation and melodious chant 
from the Ṛgveda and Sāmaveda by Pdt. Shri Chaitanya Kale, Maha
rashtra (India), made the occasion graceful and deserve a special 
thankful mention. Sincere thanks goes to Dr. Dory Heilijgers, Librar

ian, and Drs. Raju Bakker, staff of the then Kern Institute, for their 
continuous assistance. The editorial advice of an anonymous reviewer 
of Brill publishers is gratefully acknowledged. Finally, the editor 
records her gratitude to Brill publishers for accepting the volume in 
their series. 
Dr. Saraju Rath
International Institute for Asian Studies
Leiden

CHAPTER ONE
Introduction 
Manuscript Culture and its impact in “India”: 
Contours and Parameters
Jan E.M. Houben and Saraju Rath
1.  Introducing Indian, Including South Indian, 
Manuscript Culture
1.1  For around two millennia, India transmitted its knowledge and 
culture through manuscripts. This is not just a marginal and isolated 
curiosity in the history of Indian science and culture, but a determining factor in the rich heritage of India’s traditions. Modern scholarship is well aware of the profound and multifaceted impact of print 
culture on intellectual traditions, on the arts and sciences, and on the 
construction of social and political communities. On the basis of various studies it is also well known that in an oral environment where no 
writing is used, traditions of knowledge and culture have distinctive 
features that change significantly once these oral traditions become 
literary traditions. There is no similar body of research on manuscript 
culture. 
Moreover, our understanding of print culture and its impact, for 
which Elizabeth Eisenstein’s The Printing Press as an Agent of Change 
(1979) is the classical study, is predominantly based on research of 
western and more specifically European history.1 Oral culture, in 
1  Eisenstein 1979: 9 note 18: “As noted in my preface, the term ‘print culture’ is 
used to refer only to post-Gutenberg developments in the West. How printing 
affected pre-Gutenberg Asia must be left to others to investigate.” Two decades after 
its publication the importance of Eisenstein’s work in print culture scholarship is 
confirmed by a number of studies “challenging Eisenstein” (Hudson 2002); but even 
the study which “makes the boldest claims to be defying the orthodoxies of previous 
scholarship,” Adrian Johns The Nature of the Book (1998), agrees that “studying the 
physical book provides one significant, even essential, key to unlocking the intellectual progress of Western thought” (Hudson 2002: 84). In Johns’ own words (1998: 
10-19), his problem with Eisenstein is that among three features of print culture—
standardization, dissemination and fixity—she focuses on fixity, which, however, as

--- Page 18 of 338 ---
jan e.m. houben and saraju rath
2
either a pre-literary or a non-literary context, we understand on the 
basis of the works of Eric A. Havelock (1903-1988), Walter J. Ong 
(1912-2003), and Jack Goody. 
Eric A. Havelock, a British classicist, discovered that there is a sharp 
divide between Greek literature from the 4th century bce onward, 
influenced by the gradually more and more intensive use of writing, 
and Greek literature before the 4th century bce, which was still exclusively or predominantly oral (and hence in a style appropriate for 
orality).2 Father Walter J. Ong, S.J., who was professor of English and 
later of Humanities at St. Louis University, Missouri, from 1955 to 
1991, published major studies in cultural history with a focus on oral 
culture in contrast with literacy. Jack Goody is retired professor at 
Cambridge University interested in the impact on society of agriculture, urbanization and technologies of communication. He did fieldwork in Africa, especially in northern Ghana. Without much 
familiarity with basic results of more than a century of intensive 
research on India, he has tried to extrapolate his theoretical results to 
Asia including India.3 
1.2  For “India,” in the classical sense of the word,4 primary sources 
on manuscript culture are more than abundant, although very 
unevenly distributed over the relevant periods. Because of their limited lifespan in India’s tropical climate, manuscripts older than three 
to four hundred years are rare. This applies especially to South India, 
since manuscripts in the north of the Indian subcontinent and even 
more manuscripts (with Indic language texts) in Central Asia have a 
better chance to survive long due to the dryness of the climate. Indeed, 
the oldest preserved manuscripts on birch bark and palm leaf are 
those discovered around a century ago in Turfan and other places in 
Johns tries to show, was not so easily achieved but required centuries of effort and 
negotiation and is even at present more “a matter of convention and of trust, of culture and practice” (Johns 1998: 633). The words Eisenstein used to characterize print 
culture were, however, not the trio “standardization, dissemination and fixity,” but 
the following list: “dissemination, standardization, reorganization, data collection, 
preservation, and amplification and reinforcement” (Eisenstein 1979: 43-129), 
among which “preservation” comes closest to, but is definitely not identical with, 
Johns’ “fixity.” 
2  Havelock’s most important publication in this field is The Muse Learns to Write 
(1986), but his ideas started to develop earlier (e.g., 1957 and 1963). 
3  See Staal 1986; Falk 1990, 1993: 324-327. 
4  Cf. Renou & Filliozat 1949-1953; geographically, classical India would correspond roughly to the Indian subcontinent or to South Asia including Afghanistan.

--- Page 19 of 338 ---
manuscript culture: contours and parameters
3
and around the Tarim Basin (currently in the Chinese province 
Xinjiang), and in Northern Pakistan (Gilgit). Discoveries over the last 
fifteen years of remnants of even older Indic manuscripts on birch 
bark in the ancient region of Gandhāra (modern Pakistan and 
Afghanistan) have recently led to a new upsurge of interest in the early 
Buddhist and other texts and of the early history of writing in this 
area.5 Perhaps this has in turn contributed to a relative neglect of other 
regions important for South Asian manuscript culture, such as South 
India where early manuscript writing must have started not or not 
much later than in Gandhāra, though there is little hope that equally 
ancient remnants will ever be found. It is remarkable that the palm 
leaf for Central Asian manuscripts, dated to around the 3rd to the 7th 
century ce, must have originated in the South where the palm from 
which it is produced (Corypha umbraculifera Linn.) grows in abundance. The writing on them was nevertheless done in North India or 
in Central Asia.6 In sculpture, the earliest representations of writing 
depict wooden planks which were coated with wax for this pur- 
pose.7 
5  See, for instance, Salomon 1999; Baums forthc. 
6  Palm leaves of documents found in Central Asia were apparently imported 
from the South where the raw material, the leaves of the Corypha palm, was abundantly available. It is likely that these palm leaves were imported from South India 
(over land) or Sri Lanka (involving transport over sea), although no direct evidence 
is available for such transports. As palm leaves need to undergo extensive treatment 
before they become suitable for writing they must have been rather precious in the 
North, as can be deduced from the presence of numerous re-used manuscripts (palimpsests), cf. L. Sander 1968: 25-27. It is to be noted that the conclusions of A.F.R. 
Hoernle (1900) regarding the relationship between the Corypha palm or Śrītāla 
(Corypha umbraculifera L., syn. “wilde lontar boom”, Hinzler 1993: 469) and the 
Palmyra palm or Tāla (Borassus flabellifer L., syn. Lontarus domesticus Gaertn., Balinese rontal or lontar), often referred to as established facts, are to be reviewed in the 
light of Moore & Dransfield (1979) and Jahn (2006). Although the palm leaf writing 
material came from the South, no southern scripts or (Buddhist) texts were found in 
the Turfan collections studied by Sander. Can we conclude that southern Buddhist 
schools, if they had any independent existence, were not authoritative in the North? 
Nevertheless, the use of palm leaf from the South as writing material in the North 
strongly suggests that palm leaves were also intensively used for writing in the South 
(South India and/or Sri Lanka), probably a little earlier than the oldest palm leaf 
manuscripts found in the North, although no early specimens of palm leaf manuscripts have been preserved in South India. Useful observations on palm leaf manuscripts in various Indian languages can be found in the volume edited by Hikosaka 
et al., 1995. 
7  Since wax-coated wooden planks were also the main writing material for 
ephemeral writing in “the West” since antiquity (an early literary reference is Homer’s Iliad VI.169), “India” was on this point and in this early period remarkably sim-

--- Page 20 of 338 ---
jan e.m. houben and saraju rath
4
Among currently available collections of manuscripts, many originated as late as in the 19th century and some, such as the Sarasvati 
Mahal library in Thanjavur (Tanjore), considerably earlier. Libraries 
did exist in India much earlier, as is clear on the basis of inscriptional 
evidence. A copper plate grant of king Trailokyamalla (Nāgai, Karna
taka, 1058 ce) is often mentioned to illustrate this. It specifies the distribution of land to the staff of a temple college as follows: 
35 mattar of land under the dam at Aratura and Diggavige (lower Cave) 
at Nāgavāvi to the expounder of Bhaṭṭadarśana, 30 mattar of land to 
the expounder of Nyāya, 45 mattar to the expounder of Prabhākara 
and 30 mattar to each librarian.8 
From this we understand that the librarians (six in number, as stated 
elsewhere in the grant) were on a similar remuneration scale as the 
teachers. Many other inscriptions throughout India testify to the regular funding of educational centres, small and big, with restricted or 
wide access, etc., where texts are usually learned by heart by reciting 
them until they are fully mastered.9 
ilar to “the West” (cf. Sreeramula R. Sarma 1985). A scribe with wax-coated writing 
planks and next to him a scribe with a leather scroll are probably represented in a 
Mesopotamian sculpture of the 7th cent. bce (“Stone panel from the South-West 
Palace of Sennacherib (Room 28, Panel 9)” at www.britishmuseum.org under 
explore highlights). An early Indian representation of a scribe with a writing plank is 
found in a Buddhist sculpture in Nagarjunakonda (Colour Plate 1a, 1b, 1c). A general overview of writing materials used in Ancient India is given in Sircar 1965: 61ff 
“Chapter III: Writing materials.” Literary evidence for various other writing materials is discussed in Sarma 1986; also scientific instruments such as the astrolabe provided a basis for writing: Sarma 2008: 240ff. and 257ff. The importance of the scribe 
in the court in early India is briefly indicated in the section on decrees 
(śāsanādhikāra), Arthaśāstra 2.10 (Kangle 1986, I: 48; II: 92; III: 88ff). The scribe is 
expected “to have the qualities of a minister (amātya-saṁpadopetaḥ), he should be 
conversant with all conventions (sarva-samaya-vid), be quick in composing (āśugranthaḥ), have agreeable handwriting (cārv-akṣaraḥ), and be capable of reading 
aloud a document (lekha-vācana-samarthaḥ).” The scribe remains important at the 
court in subsequent centuries as is clear from a text such as the Lekhapaddhati of 
pre-sultanate Gujarat (Strauch 2002, Prasad 2007). 
8  Datta 1970 : 42; Nair 1998: 143; Scharfe 2002: 184; Scharfe takes 1 mattar as 4 
acres. 30 mattar would then correspond to almost 500 m2. 
9  Scharfe 2002: 183ff gives several examples from South India. We found several 
examples from the north as well, for instance the following two from Orissa: (1) the 
Asankhali plates (Mayurbhañj dist. Orissa), dated 1225 Śaka = 1302/03 ce, document king Narasimha II’s donation of land for the construction of two colleges 
(maṭha) for Veda and Vyākaraṇa: (plate 5, line 201) vedamaṭhārthaṁ sapta vāṭikā 
[ḥ], vyākaraṇamaṭhārthaṁ daśa vātikā[ḥ] ; titles attached to the names of the donees 
such as pāṭhin, tripāṭhin suggest that the teachers had fully interiorized the texts 
(D.C. Sircar, EI 31 (1955-56): 109-128); (2) the Sakhigopal copper plates (Puri dist.,

manuscript culture: contours and parameters
5
At present, manuscripts are kept in South Asia’s libraries and 
musea10 and in libraries throughout the world. For many of these collections there are catalogues, and as early as in 1891 a “catalogue of 
catalogues” appeared from the hand of Theodor Aufrecht, soon followed by a second volume by the same author in 1896 in order to take 
into account catalogues that could not be included in volume one. A 
New Catalogus Catalogorum project started in 1935 at the University 
of Madras and gained momentum in the 1950s, under the guidance of 
V. Raghavan. Nineteen volumes have appeared since 1949 covering 
Sanskrit titles from A up to, at the end of volume nineteen, Mahyā
didānaprayoga.11 Studies of specific manuscripts of specific texts and 
manuscript collections are available, 12 and some studies dealing with 
Orissa), dated 1151-52 śaka = 1229-30 ce, record the donation of land for a vedamaṭha, a vyākaraṇa-maṭha and a purāṇa-maṭha (9 vāṭī, 3 to each) and also to a 
bhad-vddha-maṭha (?), (EI 37 (1967-68): 317-332). See further Rath 2011. 
10  According to Sheldon Pollock 2006: 558 “over thirty million manuscripts are 
still extant (eight million in Rajasthan alone)”; in a note he refers to “the Indira Gandhi National Centre for the Arts” without further specification. According to a paper 
recently presented at the International Conference on Academic Libraries (ICAL2009), the total number of mss in India is 5.000.000 (Gaur & Chakraborty 2009); to 
this number, estimates for Indian mss. abroad and those not yet catalogued are to be 
added. 
11  According to its title this is a guide to the ceremonial donation of objects such 
as the earth.
12  Katre 1954, Appendix III “On some important manuscripts and critical editions” gives a list of fifteen domains of textual criticism and briefly discusses the 
manuscripts important in them. Most studies of Indian and Central Asian are undertaken in the context of textual criticism and their main aim is to establish historically 
correct texts. Textual criticism of Indian and Indic texts has been able to advance on 
the methodological basis of centuries of classical textual criticism which concerns, 
however, only one typical situation: that in which the production of the text is separated from the critical study of the text through several historical ruptures (Houben 
2009b: 82-83). A large number of the Indian and Indic texts transmitted in manuscripts participate, or participated for a considerable time, in some lively oral or scientific or ritual tradition, so that the strongly preferred situation in classical textual 
criticism, that of an “uncontaminated” lineage of manuscripts, is quite rare in India. 
By the same token, the ritual, cultural, scientific context of a text, apart from commentaries and parallel texts, often provide a predictability of incomplete or problematic passages which allow “higher textual criticism” and even convincing 
reconstruction on a quite limited manuscript basis (cf. Colas 2009, Houben 2009b; 
M. Witzel, 1972, 2004, reconstructs the Kaṭha-Āraṇyaka on the basis of the 
“Zwangsläufichkeit” of the text; on the special literary conditions of scientific texts 
cp. Sarma 2008, Plofker 2008). One may accordingly wonder to what extent methods 
and terminology of classical textual criticism can provide a fruitful basis in the 
domain of Indian and Indic texts which is often entirely neglected in recent publications (Maniaci 2005; Muzerelle 1985, extended and updated at http://vocabulaire.
irht.cnrs.fr/pages/vocab2.htm). A symptomatic case is Muzerelle’s overspecialized

--- Page 22 of 338 ---
jan e.m. houben and saraju rath
6
the material basis of manuscripts and their production.13 There is a 
smaller number of studies dealing with manuscript culture in the 
sense of the immediate context of the production and use of manuscripts.14 
With all these collections, catalogues and studies of manuscripts 
and of the texts they contain, a study focused on manuscript culture 
and its cultural impact in India—a study in which this enormous area 
and its original developments are not forcefully fitted into pre-conceived theorical frameworks based on external data—is still absent.15 
There are at the most dispersed observations in indological and especially in buddhological studies that point to an impact of writing on 
culture in the widest sense. For manuscript culture in India, or South 
and South-East Asia, there is nothing that can even distantly be compared with what Eisenstein’s 1979 study has been for print in the 
West. There is hence no proper basis to understand the extent and 
impact of some two thousand years of intensive manuscript culture in 
India, nor of what preceded and what gradually superceded it: orality—including highly sophisticated forms of oral transmission which 
partly survive till the present day—and, especially from the 19th century onwards, print culture.

very insufficiently known and understood cultural phenomenon, 
manuscript culture, with a focus on Sanskrit manuscripts in South

and parameters of Indian manuscript culture. In this context, South

quite homogeous, “pan-Indian” Aśokan Brāhmī). These contours, in 
particular for the period of the first transition from orality to (manuscript) writing in various religious and cultural domains, have not 
remained undisputed. We will also briefly deal with parameters of the 
interaction between manuscript writing and intellectual traditions, 
arts and sciences, and social and political communities, or, to put it 
briefly, with the context and the wide-ranging impact of manuscript 
culture on India’s intellectual and cultural history. A Timeline will be

2.  Manuscript Culture in India: When and How Did It 
Start and Develop?
2.1  Even if the profound impact of manuscript culture in India, just 
as the profound impact of print culture in contemporary Europe, was 
only very dimly understood by the eighteenth and nineteenth century 
western explorers of India’s rich literary heritage in the domains of 
art, philosophy, science and religion, these early pioneers of the scientific study of India were much interested in the first beginnings of 
writing, which was generally considered as a sign of “civilization.” The 
nature and date of these first beginnings and especially their relationship with writing traditions outside India have been intensely disputed. Western scholars who had a chance to stay in India reported 
impressive feats of memorization by traditionally educated Indians,19 
but what this implied for the transmission of knowledge and literature 
in India they and especially their colleagues who could not come to 
India were either unable or unwilling to understand.20 As long as literacy was considered as a necessary precondition of civilization, both 
Indian and western scholars who were convinced of a long history of 
India’s literary culture have frantically searched for direct or indirect 
evidence of early writing in South Asia. To some it became gradually 
clear, however, that sophisticated civilization was possible also with19  Harry Falk’s work on writing in ancient India (1993) contains several references to western scholars who could not or cannot imagine that enormous texts such 
as the Ṛgveda were committed to memory and faithfully transmitted orally. On 
p. 321-324 Falk refers not only to a very late case of a western indological scholar 
(W. Kirfel) who cannot imagine the faithful memorization and oral transmission of 
large texts, but also to a number of testimonies of western scholars, from Colebrooke 
(1837) onwards, who went to India and observed how students learned texts and 
pandits reproduced them from memory. 
20  Falk 1993: 321 refers to W. Kirfel who insisted that a long period of oral transmission without writing is impossible so that, whatever the data and testimonies or 
their absence, we must assume there was a written tradition parallel with that oral 
tradition also for the oldest periods. Also Johannes Bronkhorst assumes very early 
written forms of the Veda when he proposes that the word-by-word version (Padapāṭha) was written down “from its beginning” (Bronkhorst 1982: 184). In his latest 
contribution to the discussion (2002) he focuses on the traditions of grammar and 
philosophy. Although aware of Scharfe’s Education in Ancient India, which appeared 
in the same year and to which he refers once, he does not reply to Scharfe’s arguments to reject the “originally written Padapāṭha” theory (2002: 10f) nor does he 
reply to Scharfe’s explanations of early orality in India (2002, chapters 2, 12, 13). On 
this theme see now also Scharfe 2009 and Houben 2009b.

out writing. Or, in the negative formulation of Jean Filliozat (1963): 
“In a country of oral education, illiterates are not all ignorants.”21 
2.2  A very early, so far not convincingly deciphered and possibly 
largely pictographic system of writing found in the area of the Indus 
civilization in the north-west of the South Indian subcontinent, ca. 
2600-1900 bce,22 has no demonstrable link with Kharoṣṭhī and 
Brāhmī, the two earliest “syllabic alphabetic” scripts for Indian languages.23 The earliest tangible and indisputable evidence of a significant use of writing in South Asia is found in the pillar and rock edicts 
of king Aśoka, ca. 250 bce24 Are the Aśokan inscriptions then also the 
first writings in South Asian scripts after the disappearance of the 
Indus script? 
With this question we enter into a difficult problem area. The main 
issue is that the content and nature of ancient Indian texts point to an 
elaborate phase of Vedic literary production which must have largely 
preceded early Buddhism, which in turn preceded the writings of king 
21  J. Filliozat 1963: 156 “Dans un pays à pédagogie orale, les analphabètes ne sont 
pas tous des ignorants.” 
22  Parpola 1994 gives a comprehensive overview of the signs and analyzes them 
in the light of the hypothesis that it is a script of a Dravidian language. Whether the 
Indus symbols encode any language at all—on phonemic, syllabic or word level—has 
been questioned: Farmer et al. 2004. 
23  As it was observed by Salomon (1998: 15): “The Indic system of writing is difficult to classify in terms of traditional typology of writing systems which recognizes 
three main script types, namely, logographic, syllabic, and alphabetic.” Scripts of the 
type of Brāhmī, Grantha and Devanāgarī, which give—ideally—a one to one representation of each phoneme, consonant and vowel, are typologically far removed 
from other (non-alphabetic) syllabaries, where characters do not show any particular 
resemblance to each other if the syllable referred to shares certain phonemes (vowels 
or consonants). That is why the term “alphasyllabary” (Bright 1999), which is perhaps suitable for old-Persian cuneiform and Aramaic, is not a very felicitous one for 
Brāhmī and derived scripts, as it would suggest the script is basically a syllabary that 
shares some characteristics with the alphabet. In the encyclopedic Daniels & Bright 
1996 we find, somewhat confusingly, both “alphasyllabary” and “abugida” used to 
refer to writing systems of the Brāhmī-type and the Ethiopian Ge’ez script. From a 
functional point of view, however, Brāhmī etc. are better characterized as “syllabically organized alphabetic writing systems,” or, more compactly, as “syllabic alphabets” (for the term, proposed with a different argumentation, cp. Coulmas 1996: 483; 
2003: 131f). The main point for now is that Kharoṣṭhī and Brāhmī are apparently 
typologically far removed from the still undeciphered Indus script or symbol system. 
24  The first extensive authoritative description is found in CII, vol. I, ed. by A.

10
Aśoka. Not only Vedic literature, also Buddhist literature and the literature of another major ancient religious tradition of the South 
Indian continent, Jainism, are full of indications of oral conditions of 
the early composition and transmission of their precious ancient texts. 
In spite of these indications, investigators since the nineteenth century of the transmitted texts and associated knowledge systems such 
as grammar have found it difficult to imagine that they really originated in a purely oral environment. 
The Aśokan inscriptions throughout the Indian subcontinent are 
mostly in an early Magadhan Prakritic (MIA or Middle Indo-Aryan) 
language or dialect and written in Brāhmī, those in the north-west are 
in a language closer to Sanskrit and written in the Kharoṣṭhī script, 
two are in scripts and languages that did not originate in South Asia: 
one in Greek, and one, bilingual, in Greek and Aramaic. With regard 
to one of the two ancient scripts for Indian languages, Kharoṣṭhī, its 
dependence on Aramaic is generally accepted. It is hence natural to 
assume that earlier stages of Kharoṣṭhī, preceding the attested form of 
the Aśokan inscriptions and perhaps written on perishable material, 
are lost. From the Aśokan inscriptions onward till the, recently discovered, earliest attested manuscripts of the first century bce—first 
centuries ce, Kharoṣṭhī appears as an administrative and commercial 
script, which was also employed for writing Buddhist texts. 
It is, however, Brāhmī that would at first sight be a more likely candidate for being the script in which Vedic and Sanskritic texts would 
have been written down, to make up, at least hypothetically, for the 
absence of written testimonies of the elaborate early Vedic and 
Sanskrit texts. And indeed, a detailed theory of a gradual development 
of Brāhmī, on the basis of a “northsemitic” example, and of an early, 
pre-Aśokan form suitable for Sanskrit, was proposed by the indologist 
and paleographist Georg Bühler (1837-1898). This theory was widely 
accepted, for instance by Monier-Williams in his introduction to his 
Sanskrit-English dictionary (1899) where it has found, till today, wide 
and prominent exposure. In this theory, all Brāhmī characters are 
derived from Phoenician letters, either directly or through turning, 
inverting, opening or closing. According to Bühler, Sanskrit grammarians contributed significantly to the early development of the 
script. It is argued that the Aśokan inscriptions, which show variations in script-styles, presuppose a long development of pre-Aśokan 
Brāhmī writing. In the formulation of Monier-Williams (1899: xxvi), 
the Brāhmī script “must have been the first kind of writing used when

--- Page 27 of 338 ---
manuscript culture: contours and parameters
11
Sanskrit literature began to be written down (perhaps six centuries 
bc)”. This pre-Aśokan Brāhmī would have been used for writing 
Sanskrit and Vedic texts, although no traces of such early Sanskrit 
writing have been found. The—unattested—pre-Aśokan use of 
Brāhmī would explain how there apparently was an extensive reading 
public in the time of Aśoka when he set up a program of inscriptions 
throughout his extensive empire. 
In a comprehensive review of the available evidence and all major 
contributions to the discussion till the 1990s, Harry Falk (1993) demonstrated that, in fact, accepting the existence of Vedic texts long 
before Aśoka does not necessitate the acceptance of equally early writing. Falk argued, moreover, that the homogeneity and regularity of 
Aśoka’s Brāhmī are such that it must have been an invention under 
his rule, after the example of the royal inscriptions of Iran. In this he is 
in line with the conclusion reached by Goyal in 1979. 
Falk demonstrates that the earliest Brāhmī, from Aśoka onwards, 
was designed, just as Kharoṣṭhī, only for the Prakritic dialects current 
in that time, but not for Sanskrit. Here, as on a few other points, Falk 
developed (and significantly modified) the position which the French 
Assyriologist Joseph Halévy (1827-1917) had adopted and later on 
vehemently defended against Bühler. It was Halévy’s significant observation on the basis of inscriptional evidence that from Aśoka onwards 
it took several centuries before the script was adapted to the requirements of Sankrit through various tentative amendments (esp. with 
regard to word concluding consonants, double consonants and ligatures, the representation of , ,  and ḥ, which do not occur in Prakrits 
or MIA dialects). If Sanskrit grammarians had contributed seriously 
to the development of a script in Aśoka’s time or, as Bühler had proposed, before Aśoka, they could have solved easily (Falk: within a 
week) all the problems for which we have the testimony of trials for 
two to four centuries.25 The original creation of Brāhmī (and of 
25  Falk 1993: 134-135: “Da man eigentlich voraussetzen sollte, daß ein Grammatiker des Sanskrit, der sich daran macht, eine Schrift zu erfinden, auch an seine

Kharoṣṭhī) for an early Prakritic language and its slow adaptation to 
Sanskrit afterwards allow us to infer, “with an almost mathematical 
certainty,”26 that Vedic and Sanskrit texts were not written down 
before Aśoka and must therefore have been fixed in an alternative way 
through oral transmission and memory culture. 
Expanding and adapting the argument of Bühler’s opponent 
Halévy, Falk (1993: 337ff) accepted that Brāhmī did not exist before 
Aśoka: it must have been designed by specialists under the rule of 
Aśoka, who were already familiar with Kharoṣṭhī which was derived 
from Aramaic. This had been proposed by Goyal (1979), it was the 
conclusion reached by von Hinüber in his investigations of the beginnings of script in ancient India (1989) and it was still more or less 
confirmed in Salomon’s review “On the Origin of the Early Indian 
Scripts” (1995). We can call it the “invention theory” of the origin of 
Brāhmī. Falk (1993: 165) argues for acceptance of this theory by refuting or subverting the arguments Bühler used for accepting a considerable period of development of Brāhmī before Aśoka. For Bühler the 
variation and fast development seen in the Brāhmī of Aśokan inscriptions point to a considerable period of development preceding Aśoka. 
For Falk, however, the same facts—variation and fast development—
indicate that it is a very young script, just created on the request of 
Aśoka. For Falk it is precisely the pace of developments within the less 
than 20 years of Aśoka’s inscriptions which shows how fast a script 
can progress, and which hence takes away the necessity of assuming a 
long period of preceding development. Falk further points out that it 
is significant that the texts of royal inscriptions do not employ terms 
pointing to the literacy of the public for whom they are intended. On 
the contrary, they refer to vacanika “proclaimers” who have to read a 
message aloud for an apparently analphabetic public. 
Falk’s meticulous argumentation, and, in general, the “invention 
theory,” would have remained perfectly convincing if indeeed there 
would have been no scratch of objective evidence of the use of Brāhmī 
script before Aśoka. Such evidence was indeed absent in the time of 
26  From these premisses also Halévy concluded “avec une certitude presque 
mathématique” (Halévy referred to after Falk 1993: 129) that Vedic and Sanskrit 
texts were not written down before Aśoka. Since faithful transmission without writing—through orality and memory culture—was not a theoretical option for Halévy, 
this automatically led him to accept a subsequent conclusion: that the Vedic hymns 
were composed after Alexander the Great. With this second conclusion, untenable in 
the light of extensive comparative linguistic, ritual and cultural-historical evidence, 
he made it easy to indologists to reject at once his entire view on Indian scripts.

only, esp. that of commerce. Because the option of writing must have 
remained no secret, we may speak, in this period, of a “conscious” or 
“determined” orality for Vedic and Sanskritic texts, an orality that 
could make use of a sound alternative to some of the functions of writing in the form of a textual memory culture. 
2.3  On the basis of his extensive fieldwork among non-literate societies in West Africa, Jack Goody developed a theoretical framework 
for dealing with what has been regarded as differences in “mentality” 
between societies. Goody’s earliest formulation is found in the article 
he wrote together with Ian Watt on “the consequences of literacy” 
(1963): 
In oral societies the cultural tradition is transmitted almost entirely by 
face-to-face communication; and changes in its content are accompanied by the homeostasic process of forgetting or transforming those 
parts of the tradition that cease to be either necessary or relevant. Literate societies, on the other hand, cannot discard, absorb, or transmute 
the past in the same way. Instead, their members are faced with permanently recorded versions of the past and its beliefs; and because the 
past is thus set apart from the present, historical enquiry becomes possible. This in turn encourages scepticism; and scepticism, not only 
about the legendary past, but about received ideas about the universe 
as a whole. From here the next step is to see how to build up and to 
test alternative explanations: and out of this there arose the kind of 
logical, specialized, and cumulative intellectual tradition of sixth-century Ionia. The kinds of analysis involved in the syllogism, and in the 
30  When new findings were reported in Sri Lanka, Allchin (1995: 209f) is happy

Vedic texts are transmitted to writing later: this is also necessary in the light of the 
evidence for the initial incapacity and later slow adaptation of the early script to

Among the two authors it was Jack Goody who in subsequent studies 
elaborated this “Goody-Watt” thesis, which can be summarized as: 
“science and even rationality originate from literacy.”31 While doing 
so Goody remained basically loyal to its original formulation. In the 
analysis of Goody’s critic John Halverson, however, the original thesis 
was subject to much rethinking and revision and it became “so attenuated that it virtually collapsed on itself” (1992b: 302). After reviewing 
Goody & Watt’s article and two works of Goody (1977, 1987), Halver
son concludes that a “genetic relationship between writing and rationality” which was alternatively posited and suggested from the 
beginning receives at the end no adequate argumentative underpinning. A positive point Halverson mentions about Goody is that he 
(according to Halverson: unlike Havelock32) responds to criticism 
from other scholars which makes it possible to improve his theory. 
In subsequent publications Goody gradually seeks to expand the 
basis of his argument33: in his next monograph The Domestication of 
the Savage Mind (1977) the ancient Near East receives much attention; ten years later, his Interface Between the Written and the Oral 
(1987), contains a chapter—originally a contribution to a conference 
on orality in 1985—on what is apparently the most recalcitrant case 
for his theory, the orally composed and transmitted Vedas. Goody 
studies this case on the basis of observations by non-specialists in 
Vedic studies and concludes, just as earlier generations of indologists 
did but in the absence of any evidence for Vedic writing before the 
first millennium ce, that it “seems clear that not only is the organization of the Vedas influenced by writing, but also the mnemonic 
devices used by those who teach and learn them” (1987: 122).

of this Vedic oral tradition “demonstrates in passing that the GoodyWatt thesis is not without important exceptions and therefore not 
generally tenable” (1986: 27). Staal distinguished, in fact, “two traditions of transmission” in India “that are formal and more or less reliable in their preserving function.” One of these two traditions, the one 
of “oral epics” is first orally transmitted and shows a certain “instability and change, at least on that verbatim level that is significantly called 
‘literal’,” before it is written down and achieves “much greater stability” (1986: 26). The other tradition of transmission concerns especially 
the Ṛgveda (and to varying degrees other Vedic texts). The Ṛgveda, 
too, “may have gone through a period of change that is not entirely 
dissimilar to the oral vagaries of the epics: but if it ever took place, it 
happened before its codification around 1200 bce After that it was not 
written down, but entered the period of oral transmission ... which 
fixed it for more than 3000 years in the course of which it preserved as 
stable and solid a state as any textual ‘archetype’ ever has.” Moreover, 
there is good reason “to doubt that it underwent major changes even 
on the formal level prior to its codification: for unlike epics, with their 
simple and identical meters, ‘gnomic phrases and commonplaces’, the 
Ṛgveda exhibited from the beginning a high level of poetic sophistication comprising a variety of meters—about fifteen in all—that sets it 
apart also in formal terms from the later epic literature of India, and 
prevents the facile application of concepts that have elsewhere been 
successfully applied to more popular oral epics.” Another difference 
“that goes a long way to explain the contrast between the loose orality 
of the epic and the fixed orality of the Vedas: the former were transmitted by bards, addressing villagers in open settings; but the latter 
were transmitted from father to son and from teacher to pupil, in 
strict and often secret isolation” (1986: 27). 
Falk (1990; 1993: 326) analyses Goody’s suggestions with regard to 
the Vedas in more detail and demonstrates their untenability in the 
light of the available evidence: the first (syllabic alphabetic) Indian 
scripts which appear in Aśoka’s inscriptions still required a considerable period of adaptation before they became suitable for Vedic and 
Sanskrit. With reference to BaudhDhS 3.9.8-9, Falk (1993: 255) further demonstrates that Vedic ritual culture gives unambiguous indications of an originally exclusively oral transmission and use of Vedic 
texts. 
In order to be able to appreciate Falk’s example and his argument, 
we will have a brief look at the judgements of Louis Renou four to five

--- Page 33 of 338 ---
manuscript culture: contours and parameters
17
decades earlier. In his comprehensive study of the Vedic schools, 
Louis Renou (1947 : 33-34, note 3) observed that “oral transmission is 
silently admitted for the entire or the major part of Vedic literature, 
because the Indian tradition says this, and because various indications, mostly negative ones, invite us to believe so.” In the Addenda 
(1947 : 222-223), however, Renou shows much hesitation and gives 
several references which in his view could point to “writing in the 
Vedic texts” ; the most important among these would be his reference 
to a Śrauta-sūtra of the Sāmavedins, LŚS 5.12.16 yathādhītam (gāyet), 
which Renou translates as “(he should chant) according to what is 
read” (“conformément à ce qui est lu”). However, this expression 
rather indicates that the priest has to chant the text as he learned it 
(when he was a student), without modification. By no means does the 
choice of words or the context necessitate an interpretation in terms 
of reading a written text. On the other hand, if we understand the 
sūtra as a reference to the period of study of the priest, the statement is 
in accordance with the practice of the Sāmavedic priest during the 
Vājapeya (in a ritual episode where he first has to sing SV 435 with 
modifications such as leaving out ā at the beginning of the Udgīthapart and then “as studied” before chanting it) and it also suits the 
commentary that explains yathādhītam as aviktam “not modified 
(without leaving out ā, etc.).” Another example from which Renou 
tries to derive an indication for writing is LŚS 1.1.25 yajur-antaraye 
’nvāhāran dhānañjayaḥ, according to which, in the view of the ritual 
authority Dhānañjaya, a formula is to be uttered subsequently in case 
it has been (inadvertently) skipped; according to the next sūtra LŚS 
the ritual authority Śāṇḍilya is of the opinion that in such case the 
omission is to be neglected. Renou somehow wants to conclude from 
this the existence of a written help as a kind of backup. However, the 
performer forgot to employ a formula at the required moment in the 
ritual, but he did not forget its text altogether and forever. If everything goes from memory, as is still the ideal in modern Vedic performances, there is nothing that would point to such a written text, 
whether or not anything is immediately recited at the proper occasion 
or remembered afterwards and then recited subsequently (according 
to Dhānañjaya) or neglected (Śāṇḍilya). 
In contrast with Renou’s desperate attempts to find indications for 
writing in the early Vedic tradition, Falk (1993 : 255, 284, 321-327) 
argues convincingly in favour of the orality and non-familiarity with 
writing in ancient Indian texts. Rules concerned with possible omis-

--- Page 34 of 338 ---
jan e.m. houben and saraju rath
18
sions of a Vedic priest are, indeed, of particular interest for the 
question of the availability or absence of a written text of the Veda. 
Falk refers (1993 : 255) to Baudhāyana’s Dharma-sūtra BDhS 3.9.8 
apratibhāyāṃ yāvatā kālena na veda tāvantaṃ kālaṃ tad adhīyīta sa 
yadā jānīyād ṛkto yajuṣṭaḥ sāmata iti / 3.9.9 tad-brāhmaṇaṃ tacchāndasaṃ tad-daivatam adhīyīta “If he has forgotten (a passage), he 
shall recite for as long a time as he does not recollect it, what (he may 
know, k-verses) for k-verses, (yajus-formulae) for yajus-formulae, 
(sāmans) for sāmans. He may (also) recite the Brāhmaṇa of that (forgotten passage) or (the passage in the Anukramaṇī regarding) its 
metre and deity” (transl. follows Bühler, with adaptations). Here, 
apratibhāyām really expresses that a passage does not come to mind, 
and the performer does not search for a written text to help his memory but continues with a text which is similar in nature (as verse, formula or chant) or which is a Brāhmaṇa (explanatory prose-passage) 
corresponding to it or which concerns the metre or deity of the forgotten text. 
2.5  Buddhist texts provide several clear statements and indications 
that their tradition was at first entirely oral and that the written fixation of the oral tradition required conscious and extensive efforts a 
considerable time after the Buddha (cf. Falk 1993: 284-289). Among 
the relevant passages there is one that shows that this tradition was 
occasionally confronted with a problem similar to the one discussed 
for Vedic culture. A community of monks could forget a section or 
part of the several texts it was expected to recite at specific regular 
occasions. A single out of several examples discussed by Scharfe (2002: 
23-24) should do. 
“In the Mahāvagga II 17.3f (PTS p. 116) the Buddha responds to the 
question what a community of monks is to do when none of them are 
competent to recite the pātimokkha at their uposatha-ceremony at the 
evenings of the full and new moon. ‘These monks, o monks, are 
instantly to send one monk to the neighboring residence [of monks] 
(with the words): Go, friend, and come back when you have learned 
the pātimokkha abridged or in full extent’.”
The preceding story is set in a time which was most probably indeed 
predominantly oral, a few centuries before Aśoka, but the history of 
the Chinese Buddhist monk Fa Xian (Fa-Hsien), around 400 ce, 
belongs to a time when written documents played an important role 
in the transmission of Buddhist doctrine. From his home country in

--- Page 35 of 338 ---
manuscript culture: contours and parameters
19
China, which had its own long tradition of (non-alphabetical) writing, 
Fa Xian set out to search manuscripts of the Vinaya in India but found 
that they are transmitted orally wherever he went in Northern India. 
Among Buddhist texts the Vinaya, dealing with the internal order in 
monasteries, is considered to be a relatively intimate and secret part in 
the Piṭaka. Finally, Fa Xian could obtain a written text in Central 
India, in a Mahāyāna monastery in Pāṭaliputra patronized by lay followers (Demiéville 1951: 246 note 1). 
Two centuries later the Chinese Buddhist monk Yì jìng (I-Tsing) 
had a similar purpose when he undertook a travel to India and neighbouring countries. In the report of his travel he has the following to 
say on the Brāhmans (Brahmins) and their Vedas. “The scriptures 
[which] they [the Brahmins] revere are the four Vedas, containing 
about 100,000 verses; ... the meaning of the word [Veda] is ‘clear 
understanding’ or ‘knowledge’. The Vedas have been handed down 
from mouth to mouth, not transcribed on paper or leaves. In every 
generation there exist some intelligent Brāhmans who can recite the 
100,000 verses. In India there are two traditional ways by which one 
can attain to great intellectual power. Firstly, by repeatedly committing to memory the intellect is developed; secondly, the alphabet fixes 
one’s ideas.34 By this way, after a practice of ten days or a month, a 
student feels his thoughts rise like a fountain, and can commit to 
memory whatever he has once heard [not requiring to be told twice]. 
This is far from being a myth, for I myself have met such men.”35 
From China itself the following report is of interest. “When in 410 
Buddhayaśas, the Kashmiri, master of Kumārajīva, who in China was 
nicknamed ‘the great Vibhāṣā’ and ‘the Vibhāṣā with red moustache’, 
claimed to start a translation of the Vinaya of Dharmagupta from 
memory, some suspicion arose in the Chinese clergy. To put him to a 
test, the Emperor Yao Hing of the Ts’in gave him three days to learn 
by heart two text-scrolls (kiuan) of medicinal recipes and of records of 
feudal rent payments (he knew Chinese, having long lived at Kashgar 
and Kucha): in all, according to various sources, some forty or eighty 
leaflets, or 50,000 Chinese characters. Buddhayaśas recited [the texts 
of these scrolls] without making a mistake in the weight of an ingredi34  H.-Y. Hu-von Hinüber quoted by Falk (1993: 289) checked the Chinese original and proposes a different translation for the passage dealing with the second way, 
which would not involve any reference to the alphabet but to fixing the mind or 
calming the nerves. 
35  Translation of J. Takakusu 1896:182-183.

ent [in the medicinal recipe] nor in a figure of the inventory of rent

The last three stories point to the existence of a strong memory culture, but they are to be placed in a long transitional period in which 
writing was at first not at all used for philosophical, religious and scientific texts (at least till Aśoka, mid-third century bce) and later on 
gradually developed for this purpose over several centuries. This was 
parallel to a turn from Prakritic dialects to Sanskrit.37 Whatever 
remained of the ancient memory culture in the early centuries ce does 
point to a preceding strong memory culture in a period of primary 
orality in all fields of textual transmission except perhaps the ephemeral domain of commercial administration. 
After Aśoka a period of several centuries starts in which written 
texts competed with an oral tradition that remained relatively strong 
in the central land of Buddhism, in northern India. Whatever this 
combination of oral and written transmission could preserve, it also 
allowed for considerable variation and change. This is clear from the 
various Chinese translations of fundamental Buddhist texts which 
have the same title but are nevertheless every one or two centuries 
again different (Vetter 1994: 138-139, 159). Even if the Buddhists 
might have borrowed techniques of memory culture from the traditional transmitters of the Ṛgveda, they did not insist in all respects on 
the same literally identical transmission as in the case of the Ṛgveda.38 
Especially in Buddhism we see the validity of the general statement of 
Ong (1982: 15), approvingly quoted by Scharfe (2002: 20), that “literacy ... consumes its own oral antecedents and, unless it is carefully 
monitored, even destroys their memory.” As we saw above, Fa Xian, 
after extensive searches, could finally obtain a manuscript of the

Vinaya in a Mahāyāna monastery patronized by lay followers. There 
he was apparently told that it dated from the time of the Buddha.39
With regard to textual transmission the situation in India around 
the beginning of the Common Era, from a few centuries before till a 
few centuries after the year zero, can now be summarized as follows. 
Three types of textual transmission can be distinguished. Around two 
centuries after the Buddha, the transmission of Buddhist texts was still 
predominantly oral in the heartland (northern part of the Indian subcontinent) but the transmission through manuscripts was on the 
increase. It initially may have developed not only in northwestern 
border areas (Gandhāra) and areas outside India (Central Asia) where 
ancient writing systems (old-Persian cuneiform, Aramaic) were 
already current, but also in South India, including the area around 
Nagarjunakonda where an important early artistic representation of a 
scribe is found in connection with a scene on the life of the Buddha 
(see colour Plate 1a, 1b, 1c). Buddhist monks displayed remarkable 
skills in memorization, but the transmission left scope for usually only 
marginal and formulaic variation. The quality of transmission was 
therefore comparable to that of the oral epic tradition. The Indian 
epics, Mahābhārata and Rāmāyaṇa, are estimated to have started their 
careers as written texts (after an initial oral period of undeterminable 
extent) in this period, from a few centuries before till a few centuries 
after the beginning of Common Era. The Jainas organized their canon 
and wrote it down in around the same period, in the 5th century ce 
“Devarddhi Gaṇin, says the tradition, perceiving the Siddhānta in 
danger of becoming extinct, caused it to be written in books. Before 
that time teachers made no use of written books when teaching the 
Siddhānta to novices, but after that time they did use books.”40

to forgetting the transition from orality to written transmission of Buddhist scriptures.

An oral textual tradition of a different nature is the one concerned 
with the Ṛgveda and other Vedic texts. It does, in fact, not suffice to 
characterize it as “oral” since it also depends significantly on a memory culture. In the words of Staal (1986: 27), this tradition “is by far 
the more remarkable, not merely because it is characteristically Indian 
and unlike anything we find elsewhere, but also because it has led to 
scientific discoveries that are of enduring interest …”
Which evidence is there to show that the Ṛgveda was indeed transmitted orally in a fixed way since at least 1200 bce? That they must 
precede the early Buddhists41 and Pāṇini (4th cent. bce42) is evident 
because both presuppose a developed system of Vedic rituals and 
texts, but that still leaves a large margin. Staal, as we have seen, gave an 
impressionistic argument why the Ṛgveda must be so old even if it was 
transmitted orally: it “exhibited from the beginning a high level of 
poetic sophistication comprising a variety of meters—about fifteen in 
all—that sets it apart also in formal terms from the later epic literature 
of India.” In reply to a remark by G. Possehl (1998: 121) that the long 
period of oral transmission of the Ṛgveda would leave so much scope 
for substantial change, Scharfe pointed out (2002: 27-28 note 102) 
that the very early stability of the text of the Ṛgveda is confirmed by 
the “quotations in the traditions of other Vedic schools” such as the 
Sāmaveda and in the ancient Brāhmaṇas, “with minimal deviations—
and most of these deviations can be shown to be secondary.” The net41  Because of the “epic oral” character of the transmission in early centuries after 
the Buddha it is not always possible to clearly distinguish a contribution of the Buddha and one of early Buddhists. The date of the Buddha’s mahāparinibbāna is now 
considered to be around 400 BCE: Bechert 1991-1997; Cousins 1996.
42  Both von Hinüber 1989: 34 and Falk 1993: 304, refer to AA 5.2.120 and currently available reasonably secure datings in numismatics (Cribb 1985; cf. now 2005) 
to arrive at a date for Pāṇini around 350 bce (von Hinüber: “kaum lange vor etwa 
350 v. Chr.”; Falk: “in den Jahrzehnten nach 350 v. Chr.”). Since Pāṇini belonged to 
the north-west of the Indian subcontinent—Śalātura, north of the Kabul river 
(kúbhā) and west of the Indus, is mentioned as his native place—he must have been 
well aware of the political and economic importance of Persia (even if he does not 
“mention most satrapies of the Achaemenid Empire within which [he] lived or even 
the empire itself,” Schwartzberg 1992: 168 col. 2). It therefore cannot be entirely 
excluded that his rūpya (formed according to AA 5.2.120) referred to Persian or 
Greek coins as Scharfe (2009: 28) pointed out. In view of Pāṇini’s linguistic and cultural orientation towards the north of the Indian subcontinent (Schwartzberg 1992: 
16, 167-168) it would nevertheless be more likely that reference is made to an Indian 
coin, perhaps still locally issued but with “pan-Indian” circulation (cf. Cribb 2005: 
69). Pāṇini brought to perfection a vast body of existing knowledge as his grammar 
refers to various preceding authorities, including the author of the Padapāṭha of the 
Ṛg-veda, Śākalya, and the author of the Padapāṭha of the Sāma-veda, Gārgya.

manuscript culture: contours and parameters
23
work of quotations and minor variations that substantiates Scharfe’s 
remark is analyzed in voluminous works by the American indologist 
Maurice Bloomfield, the Vedic Concordance (1906), Ṛgveda Repetitions 
(1916), and Vedic Variants (1930-1934). Most Vedic textual variants 
such as the quotations of the Ṛgveda in the Yajurvedic and Sāmavedic 
Saṁhitās predate Pāṇini, whose grammar shows evidence of a preceding tradition of grammatical and linguistic concerns with details in 
these texts—details in accent and phonetic variation which are so 
minute that the overarching, long-term stability of the main texts is all 
the stronger confirmed. 
The third major mode of textual transmission in this period, apart 
from the “oral epic” transmission and the Vedic one, is the transmission through written materials, especially manuscripts. Bordering to 
the Chinese cultural sphere in the north-east and to Greater Iran and 
indirectly to the Near East in the west where different systems of writing had been intensively used (some since several millennia bce), the 
Indian subcontinent is remarkably late in turning to writing for the 
transmission of its texts. The continuous presence of a strong oral 
tradition and textual memory culture (manifest in its “oral epic” 
mode—also employed for Buddhism etc.—and in its “Vedic” mode) 
determines the attitude towards written sources over a long transitional period. 
2.6  Having reviewed Staal’s and Falk’s important criticisms of 
Goody’s theory and its application to the Veda—first, science and 
even rationality originate from literacy, and, second, the meticulous 
transmission of Vedic texts must be due to the use of writing—and 
some of the relevant evidence at the basis of these criticisms,43 we may 
briefly return to Goody. 
Goody, who was praised by Halverson for responding to criticisms, 
subsequently neglected not only the criticism of Halverson, but also 
that of Staal and Falk. In his next important publication on the theme 
of orality and writing, The Power of the Written Tradition (2000), he 
43  Supplements to these criticisms are found in the chapter on Oral Tradition in 
Scharfe 2002: 8-37, who observes (2002: 23) : “Ong’s and Goody’s denial of the orality of much of the Indian tradition contradicts evidence from a multitude of 
sources.” Relevant is also Scharfe’s chapter on Memorizing the Veda (2002: 240-251) 
and the chapter in a more recent work of Scharfe on Pāṇini’s grammar (2009: 66-84) 
where the author explains why this grammar could not have been written (and must

24
shifts his discussion to a new area, that of the social implications of 
writing—the power which the written word gives to “cultures that 
possess writing over purely oral ones” and the power it gives to those 
who possess it to those who do not within the same society (Goody 
2000: 1)—without responding either to earlier general objections to, 
what Halverson (1992b: 301) called, the “‘cognitive’ claims of the literacy thesis,” or to the specific refutations (by Staal and Falk) regarding his attempts to apply the general theory to South Asia. A detailed 
analysis of the various arguments of Goody in his various books and 
those of his (partial) critics is here not possible. Here we only ask: 
what does it mean if Goody in 2000 can give several more or less convincing examples of parallels between the possession of writing and 
the occupation of a socially and politically powerful position, of a literate society versus an oral society or of a literate segment of a society 
versus an oral one, without having properly established the cognitive 
claims of the literacy thesis—with regard to which it is suggested that 
it can replace earlier attempts to explain cultural differences by invoking properties of a “primitive mind” (Lévy-Bruhl)? This can only 
mean that Goody’s “social power” claims regarding literacy are hanging in the air as they can have no logical connection with the nonestablished “cognitive claims.” It leaves open the possibility that 
Goody did not identify the appropriate parameter in the “cognitive” 
level. 
Indeed, it is likely that this is what happened with regard to orality 
and writing in India: Goody’s general theory is to be reconsidered in 
the light of the evidence provided by India, just as the theory that all 
swans are white is to be reconsidered if we see even a single black 
swan. In ancient India, in the light of available evidence (as reviewed 
above), it is not the writing per se which provides special access to 
stable knowledge and a cognitively dominant position to some groups 
versus others who would be deprived of it, but rather the use of techniques for an explicit transmission of knowledge from generation to 
generation. Such techniques may consist in writing, if those who possess it are opposed to those who have neither writing nor an alternative technique. But these techniques for the explicit transmission of 
knowledge may also consist in techniques of oral transmission and 
memory culture, if these techniques oppose those who possess them 
to those who possess neither these nor writing. Even if writing is 
adopted, the possession of techniques of oral transmission and memory culture may provide the group possessing them a definite edge

--- Page 41 of 338 ---
manuscript culture: contours and parameters
25
over those that have no access to it. If the parameter of “writing” is to 
be given up in favour of “technique for reliable transmission” which 
comprises, among other things, writing, then orality and memory culture in ancient India—and their scarce and pale remnants in a handful 
of traditional schools, pāṭha-śālās, in present-day India—may also be 
able to prove Derrida right versus Goody, when Goody (2000: 109118) is unwilling to accept even the logical possibility of “difference” 
without physical “writing,” i.e., the logical possibility that the orally 
transmitted word would possess some of the properties otherwise 
observed with the written word.44 
Were techniques of oral transmission and memory culture sufficiently advanced to give ancient India a significant—culturally, 
socially, scientifically significant—edge over neighbouring areas, and 
to make it, at least for a considerable period, immune to attempts to 
increase the domain of writing? The outsiders’ perspective provided 
by Chinese Buddhist monks, the sincere surprise they evince with the 
memorization skills of the Indian monks, suggests we should reply to 
this question positively. This leads us to a subsequent question: how 
“unique” are the Indian oral tradition and Indian textual memory culture? We should ask this question especially with regard to the Vedic 
tradition, as the other mode of oral tradition, the one of the “oral 
epics” and of Buddhism etc., is more similar to epic traditions world 
wide. 
2.7  A text that is in several respects similar to the Ṛgveda, the Avesta, 
has been transmitted over numerous centuries by the Zoroastrians, 
those of Iran and those whose forefathers had come to India. The 
Avesta, the older parts of which have been dated to around a millennium bce,45 is similar to the Ṛgveda linguistically, in the use of formulas and epithets, in religious and mythological conceptions, and in the 
ritual presupposed. The manuscripts at the basis of the text currently 
available in print go back to a Sassanid archetype of ca. 400 ce or one 
to two centuries later (Hoffmann & Narten 1989: 90-91). This written

archetypal text itself stood at the end of a long development on the 
basis of early liturgical texts in Old Avestan which were orally transmitted by priests who already spoke Young Avestan in various stages 
of development. The transmission of texts which were linguistically 
older than the current language of the transmittors involved a redaction, an orthoepic diaskeuasis, resulting in a fixation of the text, perhaps first orally but eventually in writing, in a form that is closer to the 
word-by-word version (Pada-pāṭha) than to the continuous version 
(Saṁhitā-pāṭha) of the Ṛgveda.46 Can the Padapāṭha-like form of the 
Avesta be taken as an indication of a memory culture similar to that 
current in ancient India? Does one tradition derive from the other—if 
so, which one from which—or both from an earlier, Indo-Iranian 
one? It has been suggested, for instance by Michael Witzel, that the 
Padapāṭha-like character of the Avesta, together with a few other indications, would point to an “old Indo-Iranian tradition of dealing with 
texts” (Witzel 1996: 323). 
Recently, Hartmut Scharfe (2009: 80-83) had a closer look at the 
evidence and asked: How do Vedic Padapāṭhas and the Avestan 
manuscripts compare in detail? First of all, the Ṛgveda Padapāṭha 
consistently gives words in their non-sandhi form, but the Avesta is 
quite inconsistent. Nominal compounds are separated in the Veda 
with only one separation in case of compounds with multiple members; in the Avesta a compound may be separated twice resulting into 
a division into three members. The ṚV Padapāṭha consistently separates certain nominal endings such as -bhis, but the Avesta sometimes 
does this and often not. The ṚV Padapāṭha never separates verbal 
endings, but the Avesta does it a few times. From these observations 
Scharfe inferred that a sophisticated Indian tradition has given rise to 
an imperfect application in Iran. Other indications he brought forward to show that “Indian grammatical science was available in 
Sassanid Iran” (Scharfe 2009: 84).47 
Perhaps the Avesta scholars of the Sassanid period were indeed 
influenced by Indian grammatical science and techniques for textual 
transmission. The text to which Scharfe referred to support his point, 
46  Cf. also Malandra (2002: 223): “To a certain extent the received text or Vulgate 
is analogous to the Padapāṭha of the ṚgVeda rather than to the Saṁhitā.” 
47  Scharfe (2009: 84) points out that the Dēnkart (Dēnkard), “a Zoroastrian text 
based on material from the Sassanid period,” contains, at IV 99-100, the Sanskrit 
terms “‘βy’krn, i.e. vyākaraṇa ‘grammar’,” together with kōšāk (i.e., Sanskrit kośa

the Dēnkart (Dēnkard), is regarded as an “encyclopedia of the religious sciences as known in the 9th-10th centuries”; it “is not ... a systematic treatment” but rather “a compilation of preserved materials” 
(Gignoux 1994). The work contains an “enumeration of scientific 
works from Greece and India,” which probably includes the references to Indian grammatical science, and which is considered to reveal 
“foreign influence from the 3rd century onward” (Gignoux 1994). The 
third century would be the time when Avesta ritualists would be starting to commit their text to writing. But at that time the older parts of 
the collection, those in Old Avestan, had already been transmitted, 
apparently orally, for more than a millennium (Hoffmann & Narten 
1989: 88ff.). 
2.8  We may therefore approach the question regarding textual 
memory culture from a different angle. Whatever the specific technique or techniques that were followed to memorize the text and to 
transmit it faithfully, there was in the Avestan milieu in Iran, as in the 
Vedic environment in India, an enormous interest—a passion, perhaps even an obsession—to transmit the sacred text in a phonetically 
exact way and next to pronounce it completely correct at required 
occasions such as regular rituals. Moreover, if, as it seems to be the 
case, the Avesta ritualists started to commit their texts to writing only 
in the fifth or sixth century ce, they were earlier than in India, but they 
had apparently been exposed more intensively and for a much longer 
time to writing, as writing was intensively used in the Near East and in 
ancient Iran much earlier than in India. Both in ancient Iran and in 
India from the beginning of ce the Avestan milieu and the Vedic 
milieu were for a long time quite immune to pressures or temptations 
to put their precious texts to writing. From this circumstantial evidence we would infer that some techniques for faithful oral transmission and memorization were probably in use among Zoroastrian 
priests in Iran from an ancient period onwards. 
That this enormous interest to transmit a text in a phonetically 
exact way and next to recite it at special occasions—in other words, a 
culture of textual memorization and recitation—dates indeed back to 
an earlier date than what is usually regarded as the earliest Vedic 
period, ca. 1200-1750 bce, is also suggested from a different perspective, by comparing esp. Greek and Indian traditions and conceptualizations of sacred texts. Whereas it is more or less a universal 
phenomenon that ancient cultures employ prayers in rituals and take

--- Page 44 of 338 ---
jan e.m. houben and saraju rath
28
special care of the most sacred and powerful texts which are collected 
and transmitted, it is not a matter of course that the preserved texts try 
to capture divine speech rather than human speech addressed to the 
gods, and that these texts containing divine speech should be memorized and should be recited at specific ritual or regular occasions, with 
subsequent generations ending up spending a large part of their time 
in memorizing, repeating and reciting the speech of their ancestors. 
In a recent brilliant philosophical-historical study, Alexis Pinchard 
(2009) has shown that Plato was not only standing at the beginning of 
the tradition we celebrate as “western philosophy and science” but 
also at the end of a preceding one which distinguishes between names 
in the language of men and names in the language of the gods, a difference that was originally established by poets and that was present in 
most Indo-European literary traditions, especially also in the one that 
finds expression in the Ṛgveda. Among several “family-resemblances” 
between Indo-European poetic traditions there is, indeed, the profound mistrust of writing that is apparently the other side of the coin 
of textual memory culture which we have encountered in the Vedic 
and early Avestan traditions. 
“We find in Plato, indeed, a restriction in the use of writing. Such 
restriction, in force in the oldest Indo-European poetic traditions, has 
a purpose: to preserve, from one individual to the other, the vitality of 
knowledge, that is, wisdom. For Plato, writing puts this vitality in danger because, as a material support, it provides information from the 
outside, through the senses, and thus it risks to divert the soul from its 
inner quest.”
“... writing taken too seriously tends to hinder the personal reminiscence of realities perceived before birth ...” 48
“Writing does not go well with the primitive language of the gods : as 
it crystallizes a message once and for all instead of leaving it open to 
the variation of a “hermetic” tradition, it prevents its anchorage in the 
spontaneity of the speaker.”49
48  “Nous trouvons chez Platon, en effet, une limitation dans l’usage de l’écrit, 
dont la finalité demeure congruente à celle en vigueur dans les plus anciennes traditions poétiques indo-européennes : préserver, d’un individu à l’autre, la vitalité du 
savoir, autrement dit la sagesse. Chez Platon, l’écriture met cette vitalité en danger 
parce que, en tant que support matériel, elle apporte des informations depuis le 
dehors, par l’intermédiaire des sens, et risque ainsi de détourner l’âme de sa quête 
intérieure. ... l’écriture prise trop au sérieux tend à faire obstacle à la réminiscence 
personnelle des réalités perçues avant la naissance ...” (Pinchard 2009 : 305). 
49  “L’écriture ne saurait faire bon ménage avec la langue des dieux primitive car, 
en figeant un message une fois pour toutes au lieu de le laisser ouvert à la variation

“It should by no means surprise us, therefore, that the evidence of a 
refusal of writing by members of the priestly or divinatory class in 
Indo-European civilizations is so abundant, even when writing was 
often known and used in other contexts. Such a refusal is merely a 
matter of recognizing the natural impossibility of wisdom to be passed 
on as a book. Wisdom is what it is only as long as it establishes by itself 
its distance from writing.”50 
“It is therefore a fatal misinterpretation to see in the refusal of writing 
by the wise the will to keep from the public some mythological or 
ritual information; ... the problem is rather to maintain the memory as 
an intellectual faculty to regain access at the ancient by always new 
paths ...”51
If the Indian Vedic priests had in common with the “priestly or divinatory class in Indo-European civilizations” not only a related language and poetic devices, but also a profound aversion to writing, this 
would indeed contribute significantly to an explanation of their 
remarkable investments in a textual memory culture that was unique 
in its perfection and efficiency even if it has survived in modern India 
only as an entirely marginalised institution. This would suffice to show 
not only that Goody’s (and Ong’s) theories are not universally valid—
that they have failed to properly identify the parameters at play in 
India—but also why they are not valid. 
Incidentally, recognizing Indo-European parallels and patterns 
does not imply the acceptance of massive invasions of Aryan tribes, or 
in some other way massive displacements of genetic material. The 
basis of the perceived parallels and patterns is rather to be sought in 
“memetic” relations, rendered possible by the attested forms of “memory culture” and their antecedents. The links may have been established by numerically marginal but culturally significant bands or 
hermétique, elle empêche son ancrage dans la spontanéité du locuteur.” (Pinchard 
2009 : 305)
50  “Point ne doit nous surprendre, donc, que les témoignages d’un refus de 
l’écriture par les membres de la classe sacerdotale ou divinatoire dans les civilisations 
indo-européennes soient si nombreux, alors même que l’écriture y était souvent connue et utilisée dans d’autres contextes. Un tel refus ne fait que prendre acte d’une 
impossibilité congénitale, pour la sagesse, de se transmettre comme on transmet un 
livre. La sagesse n’est ce qu’elle est qu’autant qu’elle instaure elle-même son écart face 
à l’écriture.” (Pinchard 2009 : 306)

“guilds” of itinerant priests of the type of Vasiṣṭha’s descendants of
