It is to be noted, moreover, that Greece and ancient India, the two 
civilizations which provide the best illustrations for Pinchard’s philosophical Indo-European thesis, also with regard to their aversion to 
writing, are at the boundary of the Indo-European expansive “wave” 
where they were in contact with various highly developed non-IndoEuropean civilizations which possess ancient traditions of (some form 
of) writing (in Egypt, in Mesopotamia, perhaps in the Indus-civili
zation). Even if the aversion to writing has Indo-European parallels, 
the specific form of the oral technique of memorization and transmission, the splitting of sentences into words, has no clear parallel among 
other Indo-European peoples. Is it not rather a mnemotechnic device 
within orality that tries to compete with and even to outdo a form of 
writing which represents speech first of all in a form that separates 
words and obvious members of compounds? The proximity of the 
intensive use of (forms of) writing may indeed have inspired or incited 
the Iranians and Indians responsible for the Avestan and Vedic texts 
to pay extraordinary attention to their faithful, oral transmission. The 
form of writing would be one in which words are emphatically separated, either a (largely) logographic form of writing, or a syllabic 
alphabetic form, in use in a community with which the Indo-Iranians 
would have been in close contact, geographically and/or culturally. 
Because the interest in a word-for-word version is evident both in the 
Avestan and in the Vedic tradition, one could think of a very early 
influence at the time the two were geographically close or partly overlapping: this would require the influence of a very early script in the 
second millennium bce However, ancient Vedic poets, even if they 
are remarkably reflective about the creative processes at the basis of 
their own poetry, show nowhere any interest in a distinction between 
a word-for-word version and a continuous version, so that the former 
seems to be a later invention imposed on the latter. Pāṇini is aware of 
an author of the word-for-word version, Śākalya, who must have preceded Pāṇini, but not too much.52 We could then think of the influ52  The mature work of Śākalya must have had predecessors. Pāṇini himself refers

It is in any case remarkable that, as the Vedic Padapāṭha marks the

continuous text is to be written down in a script that marks word 
boundaries and gives phonetic details of these words. The Padapāṭha,

to the realm of early near-alphabetic writing, it would be easier to 
imagine that attempts had already been made to actually write it down 
before the production of the “Sassanid archetype,” and that it is these 
early attempts which have given the Avesta a partially “Padapāṭhalike” look.

Within a few centuries and starting in Magadha, an area ca. 1000 
km to the east, young Brahmins trained in the Vedic system of oral 
education must have taken along their intellectual baggage and especially their mnemotechnic skills when they converted to Buddhism or 
Jainism55—a not infrequent event in the early centuries before and 
after the beginning of ce—so that these religions too continued to 
value oral transmission and textual memory culture, and shifted comparatively late and slowly to a written transmission of their sacred 
texts. The first to shift to writing was no doubt the religion with the 
most intensive and extensive international ties, Buddhism, and it 
slowly but surely drew along the epics, various sciences such as medicine and grammar, Jainism, auxiliary disciplines of the Vedic tradition, and finally, but only by way of back up, the central Vedic texts of 
mantra and brāhmaṇa. The extent of the use of writing for text transmission can therefore be regarded as inversely proportional to the 
strength of techniques of oral transmission and textual memory culture. With this we have at once the earliest contour of manuscript culture—the initial period after which it became well established—and a 
parameter for manuscript culture when it is already well established, a 
parameter that in India remained valid as long as oral text transmission and memory culture were in vogue, which means minimally till 
the last century (Kerala, Godavari delta, Maharashtra) if we consider 
present day relics, vestiges and revitalizations of the Vedic tradition 
too marginal or too artificial. 
In the light of the preceding we propose the following scenario for 
the emergence and development of manuscript culture on the Indian 
subcontinent: 
A. If there is an Indo-European dimension to the attested forms of 
aversion to writing in India and perhaps in ancient Zoroastrianism, this aversion led to the development of an exceptionally 
strong memory culture here (but not in other areas of Indo-European expansion) because of the proximity of cultures of writing 
which nevertheless, for a certain period in Iran and for a longer 
55  Moreover, as Gombrich (1988: 31) observed: “it would never have occurred to 
the Buddhists that such a feat of preservation was even possible had they not had 
before them the example of the brahmins.”

--- Page 49 of 338 ---
manuscript culture: contours and parameters
33
period in India, did not succeed in overwhelming the oral memory 
culture.56 
B. In any case, Indian manuscript culture started to develop not earlier than the 3rd cent. bce (Aśokan inscriptions) against the background of an extraordinarily strong, purely oral memory culture 
for “sacred” texts, Vedic and initially also Buddhist and Jaina, 
which for some undetermined period went parallel with writing 
for ephemeral contexts.
C. Once writing is accepted to transmit “sacred” texts, first those of 
Buddhism and perhaps first in the north-west and in “marginal” 
areas such as the south, a masssive transition took place which 
gradually also involved secular disciplines such as medicine and 
grammar, philosophical Brahmanism and Jainism and other religious currents, and finally, but still as if unwillingly, central Vedic 
texts. The shift from oral to written text involved also a shift from 
Prakritic (MIA) to Sanskrit. 
2.9  The contour of the earliest beginnings of manuscript culture can 
therefore be placed at around the time of Aśoka’s inscriptions, middle 
of the third century bce, assuming that it was a landmark after which 
textual transmission in writing and on manuscripts appeared at the 
horizon of possibilities also for larger texts than the royal announcements of Aśoka. Soon afterwards the currently available oldest 
manuscripts in Kharoṣṭhī appear to the North West of the Indian sub
continent. It is tempting to conclude that they are not by chance the 
oldest ones found but indeed the remains of the first commitments to 
writing. The association of this emerging manuscript culture with a 
still strong culture of oral textual transmission make it likely that no 
great impact is perceived initially, that the written texts, for instance 
in Buddhism, are nothing more than an additional tool, felt to be 
somewhat superfluous, for the oral transmission and memory culture. 
56  The Iranian and Indian ancestors of the authors of the Avesta and the authors 
of the Ṛgveda, who shared an interest in exact oral transmission with other IndoEuropean communities but who were unique in the level of technical sophistication 
they reached herein, were sufficiently close to and familiar with current writing systems to be incited to create a competitive alternative in the form of oral techniques 
for faithful transmission, but they were far enough removed geographically (esp. the 
authors of the Veda after the collapse of the Indus civilization who were expanding 
towards the east in the northern part of the Indian subcontinent), to develop their 
literature and linguistic disciplines significantly without being overwhelmed by writing.

--- Page 50 of 338 ---
jan e.m. houben and saraju rath
34
Did the change in medium of text transmission lead to a change in 
thought, as it was argued for ancient Greece at the juncture of orality 
(pre-Socratics, Socrates) and literacy (Plato, Aristotle)? In order to 
judge this, we should know what can be regarded as results or implications of the oral transmission of texts. A few points have been enumerated by Scharfe (2002). 
First, “oral tradition has no pity for outdated material, since it 
would be a waste of effort and memory for students to memorize 
obsolete instructions. Pāṇini’s grammar made all previous grammatical analyses of Sanskrit obsolete, and not much more than some 
names remain of his predecessors.” A consequence of this is that “In 
India, perfected systems seem to appear ex nihilo, which led in time to 
the assumption of supernatural revelation” Scharfe (2002: 20f). 
Another characteristic of oral literature—and Scharfe thinks here 
apparently of “oral epic” orality, not of the orality of the Vedic tradition nor of the one of a knowledge system such as grammar—is “frequent repetition, found most prominently in the Buddhist canonical 
texts. ... Furthermore, there is frequent patchwork ... Since corrections 
would be awkward, any improvements are added on like so many 
patches. ... In oral literature there is no sense of copyright” Scharfe 
(2002: 30f). Elaborating on the last point Scharfe shifts to a discussion 
of the absence of “copyright” in the case of manuscripts as well, which, 
moreover, do not have “indices (in our sense of the word), since each 
manuscript of a text would have a different pagination making a page 
index almost useless” (2002: 32). To Scharfe’s observations we may 
add that there is a tendency that expressions of orality remain in vogue 
even when literacy has already spread and has become widely accepted.57 Indeed, several of the properties of purely orally transmitted 
texts are continued in the case of transmission through manuscripts, 
57  For instance, when Bharthari (4th-5th cent.) refers to other authors his references still reflect a mainly oral environment (just as old Greek prose of, for instance, 
Plato still strongly reflects an oral style, Havelock 1986) whereas, more than a millennium later, the references of Bhaṭṭoji Dīkṣita reveal that he is making extensive use 
of written sources. There does not seem to be an absolute difference between the two 
but rather a shift in the balance between orality and literacy. In the MahābhāṣyaDīpikā passages studied in Houben 1999 expressions such as ity etad ācakṣate, ihabhavantas tv āhuḥ which suggest speaking, and varṇayanti which is neutral to the 
contrast speaking/writing, may be compared with passages in Bhaṭṭoji Dīkṣita’s 
Prauḍha-Manoramā, where we find expressions clearly reflecting a scholar making 
an extensive study of written sources, such as yady apīdaṁ kaiyaṭapustakeṣu ... 
dśyate and idānīntanapustakeṣu ... dśyate (cf. Houben 2002:).

35
especially if the manuscript transmission is part of a still reasonably 
strong oral culture. More importantly, there has been hardly any 
reflection on properties of manuscript transmission vis à vis purely 
oral transmission. 
2.10  We would nevertheless like to give some reflections regarding a 
knowledge system that was originally not depending on the transmission of large volumes of text, but in which conceptual criticism of traditional, Brahminical ideas and rituals was central: Sāṁkhya.58 
Especially in its classical and post-classical forms, but also pre-classically, Sāṁkhya has appeared to several Western observers as an 
Indian knowledge system in which “rationality”—distinguished by 
“the presence of reasoned argument for preferring one alternative to 
others”59—plays a remarkable role. To make this definition operational we think of systems in which one of the means to gain knowledge (pramāṇa), namely inference (anumāna), is regarded as more 
important than other means such as verbal testimony (śabda) and 
direct perception (pratyakṣa). Sāṁkhya shared circumstances favoring critical reflection and reconsiderations of established beliefs with 
other early movements and schools, including Buddhism and Jainism. 
A closer study of the available evidence shows that rationality was 
indeed thriving in Sāṁkhya—that anumāna was indeed, nominally 
and de facto, on a primary position—in the earlier stages of its development, the stages that are reflected in the Mahābhārata, for instance. 
In the classical and post-classical time, however, we see that Sāṁkhya 
transforms from a brilliant and critical knowledge system into a somewhat dullish, entirely dogmatic system. 
It can now be argued that one factor contributing to the disappearance of the ancient Sāṁkhya-text the Ṣaṣṭitantra, which apparently 
displayed a high degree of “rationality”, was the method of knowledge 
transmission: this was an extremely laborious process, and investing 
in a laborious process of manuscript transmission presupposes devotion to a tradition. In the course of time, the method of knowledge 
transmission through manuscripts quite generally strengthened and 
reinforced traditionality. It marginalized rational criticism on traditional truths, by a sort of “natural selection”: who wants to invest 
much time in transmitting criticisms which undermine the system to 
58  For a more expanded form of the argument see Houben 1999 and 2001. 
59  Furley 1973: 43.

--- Page 52 of 338 ---
jan e.m. houben and saraju rath
36
which one is devoted, precisely the system for which one is investing 
time in transmitting? A marginalization of rational criticism also took 
place in Sāṁkhya which gradually transformed from a relatively heterogeneous rational movement (reflected e.g. in passages in the 
Mahābhārata, the epic which is thought to have arisen roughly from 
2nd bce—4th ce) into the doctrinal system represented in the 
Sāṁkhya-kārikās (ca. 4th ce) for which a very late Sāṁkhya-sūtra 
appeared in the fifteenth century, with as most important commentary the one by Vijñānabhikṣu (16th century). 
A change in the mode of knowledge transmission was apparently, 
and with some temporal delay to let natural selection do its work, correlated with a change in the balance between the pramāṇas, viz. perception, inference and tradition, the sources of reliable knowledge as 
accepted in most of the Indian philosophical schools. The change in 
mode of knowledge transmission was also correlated with a change in 
social relations and access to knowledge. When philosophical schools 
were switching from a purely oral mode of knowledge transmission to 
the mode of the written, that is, hand-written, text, traditionality was 
reinforced in Sāṁkhya, at the cost of rationality which was its hallmark at earlier times.60
2.11  Here we considered Sāṁkhya of a later period in which the 
transmission went through manuscripts with Sāṁkhya of an earlier 
period when it functioned in an oral environment. Another case concerns Indian Āyurvedic texts, especially passages in these texts that 
deal with the classification of animals. In a study on animals in Indian 
ecology and medicine, Francis Zimmermann (1982, 1987) studied 
and analysed the “styles of thought” in the Indian and the Western, 
Greco-Latin tradition, in order to find an answer to this question. 
More concretely, Zimmermann studied, on the one hand, the “scales 
of beings” found in Indian texts, especially in Āyurveda, the Indian 
system of health and longevity, and, on the other hand, the origins of 
Western botany and zoology for which he used especially H. Daudin’s 
study on the methods of classification in botany and zoology, from 
Linnaeus to Lamarck. In his book, Zimmermann argued for two positions. First, following the suggestions of Daudin, Zimmermann finds 
60  A somewhat similar “natural selection” argument has been employed with 
regard to the Kashmirian Udbhaṭa, the undogmatic grammarian and Cārvāka, whose 
positions have not been transmitted to modern times except through references for 
refutation: Bronkhorst 2008.

--- Page 53 of 338 ---
manuscript culture: contours and parameters
37
that two aristotelian ideas have opened the way that led European 
thought from theology to the natural sciences: (a) the idea of a hierarchy of beings (on the basis of a hierarchy of their mental functions); 
and (b) the idea of continuity. Second, on the basis of a survey of 
ancient Indian sources, Zimmermann concludes that in India the 
classification was always ethical, juridical and religious. 
However, passages from texts of the philosophical schools of Vai
śeṣika and Sāṁkhya,61 clearly show that the latter position is untenable, and that India, too, shows a rigour of thought in its dealing with 
the classification of animals. A review of the conditions of the development of botany and zoology in the 17th and 18th century further 
reveals that there is an important difference between the West and 
India not taken into account by Zimmermann. The work and thought 
of Linnaeus presuppose an international community of scholars that 
cannot exist without a means of communication, namely printing, 
that was absent in brahmanical and Hindu India whose texts Zimmer
mann studied. It is further analysed how the difference between India 
and the West is not absolute but a matter of the balance between different ways of fixing and transmitting texts, each of which has profound implications for the organisation of knowledge in the text. 
These different ways include: various ways of oral fixation (e.g. in 
poetry), handwritten texts, and, since the 15th century in Europe, 
printing. The “style of thought” regarding living beings—plants and 
animals, including man—thus appears as heavily conditioned by the 
means of knowledge transmission, which makes the explanatory 
device of a postulated “Indian mind” or “Indian mentality” entirely 
inappropriate, even if we may recognise, impressionistically, a special 
“way of thinking” in the ancient Indian works at our disposal. 
We may here also refer to the theory proposed by Richard Gombrich 
(1988: 29), that “the rise of the Mahāyāna is due to the use of writing.” 
This he clarifies as follows: “the early Mahāyāna texts owe their survival to the fact that they were written down; any earlier texts which 
deviated from or criticized the canonical norms (by which I mean 
approximately the contents of the Vinaya Khandhaka and Sutta 
Vibhaṅga and the Four Nikāya of the Sutta Piṭaka) could not survive 
because they were not included among the texts which the Saṅgha 
61  For instance Padārthadharmasaṁgraha 1.1.30-31. These passages have been 
discussed in Houben 2009a par. 3.1-2.

--- Page 54 of 338 ---
jan e.m. houben and saraju rath
38
preserved orally.”62 With this explanation, if we accept it, we should 
rather say that the emergence of Mahāyāna has been facilitated by 
writing. But even this much would be an important achievement of 
“writing” which means, contextually, mainly manuscript writing. 
In a quite different period and context, a perhaps somewhat similar 
pattern can be discerned. On the basis of an extensive study of textual 
evidence and of the religious background and context of Madhva 
(13th cent.), Roque Mesquita (1997, 2000; review: Houben 2000) concludes that the passages that are cited from “unknown sources,” usually in order to establish points that deviate from the Brahminical 
tradition and from doctrines adhered to by Śaṅkara and other 
Vedāntic teachers, are mainly composed by no-one else than Madhva 
himself. According to Mesquita this does not necessarily mean that 
Madhva was a fraud and forger, as was claimed by roughly contemporaneous opponents and later by Appayya Dīkṣita. In the light of his 
religious background and on the basis of his own statements it can be 
inferred that Madhva sincerely believed that he wrote the passages in 
question under direct inspiration of Viṣṇu, as he regarded himself as 
an incarnation of Vāyu, Viṣṇu’s son.
From the work of Madhva and its reception it is clear that in his 
time (13th century) the study of texts of great cultural and religious 
importance, such as the Mahābhārata, could imply the search and 
study of manuscripts. Contemporary critics were not convinced that 
Madhva had followed proper and generally accepted methods in his 
dealing with the sources. Cf. Mesquita 1997 and Houben 2000: 467. It 
would thus seem that Madhva could innovate in the domain of 
Mahābhārata interpretation (and in the domain of the interpretation 
of the Brahma-sūtra) through references to (real or virtual) written 
sources. 
62  Falk 1993: 327 on this thesis of Gombrich: “Neu ist seine Erklärung für das 
Aufkommen der ältesten erhaltenen Mahāyāna-Texte ... Schriftlich fixierte Texte 
jedoch konnten von einem einzigen Autor verfasst werden, ohne dass eine Ablehnung durch den Saṅgha ihnen hätte schaden können.” Gombrich’s view has been 
criticized as inadequate: Bronkhorst 2000: 129 note 10 and references given there. 
The content of recently discovered very early Buddhist manuscripts in the northwest has not shown any presence of particular Mahāyānist ideas (Salomon 1999). 
This leaves the southern part of the Indian subcontinent, where we find early testimonies of writing and where we find places associated with the name of Buddhist 
teachers such as Nāgārjuna who were important for Mahāyāna, as a possible or even 
likely area of origin of these ideas.

--- Page 55 of 338 ---
manuscript culture: contours and parameters
39
With regard to “features” of the Indian intellectual world, are there 
some that can be attributed to manuscript culture in its peculiar association with orality and memory culture? Have India and its manuscript culture been more favourable to the maintenance of an 
ideo-diversity and doxographic plurality that disappeared in Europe 
due to the early adoption of printing? Would a cooperation between 
the state and the church as witnessed in Europe after the adoption of 
printing, a cooperation which presupposes, on the one hand, the possibility to distribute and impose “correct” translations of the Bible, 
and, on the other hand, the possibility to track down and burn heretical works together with their authors, not have been entirely impossible in a society communicating only through manuscripts?63
The linguistic, rhetorical and philological techniques and devices 
which were developed to enable the thinkers of South Asian philosophical systems to represent their knowledge in a compact, objectified and (also orally) reproducible way (making use, for instance, of 
the sūtra style), for the sake of polemical discussion and for the sake of 
transmission, did they not contribute to a considerably objectified or 
“exosomatic” tradition of knowledge (cf. Popper’s use of the term64), 
which, at least in some periods, allowed for dynamic progress through 
continuing dialectics and refinements, and the work of which was 
hence to some extent equivalent to the work done by “printing” in 
Europe? Was Indian manuscript culture on the other hand conducive 
to endless scholasticism from which Europe could liberate itself in the 
Renaissance while employing the printing press (cf. Eisenstein)? 
Turning to literature and art rather than science, we can, however, 
agree with Sheldon Pollock, that the Indian world is “most probably 
the longest-lived continuous multi-language literary culture” on earth, 
63  There was already an Inquisition in Europe before printing, but it was far 
removed from the efficiency of the later Inquisition. Cf. Lea 1889 in general, and vol. 
3 p. 551ff on Bacon. 
64  Popper, A pluralist approach to the philosophy of history, revised version with 
some additional remarks in Popper 1994:130-153, esp. 134, 149-150: “For me, 
knowledge consists essentially of exosomatic artefacts, or products, or institutions. (It 
is their exosomatic character that makes them rationally criticizable.) There is 
knowledge without a knowing subject—that knowledge, for instance, which is stored 
in our libraries. Thus there can be growth of knowledge without any growth of 
awareness in the knower.” South Asia and its millennia-old oral and written traditions provides large amounts of data with a deep historical dimension to test these 
ideas which were formulated without any reference to this part of the world. On the 
importance of methods of representation and transmission of knowledge incl. the 
development of an artificial language for the growth of knowledge cf. also Staal 1995.

--- Page 56 of 338 ---
jan e.m. houben and saraju rath
40
and that literary culture in pre-modern South Asia “was actually constituted by a manuscript culture that in its material and economic 
aspect was also specific to the subcontinent” and large parts of Asia.65 
3.  Manuscript Culture in India: When and How Did It End? 
3.1  One contour, a temporal boundary, of the huge domain of manuscript culture in India has been determined: it is the time of the first 
emergence of manuscript writing, which started a long transitional 
period in which manuscript culture established itself partly by taking 
over some of the space occupied till then by orality and memory culture. Another contour is the time in which manuscript writing started 
to lose terrain to printing. In Europe this started at the end of the fifteenth century, when Johannes Gutenberg was the first to print Bibles 
instead of having them copied by hand. 
When did India adopt printing? As we have seen in our brief discussion of categorizations of animals in Āyurvedic texts and in the 
emerging zoology in pre-modern Europe, India remained remarkably 
immune to temptations to adopt printing for the transmission of its 
sacred texts, knowledge systems and literatures, just as it had remained 
remarkably immune to temptations to adopt writing for the transmission of its sacred texts, knowledge systems and literatures one and a 
half to two millennia earlier. As at the time of the adoption of writing 
a few centuries before ce, India, with mainly oral text transmission, 
was surrounded by countries in which writing was important, India 
was now, in the last quarter of the second millennium ce, surrounded 
and even invaded by countries and powers where printing was important. 
65  Both quotes from Pollock 2007: 77-78. By no means, however, can we agree 
with Pollock’s assimilation of manuscript use and print. The extreme and exceptional 
example (Pollock 2006: 558) of king Siddharāja Jayasiṁha’s investment of 300.000 
coins to spread Hemacandra’s grammar (and his own fame) “in all lands” cannot 
hide the fact that reproducing a large number of manuscripts is cumbersome and 
implies variation and uncertainty (scribal mistakes) to a far greater extent than in 
printing. There may have been a rudimentary form of script-mercantilism in premodern India as Pollock points out, but where were the manuscript/book shops, 
where the equivalents to print-workshops, where the Grub street hacks of the emerging print-culture in Renaissance and pre-modern Europe? In India “pre-Gutenberg 
Asia” (Eisenstein 1979: 9 note 18 quoted above) continued till 1800 when printing 
houses established in Calcutta had started to fulfil functions in the interest both of 
the colonial powers and of foreign and Indian authors and public.

--- Page 57 of 338 ---
manuscript culture: contours and parameters
41
Quite early in the history of western printing a printing press which 
the Portuguese wanted to send to Abyssinia (Ethiopia) arrived by 
accident in Goa, in 1556, where it was put into use by the Jesuits for 
the printing of missionary publications. The press functioned till 1684. 
Other printing presses started to function seriously only in the eighteenth century, especially in Madras, Bombay and Calcutta. On the 
whole, however, “early attempts proved short-lived and did not lead 
to a development of the press on a nation-wide scale” (Priolkar 1958: 
129). They remained mostly in the hands of missionaries who printed 
Bibles, catechisms, dictionaries, and, towards of the end of the pre1800 period, indigenous books of historical, political and scientific 
interest. Two major obstacles have been mentioned to the development of printing in India. “One was the range and diversity of Indian 
languages and alphabets. The other was political restraint imposed by 
colonial administrations” (Winger 1959: 274). To this we may add 
that text transmission through manuscripts and the sparse remaining 
oral traditions were apparently quite satisfactory to the users, so that 
there was no interest or eagerness to make the heavy investments 
needed to start a printing press. To deflect the mind of those involved 
in transmitting texts would not have been an easy thing. It could have 
been accomplished by a strong administrator. Indeed, it has been proposed that the Maratha king Shivaji got much interested in printing. 
He apparently did make a serious attempt to start a printing press 
when in 1664, during the second sack of Surat, he captured a wooden 
press. Probably he had already understood about the use of the press 
from the Italian traveller Manuchi. With the help of a certain Bhimjee 
Parakh, Shivaji tried to get the required operating knowledge from the 
English, who, however, kept the Indians hanging for years without 
saying no. Apparently, Shivaji got edited books on Rāja-vyavahāraKośa (administrative code), a religious poem Śiva-bhārata by Para
mānanda etc. But they remained in manuscript form only (“Aagastya” 
1984).
3.2  The printing press effectively remained in the hands of missionaries and colonial powers. The printing press was de facto adopted for 
the transmission of Indian texts in Sanskrit and other languages only 
in the early nineteenth century. We are aware of two early cases. 
Serfoji II of Thanjavur, whose political power within colonial India 
was virtually zero, set up a printing press in 1805 and printed some 
Sanskrit and Marathi books. In the mean time several presses in the

--- Page 58 of 338 ---
jan e.m. houben and saraju rath
42
hands of British entrepreneurs were in function in Calcutta since a 
few decades. When the freedom of the Press in India became an issue, 
Englishmen were threatened with deportion in case they would not 
comply with instructions. In spring 1823, in the course of another 
conflict between printers of journals and periodicals and the Governor, 
no Englishman dared to protest a recent encroachment on the freedom of press out of fear of being transshipped. 
At that moment Rammohan Roy and five other Indians stepped 
forward to promote an objection petition in the Supreme Court. This 
was the beginning of the conscious appropriation of the printing press 
by Indians for the diffusion of writings in various languages, from 
newspaper articles to traditional texts. This, in turn, was an important 
factor in the creation of a new “Sanskrit and Hindu” national identity, 
including a printed canon of national texts, in nineteenth century 
Bengal (van Bijlert 1996). The powers of the printing press were 
quickly discovered throughout India. The political successes of Bal 
Gangadhar Tilak were greatly promoted by two newspapers, one in 
English and one in Marathi, co-founded by him in the early 1880s. 
With the whole-hearted adoption of the printing press for all types 
of texts, traditional, educational, political, etc., and with some of the 
Europeans taking more interest in ancient literature than the Indians 
themselves, the production of manuscripts was quickly abandoned—
more quickly and more completely than orality was abandoned at the 
time of the adoption of manuscript writing two millennia earlier. 
Instead of the drive to transmit texts through manuscripts which 
seemed impossible to deflect a century earlier, we now see a relatively 
fast transition to a different trend which gains momentum towards 
the end of the nineteenth century and the beginning of the twentieth: 
the collection of manuscripts in private libraries and government 
institutions. The printing press also enables attempts to give shape, for 
the first time, to a nation-wide canon of edited texts. Among the first 
texts Rammohan Roy brought to the press for publication—between 
1815 and 1819, even before he stepped forward to defend the freedom 
of press—were the five Upaniṣads and the Brahma Sūtra in Sanskrit 
and with Bengali translations (van Bijlert 1996: 353). The problem of 
the production and distribution of manuscripts starts to give way to 
the problem of the collection and conservation of manuscripts and of 
the creation of critical editions on their basis. Manuscript culture 
gives way to print culture. Instead of a King such as Jayasiṁha 
Siddharāja in 12th century Gujarat giving order to 300 copyists to dis-

--- Page 59 of 338 ---
manuscript culture: contours and parameters
43
tribute manuscripts of Hemacandra’s Sanskrit grammar, there is a 
Government of Bombay, or Government of India that sets up manuscript search expeditions and oriental libraries. 
The contour which concludes an Indian manuscript culture is 
therefore to be placed, without much hesitation, at 1800 or at the 
beginning of the nineteenth century, when the conscious adoption 
and institution of print culture starts which brings to a definitive conclusion, in less than a century, the manuscript culture that had been 
blossoming for two millennia. Important dates in this period of emergence of a new Indian print culture and abandonment of manuscript 
culture are 1805 (Serfoji II, Thanjavur, starts a printing press, the 
Navavidya Kalanidhi); 1815-1819 Rammohan Roy prints Upaniṣads 
and Brahma Sūtra with, according to his correspondence, the explicit 
purpose to re-educate the “Hindoos” as nation; and 1823: Rammohan 
Roy and five other Indians step forward to defend freedom of press in 
Bengal. 
4.  (South) Indian Manuscript Culture and 
the Contributions in this Book
4.1  From this brief overview we derive two temporal contours of 
Indian manuscript culture: an earlier one, around the second or third 
century before ce; and a concluding one, more than two millennia 
later, in the first decades of the nineteenth century. We encountered 
some crucial parameters, which have till now hardly been taken into 
consideration. An important and decisive parameter is the balance 
between (a) manuscript culture and (b) orality and memory culture. A 
significant difference with shifts towards alphabetic writing in ancient 
Greece and in other cultures is the availability of extraordinarily efficient techniques of memory culture, not only in a somewhat loose 
“oral epic” mode, but also in an extraordinarily precise “Vedic” mode, 
that precedes literacy and that accompanies a slow transition towards 
writing. Other parameters of Indian manuscript culture are decisive 
for its internal history, but have not surfaced in our preceding brief 
overview: the interaction between different regional languages, different scripts adapted to these languages and to Sanskrit. Moreover, in 
addition to the temporal contours discussed above there are geographic contours to India’s manuscript culture. On the one hand, 
Indian and Indic scripts have transcended the boundaries of the area

--- Page 60 of 338 ---
jan e.m. houben and saraju rath
44
where they developed and have been used far beyond the political 
boundaries of Indian kingdoms and empires, especially in Central 
Asia, South East Asia and the Far East. On the other hand, within 
India boundaries are distinguishable, especially the one between 
“north” and “south” which shows itself in several domains, not only in 
script but also in linguistic and sociolinguistic conditions and in textual transmission. 
 
4.2  The temporal contours and the major parameters of competing 
means of textual transmission are here presented in a diagram. The 
pre-Aśokan use of Indic writing for ephemeral purposes of which 
there is no trace except for the very scanty evidence of Sri Lanka has 
here been neglected. Early “Padapāṭha” orality has also been left out 
from the diagram. It is to be noted that at the end of India’s period 
of manuscript culture its disappearance seems almost immediate and 
more or less total in comparison with the marginal continuation of oral 
memory culture over centuries, in a hybrid form till the pāṭhaśālās of 
modern India.

4.3  With the preceding discussion and the concluding Timeline we 
have a backdrop against which the papers of this book can be presented. A considerable number of the chapters that follow do not deal 
with nicely wrapped and stored manuscripts but with manuscripts 
which were recently, or which still are, part of a living culture, in an 
environment where manuscript culture is intertwined with oral transmission and a cultural or religious tradition. The manuscript culture 
directly or indirectly referred to in the following chapters is usually 
coexisting with strong oral and ritual traditions. 
The second chapter, by Saraju Rath, deals with the origin of the van 
Manen collection. It is the collection prepared by Johan van Manen in 
the 1920s on the basis of small-scale searches in South India, and it is 
this collection which provided the occasion to organize the workshop 
and to collect the papers in a volume. In time the manuscripts may go 
back to the sixteenth or seventeenth century, hence to a time when 
manuscript culture was still in full blossom. 
The third chapter, by Gérard Colas, deals with a collection of Indian 
manuscripts that were among the earliest ones acquired in Europe 
after having been collected in South India (by Le Gac, Gargam and 
others) and in Bengal (by Pons) and that were sent to the King’s 
Library by French Jesuits at the beginning of 18th century (1729 to 
1735). This is one of the earliest collections of Indian manuscripts outside India, which was later on of the greatest importance for the development of Indology in Europe. Among the manuscripts in Sanskrit, 
Tamil and Telugu language Colas deals here especially with the latter. 
In the fourth chapter, Anna Esposito deals with South Indian 
drama manuscripts which represent a quite uniform tradition. The 
famous “Trivandrum plays” attributed to Bhāsa belong to this category. A detailed description is given of the material and the script. 
They possess minute specific features that have a clear distribution 
over time so that they can help in dating a manuscript if no date is 
indicated, which is usually the case. 
Masato Fujii, in the fifth chapter, deals with Jaiminīya Sāmaveda 
traditions and the use they made and still make of manuscripts. The 
rare Sāmaveda of the Jaiminīyas is found only in South India, in 
Kerala and Tamil Nadu. The author gives a historical and geographical survey of the Jaiminīya traditions and manuscripts which he and 
Asko Parpola have documented in a joint research enterprise with 
extensive field investigations. The private libraries in Kerala are of 
considerable interest in their own right as they contain various other

--- Page 62 of 338 ---
jan e.m. houben and saraju rath
46
works such as grammar apart from the technical literature of the 
Sāmaveda and thus provide precious insights into Brahminical erudition and manuscript culture at the level of South Indian villages. 
Cezary Galewicz, in chapter six, deals with the search for and collection of manuscripts of a specific text of which initially only a title 
was known, the Yamalāṣṭakatantra. This text is transmitted in a milieu 
of Ṛgvedic paṇḍits in which the orally produced text has the highest 
status. This is consequential for the problems of identifying a specific 
text in manuscripts under this title.
Chapter seven, by Heike Moser, explains the “Bhāsa research 
project,” which started in Tübingen and shifted in 2001 to Würzburg. 
In this project a multi-media data bank is in preparation. The project 
participants got by now acquainted with a large number of pages of 
handwritings and have become experts in reading Sanskrit and Prakrit 
in Malayalam script. The manuscript transmission is intimately intertwined with a still living cultural transmission. 
P. Perumal is senior conservator and librarian at one of the largest 
libraries in India, the Thanjavur Maharaja Serfoji’s Sarasvati Mahal 
Library. In his chapter, no. eight, he gives an overview of procedures 
for the production, distribution and collection of manuscript and 
explains details on writing instruments and writing materials. 
In chapter nine, Kim Plofker deals with the contribution of Indian 
mathematics and calendrical time-calculation to the study of manuscripts (whether belonging to mathematics or any other domain). 
Making full use of the Indian exact sciences one is often able to attribute a more precise date to a manuscript through the analysis of a 
horoscope or calendric computations. Important information can 
also be derived from numeric and calendric systems that are abundantly represented in dates found in scribal colophons of astronomical and astrological texts (on which see now also Plofker 2008).
Saraju Rath explores, in chapter ten, distinct styles of Grantha script 
in manuscripts which have so far not been properly described in currently available studies and handbooks. With the help of test-characters it can be shown that these distinct styles are linked with different 
periods and regions. This correlation can be used as one of the crucial 
factors for the determination of the date and place of origin of a manu
script.
In chapter eleven, Sreeramula R. Sarma, demonstrates the importance of the innumerable individual collections of priestly or other 
Brahmin householders that became a major constituent of most

--- Page 63 of 338 ---
manuscript culture: contours and parameters
47
manuscript depositories. On the basis of the palm leaf manuscripts of 
his own family he provides the reader a rare insight into the conditions of collecting and preserving these documents, and shows how 
they narrate the family tradition. The detailed information on the 
maintenance and transmission of small private collections over many 
centuries of (South) Indian manuscript culture, make Sarma’s description all the more precious. Appendix I contains the edition of a small 
text, Daśāvatāra-arghyālu, Appendix II describes another text, the 
Varṣaphala. 
In chapter twelve, Dominik Wujastyk reports on a research visit to 
the village on the banks of the river Kaveri near Kumbakonam which 
was founded by King Shahaji of Thanjavur as academic community, 
in or just before 1693. Special attention is paid to the work and manuscript library of Ramasubba Sastrigal, an active scholar from about 
1900 whose family members today still energetically maintain the 
intellectual tradition started by members of King Shahaji’s academy, 
now more than 300 years ago.
In chapter thirteen, finally, Kenneth G. Zysk takes a modest collection of Sanskrit manuscripts from Mithila as starting point to create a 
map of Indian intellectual history for that geographical area. This is 
then used as a working model to be applied to similar central repositories of manuscript collections in South India, which should maintain 
as much information as possible on provenance and date of acceptance. The collection of Sanskrit manuscript and their centralized 
storage is, however, not one that reflects Indian intellectual history, 
which was, for the most part based on individual thinkers in different 
parts of the country, where manuscripts are tools for to a large part 
still oral teaching.

CHAPTER TWO
On the Johan van Manen collection: 
Its origin and background
Saraju Rath
1.  Two crates of sanskrit palm-leaf manuscripts arrived in Leiden at the Kern Institute 
in 1929. Seventy five years later, in January 
2004, a project started that had as aim to prepare a Catalogue of these manuscripts which 
Johan van Manen and a helper of his collected 
in India. The workshop on the “production, 
distribution and collection of manuscripts in 
ancient South India” (International Institute 
for Asian Studies, Leiden, 20-21 April 2007) 
was organized in connection with this project. The studies in this volume were first presented and discussed at this workshop. 
The problems addressed in this workshop 
apply very much to the manuscripts of the 
van Manen collection.1 Thanks to the good care taken of the manuscripts over the decades, they are physically preserved quite well. 
However, the collection is relatively poorly documented and we have 
practically no direct information on the time and circumstances of the 
production of these manuscripts, nor do we know in sufficient detail 
how they were distributed and collected perhaps first in pre-modern 
times and next, at the request of Prof. J.Ph. Vogel, by the manuscript 
collector employed by van Manen. Finally, after their arrival in Leiden 
they have received limited scholarly attention as they are in majority 
1  I will focus here on an overview of the origin and the Dutch and European 
context of the Johan van Manen collection. 395 texts were known to be included in 
the collection. In the cataloguing project 193 additional texts (almost 50% of the 
initial number) have been discovered in the manuscripts. A complete list of titles of 
these texts (588 in total) is available at http://www.iias.nl/profile/saraju-rath. A Catalogue providing more details on the manuscripts and the texts they contain is under 
preparation. 
The Dutch Orientalist 
Johan van Manen in 1932. 
Courtesy: Kern library

--- Page 72 of 338 ---
saraju rath
56
in some of the less accessible, and currently obsolete, South Indian 
scripts. 
2.1  For a discussion of the origins of van Manen’s collection of Sans
krit manuscripts a few biographical remarks on Johan van Manen are 
required. Johan van Manen was born on 16 April 1877 in Nijmegen 
and died in Calcutta on 17 March 1943, almost a month before his 
sixty-sixth birthday. At the age of eighteen, Johan van Manen got 
interested in theosophy and enthusiastically joined the Theosophical 
Society which was founded in New York in 1875 and which had a 
branch in the Netherlands since 1881.2 In 1908 van Manen left for 
India where he arrived in February 1909 at the Theosophical Society’s 
International Headquarters in Adyar near Madras, India.3 He became 
the joint-librarian of the Adyar Library which at that time contained 
already a large number of books on theosophy and philosophy, and a 
collection of 16.000 manuscripts.4 Van Manen adds another thousand 
manuscripts with the help of an Indian collector whose assistance he 
will invoke again fifteen years later. In 1916, after a conflict with the 
then president of the Theosophical Society, Mrs. Annie Besant, he 
leaves Adyar. He moves to Darjeeling in order to familiarize himself 
with the Tibetan language and with Tibetan Buddhism on the basis of 
a direct study of the sources. In 1918 he became Ordinary Member of 
the Asiatic Society of Bengal, and at the end of 1918 he started to 
replace the acting Librarian of the Imperial Library in Calcutta. In this 
period he co-organized the collection of a large number of Tibetan 
2  In 1903 van Manen had the opportunity to go to the Dutch East Indies where 
he participated actively in newly founded lodges of the Theosophical Society. After 
his return to Europe in 1904 he was involved in the organisation of the annual congress of the federation of the Theosophical Society’s European Sections in 1904 in 
Amsterdam, in 1905 in London, and in 1906 in Paris. At the latter occasion, Dr. 
Steiner, the later founder of Anthroposophy, expressed on behalf of everyone his 
satisfaction with the hard work of van Manen. 
3  He must have witnessed the discovery in 1909 of the young Krishna who was 
selected to become the new world teacher by C.W. Leadbeater but who, as Krishnamurti, rejected not only the role of world teacher but also the Theosophical Society 
in 1929.
4  In this function he helped many scholars in their research; the German Sans
kritist and main librarian at Adyar, F. Otto Schrader, writes in his work on Minor 
Upaniṣads (1912, p. liv): “ I am further under deep obligation to my assistant and 
friend Mr Johan van Manen for his useful typographical suggestions and the pains 
he has taken in reading the proofs of the larger part of this volume.”

--- Page 73 of 338 ---
on the johan van manen collection
57
blockprints and manuscripts, partly for the Imperial Library, partly 
for the University of Calcutta and partly for himself. 
In 1923 he was elected General Secretary of the Asiatic Society of 
Bengal in which function he would invest most of his time and energy 
for sixteen years. Ten years later, in 1933, it is written in the Annual 
Report of the Society for 1932 that van Manen “continued to perform 
the amalgamated duties of Secretary and Assistant Secretary and was 
not absent on leave during the year” (Richardus 1989: 48). It is also 
noted that the “General Secretary has written in his decennial period 
of office nearly 30,000 letters and dealt with an equal number of 
incoming ones, the bulk representing almost that of an Encyclopaedia 
Brittanica” (ibid.). In the mean time he maintained excellent relations 
with international scholars visiting or staying in Calcutta, assisting 
them in their research, for instance when they make use of the rich 
collections of the Society.5 The Romanian scholar Mircea Eliade, who 
came to study in Calcutta in 1928, made van Manen one of the characters in his short story Nopţi la Serampore (“Nights at Serampore”6) 
which contains partially fictional locations and events, but must have 
been partly based on Eliade’s experiences in Calcutta. In this story, 
van Manen is portrayed as a middle-aged Dutch who had devoted 
some twenty-five years to the study of Tibetan languages which he 
understood better than anyone else. He studied and increased his 
knowledge solely for his own satisfaction, and had not the slightest 
respect for academic titles. He was easy-going and enjoyed a good living.7 
5  Apart from Mircea Eliade, Richardus (1989: 45ff) refers to the French scholar 
Sylvain Lévi, and the Dutch scholar R.H. van Gulik. The latter dedicated his book 
Siddham: An Essay on the History of Sanskrit Studies in China and Japan (1956) to 
van Manen. Honors and titles bestowed on him are indicative of his fame. In 1933 
van Manen became Honorary Member of the Royal Batavian Society of Arts and 
Sciences in Batavia, and the next year the French Government conferred on him the 
title of Officier de l’Instruction Publique. In 1937 Queen Wilhelmina of the Netherlands conferred on him the rank of Officer of Oranje-Nassau.
6  Nopţi la Serampore (Eliade s.d. [1940]) appears later on in English as one of the 
two stories in Two Tales of the Occult (Eliade 1970). In this story, three Europeans, 
Eliade, van Manen and a Russian orientalist and ex-diplomat, Bogdanof, have a collective extraordinary experience which includes a shift in time. The experience is 
later on interpreted in two divergent ways, from the point of view of tantric practice 
and from the point of view of an ascetic from the Himālaya, Swami Shivananda. Van 
Manen here apparently represents “knowledge for the sake of knowledge” or “vast 
erudition” which, however, is incapable of dealing with the extraordinary experience. 
7  Eliade 1970 : 4. The English translation has ‘lazy’ for Ro. leneş which in the 
immediate and larger context of the story, however, rather means ‘easy-going’.

--- Page 74 of 338 ---
saraju rath
58
In the course of the years, health problems troubled him more and 
more frequently, and forced him to take breaks from his heavy function, from which he finally resigned on the 1st of July, 1939. In 1943 
van Manen planned to leave Calcutta and its unhealthy climate. He 
wanted to move to Kalimpong, and to continue his study and writing 
there at the feet of the Himalayas. Before this plan could be realized he 
died from a stroke on 17 March 1943. 
In the first part of his life, the publications of van Manen concern 
mostly reviews of theosophical works and articles on theosophical 
concepts. From 1918 his articles, unfortunately not very large in 
number, mainly concern Tibetan texts and culture. His collections of 
Tibetan texts and ethnographical objects, of Lepcha manuscripts and 
of Sanskrit palm-leaf manuscripts are major contributions to oriental 
scholarship in their own right. 
2.2  How and why were the Sanskrit palm-leaf manuscripts of van 
Manen collected from around 1928 onwards? Two existing biographies of Johan van Manen, one in the form of an appendix to P.H. 
Pott’s Introduction to the Tibetan collection of the National Museum of 
Ethnology, Leiden, and the other in Peter Richardus’s description of 
the life and works of van Manen, provide much valuable information 
about Johan van Manen’s life, but unfortunately both remain almost 
entirely silent about the collection of Sanskrit palmleaf manuscripts 
for the Kern Institute. From events recorded in his biographies and 
from letters conserved at the Kern Institute8 we can nevertheless 
reconstruct in main outlines how this specific collection arose and 
with which, rather modest, expectations it was made. 
Apparently Prof. J. Ph. Vogel got interested in acquiring a collection of Sanskrit manuscripts soon after he founded, on 4 April 1925, 
the Indological Institute Kern. This institute was called after Prof. 
Hendrik Kern, who became the first Dutch Professor of Sanskrit in 
1865, when the Netherlands obtained a chair in Sanskrit several 
decades after France, Great Britain, Germany, Austria, Denmark, 
Russia, had established chairs at one or more of their universities for 
this field of studies which at that time was widely regarded as a most 
promising one. Prof. Vogel and van Manen must have met when 
8  Most of the letters written by van Manen, who is at that time General Secretary 
of the Asiatic Society of Bengal, are in English; cf. in his letter of 8 November 1928: 
“Excuse my writing in English, in order to keep a copy for my file”; and on 21 February 1929: “In ’t Engelsch geschreven terwille van den klerk.”

--- Page 75 of 338 ---
on the johan van manen collection
59
Vogel came to deliver a lecture on “Buddhist remains on Java” at a 
meeting of the Asiatic Society of Bengal in Calcutta on 7 December 
1925. Perhaps at that occasion Prof. Vogel spoke with van Manen 
about his wish to have a collection of Sanskrit manuscripts for his 
newly founded institute, at a time that other European centers of 
Sanskrit learning, Paris, London, Oxford, Munich, Berlin, and Vienna, 
had already important collections of Sanskrit manuscripts for around 
a century or more. 
2.3  A brief account of the European situation9 can here be inserted 
as it provides a historical European perspective for the van Manen 
collection. The earliest available collection of Sanskrit manuscripts in 
Europe was the one in the Imperial Library in Paris. Contributions to 
this collection dated from the first half of the 18th century (Colas in 
this volume). A (partial) Catalogue appeared in 1807 (by A. Hamilton 
and L. Langlès); it was used by Friedrich Schlegel and Franz Bopp for 
their Sanskrit studies. The studies of Colebrooke were partly based on 
his own collection of manuscripts, which he donated in 1819 to the 
East India Company where it formed the basis of the India Office 
Library that was further enriched by the collection of Lieut. Colonel 
Colin Mackenzie of which H.H. Wilson prepared a catalogue in 1828, 
published in Calcutta. The Bodleian Library in Oxford arose in 1837 
but it received the older collections of H.H. Wilson, W.H. Mill and A. 
Walker. The Royal Library in Berlin acquired the collection of Sir 
Robert Chambers (1737-1803) which was famous for its Vedic manuscripts and which took an important place in A. Weber’s Catalogue of 
Sanskrit manuscripts, 1853 (Verzeichniss der Sanskrithandschriften, 
Berlin, 1853). Many more catalogues appeared and by the time J.Ph. 
Vogel founded the Kern Institute at Leiden University in 1925, ca. 125 
catalogues of Sanskrit and Indian manuscripts had appeared out of 
which more than 20 concerned collections in various cities in Europe. 
9  Cp. Windisch 1917: 53. An overview, in the form of a table and a bibliography, 
of major catalogues of Sanskrit and Indian manuscripts starting with a list compiled 
by the editor of Sir William Jones’ works which appeared in London in 1807 (omitting the catalogue by A. Hamilton and L. Langlès of Indian manuscripts in the Imperial Library in Paris, which appeared in the same year) is found in Katre 1954: 
104-129. Pandurangi (1978) gives an overview of catalogued and uncatalogued Sanskrit manuscript collections inside and outside India. More recently, collections of 
Indian manuscripts have been inventoried and made accessible where possible in the 
National Mission of Manuscripts, an organization under the Ministry of Culture, 
Government of India, established in 2003 (www.namami.org).

2.4  In a letter dated 28 January 1927, the curators of the State 
University Leiden gave permission to the librarian to spend 600 Dutch 
guilders for the acquisition of Sanskrit manuscripts. The librarian 
informed Prof. Vogel of this permission in a letter of 8 October 1928. 
In the meantime, van Manen had already started collecting with the 
help of the person to whom he refers in 1928 as “a manuscript hunter 
who collected for me 15 years ago in Madras.” 15 years ago means: 
during van Manen’s time at the Theosophical Society in Adyar. After 
the first sending of manuscripts have arrived, van Manen writes (30 
May 1929) that new possibilities for manuscripts are there but that he 
regrets that “finance is so great a difficulty.” He adds: “If I had been 
able to go on buying, this special man who is a curious mixture of a 
mystic and business man, would have gone on getting a really good 
collection. He is a kind of Yogi who has made a vow to collect and 
rescue MSS. and does this as a religious piety.” 
Unfortunately, this is all we know at present about the collector in 
South India. There are no records of the exact source of his manuscripts, or about the families, private owners or perhaps temples in 
whose possession they were. In one letter he writes that one small lot 
of seven manuscripts from Trichinapoli has not reached him. From a 
note which seems to represent a wired message of van Manen’s 
“manuscript hunter” it can be inferred that he himself got the manuscripts from other agents with whom he negotiated for the best price. 
He writes about these agents: “They wanted Rs. 10/- per thousand 
Shlokas for rare manuscripts considering the copying fee etc., and surrendering the original to us; after much discussion I brought them to 
Rs. 7/-. The mss. are very old and well preserved in good condition. 
I like to send them by post if accepted registered. Besides, [the] 
Madras—Calcutta line is drowned [due to inundations]. In case you 
like to increase the rate to Rs. 8 or so the Sastry will thankfully receive. 
We have not as yet such a good collection in original mss. secured in 
one sweep. It is a chance and luck. If you like to have them please wire 
to me to the above address as usual and oblige. My next move to Salem 
district after hearing from you.” In a handwritten note on this apparently wired message another message appears in cursive writing and 
signed by van Manen: “I have accepted, and wired him 250 rupees; 
promising an extra 20 rupees after the manuscripts prove satisfactory.” 
From the correspondence between Vogel and van Manen we 
understand that van Manen feels that he acquires the manuscripts for

--- Page 77 of 338 ---

“very moderate prices” (letter of 30 August 1928). They are “mostly in 
Tamil10 and Telugu script, and almost exclusively on palm-leaf” 
(ibid.). van Manen adds that “Much of it has been published, but I 
understand you want to form a collection on a representative basis 
with a view to get varieties of script and date, and various recensions. 
There are also a number of quite valuable and rare works, especially 
on medicine, and two Drahyayanas of which MSS are very rare” 
(ibid.). 
It appears that the first consignment that arrived in 1929 contained 
five lots, for which van Manen had joined five typed lists numbered 
roman I—roman V. On 30 May 1929 he wrote that he has received 
Prof. Vogel’s confirmation of receipt of the manuscripts, and that he 
adds typed lists of the further lots, roman VI, VII, VIII, X and XI.11 
From the available letters we can conclude that at least 600 plus 500 
guilders and possibly one or a few hundred guilders more were placed 
at the disposal of Prof. Vogel for the acquisition of Sanskrit manuscripts through Dr. van Manen and his Indian agents. From van 
Manen’s letter of 10 June 1929 we understand that many more valuable manuscripts could have been acquired but this could not be realised due to lack of funds.

2.5  Not long after their arrival in Leiden, Prof. Willem Caland, 
Sanskritist and Vedic scholar at the University in the neighbouring 
city of Utrecht (where a chair for Sanskrit had been established in 
1917), requests Prof. Vogel in a letter dated 17 February 193212 to 
send him a list of the newly acquired Sanskrit palm leaf manuscripts.13 
Due to poor health, Caland had not not been able to go out of his 
house for the winter months of 1931-1932, but his strong interest in 
research work had not diminished. It was agreed that Vogel would 
send batches of manuscripts to Utrecht. Caland wanted to see which 
of these manuscripts would be of interest to him and at the same time 
he wanted to volunteer to make a small catalogue of the selected manuscripts, which would later on help in the catalogisation of the entire 
collection. On the basis of the list which he had received, Caland wrote 
on 25 February which manuscripts he wanted to investigate. On 5 
March he wrote a postcard to Vogel that the manuscripts had arrived 
well. On J.Ph. Vogel’s question whether the fact that the manuscripts 
were in South Indian scripts would not create any problems, he replied 
that he was able to read Grantha and Telugu easily. His wish to begin 
the study of the manuscripts that had arrived could not be fulfilled as 
he died within two and a half weeks, on 23 March. 
After this event which Prof. Vogel recalled in his obituary of Prof. 
Caland, Vogel remained remarkably silent about the palm leaf manuscripts. In a rather detailed report of “the work done at the Institute 
Kern, 1925-1935” (Vogel 1935), he discusses the achievements of the 
institute and the books and collections in its care. Nowhere any mention is made of the palm leaf manuscripts which van Manen had been 
able to collect with considerable effort after Vogel had succeeded to 
get funding for it from the University Library. Also in his farewell lecture in which he recalls remarkable moments of his career as Professor 
of Sanskrit, there is no word of the collection of palm leaf manuscripts, 
or of its study or of plans to catalogise it.

2.6  It is clear that Vogel had always been much attracted by archaeology, art history and Sanskrit literature, which are the areas of his 
greatest achievements.14 As he had started his scientific career as 
“superintendant” of the Archeological Survey of India (1902-1914), 
first in the Punjab and later in the United Provinces and in the 
Northern Circle, he was well familiar with ancient and current scripts 
in the north but may not have been equally acquainted with those of 
the south. If Vogel did not master South Indian scripts, why did he 
invest so much effort to gather funds for a collection of South Indian 
manuscripts? 
Obviously, at the moment of the establishment of the Institute Kern 
in 1925, Vogel had a broader perspective than only his own research 
interests. As Vogel was familiar with the journal Indian Antiquary 
which contained important contributions on archeology and art history, he must have been aware of the opinion which Ernst Leumann 
once expressed there in connection with the work of Georg Bühler 
(1837-1898) as collector of manuscripts, namely, that the presence of 
manuscript collections is decisive for the direction of research in a 
certain country (Leumann 1898: 368-369): 
It is generally not known or scarcely noticed to what an extent history 
is dependent on the local distribution of materials. When a country 
shows some predilection for this or that branch of research we are, at 
first, inclined to find the reasons in some national instinct, or in the 
efficacy of the teaching and writing of some scholar. This does not, 
however, explain how it is that—to turn to Indian research—Denmark 
has only produced Pali scholars (Westergaard, Fausböll, Trenckner, 
Andersen), that Northern Buddhism is chiefly cultivated in Paris 
(Burnouf, Feer, Senart, Lévi, Guimet), and that other branches of 
Indian studies are more or less equally confined to particular seats of 
learning. The real explanation lies in the dispersion of the materials. 
Rask furnished Copenhagen with a splendid collection of Pali manuscripts which roused the interest of Danish scholars, just as Hodgson 
sent to Paris an excellent collection of the writings of the Northern 
Buddhists as preserved in Nepal. So the famous general Sanskrit Library 
of Chambers went to Berlin and found there an indefatigable inter14  As it was observed in the obituary of J. Ph. Vogel by F.D.K. Bosch (1959: 356), 
“It was as an archaeologist that [Prof. Vogel] was meeting Indian culture, and an 
archaeologist he remained, even after the caesura in his life which coincided with his 
departure from India and his assuming the duties of professor at Leiden.” (“Het was 
als archeoloog dat hij de Indische cultuur tegemoet was getreden en archeoloog is hij 
gebleven, ook na de cesuur in zijn leven samenvallend met zijn vertrek uit India en 
de aanvaarding van zijn hoogleraarsambt te Leiden.”)

--- Page 80 of 338 ---
saraju rath
64
preter in Weber, while, the India Office and the Bodleian have become 
seats of Indian philology through the manuscript libraries of Colebrooke and Wilson. In later years also Cambridge received a series of 
manuscript treasures from the enlightened activity of Daniel Wright, 
with the consequence that two Cambridge scholars (Cowell and Bendall) have made their special study.
It seems, then, that the starting point of the van Manen collection was 
J.Ph. Vogel’s wish to have “a collection on a representative basis” for 
his new Institute which he founded in 1925 and guided for decades 
with much care and devotion. The criteria on which van Manen and 
his assistant had to base their choice of manuscripts did not go beyond 
this general wish. The choice to go for a collection of South Indian 
manuscripts was then no doubt dictated by the circumstances and 
shaped by van Manen, who had extensive experience with the search 
of Sanskrit manuscripts in the South. This was an area where manuscripts at that time were perhaps more easily accessible, or felt to be 
more accesible, than in the north.15 In any case, for this area he could 
dispose of the help of the “manuscript hunter” who helped him when 
he was still at the Adyar Library. However, when he was requested to 
make a collection for the University of Leiden, van Manen was most 
actively involved in Tibetan studies rather than in Sanskrit studies. 
2.7  After their arrival in the Netherlands the manuscripts of the collection known as “van Manen collection of Sanskrit palm leaf manuscripts” have been well taken care of, but from a scientific point of 
view they received, from the beginning, a stepmotherly treatment due 
to several circumstances. First from van Manen, whose interest had 
already shifted towards Tibetan at the moment he started to collect 
the manuscripts for J.Ph. Vogel. Next from the side of J.Ph. Vogel who 
was more interested in archeology and art history than in manuscript 
studies. And Vogel’s successor, F.B.J. Kuiper (1907-2003, professor of 
Sanskrit at the Kern Institute from 1939-1975), was again more interested in Sanskrit and comparative linguistics. The one who took an

active interest in the collection, W. Caland, did plan to have a closer

3.  Although this will be the subject of a different publication, I would 
like to conclude with a a few remarks on the substance of the collection. The manuscripts contain mainly texts on Vedic ritual (Sūtras 
and commentaries, prayoga texts), Upaniṣads among which several 
rare ones,17 philosophical texts, epics (Mahābhārata and Rāmāyaṇa) 
and commentaries, purāṇas, kāvyas and campūs, stotras, māhātmyas 
on religious places, a few medical texts.18 The collection is organised 
in eleven lots, numbered I-XI, for which the archive has eleven typed 
lists which are probably those prepared by Johan van Manen in 
Calcutta on the basis of the information provided by his South Indian 
assistant. The typed lists contain a number of handwritten notes, corrections and additions which may (partly) derive from the study of the 
manuscripts by V. Raghavan in the 1950s. The manuscripts are almost 
all on palmleaf, and the scripts employed are southern ones: Grantha 
(478 out of the 588 identified texts), Telugu (33), Nandināgarī (72); 
Malayalam (2) and Vaṭṭeluttu (passages in one manuscript that is 
mainly in Grantha). Two paper manuscripts in Devanāgarī19 and one
