CHAPTER THREE
A cultural encounter in the early 
18th century: the collection of South Indian 
manuscripts by the French Jesuit fathers  
of the Carnatic Mission
Gérard Colas
The nature of the interest for manuscripts varies according to the 
milieu in which they are written, read, transmitted and used. 
Considered as historical documentation by academics, as aesthetic or 
cultural objects by antiquarians in 21st-century Western societies, 
Indian manuscripts had entirely different functions in the country 
where they were written. The historical circumstances surrounding 
the search for Indian manuscripts by French Jesuit fathers in the early 
18th century also reflect the heterogeneous intellectual and religious 
interests of the social and institutional milieus associated with it. This 
search for the manuscripts also highlights in a singular way the 
encounter between two cultures, Indian and European, around an 
object—the manuscript—for as we will see, the conception and use of 
books among Hindus were very different from those of Europeans. 
The French Jesuit fathers of the Carnatic Mission in India sent 
around one hundred and sixty volumes to the King’s library in Paris 
between 1729 and 1735. Most of them were manuscripts written in 
the Sanskrit, Telugu and Tamil languages, which nobody in France 
could read at that time. These documents formed the nucleus of what 
are now called the Sanscrit and Indien collections in the Bibliothèque 
nationale de France (BnF). This paper will examine the historical and 
cultural factors accompanying the acquisition of Indian manuscripts 
by the King’s library. 
I examined two kinds of documents to understand the various 
Indian and European dimensions of the Jesuit search for manuscripts: 
the correspondence of the Jesuits of the Carnatic Mission (preserved 
in the Jesuit archives [JA] in Vanves, near Paris, under the name of the

--- Page 86 of 338 ---
gérard colas
70
Fonds Brotier)1 and the manuscripts they sent from India. My investigation is confined to those manuscripts written in Telugu.2 
The Carnatic Mission was founded in 1695 by Louis XIV, King of 
France, who fixed its two aims: to propagate the Catholic faith and to 
promote a better understanding of Indian civilization. It was almost 
exclusively composed of French Jesuit fathers and thus differed from 
the older Jesuit Maduré Mission, composed of Europeans. The mission started to develop in 1699, when the French were able to return 
to Pondicherry (after the Dutch had expelled them in 1693), as an 
application of the Ryswick treatise (1697). It extended its area of apostolate from Pondicherry to a few other places in the Tamil speaking 
area, but much more in the Telugu-speaking area towards the north of 
Pondicherry.3 It also set up a branch in Chandernagore in Bengal. The 
Jesuit fathers hoped one day to join up the Bengal and the Southern 
ends of the Mission to form a single network, a dream which never 
came true. Their strategy to convert Indians to Christianity consisted 
in the method of accomodatio “adaptation” used earlier by Roberto 
De Nobili (1577-1656). The idea was that if the educated strata were 
converted to Christianity, the rest of Indian society would follow. 
Jesuits adopted the dress and behaviour of Hindu ascetics and learnt 
the languages, sciences and literatures of the higher castes in order to 
interact with them and finally to convert the representatives of these 
milieus.4 Although all the Jesuit fathers of the Carnatic Mission 
endorsed the accomodatio method, some were not convinced of the 
intellectual value of indigenous literature. But some others showed a 
1  The published collection of the Lettres édifiantes et curieuses contains only 
revised and abridged versions of a selection of letters from the French Jesuits.
2  Other South Indian manuscripts are in Tamil and Sanskrit. An important 
number of manuscripts in Bengali script were also sent by the Mission. They had 
been gathered from the Chandernagore branch of the Mission by Father Jean 
François Pons (1713-1752 or 1753), a well-known figure in the history of French 
Indology, who wrote a Sanskrit grammar (see Filliozat, J. 1937; Muller, J.-Cl. 1985: 
134-139). For the dates of the French Jesuit missionaries of the Carnatic Mission in 
the 18th century, see Besse, L. 1918.
3  A map prepared by Father Duchamp (1692-1739 or 1740) in 1730 gives an idea 
of the extent of the mission at that time: see AJ, Brotier 88, f. 158 (reproduced in 
Colas, G. 1997b, plate 1).
4  In this respect, the French Jesuits were inheritors of the baroque practice of 
acting and illusion (Colas, G. 1997a). For similar attitudes among their predecessors 
in China, see Gernet, J. 1991.

--- Page 87 of 338 ---
south indian manuscripts of french jesuit fathers
71
genuine personal interest in philosophical, belletristic and scientific 
works by Indians.5 
Before going further, we should briefly recall certain aspects of the 
cultural context of that period in both France and South India. Interest 
in Eastern civilizations and literatures flourished in an early 18th-century France in search of knowledge and novelty. The visit of Mehmet 
Effendi, ambassador of the Ottoman emperor, in 1721, aroused enormous enthusiasm both among the higher classes of society and among 
the ordinary people, who thronged the passage of the diplomat accompanied by his exotic retinue. The interest of the intellectual circles for 
the East stemmed from genuine curiosity, cultural snobbery6 and a 
search for non-Western models which they could use to criticize 
French social and political institutions without displaying overt opposition to them.7 In 1721, eight years before the French Jesuits sent 
their first parcel of Indian books to the King’s library, Montesquieu 
published his Lettres persanes, a collection of letters attributed to an 
imaginary Persian which satirized French society and subtly criticized 
the French regime of absolute monarchy. In those days, the possession of rare and exotic books was a symbol of intellectual or political 
superiority and personal prestige. This was in keeping with the thenprevalent European fashion for cabinets de curiosités and Wunder
kammern, which started during the Renaissance and is the origin of 
our museums.8 
Two important libraries in Paris competed to acquire Indian manu
scripts, particularly between 1725 and 1735. One was the Jesuit library 
of the Louis-le-Grand College. The Abbé Etienne Souciet (1671-1745), 
a scholar who published the second edition (1721) of the encyclopaedic Dictionnaire de Trévoux with his brother and who wrote a book on 
Chinese and Indian astronomical and mathematical traditions,9 
5  See Colas, G. 1997b: 350, notes 7 (Le Gac’s attitude) and 8 (Calmette’s attitude).
6  Such cultural snobbery had been satirized by Molière in his Bourgeois gentilhomme (1670), which portrays a bourgeois man, Monsieur Jourdain, ennobled by 
“the son of the Grand Turk”, a caricature of a Turkish potentate. Molière wrote the 
Bourgeois gentilhomme soon after the visit of Suleyman Aga Mustapha Raca (an 
emissary of Mehmet IV, sultan of the Ottoman Empire) to France in 1669.
7  See P. Hazard (1963, 1968 [1st ed. 1961]) (quotations in Colas, G. 1997a: 211).
8  See von Schlosser’s study (1908). See also Pomian, K. 2003: 333-355. 
9  Observations mathématiques, astronomiques, géographiques, chronologiques et 
physiques, tirées des anciens livres chinois, ou faites nouvellement aux Indes et à la 
Chine, par les Pères de la compagnie de Jésus. Rédigées et publiées par le P. E. Souciet, 
Paris: Rollin, 1729.

--- Page 88 of 338 ---
gérard colas
72
represented this library. The other was the King’s Library, whose 
librarian was the Abbé Jean-Paul Bignon (1662-1743). An erudite in a 
wide range of subjects, Bignon actively participated in the Journal des 
Sçavans. He showed a particular interest in Eastern cultures and met 
Mehmet Effendi in 1721. He also wrote (under a pseudonym) a novel 
describing the adventures of Abdalla, “sent by the Sultan of the Indies 
to discover the island of Borico.”10 Soon after his appointment as 
librarian to the King’s library in 1718, he asked Étienne Fourmont, 
professor of Arabic at the Collège Royal, to draw up a list of Chinese, 
Tartaric, Indian and Siamese books to be procured for the library. 
In 1719 Fourmont provided a list in which Indian titles such as 
“Panjangam”, “Vedam”, “Ramayenam” and the works of “Bartrou
herri” appeared.11
To obtain Indian manuscripts, the Jesuit library contacted the 
Jesuits of the Carnatic Mission directly; the King’s library contacted 
them indirectly.12 The reaction of Étienne Le Gac (1671-1738), at that 
time Superior of the Carnatic Mission, was different in each case. He 
tried to dissuade Souciet, his fellow Jesuit, from this search. He did not 
see the utility of keeping Indian manuscripts in Paris, for according to 
him, this could serve only a vain ostentation and not the advancement 
of knowledge, since nobody in Paris could read them.13 But Le Gac’s 
advice went unheeded: Souciet, motivated either by genuine curiosity 
or by a concern for appearances (his own or those of his religious 
order), persisted and finally obtained (perhaps at the beginning of 
1733) three volumes which contained the “Rougvedam”.14 While Le 
Gac could respond negatively to a fellow Jesuit, he could not refuse 
the Royal Librarian. This would have had serious consequences for the 
working of the Carnatic Mission. Bignon’s request was sent through 
the intermediary of the Abbé Gilles-Bernard Raguet (1668-1748), one 
of the directors of the French Company of the Indies whose favours 
were important to the Jesuits in India, especially because he allowed

south indian manuscripts of french jesuit fathers
73
them to travel to and from the subcontinent in Company ships. 
Moreover, Le Gac was trying to set up a printing press in Pondicherry 
(to counter the Lutheran missionary printing activity in Tranquebar), 
a project for which he needed support in Paris.15 Under these circumstances, he had no other choice than to comply with the request from 
the King’s library for manuscripts.
Several pre-modern South Indian conceptions about texts and their 
copies may be mentioned, which contributed to the Jesuit successes 
and failures in getting manuscripts. Firstly, access to manuscripts was 
limited in India, not only because of the limited spread of education, 
but also because of the notion of religious competency. Only a person 
identified as competent could receive a religious text. The competency 
factor not only excluded many sections of the society, but also operated across the sectarian Hindu world: for instance, a Hindu from a 
certain sect could not have access to the texts of another sect. The second point which needs to be emphasized is related to the style of composition of classical Indian works. They were composed in erudite and 
scholarly language accessible only to the educated class, and never in 
the colloquial language spoken by the common man. The third aspect 
concerns the nature of the material on which the manuscripts were 
written. Traditional Hindu scholars and scribes in several regions 
were reluctant to write on paper, perhaps because it was traditionally 
produced by Muslims16 or because palm-leaves were cheaper, durable 
and easily available. In general, these scholars and scribes would have 
been opposed to any kind of material which they considered as nontraditional. It is important to bear these three aspects in mind because 
they greatly influenced the collection of manuscripts by the Jesuits.
In November 1727, Bignon sent a new list from Fourmont to the 
French Company of the Indies in Pondicherry. This list requested to 
have only works in Persian, dictionaries and grammars of Indian languages explained in Persian.17 To begin with, the Company searched 
for manuscripts on its own and obtained several Christian works from 
the White Friars (Carmelites). But it soon turned to the French Jesuits. 
Before the end of September 1728, Le Noir, the “commandant général”

74
of the French Company in Pondicherry, gave them a copy of 
Fourmont’s list.18 Between January 1729 and 1735, the Jesuits sent six 
parcels of books, mostly manuscripts.19 We can distinguish between 
two periods. The first consists of two consignments, those of 1729 and 
1730, where the manuscripts were exclusively on paper, except one 
written on palm-leaves, added on the order of Le Noir.20 The second 
period consists of the yearly consignments from 1732 to 1735, which 
mostly contained palm-leaf manuscripts. A gap of two years separates 
these two periods. 
The explanation for the change in the material of the manuscripts 
between the first period and the second can be found in the correspondence between Bignon and Le Gac. In 1729, Le Gac asked Bignon 
whether he would be interested in palm-leaf manuscripts, but in the 
same letter he also tried to discourage the librarian, arguing that palmleaf manuscripts were prone to worm attacks. Bignon, probably fascinated by the palm-leaf manuscript he had already received, expressed 
his preference for manuscripts written on palm-leaf and suggested 
that the missionaries could easily get more of them from their converts at a lower cost than paper copies.21 Bignon did not explain the 
reason for his choice, but his wording suggests that he preferred 
“authentic” manuscripts;22 perhaps he suspected that paper manuscripts could only contain Christian, in other words not typically 
Indian, works. 
A study of the palaeographical characteristics of several paper manuscripts of the first period provided significant results. The following 
observations bear only on the nine Telugu manuscripts (including 
two dictionaries and a grammar) in the total of twenty-four documents sent in 1729 and 1730. Analysis of the handwriting of these 
paper manuscripts enabled us23 to conclude that they had been copied

by the French Jesuits.24 This is supported by a statement by Le Noir 
suggesting that the Jesuits were the first to write Telugu manuscripts 
on paper.25 This is also confirmed by the fact that the inexperienced 
handwriting of the manuscripts matches the handwriting of two 
Jesuits, Memmius René Gargam (1686-1754) and Pierre FrançoisXavier Duchamp (1692-ca.1739),26 in their correspondence. More
over, these consignments contained a grammar and two dictionaries 
written in both French and Telugu. Furthermore, these manuscripts 
contain specifically Christian designs, such as crosses. But above all, 
most of these works (except the Vēmanaśatakamu, Indien 1730) are 
composed in colloquial Telugu, that is, in a style which was never used 
for literature and probably never before put down in writing.27 We 
will return to this point below. 
The second period of consignments corresponds to another type of 
document. Two manuscripts written on paper show a Jesuit hand.28 
The other six manuscripts are on palm-leaf29 and could not have been 
written by Jesuits, because they are written by experienced scribal 
hands. Father Jean Calmette (1692-1739 or 1740) had been given the 
task of collecting palm-leaf manuscripts, and he threw himself into 
the project with great enthusiasm, but it proved to be difficult to 
employ Hindu scribes. Being non-Hindus, the French Jesuit fathers 
could not have direct access to Hindu (including non-religious) 
manuscripts or to the knowledge contained in Hindu texts. They

76
found two sources of Hindu manuscripts: Christian converts and 
Hindu scholars, but through intermediaries.30 
Christian converts could provide two kinds of palm-leaf manuscript: those which they already possessed before their conversion to 
Christianity, which they could donate or sell to the Jesuits, and those 
they copied after their conversion. In one of his letters, Le Noir mentions that the Jesuits obtained manuscripts from the personal libraries 
of the converts.31 Although we found no definite proof, it is possible 
that several such manuscripts found their way into the parcels sent to 
the King’s library. Three facts support the hypothesis that a certain 
number of manuscripts sent to France were copied by converts. 
Firstly, two manuscripts (Indien 582 and Sanscrit 876) contain 
Christian works. Secondly, several manuscripts of classical Hindu literature contain initial and final formulas which are either specifically 
Christian or favoured by Christians.32 Thirdly, in their letters, the 
Jesuits relate that they had Christian texts translated from non-Sanskrit languages to Sanskrit.33 The Indian literate scribes could only be 
Christian converts, for Hindu Brahmins would not have translated 
Christian texts. Since the Jesuits asked converts to translate, one may 
imagine that they also asked converts to copy Hindu texts on palmleaves.
In addition to the converts who gave their manuscripts to the 
Jesuits or copied texts after their conversion to Christianity, Hindu 
Brahmins were another source of manuscripts. As we mentioned earlier, the French Jesuits had little hope of procuring manuscripts 
directly from Hindu scholars. However, there is evidence that they 
obtained manuscripts through secret intermediaries. This was a complex process. Jesuits used the services of Brahmin converts who had

31  See Colas, G. 1997b: 359, note 33.

77
not revealed their conversion to their Hindu brethren. Such converts 
could easily obtain Hindu texts and could also have them copied. 
Calmette applied this stratagem from the year 1731 onwards. However, 
it had its shortcomings. It happened that one intermediary hired 
Indian scribes for Calmette, but when Calmette was identified as the 
ultimate patron of the copying, the Indian scribes immediately 
stopped their work and it was impossible to recruit any others. The 
stratagem also occasionally failed due to sectarian divides within the 
Hindu world. A crypto-Christian Brahmin, who maintained the outward appearances of a Hindu, probably Śaiva, tried to procure manuscripts from another Brahmin who was a Vaiṣṇava. But the Vaiṣṇava 
Brahmin refused at any price. The crypto-Christian Brahmin then 
turned to the brother of this Vaiṣṇava, who was corruptible and from 
whom manuscripts could be obtained.34 
It would be interesting to see if the above observations also apply to 
manuscripts in languages other than Telugu. The Sanskrit and Tamil 
manuscripts gathered by the French Jesuits of the Carnatic Mission 
also require palaeographical and codicological study. Those sent by 
Father Pons from Bengal35 also need to be examined. The social and 
intellectual conditions which he faced in Bengal could have been of a 
different nature.
 
The search for Indian manuscripts by French Jesuits in the early 18th 
century thus illustrates the meeting of two cultures, but also of two 
conceptions of the Indian book: as an exotic curio to be displayed and 
as an object to be protected from the “unfit”. It also testifies to the 
complex relationship between text and its copy in traditional India 
that persisted in 18th-century South India.36 Above all, the French 
Jesuits inadvertently created an exceptional situation and made a singular contribution to the field of Telugu literature37, for their efforts to 
collect Indian texts caused the emergence of a new kind of Telugu literature. Unable to obtain the manuscripts of classical works from 
34  For more details, see Colas, G. 1997b: 359-360.
35  See note 2 above.
36  Danish missionaries in 1737 also reported that a South Indian Brahmin was 
prepared to copy śāstric works, but not Vedic texts. He would only occasionally draw 
some Vedic words with his finger or write them on a material which he then immediately destroyed. See Graham, W.A. 1987: 73-74.
37  See Rajamanickam, S. 1972, for the contribution of De Nobili and other early

gérard colas
78
Hindu scholars, they themselves recorded them in writing, probably 
from converts who transmitted the contents of these works in a colloquial style. Thus the Dharmarājacaritra (Indien 617-618), Ruk
māṃgadacaritra (Indien 620) and Vikramārkacaritra kathalu (Indien 
619) are vernacular digests of Telugu classics, the Āṃdhramahābhā
ratamu (by Nannaya, Tikkana and Eṟṟāpregaḍa, 11th-14th century), 
Mallana’s Rukmāṃgadacaritra (15th century) and Koravi Gōparāju’s 
Siṃhāsanadvātriṃśika (15th century) respectively. While the classics 
are composed in an elaborate and Sanskritized style, these digests contain the mere story and are written in conversational language, free of 
all literary ornaments. These early 18th-century manuscripts are probably the first extant works written in colloquial Telugu. They contain a 
wealth of vocabulary and idiom of the popular classes of early 18thcentury Telugu society. They are the outcome of the French Jesuit 
strategy of gathering cultural, religious and linguistic information for 
the advancement of knowledge and for proselytizing. The Telugu dictionaries (Indien 597 and Indien 600) composed by the French Jesuits38 
also testify to these efforts. In this respect, these compositions do not 
correspond to the declared intellectual aims of the Carnatic mission 
(to convert highly educated Brahmin scholars), but were tools for 
acquiring further knowledge of Telugu language and culture and for 
facilitating transactions between the missionaries and the uneducated 
classes.39 Thus, while the search for Indian manuscripts by the King’s 
library could be judged premature and futile in early 18th-century 
France, these manuscripts prove to be a precious material for academic research centuries later, since they contain data not yet fully 
exploited.

CHAPTER FOUR
The South Indian Drama Manuscripts*
Anna Aurelia Esposito
The South Indian drama manuscripts are well known owing to 
Gaṇapati Śāstrī’s editions, published from 1911 onward in the Trivan
drum Sanskrit Series (TSS). Among the texts edited by Gaṇapati Śāstrī 
are the thirteen “Trivandrum plays” attributed to Bhāsa, Mahendravi
kramavarman’s Mattavilāsa, the two dramas of Kulaśekharavarman, 
Tapatīsaṃvaraṇa and Subhadrādhanañjaya, and Harṣadeva’s Nāgā
nanda.1 Other dramas that are often represented in the South Indian 
manuscript tradition are Śaktibhadra’s Āścaryacūḍāmaṇi, Nīla
kaṇṭha’s Kalyāṇasaugandhika and Bodhāyana’s Bhagavadajjukīya. 
Today most of the South Indian drama manuscripts are preserved in 
the Oriental Research Institute and Manuscripts Library, University 
of Kerala, Trivandrum (hereafter TR). A good collection is located in 
the Sanskrit College Manuscript Library Tripunithura, Cochin (TP). 
Other manuscripts can be found in the Adyar Library and Research 
Institute, Adyar, Madras, in the Government Oriental Manuscripts 
Library, Madras (Chennai), at Calicut University (CAL), in the Lal
chand Research Library (Lahore) D. A. V. College, Chandigarh (CG),2 
in the Punjab University Library, Lahore (Lahore), and in various private collections.3 Even in UK and Europe some individual South 
* This is a revised and expanded version of Esposito, 2004, chapter III.1.3, “Die 
Eigenheiten der Malayāḷam-Manuskripte.” I would like to thank Dr. Heike Moser 
and Dr. Yasutaka Muroya for valuable commentary and bibliographical information 
as well as Dr. Lucinda Martin for correcting the English in this article.

Indian drama manuscripts are extant—e.g. in the School of Oriental

General Remarks
The South Indian drama manuscripts are written on palm leaf,5 usually in Malayāḷam script, but occasionally in Grantha.6 Writers incised 
the letters with a stylus. To make the incised letters visible, a paste was 
smeared over the leaf and then wiped off. The paste settled into the 
grooves, making the letters visible.7 In case the dye faded away, this 
procedure was repeated.
To bind the ms., two round holes were punched in the middle of 
each half of a leaf. A wooden board of approximately the same size as 
the leaves and with similar holes was placed on either side of a set of 
leaves. A cotton cord was drawn through the string holes. The bundle 
was then bound by the same cord (see also Murthy, 1996: 28). In this 
way single dramas or sets of dramas are kept together in a batch. Since 
single leaves can easily be removed or added, sometimes whole dramas are missing from a set. At times, texts written by different scribes 
and concerning unrelated topics are bound together.8
details concerning these private collections see the article of Heike Moser in the 
present volume.
4  In particular the manuscripts belonging to the “Trivandrum plays” attributed 
to Bhāsa are well documented: Unni, 1978 lists most of the manuscripts belonging to 
this group; further material is itemized in Brückner, 2000. Regrettably Unni does not 
include Brückner’s results in his revised edition of 2001, although he does mention 
her article. See also Brückner, 2007 and Heike Moser’s article in the present volume 
on the “Würzburg Multimedia Databank on Sanskrit Drama and Theatre,” based on 
the manuscripts of the “Trivandrum plays.”
5  For details concerning the species of palm leaf, its preparation, conservation, 
etc. see Murthy, 1996: 27-31 and Vijayan, 1996. Some recent paper copies of palm 
leaf manuscripts also exist (mostly in Devanāgarī script), e.g. mss. R 3810 (written in 
1921/22), R 5148 (written in 1925/26) and SD 1024 (written in 1906) of the Government Oriental Manuscripts Library, Madras, or ms. T 48 of the Oriental Research 
Institute and Manuscripts Library, Trivandrum.
6  The only South Indian drama manuscript written in Grantha script of which I 
am aware is ms. CAL 1488. It contains Abhiṣekanāṭaka, Bālacarita and Avimāraka. 
While Bālacarita is complete, only one folio of Abhiṣekanāṭaka is preserved (prose 
passage after v. 34 of the 7th act, up to the end); in Avimāraka the last folios (after 
verse 10 of the 4th act) are missing.
7  See also Murthy, 1996: 52-54.
8  E.g. ms. Lahore 6243 not only contains Abhiṣekanāṭaka and Pratimānāṭaka 
(written in Malayāḷam script) but also an Aśvalāyanasmṛti written in Grantha. The

Figure 4.1.  Ms. TP 835. See colour section, Plate II.
The South Indian drama manuscripts represent a quite uniform tradition. The oldest manuscripts are about 500 years old; the most recent 
were written at the beginning of the twentieth century. Since, as far as 
I know, only one out of about one-hundred manuscripts is written in 
Grantha, I will concentrate hereafter on the manuscripts written in 
Malayāḷam script. Many of the characteristics described below are by 
no means restricted to this group alone and can be found also in other 
manuscript traditions.
Figure 4.2.  Above: Ms. TR 22848 A (Svapnavāsavadatta, ca. 1500 ad) [in Malayāḷam 
script]
In the middle: Ms. TR 12356 A (Svapnavāsavadatta, ca. 1896 ad) 
[in Malayāḷam script]
Below: Ms. CAL 1488 A (Bālacarita) [in Grantha script]. See colour section, Plate III.
reason for the binding together of texts written by different scribes and concerning 
unrelated topics might be due to the use of these texts by a single individual or family.

Characteristics of the South Indian Drama Manuscripts
Foliation: The leaves of a manuscript are normally paginated. The 
numbers appear on the left margin of each recto side,9 usually midway down. Two different forms of numeration can be observed in 
manuscripts written in Malayāḷam script: Some manuscripts display 
the decimal numeration system (aṅkapalli) while others display syllabic numerals (akṣarapalli).10 The latter system is by far more frequent 
in the drama manuscripts. Since it starts with na=1 and nna=2, it is 
called sometimes the “nannādi” system. As indicated by Muroya 
(2006: 26), the first leaf is often marked with śrī and only on the second leaf does the actual Foliation with na=1 begin.11 Contrary to 
Malayāḷam, Grünendahl (2001: 55-57) lists for Grantha12 only numerals of the decimal system; apparently there is no incidence of syllabic 
numerals. Curiously enough, the only drama manuscript written in 
Grantha script known to me, ms. 1488 of the Calicut University, is not 
paginated with Grantha numerals as might be expected, but rather 
with Malayāḷam numerals.
Text flow: Normally both sides of the palm-leaves contain text. A 
margin remains on either end of the leaf. Around the string holes a 
rectangular space is also left blank. Otherwise, writing is continuous 
from the left hand top margin corner to the right hand margin corner 
below, with both left and right margins aligning perfectly. Words and 
punctuation marks are strung together with no spacing in between. 
Similarly, there are no breaks between single text passages or acts, not 
even between the end of one drama and the beginning of the next in a 
set of dramas. The end of an act is indicated through the words 
prathamo’ṅkaḥ, dvitīyo’ṅkaḥ etc. Yet phrases such as atha dvitīyo 
9  In other words, the folios are numbered and not the pages, a system that 
appears to be common in all Indian manuscripts, see Murthy, 1996: 106.
10  For an overview of numeration in Malayāḷam script, see Grünendahl, 2001: 
93 f. Cf. also Bendall, 1896. Ikari (1996: 150, fn. 10) calls the syllabic numerals “the 
older type of Malayalam numerals.” Only very few drama manuscripts are paginated 
with numerals of the decimal system. Surprisingly those manuscripts belong neither 
to the oldest nor to the youngest group of manuscripts.
11  Concerning the South Indian drama manuscript tradition, this is the case, for 
example, in ms. CAL 948, in mss. CG 3521 and 3741, in ms. Lahore 7308, as well as 
in mss. TR C 2101, 5838, 10720, 10750 and 20514. For other examples of this type of 
foliation see Ikari, 1995: 10, fn. 30 and Ikari, 1996: 150, fn. 10.
12  Grünendahl adopts the designations “Grantha Tamil” and “Grantha Malaya
lam,” while I prefer “Grantha” and “Malayāḷam.”

’ṅkaḥ etc.13 are never used at the beginning of an act. Sometimes after 
the end of a drama, one finds invocations to various deities14 or ornaments15 drawn to fill the remaining space. If in the original from 
which the manuscript was copied, some syllables were illegible or 
damaged, the copyist normally left a gap corresponding to the length 
of the illegible or damaged syllables. In a few cases the palm-leaf itself 
exhibits a damaged portion where no syllables could be incised. In 
such cases the text flow is interrupted and only restarted after the 
damaged part. The intermediate gap is normally filled with dashes or 
flourishes.

The auspicious words hariḥ |śrīgaṇapataye namaḥ| avighnam astu 
usually appear at the beginning of a manuscript or a single drama in a 
set of dramas. Some manuscripts do not contain all of the elements I 
have described here: instead of śrīgaṇapataye, mahāgaṇapataye, 
śrīmahāgaṇapataye or only gaṇapataye appears sometimes. In some 
cases, the writer places the syllable śrī at the left margin, either as a 
kind of “first numeral” as described above, or else above the actual 
Foliation.
Punctuation: The daṇḍa is usually a vertical, but occasionally a horizontal line sometimes the size of a dot. The daṇḍa appears in any case 
at the end of a prose passage, after verses and stage directions.16 Within 
a prose passage, the distribution of daṇḍas sometimes seems to be a 
bit arbitrary. The end of a sentence is at times indicated by the dissolution of the sandhi, by the preservation of the visarga or by a final m

act).

Verses: Verses are not numbered. The end of the first half of the verse 
is indicated by the dissolution of the sandhi, never by a daṇḍa.19 At the 
end of the verse we usually find a single daṇḍa. In some manuscripts, 
however, the end of the verse is also indicated by dissolution of the 
sandhi.20
Avagraha: Grünendahl (2001: 92) states that the avagraha is not 
documented in manuscripts written in Malayāḷam script.21 He is right 
concerning the South Indian drama manuscripts. However, avagrahas were discovered in manuscripts of other literary genres: Muroya 
(2006: 27) attests for his ms. T22 “the very rare usage of the avagraha, 
which occurs thrice in the first book of the N[yāya] Bh[āṣya].” Parpola 
17  The anusvāra is indicated by a small centred circle (ം), the m with virāma 
looks like a small copy of the character ma (മ) with the diagonal line protruding at 
the top (
).
18  Muroya (2006: 27, fn. 13) quotes different interpretations of this flourish: 
while Burnell (1878: 82) and Isaacson (1995: 44) regard it as a form of the word śrī, 
Maas (2006: lxxxii) considers it to be the word oṃ (referring to Grünendahl 2001: 52, 
92). Also Ikari (1995: 17) remarks that this sign in his ms. N2 looks like a script of oṃ 
in Malayāḷam, although generally it appears to be just a sign without any meaning. 
Muroya decides not to designate this sign as either śrī or oṃ since the scribe uses 
different akṣaras to denote śrī and oṃ in his ms. T. As can be seen by the illustrations 
above, the flourish of ms. TR 22848 (ca. 1500 ad) appears to be a sign without meaning, while the flourishes of mss. TR 10720 (1650 ad) and TR 12356 (1896 ad) resemble slightly the akṣaras for śrī and oṃ respectively. In my opinion the flourishes of 
the Malayāḷam manuscripts should be regarded neither as śrī, nor as oṃ, but just as 
signs marking the end of an act, a chapter, a drama, etc.
19  Occasionally the sandhi can be also dissolved between the first and the second 
or the third and the fourth pāda respectively.
20  This is especially the case in Dūtavākya of ms. Munich Cod. Malay. 5.
21  Modern editions of Sanskrit texts in Malayāḷam script use what is known as 
the florin symbol in typography (f).

mentions a rectangular, S-shaped sign for avagraha in one manuscript

in one manuscript of the Pātañjalayogaśāstravivaraṇa.25
Doubling of words or sentences: The doubling of words or short sentences is indicated through two figures for na, one put on top of the 
other (
) after the word or sentence to be doubled. Only in some very 
recent manuscripts is the ligature nna (ന്ന) to be found. 
Stage directions: All stage directions are in Sanskrit, even those concerning Prakrit speakers.26 naipa(t)thye is used constantly instead of 
nepathye.27 Names of characters as well as frequent stage directions 
like prakāśam or ātmagatam are normally abbreviated after the first 
two syllables, rarely after the first syllable; sometimes they are (quite 
arbitrarily) written in their full length. Just in the Turfan Fragments 
(Lüders 1911: 26), some dramas like Cārudatta never designate the 
hero of the play in the stage directions through his name, but through 
the word nāyaka. Since in Cārudatta the heroine is a courtesan, we 
find gaṇikā instead of nāyikā (not so, for example, in Nāgānanda). 
Concerning domestics, their names (when given) often alternate in 
the stage directions with the designation of their profession.28
Chāyā: In no manuscript is a chāyā for the Prakrit passages to be 
found. In the printed editions the editor added the chāyā.

Scribal errors and variant readings: Scribal errors are normally 
marked by a line or dot above the wrong syllable. In some manuscripts 
wrong syllables are crossed out or marked with round brackets at the 
beginning and end, especially if they belong to a longer passage. 
Missing syllables or words are generally written beneath the corresponding location; sometimes a small cross above the line marks the 
location where the syllables should be inserted.

Regarding the various readings of a manuscript, we have to take into 
account the similarities between the syllables of the Malayāḷam and 
Grantha alphabets, not the Devanāgarī script. The penultimate section 
of this article will give thus a short overview of similar letters and frequent scribal errors.
Abbreviation of sentences: If longer sentences are repeated in an 
adjacent passage, the writer often abridges the repeated sentence after 
the initial words through the formular iti (Prakrit tti) paṭhati.30
Writing of e and o: In contemporary Dravidian scripts there is—
contrary to the Devanāgarī—a distinction between short and long e 
and o. According to Andronov (1996: 19) the letters ē ഏ and ō ഓ 
were adopted into the Malayāḷam script only in the mid nineteeth 
century and for this reason do not appear before the 1870s in books 
and manuscripts. In older manuscripts and Malayāḷam inscriptions 
(see Retnamma, 1994: 103), only those signs appear that designate 
short e and o in today’s script.
Transmission: We can assume from some mistakes, such as the 
omission of whole lines or the interchange of similar letters, that the 
manuscripts were mostly transcribed, not dictated. In very few manu- 
29  Manuscript belonging to the Potiyil Chakyar family, containing Tapatīsaṃ

ṣekanāṭaka and Dūtavākya.
30  For example in Cārudatta III.14.12 ms. TP 834 L rukkhapāḍia tti paṭhati, “she

scripts, however, do mistakes occur which reveal that the scribe took 
oral dictation.31 Normally the verses are transmitted quite uniformly 
in the manuscripts,32 while larger variations can be found in the prose 
passages and in the stage directions. Some manuscripts clearly display 
contaminations from different traditions.33 For this reason, establishing a stemma for a critical edition can prove quite difficult.
Some Peculiarities in the Sanskrit of the Manuscripts
r before consonants: Contrary to sibilants or h, plosives, nasals and semivowels are usually doubled after r (e.g. karmma, pūrvva, dīrggha). This 
peculiarity also manifests itself in the Turfan Fragments (see Lüders, 
1911: 32).
Aspirated dentals + y, v: Lüders (1911: 32) has noted the gemination of plosives, nasals and l before y as well as the gemination of plosives before v.34 In the Malayāḷam manuscripts, this gemination is 
limited to aspirated dentals before y and v.35 This peculiarity, although 
frequent, is not constant throughout.
Nasals in combination with consonants: A nasal, in combination 
with a palatal, cerebral, dental or r, is written with ligature. In combination with a guttural, however, a nasal is usually replaced by the 
anusvāra (ം) in order to avoid difficult ligatures. For the same reason, 
either the anusvāra or the special sign for m with virāma ( ) appears 
before labials. Additionally, the m with virāma can sometimes be 
found before v.
Anusvāra: For final anusvāra in Sanskrit passages, the same rules as 
described for nasals in combination with consonants hold true. At the

before initial i and e if it is preceded by a vowel, like in ka yeṣa, nāstu yiti etc.
32  Variants mostly affect whole pādas and can be found in several manuscripts.
33  This is, for example, the case in ms. Munich Cod. Malay. 5 (Dūtavākya), which

Triple consonant clusters: In ligatures with three consonants of 
which two are identical, one of the two identical consonants is sometimes dropped.37
Visarga: Before sibilants, the visarga is normally assimilated.38 If 
the sibilant is already connected with another consonant, a triple consonant cluster is generated, which is often simplified by dropping one 
of the two sibilants.39 In this situation the question sometimes arises, 
whether we have an assimilation of the visarga (with a dropping of 
one of the two sibilants) or a form ending in -a, like the vocative 
instead of the nominative of masculine a-stems or even the formation 
of a compound with the following word.
ḷ in Sanskrit: In particular Sanskrit words intervocalic l is replaced 
by ḷ. This usage persevers not only in all manuscripts, but also in the 
editions printed in Malayāḷam script (see e.g. the edition of the 
“Trivandrum Plays” of Bhāskaran, 1987). The reason for this remains 
a mystery to me.40

trary. Contrary to the Devanāgarī script, a graphic confusion between 
the two letters is in Malayāḷam hardly possible (ബ = ba, വ = va).
Alteration between t and l: Since in the Malayāḷam manuscripts त् 
and ल् are both written as ൽ and pronounced as l, the sounds are 
sometimes confused in consonant clusters. Instead of the character ൽ 
for t with virāma before the following consonant, in such cases the 
character ല for la is placed above the following consonant. Conversely, 
instead of l in combination with a consonant the character ൽ for t 
with virāma is written before the following consonant.42
Sandhi: Contrary to standards, sandhis are often dissolved, especially between pādas.
Some Peculiarities in the Prakrit of the Manuscripts43
Anusvāra: Concerning the Prakrit passages, there are two spelling systems: In the first system, medial and final anusvāra are treated in the 
Prakrit passages exactly as in Sanskrit (for the rules, see above). As in 
Sanskrit, the m with virāma can also indicate the end of a sentence. In 
the second system the assimilation of the medial or final anusvāra is 
very rare; usually it is written as a small circle above the preceding 
consonant or vowel.44
Geminate consonants: The two systems differ in the treatment of 
geminate consonants as well. In the first system, geminate consonants 
are written as in Sanskrit with ligatures45 (tta = , ddha = ). In the 
Malay. 5, Potiyil 11 G, and 12.9 mss. CAL 793 D, Potiyil 11 G, TP 834 M, TR 3161, 
TR 18516 D, further 16.1 ms. Munich Cod. Malay. 5, and 18.2 mss. CAL 793 D, 
Munich Cod. Malay. 5.
42  In Cārudatta we find e.g. ulphulla (IV.3b ms. TP 834 L) or ulkaṇṭhita (III.1a 
ms. TP 834 L). Some examples of Dūtavākya are ulpanne (2a mss. CAL 793 D, CAL 
948 B, TP 835 B, TP 842 B, TR 192 C, TR 5838 B, TR 10696 B, TR 17306 C, TR 
20514), -olphulla- (7a mss. CL 948 B, TP 842 B, TR 192 C, TR 10720 A, TR 10750 A, 
TR 18516 D, TR 20514), āsanāl patitaḥ (16.13 ms. CL 948 B), tal kartum (23d ms. 
TP 834 M) or etāval paryāptaṃ (30c ms. CL 948 B), furthermore katpaiḥ instead of 
kalpaiḥ (5b ms. CG 2056 C) or phatguna instead of phalguna (33d ms. Munich Cod. 
Malay. 5). For this phenomenon see also Gundert, 1867: 19 f. § 34, Frohnmeyer, 
1913: 9 § 23, Aiyar, 1937: 279-286, Sekhar, 1953: 25, Staal, 1961: 38 and Raghavan 
Pillai, 1996: 64.
43  For a discussion of the drama Prakrit as found in the manuscripts see 
Esposito, 2004: 31-83.
44  So kiṃ dāṇi is written in the first type of manuscript കിാഞി, in the second 
type 
.
45  In order to avoid complicated ligatures, the first consonant is sometimes written in both Sanskrit and Prakrit with virāma.

--- Page 108 of 338 ---
anna aurelia esposito
92
second system, however, the doubling of consonants is indicated by a 
small circle preceding the consonant to be doubled (tta = ം ത, ddha = 
ം ധ); ligatures such as in the Sanskrit spelling are rare. െ for e in combination with consonants—in the Malayāḷam script this sign precedes 
the affiliated character—is written in this system between the circle 
indicating the doubling of the consonant and the consonant: the syllable cche appears in Sanskrit as well as in the first type of manuscript 
as െ, but is written in the second system as ം െ. Peculiar to this 
system is the writing of yya as, for example, in ayya (ārya), written 
a—circle—a (അംഅ). In the first system it is always written as double-ya as in the Sanskrit (അ).
Nasals in combination with consonants: In the first system nasals in 
combination with consonants are treated as in Sanskrit (see above). In 
some cases a doubling of aspirated consonants can be found.46 In the 
second system, a nasal in combination with a consonant is written—
like an anusvāra—as a small circle above the preceding consonant or 
vowel; ligatures occur, but are rare. In this system, confusion is no rarity between the circle denoting the gemination of consonants on the 
one hand and medial or final anusvāra as well as nasal in combination 
with consonants on the other hand.
Frequent Scribal Errors in Malayāḷam Manuscripts
In examining the various readings of South Indian drama manuscripts 
we have to take into account the similarities between the syllables of 
the Malayāḷam alphabet as well as some peculiarities of this tradition. 
In the following I would like to highlight the most frequent scribal 
errors that should be kept in mind while judging the variant readings 
of the manuscripts.
o in combination with a consonant is formed by writing the symbol 
െ before, and the symbol ാ after the respective consonant (e.g. ക�ൊ = 
ko). If either of these symbols is missing, the readings kā (കാ) or ke 
(കെ) are produced. au in combination with a consonant is formed by 
writing the symbol െ before, the symbol ൗ after the respective consonant (e.g. െ കൗ = kau). If the symbol ൗ is dropped, we again obtain 
the reading ke (കെ). ai in combination with a consonant is formed by 
46  For example, puphpha (Skr. puṣpa) in Cārudatta I.0.36 mss. Madras SR 1714 
G, TP 834 L and TR 22848 D.

--- Page 109 of 338 ---
south indian drama manuscripts
93
writing the symbol െ twice before the respective consonant (e.g. കൈ 
= kai). If one െ is left out, again we have the reading ke (െക).
Confusion is especially likely in Malayāḷam script between ca (ച), 
pa (പ), va (വ) and kha (ഖ), between ga (ഗ) and śa (ശ), between da 
(ദ) and bha (ഭ), between o and au in combination with a consonant 
(ko = ക�ൊ, kau = െകൗ), but also between i (ഇ), u (ഉ) and ḷa (ള), as 
well as between the symbol for ā in combination with a consonant (ാ) 
and the visarga (ഃ). In older manuscripts, there is still a great resemblance between ka (ക) and ta (ത).47 In some hastily written manuscripts the visarga can easily be confused with a daṇḍa.
As already described above, the visarga is normally assimilated 
before sibilants. If the sibilant is already connected with another consonant, theoretically a triple consonant cluster is generated; but in 
actual practice one of the two sibilants is often dropped.
Anusvāra and visarga are often dropped, especially at the end of a 
sentence. Sometimes a medial or final anusvāra is added. Furthermore 
a final visarga has often been inserted, especially when the scribe confused it with a daṇḍa. 
Often syllables or single consonants in ligatures have been dropped, occasionally wrong syllables or consonants have been inserted.
At times the scribe has forgotten the vowel symbols for ā, i, ī, u, ū, ṛ 
or e in combination with a consonant, resulting in a reading with a 
(e.g. ക = ka instead of കാ kā, കി ki,  ku, കെ ke etc.). Conversely, 
occasionally a vowel symbol is wrongly written where the reading 
should contain a simple a.
Sometimes short vowels are mistakenly lengthened. Yet we also 
find cases in which the elongation of vowels has been neglected.
As described above, some manuscripts exhibit a special spelling 
system in the Prakrit passages in which an anusvāra as well as a nasal 
in combination with a consonant is written as a small circle above the 
preceding consonant or vowel, and geminate consonants are indicated by a small circle preceding the consonant to be doubled. Naturally, 
the confusion of these circles is a frequent mistake in the Prakrit passages of those manuscripts.48
47  For the development of some significant symbols in Malayāḷam script and the 
possibility of a relative dating of Malayāḷam manuscripts, see below; cf. also Esposito, 
2010a.
48  E.g. in Cārudatta ms. TR 17622 D we find I.0.14 diṇṭhiā instead of diṭṭhiā 
(diṣṭyā) or I.17.4 bhaśśidavvā instead of bhaṃśidavvā (bhraṃśitavyā).

--- Page 110 of 338 ---
anna aurelia esposito
94
The Dating of the Manuscripts
Few of the drama manuscripts written in Malayāḷam script are dated. 
The year is given in all of them in kollam. As Burnell (1878: 55) 
records, the so-called “Koḷambam (or Quilon) Era” began in 
September 824 ad and is supposed to commemorate the founding of 
Kollam (Quilon).49 Based on the script in those manuscripts which do 
contain a reliable date, an approximate relative chronology can be 
established for the undated manuscripts. Certain characters and ligatures show a striking development which seems to have its origin in 
the Grantha alphabet and which culminates in today’s print. In the 
Malayāḷam script, especially initial a, the letter ka as well as the ligatures kka and kra can be used as “index fossils” to establish a relative 
chronology of manuscripts. I would like to illustrate here the development of these characters on the basis of three manuscripts. The first is 
ms. TR 22848, together with ms. TR 3168 the oldest example of the 
“Trivandrum plays.” Unfortunately this manuscript is undated, but it 
is supposedly 450-500 years old.50 The mss. TR 10720 and TR 12356 
were finished 825 kollam / 1650 ad and 1071 kollam / 1896 ad respectively.
49  As far as I know, the scribes mentioned the year during which they had copied 
the respective text only in the following manuscripts of the “Trivandrum plays”: TR 
10720 in 825 kollam / 1650 ad, TR 20469 in 841 kollam / 1666 ad, TR 557 in 998 
kollam / 1823 ad, and TR 12356 in 1071 kollam / 1896 ad.
50  This manuscript is identical with Gaṇapati Śāstrī’s ms. ka (see Esposito, 2000: 
551, fn. 1), which he declared in his first edition of Svapnavāsavadatta to be “more 
than three hundred years old” (1912: i).

CHAPTER FIVE
The Jaiminīya Sāmaveda Traditions and 
Manuscripts in South India1
Masato Fujii
Since A. C. Burnell acquired unknown manuscripts of the Jaiminīya 
Sāmaveda in South India in the second half of the 19th century, textual studies on the Jaiminīya literature have been pursued by 
H. Oertel, W. Caland, and others.2 Field researches on the present 
Jaiminīya traditions were started, a little later, by Arnold A. Bake, and 
continued by Frits Staal, E. R. Sreekrishna Sarma, Asko Parpola, 
Wayne Howard, Masato Fujii, and others. On the basis of the data 
accumulated through our previous textual and field studies on the 
Jaiminīya Sāmaveda, Asko Parpola and I have started a joint research 
project for documenting all the existent Jaiminīya traditions and 
manuscripts in the different regions where local families have kept 
their own traditions. For this project, we have carried out field research 
in India six times so far from 2002 onward.3 As a report of our research 
results, this paper will briefly survey the Jaiminīya traditions and 
manuscripts which we have located and documented in South India.
1  This interim report is a fruit of the field research for the documentation of the 
present Jaiminīya traditions and manuscripts conducted by Asko Parpola and me as 
a part of the joint project on the existent Vedic texts and traditions in South Asia 
sponsored by the Japan Society for the Promotion of Science 2005-2007 (Project 
No. 17320015). We are most thankful to Sri M.N. Nārāyaṇan Nampūtiri for his 
generous and skillful assistance during our field work in Kerala, Tamil Nadu, and 
Karnataka. I would like to express my gratitude to International Institute for Asian 
Studies (IIAS) for giving me a subsidy for expenses for my attending and presenting 
this paper at the IIAS Workshop: Production, distribution and collection of Sanskrit 
manuscripts in Ancient South India (Leiden, 20-21 April 2007, organized by Dr. 
Saraju Rath). In connection with the present article, a catalogue of the Jaiminīya 
manuscripts which we have traced in our field work will be published in the proceedings of the 12th World Sanskrit Conference, Helsinki 2003. 
2  For the history and present state of the studies on the Jaiminīya texts, see 
Parpola, A. 1973. 
3  22.2-12.3.2002 (Kerala and Tamil Nadu), 14-28.2.2003 (Kerala, Tamil Nadu), 
17.1-14.2.2004 (Kerala, Tamil Nadu), 13-27.2.2006 (Tamil Nadu, Kerala, Karnataka), 
18-26.11.2006 (Baroda, Chandigarh), 16-25.2.2008 (Varanasi).

--- Page 116 of 338 ---
masato fujii
100
1.  The Jaiminīya Sāmaveda Traditions 
As far as the living traditions, oral and written, are concerned, there 
exist three Sāmavedic schools at present, i.e., Kauthuma, Rāṇāyanīya, 
and Jaiminīya schools.4 As a minority of Sāmavedins, the Jaiminīyas are 
found now in limited areas of South India, in the southernmost states, 
Tamil Nadu and Kerala.5 According to their geographical distribution 
and the differences in their traditions, they form the following three 
regional groups:6
– Tamil Jaiminīyas in Tamil Nadu
– Tamil Jaiminīyas in Kerala
– Nampūtiri Jaiminīyas in Kerala
The Jaiminīyas of the first and second groups speak Tamil, and have 
almost the same Tamil tradition of the Jaiminīya Sāmaveda. The difference is that the Tamil Jaiminīyas in Kerala, who live near the border with Tamil Nadu, use Malayalam script not only for their ordinary 
writing but also for Sāmavedic manuscripts.7 
4  For the geographical distribution of the Vedic schools in the Vedic times, see 
Witzel 1987; 1989: passim. For the migration of the Vedic schools after the Vedic 
period, see Renou 1950; Witzel 1985; 1993. For the Jaiminīyas’ coming to South 
India, see Parpola, A. 1984: 447-449 (21-23). For the lists of Sāmavedic schools in the 
Caraṇavyūha, see Weber 1850: 60f.; 1855: 272-277; Simon 1889: 26-31; Siegling 1906: 
25-27; Tsuji 1981 [1948]: 319-323.
5  Mahidāsa, a commentator of the Caraṇavyūha, living in the middle of the 16th 
century, teaches the locations of the three existent Sāmavedic schools as follows: 
āsāṃ ṣoḍaśaśākhānāṃ madhye tisraḥ śākhā vidyante. tāś ca gurjaradeśe kauthumī 
prasiddhā, karṇāṭake jaiminī prasiddhā, mahārāṣṭradeśe rāṇāyanī prasiddheti (v.l. 
rāṇāyanīyā ceti ; Siegling 1906: 27: rāṇāyanīyā iti) “Of these sixteen schools, three 
schools are found. And those [schools are established in the following lands], the 
Kauthuma [school] is established in the land of Gurjara (= Gujarat), the Jaimini 
[school] is established in Karṇātaka, and the Rāṇāyana [school] is established in the 
land of Mahārāṣṭra'' (cf. Renou 1947: 127; Tsuji 1981 [1948]: 323). Raghavan (1957: 
59) and Staal (1961: 73) inform us of Sāmavedins near Uḍupi (Udipi) in Karnataka 
who call themselves Jaiminīyas. In 2006 at Melkote near Mysore, Karnataka, we met 
Jaiminīya people living as priests or officials at a famous Vaiṣṇava temple, Tirunā
rāyaṇa Temple. According to their tradition, their ancestors came from Śrīraṅgam, 
following Rāmānuja who was expelled by a Śaiva king. If this is historically true, 
they originally belonged to the Jaiminīya tradition in Central Tamil Nadu.
6  Cf. Raghavan 1957: 55-59; Staal 1961: 64-75; Parpola, A. 1973: 22-24; 1984: 444447 (18-21); Howard 1977: 153-249. 
7  From the fact that some old manuscripts derived from this tradition are in 
Grantha script, it is inferred that they adopted Malayalam script relatively late.

--- Page 117 of 338 ---
jaiminīya sāmaveda manuscripts
101
2.1  Tamil Jaiminīyas in Tamil Nadu
In the middle of the 1950s, V. Raghavan reported the places where 
Jaiminīyas were found in Tamil Nadu as follows:
It was noted already that old Tamil inscriptions referred to TalavakāraSāman as current in Tamilnad. Talavakāra was only the older or alternative name for the Jaiminīya. . . . the Talavakāra Śākhā of the Sāma 
Veda was still current in some parts of Tamilnad. They had it thus at 
Kiḍāmangalam, Iḷayāttangudi, Śeṣmūlai, Śendalai, Nāṅgūr, etc., in Tanjore district. ... In Tirucci district, Talavakāras were found in Uttamacēri 
and Anbil; in Tirunelvēli at Āḻvār Tirunagari, Tentiruppērai (Tenkalai 
Vaiṣṇavas) and Watrap; and at Karamanai and Tiruvēṭṭār near Trivandrum, and at Nagarkoyil and Śucīndram also.8
Raghavan’s information more or less accords with the information 
which we have acquired from the Jaiminīya people in Tamil Nadu.9 
Following the information from Raghavan and local people, we visited the villages mentioned therein one by one, and in most of them 
we found Jaiminīya families living still there, but mostly having lost 
their Sāmavedic traditions. As far as we have traced so far, Sāmavedic 
traditions including chanting and manuscripts have been attested in 
the following places in Tamil Nadu:
a)  Tiṭṭakkuṭi (Tittagudi)
Tiṭṭakkuṭi is a small town at the southwestern corner of Kaṭalūr 
(Cuddalore) District. It is this place which was mentioned as Vasiṣṭha
kuṭi in the introductory verses of Bhavatrāta’s commentary on the 
Jaiminīya-Śrautasūtra. According to the verses, Bhavatrāta’s grandfather, Hastiśarman by name, belonging to a Jaiminīya lineage of the 
8  Raghavan 1957: 56f.

Kāśyapa gotra, came to Kerala from Vasiṣṭhakuṭi in the Cola country.10 
There are still Jaiminīya families living in Tiṭṭakkuṭi. They keep some 
of the Jaiminīya traditions, but only those related to the domestic 
Ghya rites.11 
b)  Kiṭāmaṅkalam (Kidamangalam) 
Kiṭāmaṅkalam is a small village in Nākappaṭṭiṉam (Nagapattinam) 
District near Kāraikkāl (Karaikal). In Tamil Nadu, there are four 
Purohitas (chaplains) for the Jaiminīya families at present. It is those 
Purohitas who are expected to keep the Sāmaveda traditions. In 
Kiṭāmaṅgalam, one of the four Purohitas, C. Satyamūrti Aiyaṅkār by 
name, lives, keeping chanting tradition and some manuscripts.12 
c)  Tōkūr (Thogur)
Tōkūr is situated on the north bank of the Kāviri (Cauvery) River in 
Tañcāvūr (Thanjavur) District on the border with Tiruccirāppaḷḷi 
(Tiruchirappalli, Trichy) District. In this village, there is a recitation 
school (pāṭhaśālā) of the Jaiminīya Sāmaveda,13 headed by the late 
T. Rājagopāla Aiyaṅkār14 and his pupil and successor, T. N. Makara

“That noble lineage inhabits a respected village in the Cola country, Vasiṣṭhakuṭi by

Bhūṣaṇam (Makara Bhushanam, Makarapūṣaṇam). Makara Bhūṣa

d)  Tentiruppērai 
Besides the Kāviri (Cauvery) Basin, the Tamil Jaiminīya Sāmaveda has 
another center in South Tamil Nadu. There is an inscription of the 
16th century mentioning the Jaiminīyas in Tirunelvēli.16 Tentiruppērai 
is a Vaiṣṇava temple village near Āḻvār Tirunakari (Alvar Tirunagari), 
formerly in Tirunelvēli District, now in Tūttukkuṭi (Tuticorin) 
District, which consists of a large number of Jaiminīya families. It was 
this village which Bake referred to as one of the places where there was 
a strong living tradition of the Jaiminīya Sāmaveda in his time.17 When 
Howard visited there in 1971, there were a few Jaiminīyas who kept 
the Sāmavedic chanting tradition.18
We have become acquainted with a young Jaiminīya person from 
this village, K. Kaṇṇan by name. He is living now in Kalpakkam near 
Ceṉṉai (Chennai) as an engineer, and at the same time he is officiating 
as Purohita in Tentiruppērai. He learned part of the Jaiminīya chants 
under his father, the late Keśavan Aiyaṅkār.19 With the help of the 
manuscripts and notebooks that he inherited from his father, he is 
trying to keep the Tentiruppērai Jaiminīya tradition, which he said is 
slightly different from the chanting way in Tōkūr.

e)  Aḻakiyapāṇṭipuram (Alagyapandipuram) / 
Pūtapāṇṭi (Bhutapandi)
In the passage quoted above, Raghavan refers to ‘Nagarkoyil’ and 
‘Śucīndram’ in Kaṉṉiyākumari (Kanyakumari) District, the southernmost district of Tamil Nadu, in the list of the Jaiminīya places. We 
have searched these areas for Jaiminīya families, and finally located 
them in Aḻakiyapāṇṭipuram and Pūtapāṇṭi, neighbouring villages 
north of Nākarkōvil (Nagercoil). We have acquired two manuscripts 
there. We were told that the ancestors of the Jaiminīya families there 
came from the above-mentioned Tentiruppērai, but at present they 
have Makara Bhūṣaṇam in Tōkūr as their Purohita instead of Kaṇṇan, 
the Purohita of Tentiruppērai.
This region is situated near the border with Kerala, and has close 
connections with Tiruvanantapuraṃ (Trivandrum). Neṭumpiḷḷi 
Mana, one of the two main Vādhūla houses in Iriññālakkuṭa (Irinjala
kuda), Kerala,20 keeps several Sāmavedic manuscripts which were 
found in Padmanābha Svāmi Temple in Tiruvanantapuraṃ, for which 
the members of this house have been serving as the chief priest 
(tantrin). We were asked by the present master of the house to examine the manuscripts. Against our expectations, they are not Nampūtiri 
Jaiminīya manuscripts but Tamil Jaiminīya manuscripts of the Ārcika 
and Pūrva-Gāna. In all likelihood, those manuscripts came from this 
region.21 
2.2  Tamil Jaiminīyas in Kerala

Similar contact between Tamil Nadu and Kerala is seen in the Tamil 
Jaiminīyas in Kerala. There are many Cōḻiya brahmins, i.e., Tamil 
brahmins whose ancestors moved from the Cōḻa country, the present

Central Tamil Nadu, to Kerala in the remote past. They form agra

2.3  The manuscripts of the Tamil Jaiminīya Sāmaveda
The Jaiminīya manuscript collection acquired by A. C. Burnell in the 
19th century in South India has been a principal source for Jaiminīya 
studies. Almost all the editiones principes of the Jaiminīya texts including the JS, JB, JĀrB, JUB, JŚS, and JGS are based on Burnell’s manuscripts.27 It is to be noted that all the Burnell’s manuscripts belong to 
the Tamil Jaiminīya traditions and do not contain any manuscripts 
from the Nampūtiri Jaiminīya tradition which will be explained below. 
According to Burnell’s notes, quoted or summarized in the catalogue 
by Keith (1935), they came from the following three places mentioned 
above:
– From the vicinities of Tōkūr 
 
Grāmageya-Gāna (Burnell 497a), Āraṇyaka-Gāna(497b), 
 
Dhāraṇalakṣaṇa (497c), JB (500a), JĀrB(497e), JŚS (506c)

26  According to the Accession Register of the Oriental Institute, Baroda, some of

More than one century has passed since Burnell acquired the Jaiminīya 
manuscripts. As far as we have investigated so far, the manuscripts 
kept by the present Tamil Jaiminīyas are mostly limited to those of 
Jaiminīya Sāmavedic chants, i.e., the Ārcika and the Gāna. In the 
above-mentioned regions, we have ascertained the existence of many 
manuscripts of the Ārcika (at the places b, c, d, e, f, N[eṭumpiḷḷi]), and 
some manuscripts of the Grāmageya-Gāna and Āraṇyaka-Gāna with 
musical notations (c, d, e, f, N), and one very rare manuscript of the 
Uttara-Gāna (i.e. Ūha-Gāna and Ūhya-Gāna) with musical notations 
(c). Since the Nampūtiri Jaiminīyas have not handed down any manuscript of the Gāna with musical notations (see below), the Tamil 
Jaiminīya manuscripts of the complete set of the Gāna texts (Grāma
geya-Gāna, Āraṇyaka-Gāna, Ūha-Gāna, Ūhya-Gāna) with musical 
notations are very important materials for the study of the Jaiminīya 
Sāmavedic chants. 
3.1  Nampūtiri Jaiminīyas in Kerala28
Among Nampūtiri (Nambudiri, Namboodiri, Namboothiri) Brah
mins, Malayalam-speaking native Brahmins in Kerala, all the Sāma
vedins are Jaiminīyas. In contrast to the Tamil Jaiminīyas, who 
practice only the Ghya rituals now, the Nampūtiri Jaiminīyas keep 
also the Śrauta ritual traditions in which the Sāmavedic chanting is 
performed. Today, the following twenty Nampūtiri houses belong to 
the Jaiminīya school:29

chants and manuscripts, which have been handed down and copied 
from each other within those circles. The first two teacher houses, 
Muṭṭattukkāṭṭu Māmaṇṇŭ Mana and Nellikkāṭṭu Māmaṇṇŭ Mana, 
have a hereditary relationship with each other, for one Māmaṇṇŭ 
Mana was split into the two Māmaṇṇŭ Manas more than ten generations ago.33 All the three teacher houses are in the same village Pāññāḷ 
(Panjal) near Ceṟuturutti (Cheruthuruthi) in Tśśūr (Thrissur, 
Trichur) District in Central Kerala.34 It was in this village that the 
Agnicayana (or Atirātra-Agnicayana, i.e., the Soma sacrifice of the 
Atirātra type with the bird-shaped fire altar constructed with bricks) 
was performed in 1975 under the observation of outsiders including 
Indian and foreign scholars for the first time.35
The Nampūtiris are divided into two status groups: āḍhyan and 
āsyan. The āḍhyans, who have the distinctive name Nampūtirippāṭŭ 
(Nambudiripad), are ranked higher and generally wealthier than the 
āsyans. In spite of their high rank and wealthiness, the āḍhyans are 
not entitled to perform the Vedic Śrauta rites not only as the priests 
but also as the Yajamānas (sacrificers).36 Among the Jaiminīya houses, 
8. Mūttiriṅṅōṭŭ, 9. Narippaṟṟa, 10. Muṇṭāya, 11. Vaṭakkāñcēri Valiya 
are āḍhyan houses, which all belong to the Kāśyapa gotra and are connected with Bhavatrāta, the famous commentator on both the 
Jaiminīya-Śrautasūtra and Ghyasūtra.37
Another distinction among the Nampūtiri Jaiminīya houses is 
about their two ancestral villages, Śukapuraṃ (Sukapuram, Shuka
puram) and Panniyūr (Panniyoor). These two villages were the most 
prominent Nampūtiri settlements in the past, and had a long rivalry 
and conflicts with each other. Nowadays, the Nampūtiri Jaiminīya

237-240.

houses have Śukapuraṃ as their common ancestral village, but 
3. Perumaṅṅāṭŭ, 4. Tōṭṭam, 5. Koraṭṭikkara, and 6. Maṃgalattēri 
joined in Śukapuraṃ later; originally they had belonged to Panniyūr. 
This distinction has produced the difference of the Ṛgvedic traditions 
with which they collaborate in the Vedic rites. In Panniyūr, the 
Āśvalāyana tradition has been followed for the office of the Hot priest 
(hautra), while only the Kauṣītaki tradition had been followed in 
Śukapuraṃ before the immigration of Āśvalāyana houses from 
Panniyūr.38 The fact that the manuscript of the JUB belonging to 3. 
Perumaṅṅāṭŭ Mana contains the Āśvalāyana-Śrautasūtra at the end 
may reflect this historical background.
3.2  Nampūtiri Jaiminīya Sāmaveda traditions
After the Agnicayana in 1975 at Pāññāḷ, the Agnicayana was performed three times until now (2011), in 1990 at Kuṇṭūr (Kundur), 
2006 at Mūlaṃkōṭǔ (Moolamcode), 2011 again at Pāññāḷ, and the 
Agniṣṭoma nine times in 1984, 2003, 2005, 2005, 2006, 2007, 2007, 
2007, 2009 at different places in Kerala.39 For these ritual performances, the Jaiminīyas from Pāññāḷ played the roles of the Sāmavedic 
chanter priests. In addition to the activities as the chanter priests, 
householders or members of the three teacher houses (1,2,3) and 
other two houses (4,5), all in Pāññāḷ, have also performed the 
Agniṣṭoma and the Agnicayana as the Yajamānas repeatedly until 
recently.40 
38  See Parpola 1984: 433f. (7f.); cf. Staal 1983: I, 173. Śukapuram is situated 1 km 
east of Eṭappāḷ (Edappal) in Malappuṟaṃ (Malappuram) District, and Panniyūr is 
17 km west of Paṭṭāmpi (Pattambi) in Pālakkāṭǔ District. A Nampūtiri website has a 
list of the Nampūtiri houses belonging to Śukapuraṃ (http://www.namboothiri.
com/articles/sukapuram-graamam.htm), in which the Jaiminīya houses in Kōṭṭayaṃ 
(12 to 20) are not included.
39  For the Agnicayana in 1975, see n. 35; for Agnicayana 1990, Staal 1992; for the 
Agniṣṭoma in 2003, Mahadevan & Staal 2005. 
40  According to the list by C. V. Somayajipad etc. (1983), Muṭṭattukkāṭṭu 
Māmaṇṇŭ: Agniṣṭoma 1880, 1911, 1929, and once more (year unkown), Agnicayana 
1844; Nellikkāṭṭu Māmaṇṇŭ: Agniṣṭoma 1916, 1941, and once more, Agnicayana 
1901, 1918, 1956; Perumaṅṅāṭŭ: Agniṣṭoma 1904, and once more; Tōṭṭam: Agni
ṣṭoma 1912, 1925, and once more; Koraṭṭikkara: Agniṣṭoma 1915, and twice more. 
A Nampūtiri who has performed the Agnyādheya (Ādhānam in local terminology), 
the Agniṣṭoma (Sōmayāgam), and the Agnicayana (Agni) as the Yajamāna is honorifically called Aṭitiri[ppāṭŭ], Sōmayāji[ppāṭŭ], and Akkittiri[ppāṭŭ] respectively. A 
Nampūtiri can perform the Agnicayana only after he performs the Agniṣṭoma in his

--- Page 126 of 338 ---
masato fujii
110
The chanting tradition itself has been handed down from teachers 
to pupils privately in the Jaiminīya houses.41 Recently, the Nampūtiri 
Jaiminīyas have lost two great teachers: Iṭṭi Ravi Nampūtiri (19041989) of Muṭṭattukkāṭṭu Māmaṇṇŭ Mana and Nīlakaṇṭhan Akkittirip
pāṭŭ (1905?-1997) of Nellikkāṭṭu Māmaṇṇŭ Mana. Iṭṭi Ravi Nampūtiri 
was one of the greatest masters of the Nampūtiri Jaiminīya Sāmaveda. 
According to his personal history,42 he received Vedic education from 
his father, and in his turn taught twenty-five pupils during his lifetime. He was the first person who gave authentic information on the 
Nampūtiri Jaiminīya Sāmaveda to Vedic scholars from abroad already 
in the 1930s.43 Nīlakaṇṭhan Akkittirippāṭŭ was born in a very orthodox Nampūtiri house, and learned the whole of the Jaiminīya 
Sāmaveda in the traditional way.44 Ever since he married and established the Śrauta fires, he had observed strictly the daily duties of a 
Vedic Nampūtiri, i.e., the morning and evening Agnihotras until the 
death of his wife, and the morning services including the Saṃdhyāvan
lifetime. For the Agnihotra to be performed every morning and evening after the 
Agnyādheya, see n. 45. 
41  The Nampūtiri Jaiminīyas call the texts of the Sāmaveda-Saṃhitā in the following special way: “Ṛk” for the Ārcika, “Sāmaṃ” for the Grāmageya-Gāna, “Candrā
syāmaṃ” for the Āraṇyaka-Gāna, “Ūhaṃ'' for the Ūha-Gāna, and “Ūṣāṇi” for the 
Ūhya-Gāna. In the traditional curriculum, the Sāmaveda are taught in this order of 
the texts.
42  I consult his personal history attached to the official programme of the investiture ceremony of the Certificate of Honour, which he received from the President 
of India in New Delhi in 1980, and an article about him in the newspaper, The Sunday Standard, August 26, 1979.
43  The newspaper, The Sunday Standard, August 26, 1979, reads: “It all started 
with a request by the western scholar A. A. Bako (sic) for guidance while writing ‘The 
practice of Sama Veda’. He found Mamunnu a virtual reference book. Others followed. Mamunnu gave valuable assistance and information” (Mamunnu= Māmaṇṇŭ, 
i.e. Iṭṭi Ravi). For A. A. Bake’s pioneering field research on the Sāmaveda, see Bake 
1935; Brough 1964: 250; Jairazbhoy 1991: 6.
44  His memoirs to be published explain his life during the learning period as follows: “For Sāmavedi Brahmins, after Upanayanam before Samāvartanam, there are 
four Vratas which last one year each. In addition to them, there is another Vrata 
lasting one month. At the close of certain Vratas the Brahmacharin becomes entitled 
to study certain special portions of the Veda Saṃhitā. At the time of closing the 
Vrata, Acharya pronounces these portions to the disciple who is seated in front of 
him. The first 12 chapters of Chandrāsyāma (=Āraṇyaka-Gāna) which are otherwise 
called Vratāni, some chapters of Jaiminīya-Upaniṣad and Mahānāmnya portion of 
Chandrāsyāma are included in this category. After properly completing all these 
Vratas and Muttal Mura (=first complete recitation of Sāmaveda) at the age of 14, 
my Samāvartanam was conducted with a feast and dakṣiṇā to Brahmins. Thus I 
became an ‘Unni Namboodiri’” (Karmamārga: Memoirs of Brahmaśrī Nellikkāṭṭu N. 
Nīlakaṇṇthan Akkittirippāṭŭ). These memoirs will be published both in Malayalam 
and in English (ed. by A. Parpola).

--- Page 127 of 338 ---
jaiminīya sāmaveda manuscripts
111
dana, the Svādhyāya (the recitation of some parts of the Vedic texts), 
the Sūryanamaskāra, etc. until the end of his life.45
The Nampūtiri Sāmavedic chanting tradition has been declining 
quite rapidly, and has died out in most of the houses. The rapid declination of the chanting tradition, which is now on the verge of complete extinction, has been caused chiefly by the social changes that 
have been taking place in Kerala in general and in the Nampūtiri 
society in particular, including the changes of educational, occupational, matrimonial, and inheritance systems. Especially, the breakdown of the traditional primogeniture has led to repeated partition of 
the original spacious compounds and lands, which had enabled 
Nampūtiris to maintain their Vedic traditions.46 Now, there are only a 
couple of persons who have mastered a chief part (not the whole) of 
the Sāmaveda and can chant sāmans in the traditional way of the 
Nampūtiri Jaiminīya Sāmaveda.47
3.3  The manuscripts of the Nampūtiri Jaiminīya Sāmaveda
Besides the oral tradition of the Sāmavedic chants, the Nampūtiri 
Jaiminīyas have kept wealthy literal tradition in the form of palm-leaf 
manuscripts covering from Śruti and Smti texts written in Sanskrit to 
ritual manuals in Malayalam. Large numbers of manuscripts are kept 
45  According to the Nampūtiri tradition, one performs the Aupāsana (the worship of the domestic fire called aupāsana-agni) every day after his marriage. Since he 
has established the three Śrauta fires at the Agnyādheya, he performs the Agnihotra 
instead until the death of his wife. Generally the Aupāsana fire and the Śrauta fires are 
established at the place called vaḍakkini in the north of a Nampūtiri house. Probably 
there is now no Nampūtiri who performs the Aupāsana. (Oral information from M. 
Subrahmaṇyan Nampūtiri). The Agnihotra is still performed by some Nampūtiris 
every morning and evening. In 1996 and 1997, I witnessed the performances of the 
Agnihotra by two Nampūtiris, Cerumukkŭ Vallabhan Sōmayājippāṭŭ and Kavapra 
Māṟattŭ Śaṅkaranārāyaṇan Sōmayājippāṭŭ (now Akkittirippāṭŭ), both belonging to 
the Kauṣītaki Ṛgveda, at Kiḷḷimaṃgalaṃ (Killimangalam) in Tśśūr District and at 
Mūtūr (Mudur) in Malappuṟaṃ (Malappuram) District respectively, 
46  For the changes in the Nampūtiri society, see Mencher 1966b; Namboodiripad 
1976; Parpola, M. 1998: 49-51; 2000: 26-33. M. Parpola describes ethnographically 
the decline of the Vedic ritual and educational tradition in one Jaiminīya family 
through several generations up to the present (2000: 160-171).
47  Nowadays, when Sāmavedic chanting is needed among the Nampūtiris, it is 
performed by the same persons: Nīlakaṇṭhan and Vāsudēvan of Nellikkāṭṭu

Āryan, Kṣṇan and Śivakaran of Tōṭṭam Mana. They were pupils of the late

their father, the late Subrahmaṇyan Nampūtiri.

112
in the three teacher houses of the Nampūtiri Jaiminīyas: 83 at Muṭṭat
tukkāṭṭu Māmaṇṇŭ Mana, 86 at Nellikkāṭṭu Māmaṇṇŭ Mana, 73 at 
Perumaṅṅāṭŭ Mana. And one āḍhyan Jaiminīya house, Mūttiriṅṅōṭŭ 
Mana, possesses more than fifty manuscripts.48 We have examined all 
the manuscripts available in the Jaiminīya houses. The comprehensive 
documentation of the manuscripts demonstrates that the Nampūtiri 
Jaiminīyas have handed down not only Vedic texts but also a rich heritage of literature including literary, philosophical, grammatical, 
juridical, astrological texts.49
Concerning the Sāmavedic manuscripts, the Nampūtiri Jaiminīyas 
have handed down no manuscript of the Saṃhitā texts including the 
Ārcika and the Gāna in clear contrast to the Tamil Jaiminīyas who are 
in possession of a large number of palm-leaf manuscripts of both the 
Ārcika and the Gāna. It is probably because the Nampūtiri Jaiminīyas 
have kept a strong oral tradition, and have not depended on manuscripts of the chants. There are not a few ‘paper manuscripts’ of the 
Saṃhitā texts among the Nampūtiri Jaiminīyas, but they are nothing 
but notebooks written from memory to be used as learning aids without such musical notations as the manuscripts of the Tamil Jaiminīya 
Gānas have. As far as those ‘paper manuscripts’ are concerned, the 
Nampūtiri Jaiminīya Ārcika consists of the Pūrva-Ārcika (= JS1), the 
first half of the Uttara-Ārcika (= JS 3), Āraṇyaka-Saṃhitā (= JS 2), and 
the second half of the Uttara-Ārcika (= JS 4) in this order, which is different from the order in the editions of the JS (Caland 1907 and Raghu 
Vira 1938) based on a single Tamil Jaiminīya manuscript (Burnell 
506a). 
A different textual order is also seen in the manuscripts of the 
Jaiminīya-Brāhmaṇa. The present editions of the JB (Caland 1919, 
Raghu Vira & Lokesh Chandra 1954) have the following textual order: 
Book I: Agnihotra etc., Jyotiṣṭoma; II: Gavāmayana (part), Ekāha, 
Ahīna, Sattra, Gavāmayana (part); III: Dvādaśāha. Two teacher houses 
(1,2) are in possession of several manuscripts of the JB. Muṭṭattukkāṭṭu 
Māmaṇṇŭ Mana has two old partial manuscripts, and Nellikkāṭṭu 
48  Most of the manuscripts belonging to Mūttiriṅṅōṭŭ Mana have been deposited

kuḷaṃ (Kunnamkulam), Tśśūr District, where a large number of manuscripts have

No manuscripts of the Jaiminīya-Ārṣeya-Brāhmaṇa (a list of sāman 
names) are found in the present Nampūtiri Jaiminīyas. A. Parpola 
points out that the absence of this text is connected with the following 
fact: the Nampūtiri Jaiminīyas use ordinal numbers instead of sāman 
names when they refer to sāmans, while the Tamil Jaiminīyas use 
sāman names in the JĀrB. 51
In contrast to the other Sanskrit texts of the Sāmaveda including 
the JB, JŚS and JGS, whose manuscripts are relatively rare even in the 
teacher houses, manuscripts of the Jaiminīya-Upaniṣad-Brāhmaṇa 
prevail among almost all the houses that have kept (some part of) their 
Sāmavedic tradition (1,2,3,4,5,8,10).52 It is most probable that the

masato fujii
114
prevalence of the manuscripts of the JUB reflects the educational system of the Nampūtiri Jaiminīya tradition. The Nampūtiri Jaiminīyas 
do not study the JB, JŚS, nor JGS when they learn the Sāmaveda, but 
they study (actually only recite a part of) the JUB during the period of 
the aupaniṣada-vrata in their Sāmavedic curriculum.53 The situation 
of the Tamil Jaiminīyas is totally different. In the case of Makara 
Bhūṣaṇam in Tōkūr, the curriculum he followed was not based on the 
vrata system. He learned the Jaiminīya texts one by one under the 
guidance of his teacher (see n. 15). As a result of the waning of the 
vrata system among the Tamil Jaiminīyas, the JUB seems to have lost 
its importance as the text to be learned in connection with the 
aupaniṣada-vrata. Nowadays, the Upaniṣad respected by the Tamil 
Jaiminīyas is not the JUB but the Kena-Up., which is only a small part 
(4,18-21) of the JUB. Probably, the Tamil Jaiminīyas have changed 
their Upaniṣad under the influence of the Vedānta philosophy, in 
which only the Kena-Up. portion has been recognized as an authentic 
Upaniṣad.54 
Brāhmaṇa”, which has been commonly used after the title “The Jaiminīya or 
Talavakāra Upaniṣad Brāhmaṇa” given by H. Oertel to his editio princeps of this text 
(1894), see Fujii 1997: 96, n. 36.
53  For the curriculum of Nīlakaṇṭhan Akkittirippāṭŭ, see n. 44. The curriculum 
of Vāsudēvan Nampūtiri of Perumaṅṅāṭŭ Mana is as follows: he performed the 
Upanayana at the age of six, and made the start of the Vedic learning (Upākarma) 
after two months; his Brahmacārin period lasted six years, during which he learned 
the Ṛk (= Ārcika), the Sāmaṃ (= Grāmageya-Gāna), the Candrāsyāmaṃ (= 
Āraṇyaka-Gāna), and most of the Ūhaṃ (= Ūha-Gāna), but did not learn the Ūṣāṇi 
(= Ūhya-Gāna); during the aupaniṣada-vrata which lasted one year, he learned by 
ear the JUB for one day (according to my interview with him in 1990). Nīlakaṇṭhan 
Akkittirippāṭŭ had recited the shortest chapter (4,3) of the JUB every day as one of
