CHAPTER SIX
Texts and communities: 
the manuscripts of the lost 
YāmalāṢṬakatantra
Cezary Galewicz
The rich legacy of extant Indian manuscripts and historical manuscript cultures of India are gradually attracting attention from beyond 
the narrow circles of collectors, librarians and text-critical editors. 
There are several reasons for this, one of which is a growing interest in 
the multitude of distinct forms and modalities of producing knowledge and circulating texts, and in the possible implications of those 
forms for the circulation of ideas and for the shaping of cultural areas 
and civilisations. The various modalities of producing, copying, diffusing and handling manuscripts in particular socio-historical circum
stances offer a rewarding field of study that forms a rich complement 
to the study of texts and ideas. This new interest on the part of South 
Asia specialists appears to parallel, and perhaps interact with, a more 
general discussion across the humanities, and especially between 
adherents of two seemingly separate sub-disciplines of history, namely 
(a) book history and (b) intellectual history. According to some, the 
methodological separation of the two schools resulted in the partisans 
of book history preferring the history of diffusion; while the adherents 
of intellectual history instead emphasised discourse analysis.1 The 
question of whether and how it is possible to bring the two strands 
together, in such a way that the one could reinforce the other, remains 
important for the intellectual history of Indian and South Asian studies.2 In contradistinction to most other cultural milieus, what in many 
cases is still possible in the Indian framework is the confrontation, or 
at least supplementation, of text-historical evidence with the evidence 
1  A formulation by Robert Darnton [Darnton 2005: 21]. For arguments opposing 
Darnton, see, among others, Skinner 2005.
2  According to a recent study, “compared to number of excellent empirical and 
theoretical studies of the history of the book in the West, book history in South Asia 
is still in its formative phase.” [Stark 2007: 6].

--- Page 136 of 338 ---
cezary galewicz
120
of living communities of traditional text users, the actors of learned 
textual traditions.3 I will use this dichotomy—one which will probably 
never be fully bridged—as the context for a few remarks on a case 
study concerning a fragmentary history of a text and a seemingly 
absent tradition of its reading. This is going to be a brief history made 
up of bits and pieces belonging to the separate worlds of (a) material 
artefacts, library collections, historical circumstances of circulation, 
and (b) living textual communities. Piecing together these different 
elements into one picture requires the development of the historical 
perspective towards the material culture of the ways of circulation of 
written artefacts, as well as a field study of the modalities in which living textual communities expose the same texts to different strategies 
of use and interpretation.
 Before embarking on my specific case study I feel obliged to make 
a brief remark: not being a manuscriptologist in the true sense of the 
term, I decided to touch on a few problems related to this discipline in 
my contribution to the present volume, because of my utmost reverence for those who are professionals in this field, along with my enthusiasm for Indian manuscripts in general, and for the Indian manuscript 
culture in its various historical and regional avatāras in particular. In 
my view, the discipline awaits a new articulation of several problems 
on the part of those of us inclined to the questions formulated either 
within the history of books or discourse analysis, or at the intersection 
of the two. In the Indian context, both sub-disciplines of history can 
cut through manuscriptology and the set of questions specific to its 
field of interest—and they (the sub-disciplines) can hardly do without 
manuscriptology.
The discipline which boasts the right to conceptually deal with 
manuscripts is known, at least to some, as codicology. I recall being 
corrected, if not scolded, on several occasions by elder colleagues from 
other areas of study if I referred to the field as manuscriptology.4 Is it 
actually fruitful—I kept asking myself—to think about the culture of 
manuscripts from the Indian Peninsula in an Indian historical context, and to speak about this in terms and categories developed within 
the discipline of classically understood codicology? Is a medieval 
Indian manuscript the same type of artefact in its socio-historical and 
3  Cf. Jacob 2007: 13-41.
4  The title of David Diringer’s The Book Before Printing—one of the classics in 
the field—seems to still hold best for the formulation acceptable for handwritten 
artefacts from different cultures.

--- Page 137 of 338 ---
the lost yāmalāṣṭakatantra
121
cultural functions as its early European counterpart, the codex? The 
easy answer is that it is not. However, this does very little to explain 
the problem and risks neglecting the characteristic dimensions of 
Indian manuscript cultures. The study of Indian manuscripts along 
with the social practices of their handling, as well as the type of reading expectations and interpretation strategies their circulation would 
stimulate among different historical communities of interpretation, 
should combine or share characteristics of both classical codicology 
and paleography.5 Furthermore, there is a third element which would 
complement these other two. This third component relates to the 
mass of Indian manuscripts in circulation, and their relatively slow 
and reluctant giving way in history to printed matter, suggesting rich 
social and cultural transactions stimulated by their diffusion. It is perhaps related also to the developments within South Asian studies and 
the growing presence of manuscripts as objects of cultural and political transactions in the eyes of contemporary Indologists.6 In other 
words, what makes the difference in the first place is that the Indian 
world of manuscripts proved to be operational or sufficient to cater 
for the needs of its agents, administrators, intellectuals, religious people, scientists, students, believers, literary connoisseurs and casual 
readers at a time when, in Europe, the printed book exerted almost 
undivided rule (perhaps with the exception of some forms of legal 
acts) and contributed through ‘print capitalism’ to the formation of 
nations and nationalisms.7 And maybe this is enough to justify our 
use of the term ‘manuscriptology’ in the eyes of colleagues from other 
disciplines within the broad area of Indian studies.8 It has been estimated that the use of manuscripts continued for a century following 
the introduction of print in Europe, circa 1450, while in India it continued, in many respects, in tandem with print until the beginning of 
the 20th century. It remains open to discussion whether this points to 
India lagging behind in accepting the challenge of modernity, which 
5  Codicology is usually understood as “the scholarly study of manuscripts from 
the point of view of the bookmaking craft”, while paleography deals with the study 
of the practice of deciphering and reading ancient and medieval manuscripts. 
6  According to some estimates the number of extant Indian manuscripts may 
amount to “over thirty million” [Pollock 2006: 558]. This, however, has not always 
been acknowledged by scholars outside Indology—cf. Diringer 1982: 358: “many 
thousands of Indian inscriptions ... have come down to us. [...] On the other hand, 
relatively few documents written on perishable materials, such as palm leaves or 
birch bark, have been preserved.”
7  Anderson 1983: 36-46
8  For instance, Visalakshy 2003, The Fundamentals of Manuscriptology.

--- Page 138 of 338 ---
cezary galewicz
122
Europe faced much earlier, or rather that India’s different developments in social and political contexts not only shaped but were themselves also actively shaped by its manuscript culture. A remarkable 
efficiency in publishing and widely circulating a large number of copies of a text has never been foreign to this culture. In this respect we 
may refer to literary sources which register the important social function of multiple manuscript copies. For example, an 18th century 
chronicle of the small, post-Vijayanagara Nāyaka kingdom of Keladi 
describes a mass distribution of a manuscript text in multiple copies 
which rivaled anything known from the world of print.9 
My specific case concerns the supposedly lost work entitled Yāma
lāṣṭakatantra (YAT) in its relation to a compendium of texts known 
as Ṛgvedadaśagrantha (RVDG) and its contemporary users—brahmin groups of, mostly, Western Maharashtra. The case of YAT 
presents several problems for both book historians and intellectual 
historians. One of these problems concerns the socio-cultural context 
and historical circumstances for a decision to include YAT in an altogether foreign collection of texts used by a community far removed 
from the geographical area where the YAT could have originated 
(where most of its MSS survived). Another problem concerns the 
nature of the relationship between this brahmin textual community 
and the compendium of RVDG which its members see as foundational to their identity. A proper articulation of these problems, however, calls for crossing the boundaries between different disciplines of 
study and their proper methodologies.
A few years ago, during the XIIth World Sanskrit Conference in 
Helsinki, I related the case of an unorthodox list of the Ṛgvedic khilas 
which I had come across in a short text in use among members of a 
local brāhmaṇa community of Āśvalāyana Ṛgveda in the Konkan 
area. The text, somewhat enigmatically introduced as the Yāmalāṣṭaka
tantrekhilopaniṣatmantrasaṃhitāsvarūpam (YATKU), had been in- 
serted into a traditional compendium of more orthodox looking 
brahmanic ‘scriptures.’ This compendium, taking the shape of a traditional edition in the form of a bundle of loose leaflets, contained a 
collection of primary and secondary Ṛgvedic texts belonging to the 
Āśvalāyana school. It was printed in Poona by Ghaisasa Vedapāṭhaśālā 
and entitled Ṛgvedadaśagrantha (RVDG). As I was subsequently to 
discover, the compendium appeared to be closely related to a 1930 
9  See Murthy 1981.

--- Page 139 of 338 ---
the lost yāmalāṣṭakatantra
123
Nirnaya Sagara Press edition of Ṛksaṁhitā by Vasudeva Laxman 
Shastri Panshikar, with the title Sasvāhākāraprayoganirṇayasamantra
kośā ca Ṛksaṁhitā.10 This edition, however, did not feature the name 
of Daśagrantha and appeared more limited in its scope. And yet the 
compendium, named Ṛgvedadaśagrantha, contained the same recension in its first part and clearly repeated the graphic layout of Panshi
kar’s edition. This was visible also in the title page of the saṁhitāgrantha 
section of the RVDG which bore a small type caption featuring the 
name Sasvāhākāraprayoganirṇaya etc.11 I could not trace any textual 
hint for the motive behind the editorial decision to include the short—
supposedly Tantric—text of YATKU within the RVDG, a compendium of otherwise mostly orthodox Ŗgvedic and related subject 
matter. Neither could the members of the Joglekar clan of Gokarna, 
among whom I found the RVDG in use, offer any plausible explanation for this inclusion.
Neither in the RVDG, nor in the earlier editions of Panshikar, was 
the YATKU, claiming to have taken its origin from the Yāmalāṣṭa
katantra, listed in their respective tables of contents.12 Both these publications positioned the YATKU immediately after the text of 
Ṛgvidhāna which, in both cases, was cited as the very last member of 
the list of Ṛgvedic pariśiṣṭas (Ṛksaṁhitāsthapariśiṣṭaviṣayānukramaṇ
ikā). To the best of my knowledge, there is no direct textual link 
between the text of Ṛgvidhāna and that of the YATKU. Both editions 
contained what was apparently the same text claiming the essence 
(svarūpam) of khilas, upaniṣads and mantrasaṃhitās. Both pointed to 
its incomplete, mutilated or abridged form that ended abruptly at a 
certain point which also both of them admitted while reading an 
appropriate end mark before the colophon as apūrṇaṃ, ityādisaṁ
kṣepaḥ.. 
10  Panshikar 1930. Nirnaya Sagar Press is believed by some traditional Indian 
scholars to have published the first printed edition of the Daśagrantha collection 
(Nana Kale, personal correspondence). However, it does not feature the title of 
Ṛgvedīyadaśagrantha and appears to contain only the Ṛksaṁhitā with additional 
texts named Ṛksaṁhitāpariśiṣṭāni. Among these additional texts one can find the text 
of Ṛgvidhāna and the YATKU, although YATKU is not listed in the attached Table 
of Contents (Ṛksaṁhitāsthapariśiṣṭa-viṣayānukramaṇikā).
11  See, RVDG: title page.
12  However the running header, positioned at the upper right margin, is written 
in the abridged form Yāmalasaṁ. [RVDG: 66] which probably parallels the left margin running header for a bigger text unit reading Ṛksaṁ.

--- Page 140 of 338 ---
cezary galewicz
124
The compendium RVDG which I chanced upon for the first time 
during my field study in the Konkan area in 2002 is, according to its 
table of contents, composed of the following group of texts which, it is 
stated, form ten granthas (Ṛgvedīyadaśagranthāḥ) or ‘books’: 
1. 
Ṛksaṁhitā with sarvānukrama and svāhākārahomanirṇaya
prayoga, etc. [1]
2. 
Aitareyabrāhmaṇa with occasional bhāṣya readings (bhāṣya
pāṭhais tatratatrānuvadita) [2]
3. 
Aitareyāraṇyaka (upaniṣad) with occasional bhāṣya readings 
(bhāṣyapāṭhaiḥ) [3]
4. 
Gṛhyasūtra—Āśvalāyanīya [4]
5. 
Śikṣādivedaṣaḍaṅgāni—the standard six auxiliary ‘sciences’ 
[5-10]
6. 
Śikṣādicatuṣṭaya (śīkṣā, jyotiṣa, chhanda, nighaṇṭu) [apparently 
redundant and not counted]
Somewhat surprisingly, and contrary to the table of contents, a few 
more minor texts are found among the parts of this constructed collection. For instance, after the uttaraṣaṭka of Yāska’s Nirukta and a 
subsequent pariśiṣṭa portion—which is slightly longer than that in the 
Sarup edition13—there is a short addition containing what is said to be 
the 37 nirukta-śāstra-cintā-viṣaya introduced with athāsya-nirukta
syākhilapuruṣārthopakāra-vṛtta-samarthasya saṁgrahaḥ and contain
ing 37 elements starting with nāmākhyātopasarga-nipāta-lakṣaṇaṁ 
and ending with mantrārtha-nirvacana-dvāreṇa devatābhidhāna-nir
vacana-phalaṁ devatā-tādbhāvyam. As previously stated, there is no 
mention of YATKU to be found in the enumeration of the ten books. 
With regards to the contents of the YATKU, let me briefly recapitulate what I have stated elsewhere:14 the text of YATKU in both editions is very short, covering only two out of the four faces of a folded 
leaflet. It is for the most part a hierarchical enumeration of certain 
would-be Vedic khilas, upakhilas and upaniṣads declared to be representative of the essence of the Veda. The YATKU takes on the structure of a dialogue between Pārvatī and Śiva. In this dialogue, Pārvatī 
requests Śiva to reveal the secret of the most essential part of the Veda 
13  Which follows the so called “longer recension” of Nirukta—see Sarup: 1967: 
244, fn.18.
14  Galewicz: forthcoming.

--- Page 141 of 338 ---
the lost yāmalāṣṭakatantra
125
in the form of khilas and upakhilas as well as mahopaniṣads and “other 
collections of mantras,” all of which, says Pārvatī, form the essence, or 
the most excellent part, of the Veda (vedānām sārabhūta).15 According 
to the text, Lord Śiva, filled with compassion, responds to Pārvatī’s 
request and announces the names of the khilas, upakhilas and 
upaniṣads attached to each of the four Vedas. Śiva explains that it is 
the supposed inner qualities of upaniṣadic texts that will effectively 
help to dispel the “darkness of ignorance” and lead to liberation 
(mukti). The upaniṣads listed as thirty two in number are declared to 
be the “mothers”of all the four Vedas. Some of the enumerated texts, 
however, do not suit the character of the Āśvalāyana Ṛgveda which 
the RVDG claims to belong to. A case in point is the title of the text in 
question, namely the Yāmalāṣṭakatantre Khilopaniṣad. The title 
includes the word khila which suggests the idea of an appendix to a 
canonical collection, such as the Ṛgvedakhila, or khilas to other Vedic 
saṁhitās which are, incidently, also enumerated in the text in question. Looking at the names solemnly declared by Śiva to be khilas and 
upakhilas of RV, we find the following: nine khilas are presented, 
namely, vālakhilya (a group of), rājanya, lakṣmīsūkta, gāruḍa, svās
tika, bhautika, bhauma, āyuṣya, and graham; and four upakhilas, 
namely, paiśāca, rātrikāṇḍa, traiveṇya, and svapna. While the names 
of vālakhilya and lakṣmīsūkta are those usually enumerated as RV 
khailika sūktāni, the remainder are not so easily identified and do not 
find support in the classic studies in the field, such as that of 
Scheftelowitz. Due to the lack of direct references we can only state 
here that their names probably testify to an act of purposeful accommodation within additional textual units of the RVDG. 
One possible reason for this textual intrusion into the realm of the 
RVDG may have been a need at one time for including, under the 
name of khilas and upakhilas, a certain number of minor mantric 
texts probably used in the daily practices of Citpāvan group of brahmins in the domain of healing, exorcism, counteracting possession, 
magic or divination. While this may appear unorthodox for the local 
community of Gokarṇa Āśvalāyana Ṛgvedins, who form one of the 
groups of contemporary users of the RVDG compendium, we must 
consider its close proximity to the Śiva cult of the nearby Mahābaleśvara 
temple. We should also take into account their kuladevatā in the form 
of Vedapuruṣa, and other specific elements of their daily customs, 
15  RVDG: 65.

--- Page 142 of 338 ---
cezary galewicz
126
such as a special reverence for the book of the Veda which they honour with the title of Bhagavān Veda and treat almost as a sacred object. 
It is not insignificant that the same group of Citpāvan brahmins has 
been using the RVDG collection comprising the text of the Ṛgvidhāna 
with prescriptions concerning magical use of Ṛgvedic mantras. 
Keeping this in mind, and remembering that the text of YATKU follows immediately after that of the Ṛgvidhāna in the RVDG collection, 
one could surmise that, in the undefined past, the YATKU must have 
been inserted into the compendium with a view to introducing or 
legitimising certain rites and mantra-texts concerned with practices of 
magical, atharvanic or tantric character practiced by an earlier community of Daśagranthins. Using this assumption, I have tentatively 
identified certain terms met with in this text in the spirit of magical 
and healing practices.16 Further confirmation of these assumptions 
came with the results of a manuscript hunt for the YAT.
After a rather long search I was able to prove that the elusive 
YATKU does indeed have its source in the form of a quite lengthy text 
entitled Yāmalāṣṭakatantra (YAT). Although not listed in the pertinent catalogue, the Saraswati Mahal Library possesses a set of four 
manuscripts featuring the name of Yāmalāṣṭakatantra. Furthermore, 
a copy of the YAT is found in the classified list of manuscripts drawn 
up by Burnell.17 Today the long palm leaf manuscripts which make up 
the YAT have the numbers B-9335—37 and B-122322. Three of the 
four manuscripts are grouped under successive catalogue numbers 
and appear to be copies of the same older one. All are in Telugu script 
and, unfortunately, not in the best condition. Only one of the group, 
namely MS B-9336, is relatively better preserved, although some parts 
are badly damaged. It has 119 doubly inscribed folios, mostly worm 
eaten at the ends. Fortunately, the part of the manuscript which was 
my primary interest was successfully identified as being included in 
folios 21B-23B. I will refer to it below as TanjYATKU. After a number 
of readings, it proved to contain the same text as the YATKU of 
RVDG, although with several additional lines at the end.
My search for YAT manuscripts yielded one more result18: the discovery of a transcript from another manuscript of the Yāmalāṣṭaka 
16  See, Galewicz: forthcoming.
17  See Burnell 1880: 205.
18  After the present essay has been completed a few more pieces of interesting 
evidence surfaced that, however, could not be included in this volume, among else

located in the Tirupati Oriental Research Institute. It is believed to 
have been transcribed from a manuscript also written in Telugu script 
and containing 235 paper folios. I shall refer to this text as TiruYAT. 
Both TanjYAT and TiruYAT seem to be related in at least one conspicuous characteristic: both claim to contain 110 paṭalas while actually only 107 paṭalas can be found within them, three of the total 
number of paṭalas are missing either through an error by an earlier 
copyist, an incomplete source manuscript or a purposeful omission. 
Whatever the case, this feature indicates that the two manuscripts are 
linked. Both of them contain the above mentioned YATKU which 
forms paṭala 13 of the YAT. In both TanjYAT and TiruYAT, the text 
of the upaniṣad is slightly longer than that in the printed version of the 
RVDG. Furthermore, both manuscripts show a characteristic feature 
regarding the supposed title of the YATKU in question: while the 
printed version of the compendium of the RVDG records the name of 
the upaniṣad in its colophon as khilopaniṣanmantrasaṁhitāsvarū
pam,19 both the Tanjore and Tirupati manuscripts read it as khilo
pakhilopaniṣanmantrasaṁhitā-svarūpam.20 Moreover, a short note 
on the Tirupati transcript published by K. Srinivasavaradan features, 
in the viṣayāṇāṁ sūcikā, a quite different form of the same name—
akhilopaniṣanmantrasaṁhitāsvarūpam.21 I am not quite sure how to 
evaluate this difference except for suggesting that Panshikar might 
have used a different manuscript. Neither are the motifs for shortening the text of YATKU in Panshikar’s edition and in the RVDG clear 
enough.
There are several alternative readings in the texts of TanjYATKU 
and TiruYATKU and the printed version of the RVDG. The 
TanjYATKU and the TiruYATKU generally offer better readings. 
This is certainly the case for the list of YV upakhilas with baudhīyam 
in the TiruYATKU version, which seems more plausible than audtion of YAT in a seventeenth century list of MSS belonging to a private collection of 
Kāvīndrācārya who probably hailed from Pune region.

svarūpa-kathanaṁ nāmatrayodaśaḥ paṭalaḥ.

128
heyam in the Poona RVDG version (YAT 13.10, or RVDG 66:10).22 
On the whole, the TanjYATKU and TiruYATKU appear to represent 
better copies of the upaniṣad in question. The fact that the printed version of the RVDG stops suddenly after the passage numbered as line 
25, with the words: apūrṇaṁ, ityādisaṁkṣepaḥ seems to confirm this 
view. Fortunately, both the Tanjavur and Tirupati Mss contain additional lines (fourteen in the case of the Tirupati transcript). 
The complete paṭala 13 of the YAT, as it appears in TanjYAT and 
TiruYAT, ends with the following words: iti śrīmadyāmalāṣṭakatantre 
khilopakhilopaniṣan-mantra-saṃhitā-svarūpa-kathanaṁ-nāma 
trayodaśaḥ paṭalaḥ, which due to the additional element of upakhila23 
differs slightly from both Panshikar and RVDG edition. 
The entire Yāmalāṣṭakatantra has the look of an encyclopaedic 
work: 110 paṭalas —in view of the three missing ones this amounts to 
107 paṭalas—covering a variety of different subjects including an elaborate grantha-avatāra section, a history of creation, a classification of 
traditional systems of authoritative knowledge, and even strīsaun
daryakāraṇaṁ (paṭala 108). A clear intention to bridge the dividing 
lines between Śaiva, Vaiṣṇava and Śākta worlds comes to the fore, 
with an overall unifying structure and separate paṭalas devoted to 
important aspects of their respective traditions. All are cast in the 
form of a saṁvāda-dialogue between Śiva and Pārvatī. Different 
paṭalas seem to oscillate between the poles of overtly non-orthodox 
matter (avaidikatantrāṇi of paṭala 24 or paṭala 105 on the home-lizard) and those which claim the brahmanic orthodox perspective 
(vedasvarūpam of paṭala 10). Perhaps the chapter which best expresses 
the idea of marrying opposite perspectives is paṭala 23, entitled 
vaidikatantrasvarūpam. The overall strategy of bridging the divisions 
between Śaiva and Vaiṣṇava traditions, with a bias towards a hierarchy in favour of the Śaiva, can also be seen in the opening maṅgala 
verses of the whole collection [YAT: 1.1-2]:
śuklāṁbaradharaṁ viṣṇuṁ śaśivarṇaṁ caturbhujam |
prasannavadanaṁ dhyāyet sarvavighnopaśāntaye ||
22  The numbering of lines comes from the RVDG printed edition; the manuscripts do not number their lines.
23  The formula—svarūpakathanam nāma— ending the paṭala name which recurs 
in a number of paṭalas: thus the end of paṭala 1 reads: iti yāmalāṣṭakatantre mahāsa
dāśivadhyānakathanam nāma prathamaḥ paṭalaḥ.

For the removal of all the obstacles let one meditate on Lord Viṣṇu 
(who is) adorned with white robes, moon-colored, four-armed and has 
a benevolent face. 
śivaṁ śivāṁ gaṇapatiṁ śrīpatiṁ vāsareśvaram |
praṇamya jñānasiddhyarthaṁ gūḍhārthajñaṁ guruṁ bhaje ||24
Having bowed to Śiva and His Female form, to the Lord of Gaṇas, to 
the Lord of prosperity and to the Lord of the Dawn, I adore the guru, 
who knows the hidden meanings, for the perfection of knowledge. 
Within the architecture of the YAT, a glance at the immediate neighbourhood of paṭala 13 gives the following result: the preceding paṭalas 
9-12 deal with the classification of authoritative branches of learning 
or sciences; something also evidenced by the names of the respective 
paṭalas. Thus, paṭala 9 concerns the nature of a six-fold division 
scheme of the śāstras (ṣaḍvidhaśāstranirūpaṇaṁ). Paṭala 10 reveals 
the nature of the inner division of the Vedas (vedasvarūpaṁ), paṭala 
11 concerns the subdivision of the upavedas, and paṭala 12, which 
immediately precedes YATKU, concerns the nature of the aṅgavedas, 
which are summarised at the very end of the paṭala while introducing 
the subject of paṭala 13: evaṁ vedopavedāṅgavedāḥ sopaniṣadaṅgāḥ / 
khilopakhilam antāyāḥ kīrtyante sarvasādhanāḥ. Paṭala 14, which follows after the YATKU of paṭala 13, appears to deal with the nature of 
the relationship between the six darśana systems. It contains a reference to paṭala 13 inserted at the very beginning and taking the form of 
a recurring formula by which Pārvatī addresses Śiva the Mṛtyuñjaya, 
or the Conqueror of Death. The formula refers back to the topic of 
Śiva’s teaching contained in paṭala 13 in answer to an earlier request 
by Pārvatī. In the pertinent passage, a paraphrased name of paṭala 13 
is given by Pārvatī in the following words: vedasvarūpam akhilaṁ 
śrutaṁ tvadvadanān mayā.25 This wording may have been the origin 
of the name of paṭala 13 given in Śrinivasavaradan’s reconstructed 
index viṣayāṇāṁ sūcikā as akhilopaniṣan-mantrasaṁhitāsvarūpam.
Whatever the intended meaning of the paṭalas and their topics having such a concatenated structure, it stands to reason that paṭala 13 
only makes sense within the broader context of neighbouring chapters 
24  In different traditions of Śaivism the person of one’s guru may be, and often is, 
represented as merging with that of Śiva though actual concepts of this consubstantiality may look different. 
25  TiruYAT: 71.

130
whose ambition is to classify the received and established traditional 
knowledge. The only peculiar thing, however, appears to be the reversal of the relation between the mūla text, seemingly the source of all 
knowledge in the form of the Veda, and its subsequent “emanations.” 
Here, the elements of the compound khilopakhilopaniṣan-mantrasaṁhitā—the last sequence—appear to be infused with the highest 
authority, embodying the very essence of its precedents. The scheme 
to which such a pattern probably aspires to make a connection to, is 
that of the Vedānta and its idea of “lower” (apara) and “higher” (para) 
knowledge contained in the upaniṣadic mahāvākyas said to crown the 
Veda. Here, however, what we have is a particular Vedic tradition, 
namely Āśvalāyana Ṛgveda, and yet no text like YATKU is known to 
the Ṛgvedic tradition as such. Within the context of the whole of YAT 
the hints towards the classification of knowledge voiced at the end of 
paṭala 13 (YATKU) refer back to paṭala 9—ṣaḍvidhaśāstranirūpaṇa—
which specifically deals with the topic of the ordered sequence and 
hierarchy of all knowledge. This topic seems to be developed specifically in paṭalas 9 through 13, though much earlier, at the start of 
paṭala 1, it is suggested that the context of stratifying and ordering 
knowledge is to be one of the main topics of the YAT as a whole.
At this preliminary stage of the evaluation of the results, some paratextual characteristics of the YAT manuscripts should be mentioned 
in passing. Thus, paṭala 3 opens with a recurring formula of introduction in which Pārvatī is praising Śiva Parameśvara and his wisdom 
before asking him to yield to her curiosity and answer another question by revealing his divine knowledge. One of the names with which 
Pārvatī addresses lord Śiva is virūpākṣa (“Lord with extraordinary 
eyes”). Mentioning this name may well be intentional, and it may hint 
at a local cult of Śiva as Virūpākṣa which could be useful in searching 
for a possible religious and intellectual identity of the YAT. A rather 
well known example of the Virūpākṣa cult relates to the connection of 
political power and religious authority that is offered to the royal deity 
of the early Vijayanagara rulers. The cult of Virūpākṣa in Hampi goes 
back to an older, pre-Vijayanagara cult. An important reference to 
Virūpākṣa is indicated in the architecture of another region not far 
away from that where the contemporary users of RVDG and YATKU 
actually live: An 8th century temple of Virūpākṣa (dated between ca. 
733-746 ad and the rule of Vātāpi Cālukya Vikramāditya II;26 its rem26  The Western Cālukyas ruled from 543–757 ad and then from 975–1189 ad 
See, Rowland 1954 (contains an architectural plan of the Virūpākṣa temple). Cf. also

--- Page 147 of 338 ---
the lost yāmalāṣṭakatantra
131
nants can still be seen in Pattadakal) said to be founded by queen 
Lokamahādevī to commemorate the victories of her husband over the 
Pallavas of Kāñci. A few centuries later, no later than the 12th century, 
a deity of that name became the focus of a local cult in Hampi by the 
Tungabhadra River.27 A deity named Virūpākṣa was then taken over 
and accommodated as a royal tutelary deity and it became a royal 
mark of the imperial power of Vijayanagara, whose dynasties started 
using the Virūpākṣa sign manual for authorising their royal inscriptions from the mid 14th century onwards, and continued to do so for 
almost two hundred years, until the 16th century. The fact that the 
name of Virūpākṣa is mentioned in the opening verses of a few books 
of YAT may, of course, be without great significance but it may also 
suggest a purposeful act of making a connection with a temple tradition of some acknowledged authority. 
With reference to what is known of the history of Tantric literature, 
I admit to have failed to plausibly locate the YAT on the map of textual traditions usually accepted for classifying Tantric works. No classification on offer seems to acknowledge a separate Tantra text of that 
name. The recurring principle for classification is that of eight different works making a group of the so called Yāmala Tantras. The eight 
components of such a group differ from one source to another, and no 
one seems to know a distinct work named Yāmalāṣṭakatantra. 
According to one of the traditional enumerations,28 Yāmala is the 
name of an eight-member group of Tantras among 64 Bhairava 
āgamas of the śākta tradition, all classed under the category of aṣṭaka 
(“the eight”). The second aṣṭaka in the sequence of eight is called 
yāmalāṣṭaka and is said to comprise Brahmā, Viṣṇu, Rudra, Atharvan, 
Ruru, Vetāla, Indra and Svacchanda āgamas. A similar list is said to be 
given29 in Śrīkaṇṭhīsaṁhitā30 which, however, appears to state only 
seven out of eight elements, deeming the eighth one to be lost. In this 
enumeration the Yāmalāṣṭaka also appears as the second one in the 
sequence. No mention, however, seems to be made anywhere of an 
independent work like the Yāmalāṣṭakatantra. Nor of an independent 
text like the YATKU that might belong to it. Perhaps what we have 
here is indeed an instance of “an artificial locus of attribution.” Yet, if

this is the case, what reason would the editors of a compendium like 
the RVDG have for referring to a tradition of Yāmalas that appear to 
have little authority among Tantra followers, not to mention in a general Brahmanic Hindu context. The status of the so called Yāmala 
priests appears to have been not very high and Yāmala scriptures are 
said to be used by non-brahmin temple priests of South India, such as 
those known by the name of uvaccan or pāraśava.31 Perhaps this 
might have something to do with a peculiar status of the above mentioned Citpāvan Brahmins, among whom the collection of RVDG 
remains, at least, in theoretical use. 
It is too early at this stage to risk any general assessment on the 
nature and relation of this encyclopedic-like compendium to any of 
the schools within the context of original or secondary Tantric literature. Certain formulations within the YAT suggest the perspective of 
the Southern (Vaidika) Saiddhāntika current: thus, the title of paṭala 
23 reads: vaidikatantrasvarūpam, and the name of paṭala 24 is given 
as yoginītyādi-dvāttriṁśad-avaidikatantrāṇi. 
A point of both practical and, more generally, methodological 
interest presents itself with the possibility of complementing philological textual study with manuscript diffusion assessment and field 
study concerning the living tradition of learned communities among 
whose members the text has been subject of transmission and/or use. 
Given that the only extant manuscripts to have been preserved are in 
Telugu script, a cautious suggestion might be made with regards to 
the power-culture relations and literary customs of post Vijayanagara 
Nāyaka kingdoms, like that of Tanjavur, and their emulating of the 
type of imperial cultural space in which Telugu remained one of the 
privileged literary media. This would, to some extent, explain the 
apparently wide circulation of the YAT text and it reaching the 
Western Maharashtra from the cultural centres of previous Vijayana
gara kingdoms, perhaps during the Maratha rule under which such a 
circulation was at least more probable. This however remains conjecture and must wait for a thorough study in the future.
Returning to the question of a textual community who use the 
RVDG containing the upaniṣad of YATKU, my enquiry has delivered 
partial results, at least for the time being, and will need to be supplemented by additional field study. The compendium of the RVDG 
appears to be in use among members of Brāhmaṇa groups of Konkan 
31  See Sanderson 1986.

--- Page 149 of 338 ---
the lost yāmalāṣṭakatantra
133
and West Maharashtra regions, mostly those known by the names of 
Konkanstha (Citpāvan) Brahmins. According to a series of interviews 
carried out in 2003 and 2005-6, a Daśagranthin is usually said to be a 
Ṛgvedin brāhmaṇa who undertook (among other vows, including a 
strict dietary regime, certain elements of bodily discipline, etc.) a vow 
of reciting and studying Ṛgvedadaśagrantha. That is to say, the ten 
books contained in the compendium with this name.32 Some of the 
Daśagranthis of the Western Maharashtra (Pune, Alandi, Ratnagiri) 
and Konkan area (Gokarna), admit that the YATKU used to be recited 
in a relatively recent but unspecified past in a simple but ceremonial 
pārāyaṇa consisting of a simple single reading throughout of its text. 
Some seem to preserve a vague memory of a ritual connected with the 
use of the YATKU but their memory does not offer anything more 
specific. Members of other brāhmaṇa groups of Maharashtra tend to 
dismiss any real connection of the YATKU to the Ṛksaṁhitā or any 
other text of the true Ṛgvedic tradition. Interestingly, although members of the group say that the YATKU has been rightly included in the 
compendium of the RVDG, they cannot offer any explanations for 
why this is the case. In fact, many appear bewildered when asked to 
explain. Some of the contemporary brahmin informants are of the 
opinion that among some Vaidikas of Maharashtra there remains a 
memory of a custom according to which both the Ṛgvidhāna and the 
YATKU used to be contained in a sort of ceremonial reading (veda
pārāyaṇa) by being “recited just once.”33 The same informants add 
that nowadays personal Vedic recitation in the form of a vedapārā
yaṇa, by those Vaidikas in Maharashtra who keep the RVDG, is 
restricted to recitation of the saṁhitā only, while both the YATKU 
and Ṛgvidhāna are left aside, as are the other nine of the ten books of 
the RVDG. If these claims are substantive and represent more than a 
personal view, they might lead to the conclusion that, nowadays, the 
YATKU may have fallen out of use while, according to the same 
informants, certain formulas of Ṛgvidhāna are still practiced.
One more minor aspect with respect to the whole collection of the 
RVDG deserves attention: the RVDG repeatedly commences almost

every constituent text of its “ten books” with an homage to Veda
puruṣa—a deity representing the embodiment of the “holy scripture” 
of the Veda. Images of this deity happen to decorate walls of a house 
belonging to an important family among the contemporary users of 
the RVDG—the Joglekar’s Gurukul house in Gokarna. Members of 
their household proudly point out the images when they are asked 
about a deity presiding over family. Incidentally, the same deity is 
mentioned in Sāyaṇa’s introduction to the commentary on RS 1.1.1 as 
the one who actually uttered the first Ṛgvedic sūkta and who presides 
over the first stanza which begins the first hymn of the Ṛksaṁhitā.34 
This coincidence may bear some relation to the imperial legend of 
Vijayanagara noted above—the name of Virūpākṣa, the royal deity of 
the early Vijayanagara rulers mentioned in the preface to paṭala 3 of 
the YAT.
In conclusion, the manuscript finds concerning the YAT have proved 
the existence of the source text for the YATKU which is included in a 
traditionally recognised Ṛgvedic compendium called the RVDG. 
Perhaps because of a need to legitimise some non-Vedic practices by 
the Ṛgvedic Brahmins, of a specific group and locality, a text of a seemingly Tantric origin had, at one point in time, been attached to the 
authoritative collection of the RVDG. This seems to suggest that the 
Ṛgvedic Brāhmaṇa priest must have sought authorisation for these 
Tantric rites. It might be that certain (khila) texts were in use among 
Ṛgvidhāna priests while competing with their Atharvaṇic counterparts. Including these texts within the collection of RVDG may have 
been thought of as a way of endowing the Ṛgvidhāna priests with 
additional authority. This, however, remains a hypothesis and does 
not explain the historical circumstances in which a Brāhmaṇa group 
from Western Maharashtra could come into contact with the text and 
ideas of the YAT. To the best of my knowledge, there is no evidence to 
a continuation of a presumably original (if it ever was one) tradition 
of reading, interpreting or ritual use of the whole or a part of the YAT 
corpus in the broad geographical region where, historically, Telugu 
script has been used. The fact is, a part of the YAT, in the form of the 
YATKU, is to be found within a traditionally recognised35 corpus of 
34  Müller I: 22.
35  The interviews with the members of pertinent Brahmanic communities (Western Maharashtra Konkanastha and Deśastha Brahmins from which Daśagranthins 
derive) show that most of the Daśagranthins are aware of the fact that the canonical

--- Page 151 of 338 ---
the lost yāmalāṣṭakatantra
135
the “Ten Books of the Ṛgveda” (RVDG) and is circulating among the 
members of a community describing themselves as Daśagranthin. 
Scriptural canons and their guardian communities are characterised 
by “fixed limits” that happen to be transcended, and “textual bodies” 
that happen to be extended, for reasons that tend to be forgotten while 
new shapes of canons become recognised.
The nature of the relationship between the source text of the YAT 
and the compilation of the RVDG is still to be explored. Given the 
fragmentary knowledge of the history of both textual corpora, further 
study is needed combining methods pertinent to book history, discourse analysis and the study of intellectual practices of the learned 
community within which the whole or a part of the two remained in 
use. It has to touch upon the social and intellectual practices of the 
learned communities of the users of the texts and their competing 
claims towards their own cultural identities and professional competence vis-à-vis the issues of power and authority.

YATKU, the last occurrence of which may have had place, in his opinion, “some 
thirty years ago” (personal communication, 2006).

CHAPTER SEVEN
From palmleaves to a multimedia databank: 
a note on the ‘Bhāsa-project’
Heike Moser
 The ‘Bhāsa Research Group’
The ‘Bhāsa-Project’ is investigating the thirteen so-called ‘TrivandrumPlays’ ascribed to Bhāsa, namely Svapnavāsavadatta, Pratijñāyaugan
dharāyaṇa, Avimāraka, Cārudatta, Pratimānāṭaka, Abhiṣēkanāṭaka, 
Pañcarātra, Madhyamavyāyōga, Dūtavākya, Dūtaghaṭōtkaca, Karṇa
bhāra, Ūrubhaṅga and Bālacarita.1 It was initiated in the early 1990’s, 
when Heidrun Brückner, then professor of Indology at Tübingen 
University, became interested in the subject: being an admirer of the 
Mahābhārata plays ascribed to Bhāsa, she was able to witness a 
Kūṭiyāṭṭam-performance in 1993. Kūṭiyāṭṭam, recently awarded the 
title of “Masterpiece of the Oral and Intangible Heritage of Humanity” 
by the UNESCO, is the sole surviving Sanskrit theatre tradition on the 
Indian subcontinent. It is solely preserved by very few performers in 
Kerala. Some parts of the plays ascribed to Bhāsa are still in the repertoire of this theatre. With the help of a grant by the Deutsche For
schungsgemeinschaft (DFG; German Research Foundation), Brückner 
got the opportunity to see and document more Kūṭiyāṭṭam-per
formances, mainly those of the Mārgi-troupe in Thiruvananthapuram.2
She was also able to examine some of the palmleaf- manuscripts 
containing the text of the ‘Bhāsa-Plays’ kept at the Oriental Research 
Institute and Manuscripts Library (ORIML) of the University of 
Kerala at Thiruvananthapuram and at the Sanskrit College Manu
scripts Library at Tripunithura. 
1  In this article throughout short and long e and o are marked in transliteration, 
because in Dravidian languages this distinction is made. Dealing with texts in Sanskrit, Prakrit and Malayalam it is helpful for the reader to do so. See also Esposito in 
this volume.
2  The members of the project wish to thank late Appukuttan Nair, Ram Iyer, 
Ammannur Kocchukkuttan Chakyar and Margi Madhu for their immense help and 
cooperation.

With the continued support by the DFG a campaign to locate and 
photograph handwritings on palmleaf and to document Kūṭiyāṭṭam 
performances was conducted in the years thereafter.3 This was made 
possible thanks to the close cooperation with Indian colleagues and 
scholars like N.P. Unni, K.G. Paulose, C. Rajendran, L.S. Rajagopalan, 
and K. Vasudevan Namputiripad. N.P. Unni’s important publication 
“New Problems in Bhāsa Plays” (Unni 1978) guided us to the main 
collections.4 In addition, we went through the published manuscriptcatalogues like the “New Catalogus Catalogorum” (Raghavan 1968). 
We also had access to unpublished lists by K. Vasudevan Namputiripad 
and Ashok Aklujkar providing us with unique information about several private collections.5 Unni’s records were complemented by our 
own new findings (see Figure 7.1). 
We collected and reproduced all the manuscripts by way of leaf- 
by-leaf photography from the Oriental Research Institute and 
Manuscripts Library of the University of Kerala at Kariyavattom/ 
Thiruvananthapuram, the Sanskrit College Manuscripts Library at 
Tripunithura, the Manuscript Library of the Department of Malayalam 
of Calicut University (see Figure 7.1), the Adyar Library and Re- 
search Institute as well as the Government Oriental Manuscripts 
Library at Chennai, the DAV College at Chandigarh,6 the Punjab Uni
versity Library at Lahore (Pakistan), the School of Oriental and 
African Studies at London (U.K.), the Bayerische Staatsbibliothek

When H. Brückner was appointed to the chair of Indology at Würz
burg University in 2001, the ‘Bhāsa-Project’ was shifted from Tübingen 
to Würzburg. Because of this shift and in order to describe the contents of the project more accurately, it was renamed in 2003 as “The 
Würzburg Multimedia Databank on Sanskrit Drama and Theatre”. Its 
collections today consist not only of some 300 hours of video recordings and about 2000 slides and photographs displaying Kūṭiyāṭṭam 
performances, but also includes several thousands of photographs of 
79 manuscript bundles containing the ‘Trivandrum-plays’ and other 
dramas. During the last few years, all the manuscript photographs and 
8  For full bibliographical references, see Appendix II.

143
many of the Kūṭiyāṭṭam pictures and videos have been digitised with 
the help of another DFG-grant (2004-2007). Since the late 1990’s, a 
databank has been built up and is now freely accessible to a larger 
public on the Internet at <http://www.indologie.uni-wuerzburg.de/
bhasa/rahmen.html>. At present, the databank contains the texts of 
all thirteen plays ascribed to Bhāsa as well as the dramas Abhijñāna
śākuntala of Kālidāsa and Mudrārākṣasa of Viśākhadatta. It further 
provides comprehensive indices of all Sanskrit and Prakrit words. 
Exemplary scans of manuscripts are linked to some of the texts. The 
databank has been conceptualised by Matthias Ahlborn based on 
Figure 7.3.  Screenshot of <http://www.indologie.uniwuerzburg.de/bhasa/rahmen.

databank: compound words are separated, vowel sandhis dissolved; when clicking on 
“sūtradhāra” the corresponding entry in the Sanskrit-index is visible in the right column, referring to the use of this word in the other plays with the help of interactive 
links. In the same way the Prakrit-index will appear when clicking on one of the 
Prakrit-words. The links marked blue, for instance, under 0.1,[22848.61.a.4] etc., refer 
line-by-line to the digitised palmleaf-manuscripts. Here the beginning of manuscript

Extensible Markup Language (XML) and Unicode.9 By using frames it 
is possible to see text, index, and manuscript in a single window (see 
Figure 7.3). Furthermore, visual material was added and linked: to 
explain the basic performance techniques of Kūṭiyāṭṭam, a description 
with pictures and a subtitled videoclip was prepared by Heike Moser.10 
Other clips visualise specific portions of the text in a Kūṭiyāṭṭamperformance.11 Detailed information on using all the features of the 
databank is given in Brückner 2007.12
What is the Outcome of the ‘Bhāsa-Project’? 
The debate concerning the authorship of the so-called ‘Trivandrumplays’ is going on for nearly seven decades. Even this work of several 
scholars, spanning more than 10 years and using old and new technologies, did not suffice to solve the problem: neither Bhāsa nor any 
other author is mentioned in any of the manuscripts.13 More research 
is required comparing lexicon, style, syntax, Prakrit etc. The databank 
provides a starting point for such studies. It is a new tool and a reliable 
textual basis that can gradually be further extended. Utilizing its 
material, new critical editions and translations with annotations 
of Cārudatta, Dūtavākya, Karṇabhāra, Madhyamavyāyōga and Pra
tijñāyaugandharāyaṇa have already been prepared and published (see 
Appendix II). The database contains an almost complete digitised collection of all manuscripts of the plays ascribed to Bhāsa available. 
Besides, for the first time it tries to connect dramatic texts of Kerala 
with their performing tradition, Kūṭiyāṭṭam, by using the potentials of 
modern techniques, such as videoclips. We plan to publish detailed 
lists of our collections (manuscripts, videorecordings) as well as an 
annotated bibliography concerning the ‘Bhāsa-problem’. Finally, the 
work with hundreds of pages of handwritings made us experts in 
reading Sanskrit and Prakrit in Malayalam script. We learned plenty

about different mistakes typical of Sanskrit and Prakrit texts written

How were the ‘Bhāsa-plays’ performed? Which plays are still enacted?

While searching for manuscripts containing the so-called ‘Bhāsaplays’ we obtained information about a couple of manuscripts not

containing Pratimānāṭaka, Abhiṣēkanāṭaka, Svapnavāsavadatta and 
Pratijñāyaugandharāyaṇa was identified at the School of Oriental and
African Studies (SOAS), University of London.17 With the kind help 
of Dr. C. Rajendran of Calicut University we obtained the opportunity

We learned how the manuscripts were traditionally kept, how they are 
preserved today in private households (covered in cloth and stored 
either in a wooden box, a cupboard or still in the kitchen over the fireplace) and in public manuscript libraries (stored in open shelves with 
air-conditioners, which unfortunately in some places are often out of 
order). We observed old people and manuscript assistants being aware 
18  For details see Brückner 2000: 510-511.
19  See Appendix I “Private collections of palmleaf-manuscripts owned by 
Kūṭiyāṭṭam performing families”. 
20  In the course of my research I collected photographs and copies of handwritings on palmleaf and on paper dealing with the female solo sequence in Kūṭiyāṭṭam 
called Naṅṅyār Kūttu (see Moser 2008:119ff). These texts contain the verses and 
stage manuals (āṭṭaprakāram, kramadīpika, āṭṭakramam) for the flashback of the Cēṭī 
at the beginning of the second act of the play Subhadrādhanañjaya (“Śrīkṛṣṇaca
ritam”). At the same time I was able to make lists of other important texts preserved 
by these families.

--- Page 165 of 338 ---
palmleaves to databank
149
of the treasure they are guarding by handling the palmleaves with 
care, putting them at least once a year out in the sunlight to avoid fungus etc. And we came to know about librarians unaware of the value 
of the brittle handwritten materials for which they are responsible. 
We learned that the transmission of texts has not changed that much: 
in the tradition of guru-śiṣya-paramparā, students are still either transcribing texts from their teacher’s notebooks or write from his / her 
dictation. The major change is that palmleaf as writing material has 
been replaced by paper.21 The next step was to publish and print not 
only the text of the plays, but also their stage manuals (e.g. Nārāyaṇan 
Nampyār 1980 and 1984, Venu 1989 or Vēnugōpālan 2009). Nowadays 
the performers themselves started to computerise these texts for their 
everyday use. Researches like our “Bhāsa Group” edit and process 
texts in databanks and distribute them online.
All the techniques mentioned here share common problems: 
Different kinds of “worms” attack not only palmleaves and paper but 
also computer software. Scribes can make mistakes—regardless the 
technology they are using. In the depths of the Internet even large 
databases can get lost easily—still one has to know the “address” 
where the texts of interest are housed. And finally not only leaves and 
paper have fixed spans of life, the ones of computer hardware and 
software get shorter and shorter. So the work of transmission has to go 
on carefully: also a databank on the Internet has to be maintained and 
updated and has to be made “visible”.
 
To close this short note on the ‘Bhāsa-Project’ I wish to cite the colophon of the Abhiṣēkanāṭaka manuscript no. 20469 from ORIML, 
Thiruvananthapuram. This closing passage is a vivid example of how a 
palm-leaf manuscript was produced in strong association with the 
performing tradition of Kūṭiyāṭṭam in the year 1666 AD:22
21  Even at the beginning of the 20th century palmleaves were still in common use. 
For example, in my possession are photographies of a palmleaf-handwriting dating 
only to 1911.
22  Most scribal colophons in Indian manuscripts give the (more or less precise) 
date, the occasion and the place of writing of the manuscript. Still this colophon is a 
very unusual finding in the manuscripts we are dealing with here: Many of them 
have no colophon at all; in our work we did not come across any manuscript with 
such detailed specifications about place and time. And by mentioning the theatrehall kūttampalam we get an unique example of the strong association of these plays 
with the performing tradition of Kūṭiyāṭṭam in the 17th century: The scribe has com-

[…] kollaṃ 841 ta karkkaṭakañāyiṟa patineṭṭā budhanāḻiccayuṃ apara

tiruvārppil kūttampalattiṃkalinna eḻati tīrnna cūḷāmaṇināṭakam | 
samāptam | rāmalikhitam | śivam astu |
[…] [In the year] kollam 841 (1666 AD) in the month of karkkiṭakam 
(july / august) on the 18th [day], a wednesday and dvādaśi-day (12th 
day of the lunar month) in aparapakṣam (decreasing moon) with the 
star makayiram (mārgaśirṣam), on this day at noon time during pūjā

this writing [of] the play Cūḍāmaṇi(sic?)23 has been completed | It has 
been written by Rāma | (may it be auspicious) | 
Appendix I  Private Collections of Palmleaf-Manuscripts 
Owned by Kūṭiyāṭṭam Performing Families

23  Here the writer most probably made a (final) mistake: this is the end of 
Abhiṣēkanāṭaka and not of ‘Cūḍāmaṇi’ (Āścaryacūḍāmaṇi). The manuscript-bundle 
contains today no other texts, but we have one bundle in our collection containing 
both plays. So there is at least the possibility that the colophon could well have been 
copied from an antigraph containing both the Abhiṣēkanāṭaka and the Cūḍāmaṇi, 
but in which the scribal colophon was supplied only at the end of the Cūḍāmaṇi. The 
scribe may have copied only the Abhiṣēkanāṭaka—for which the colophon was missing—and then provided the manuscript with the colophon of the Cūḍāmaṇi after it. 
But at the same time it can just be a sign of carelessness after completing a long 
work.

CHAPTER EIGHT
The sanskrit manuscripts in tamilnadu*
P. Perumal
Introduction
India is rich in literatures in various languages and representing various cultures.1 Since the Vedic period the pattern of learning has followed a guru-śiṣya tradition and the process has been continued 
through oral transmission until the Buddhist period. There is no evidence of writing from the Vedic era. Aśokan rock edicts2 are considered to be the first preserved proof of script and writing. Over the 
course of time, many old documents belonging to different centuries 
have been found in different parts of India. These are in the form of 
stone inscriptions, copper plates and manuscripts (mss) of birch-bark, 
palm-leaf and paper. The language is Sanskrit and other Indian languages, written in various scripts, several of which are now obsolete. 
This paper focuses on the production, distribution and collection of 
South Indian sanskrit palm-leaf mss with special reference to the collection of the Sarasvati Mahal Library, Thanjavur (Tanjore, Tamil
nadu) patronised and expanded by Maharaja Serfoji (1777-1832).
Ancient kings of South India were patrons of the art and literature 
of Sanskrit and other (regional) languages. Jain culture spread rapidly 
from the 2nd century ad onwards and literatures in Sanskrit and 
other languages flourished. Hindu religious centres (mutts) were 
formed to undo or to prohibit the conversion to Jainism and to secure 
the development of Hinduism. To establish their religious and spiritual strength, the Śaiva and Jain saints and scholars entered into a 
debate by putting the mss in water and fire to show their authenticity. 
*  With special reference to the Sarasvati Mahal Library.
1  For recent studies of South Asia’s “literary cultures in history” see Pollock (ed.) 
2003 and Pollock 2006. 
2  The writing of seven major and thirteen minor rock edicts reveal the sociocultural life of the period of Aśoka. They are mostly written in brāhmī script and the 
language is in various Prakrits, one in Greek and Aramaic.

--- Page 174 of 338 ---
p. perumal
158
With these rivalities and scholars moving from place to place, the 
destruction of large numbers of manuscripts was inevitable. In north 
India several incidences of foreign invasion caused the destruction of 
mss and cultural artefacts. South India had the good fortune that great 
indigenous kingdoms such as those of the Chola, Pandya, Chera and 
Pallavas had existed since ancient times. Even after these, other indigenous kingdoms rose and flourished. The Vijayanagara Empire rose to 
power after the Hoysala dynasty and welded practically the whole of 
south India into one great empire. All these dynasties patronised and 
strongly supported religious literature and philosophy, allowing 
scholars and philosophers, such as Śrī Śaṅkarācārya, Śrī Rāmānu
jācārya, Śrī Madhvācārya and other brilliant thinkers, to prosper in 
their respective fields.
Tamil Nadu, situated in the south-east of India, has a rich literary 
tradition. Since several centuries bc, Tamil and Sanskrit have prevailed as the ancient languages of South India. While Tamil was used 
as the common language for regular communication and the formation of regional literature, brahmins used the Sanskrit language for 
teaching and learning the Veda and other religious texts. Over the 
course of time many Sanskrit mss written in scripts such as Grantha, 
Nandināgarī, Telugu, Malayālam, Vaṭṭeluttu etc. were collected from 
the areas close to the banks of the rivers Kaveri, Vaigai and Tamraparni, 
indicating the settlement of Sanskrit intellectuals in these areas. 
In past times adhyayana (study), adhyāpana (teaching) and lekhana 
(writing)3 were considered as honourable professions, especially for 
Brahmins. Large numbers of mss were written, copied, multiplied and 
preserved mostly in the religious centres (mutts) and temples, and 
were kept in the safe custody of monks, priests and scholars. 
Writing Materials
The materials used in South India for writing were mostly stone, metal 
sheets, wooden-boards, cloths, palm-leaves and paper. Before the 
advent of paper, palm-leaves were predominantly used. They were 
abundantly produced in the coastal belt and north-eastern part of 
India, where they were easily available and cheap. India’s hot and 
humid, tropical climate has an adverse effect on palm-leaves. However, 
3  lekhana means writing in general and in this context, it is copying down of mss.

--- Page 175 of 338 ---
sanskrit manuscripts in tamilnadu
159
if well maintained and carefully preserved, palm-leaves can be highly 
durable and have a reasonably long life. Under ‘normal’ conditions, 
palm-leaf manuscripts can be preserved for ca. 400 to 500 years. With 
controlled heat and humidity, we can extend this life-span to more 
than 1000 years.
Most of the available palm-leaf mss were written on one of the following three varieties of leaf4:
1.  Corypha umbraculifera 
2.  Borassus flabellifer 
3.  Corypha utan
The palm tree Corypha umbraculifera is known as śrītāla or tālipot5 
and it grows in comparatively dry climates like those of Gujarat, Sindh 
and Rajasthan. The leaves are broad and lengthy, with a thin and 
smooth surface and they tend to be quite fragile by nature. The second 
variety, Borassus flabellifer, is known as Palmyra. They are thick and 
lengthy but the leaves never grow to more than two feet. The size and 
thickness varies according to the fertility and water sources of the 
area. The third variety, Corypha utan, is more commonly known as 
Lontar. It grows in humid coastal areas, particularly on the west coast 
of India, in Burma and Thailand. The leaves have a combination of the 
characteristics of the tālipot and Palmyra palm leaves. The Lontar 
leaves are lengthy, broad and thick. The majority of Burmese mss are 
written on these leaves. 
(A)  Processing Palm-Leaf
Usually five- to six month-old leaves were collected for writing. They 
were separated, the edge ribs removed and then left in the shade to 
dry. After drying, the edges of the leaves were cut into convenient 
length for writing and the surfaces were burnished. Two small holes 
were made for passing through a thread to tie the mss. The holes were 
made in such a way that they divided the leaves more or less into three 
equal parts. In order to make them suitable for writing, the leaves were 
seasoned or softened. Although the process of seasoning varies from 
place to place, I describe a common method below:
4  See Shivaganesha Murthy 1996: 27f.
5  It is called as Tālapana, Coondapana or Talippanai in Tamil.

--- Page 176 of 338 ---
p. perumal
160
i. First, fresh leaves were dried in the shade.
ii. Then they were boiled in water or milk.
iii. After boiling they were buried in wet sand in order to give them 
softness.
iv. After being in a sand ‘bath’ for approximately three months, they 
obtained considerable softness.
v. After having been dried once again in the shade, the leaves were 
frequently applied with gingelly oil in order to improve smoothness. 
vi. In South India the leaves were kept alongside with wet rice straw 
for seasoning.
vii. The seasoning process produces a soft surface for writing and 
extracts the lignin. After seasoning, the soft surface was burnished 
using conch shells or polished stone to further polish and smooth 
the leaves.
(B)  Writing on Palm-Leaf
Writing on palm-leaves was done in two ways: one was to incise the 
leaves with a stylus—ink is applied afterwards; and the other way was 
to write with ink on leaves using a bamboo pen or brush. The stylus, or 
śalākā, had a pointed tip and was made from iron, steel, brass, bone or 
ivory. Some of the metal styluses had a pointed tip at one end and a 
sharp knife on the other. To write on the seasoned leaves according to 
this method, a support would be held in the left hand and the stylus is 
held in the right hand. While writing, the left-hand thumb would support the pointed tip of stylus allowing for movement. This type of 
writing is possible wherever the script is in circular form. Mostly south 
Indian scripts, up to the level of Orissa, were written using this engraving technique. Writing devanāgarī or the scripts with a headline would 
spoil and tear the leaves. The incised leaves were treated in two ways in 
order to give clarity to the letters: i. the letters were anointed with turmeric paste; or, ii. a mixture of vegetable or plant juice (Cocina indica) 
and lamp soot (prepared using castor oil) was applied over the letters. 
Nowadays lamp soot mixed with some volatile vegetable oils like citronella oil is used for this purpose. After writing, the leaves were 
arranged and strung together between two wooden planks, usually of 
soft wood like mango or bamboo. Sometimes, teak wood was also 
used to prevent insect damage. One end of the thread used for stringing the leaves was tied with the rib of a palm leaf in the shape of a par-

--- Page 177 of 338 ---
sanskrit manuscripts in tamilnadu
161
rot beak. If the ms has more than ca. 200 leaves, the thread cannot 
hold the leaves, or it will cause damage to the holes in the leaves. In 
this case a small copper rod or a bamboo strip was pierced into the 
other hole of the ms. Once completed the mss were kept in a special 
place, for instance near an offering table or image of God.6
(C)  Preservation of Palm-Leaf Mss
In ancient days, as a part of the preservation process, the palm-leaf 
mss were covered with red or yellow cloth and kept in wooden boxes, 
which could not easily be attacked by worms and insects. In some 
regions the mss boxes were kept in a dark chamber above the fire place 
(hearth) in order to avoid dampness or fungus. It is believed that red 
cloth and wooden boxes keep away worms and insects. Some indigenous vegetable materials, namely, dried leaves of the Neem tree 
(Azadirachta indica), dried Neem seed powder, tobacco, camphor, 
black cumin and sweet flag, also keep insects away. Today, most of the 
ms libraries treat the leaves with natural essential oils such as citronella oil, lemongrass oil, camphor oil etc. to act as an insect repellent. In the Sarasvati Mahal Library a mixture of some specific spices7 
in powder form are wrapped in a small cloth and are kept in the storage cupboards. This keeps away the insects for about six months. 
From the 18th century onwards, handmade paper slowly replaced 
palm-leaf. Most of the south Indian paper mss are written with Indian 
ink, prepared with a mixture of lamp soot, Acacia gum and rain water. 
The pen used for writing these mss was made from wood, reed plant 
and bamboo, and vulture or goose quills. Some of the paper mss have 
illustrations reflecting the ideas or themes of that work. They are decorated with ornamental floral and geometrical designs. Some mss have 
bright letters characteristic of illuminated mss. 
The literary treasures of ancient India were preserved in temples, 
palaces of kings who were patrons of art and literature, religious centres (mutts) and houses of learned scholars, gurukulas etc. Generally 
the libraries were called Sarasvati Bhandar or Sarasvati Mahal, which 
means ‘treasure house of knowledge’ or ‘abode of the goddess of learning’. These mss transmit knowledge in the domains of history, mythol6  Mostly the mss of Rāmayaṇa, Mahābhārata and religious texts were kept near 
God for pūjā.
7  The preparation of the spices is as follows: black cumin 4 parts, sweet flag 4 
parts, bark of cinnamon 4 parts, cloves 1 part, pepper 1 part, 10 grams of camphor.

--- Page 178 of 338 ---
p. perumal
162
ogy, medicine, astrology, art and culture. Sir Robert Chambers 
(1737-1803) was the first person to disseminate the knowledge of 
Sanskrit mss in western countries. Scholars such as Colin Mackenzie, 
William Taylor, C.P. Brown, John Leyden etc. collected mss from villages in south India which are preserved in the Oriental ms libraries in 
Chennai, Mysore, Trivanduram and Hyderabad. In 1862, following a 
request by Pandit Radhakrishna Sastry, the then Governor General Sir 
Lawrence instructed his secretary to take suitable action for the preservation of mss from the villages of India. Accordingly, the Govern
ment sanctioned a sum of Rs. 24,000 per year for the collection and 
the preservation of Sanskrit mss in the Calcutta, Bombay and Madras 
presidencies. Mss were collected and catalogued by scholars in the 
Calcutta and Bombay regions. Due to the use of regional scripts for 
writing Sanskrit mss, the ms survey was not very effective in the 
Madras Presidency, thus a separate survey was carried out for this 
region. As per the government order, dated 14 December 1868 (no. 
42), containing the resolution of the Governor General in council 
dated Simla, 3rd November 1868, (no. 4348), Gustav Oppert, a 
Sanskrit Professor and then Curator of Government Oriental Ms 
Library, Chennai, conducted a survey and published the report in two 
volumes entitled “A List of Sanskrit manuscripts in private libraries of 
Southern India.” These books give the names and addresses of individuals holding mss and the number of Sanskrit mss in various districts in the Madras Presidency. The report states the number of mss 
available in different districts such as North Arcot (446), South Arcot 
(148), Chengleput (2358), Coimba[u]tore (1445), Madras (428), 
Madurai (218), Salem (49), Thanjavur (6336), Tinnelveli (943) and 
Trichy (814). Oppert remarks in the Preface of his book (1880: iv) that 
a “methodologically conducted Government survey is the only means, 
which would bring to light the vast literary treasures.” The preservation of the existing ms collections in India and abroad is imperative.
Major Collections of Palm-Leaf Mss in Tamil Nadu
In Tamil Nadu Sanskrit mss are preserved in the following libraries 
and educational institutions: 
1. The Thanjavur Maharaja Serfoji’s Sarasvati Mahal Library, Thanjavur.

3. The Adyar Theosophical Society Library, Chennai.
4. The Kuppuswami Sastri Research Institute, Mylapore, Chennai.
5. French Institute of Indology, Pondicherry.
