These are the main libraries and institutions; other collections are 
maintained by individuals. 
Thanjavur: Its Historical Phase
The land of Thanjavur lies on the south east coast of Tamil Nadu 
between lat. 10˚.42´ N and long. 79˚.11´E and covers an area of 
3259 sq. miles. Thanjavur is 66 km east of Trichy and 310 km south of 
Chennai. It is about 159 km north from Madurai. It is east of the middle of Tamil Nadu and well connected with all the important tourist 
centres in south India. Thanjavur has been renowned as a great centre 
of art, culture and literature for more than ten centuries. It can be considered the heart of Tamil Nadu with a civilisation that had achieved 
great eminence under the successive dynasties of the Chola, Nayak 
and Maratha kingdoms. 
Thanjavur was the capital of the Imperial Cholas. Historical references to Thanjavur are available from the 7th Century ad onwards. 
Epigraphical references to Thanjavur are available in the rock fort 
edicts of Trichirapalli. The first literary reference to Thanjavur is 
found in the hymns of Saint Appar of the 7th Century. 
In 846 ad the first imperial Chola king, Vijayalaya Chola, captured 
Thanjavur territory from a Mutharayya king and established his capital at Thanjavur. Since the reign of Vijayalaya Chola, Thanjavur

--- Page 180 of 338 ---
p. perumal
164
remained the capital of the subsequent Chola rulers. Starting from 
Vijayalaya, the successive rulers of the Chola dynasty led invasions to 
Ceylon and during the days of Raja Raja Chola, far-off territories such 
as Kerala, Coorg, parts of Mysore and Kalinga (Orissa) were subjugated. His successor, Rajendra Chola, organized a north Indian campaign which led him up to the Ganges. Even though there were various 
regional languages, Sanskrit was the language used for communication. The conquests of Cholas were responsible for the long and continued stability and prosperity of the kingdom and for the resultant 
encouragement and development of cultural pursuits. 
The Pandyas and Hoysala kings ruled the Chola area for a few years 
and in the 14th century the Vijayanagara Empire took over. During 
this reign, Thanjavur regained its lost glory. In 1535 ad, Vijayanagara 
emperor Achyuta Devaraya established the Thanjavur Nayak rule. 
Sevappa Nayak was the first ruler of the Thanjavur kingdom, and the 
Nayak rule continued until 1675 ad. During the Nayak period Than
javur witnessed the flourishing of art, architecture, literature and 
other allied activities. After the Nayak, the Marathas captured and 
ruled Thanjavur from 1675 until 1855 ad. From the 9th century until 
the end of the rule of the Maratha kings in 1855, different dynasties of 
Thanjavur kings nourished and enriched Thanjavur with art, literature and architecture, including the construction of beautiful buildings, temples etc. It increasingly became known as a centre for the 
patronage of arts and literatures. Especially the periods of Raghunatha 
Nayak (1600 to 1633 ad), Shahaji II (1684-1710 ad) and of Serfoji II 
(1798-1832) are considered the golden age of the development of art 
and literature. 
The reign of the Maratha king Shahaji (1684-1710)—called ‘Abhi
nava Bhoja’ after the great king Bhoja famous for his contributions to 
culture and literature—was rightly considered a golden era. Shahaji, a 
composer of great literary merit, encouraged and patronized musicians and literary giants, who made significant contributions to 
Sanskrit and Marathi literature. He founded a village of scholars in 
Thiruvisainallor, later renamed as Shāhājirāja-puram, near Kumbako
ṇam on the banks of the river Kaveri. Scholars living in this village 
included Śrīdhara Veṅkateśa Ayyaval, Rāmacandra Dīkṣitar, Bhāska
rarāya, Trayaṁbarāya Makhin, Sadāśiva Brahmendra and Girirāja 
Kavi. During Shahaji’s reign, Tamil prosody such as kattalai, venba 
and padam was used for Sanskrit. The king himself wrote padas and 
kīrtanas on Lord Śiva of the Thiruvarur temple. On the basis of the

--- Page 181 of 338 ---
sanskrit manuscripts in tamilnadu
165
available mss, the Thanjavor library has published a book entitled 
“Sahaji Mannar Keerthanaikal.” 
Serfoji II enriched this library collection with his literary contributions. He was the author of a number of works in Sanskrit and Marathi 
languages. Notable works written by him include Kumārasaṁbhavacampu, Mohinīmaheśa-pariṇaya, and Rādhākṣṇavilāsa-nāṭaka etc. 
Pandits well versed in Dharmaśāstra, were appointed as judges in his 
court of Justice. They compiled Vyavahāra-prakāśa from various 
smti texts for day-to-day use in the conduct of administration and 
justice.
Thanjavur is rightly acclaimed as the cultural citadel of Tamil Nadu. 
It is situated amidst the vast deltaic tract of the river Kaveri, an area 
known as the ‘Granary of south India.’ Similarly, the culture and art 
produced in the area may be called the gift of the Kaveri river. Great 
poets, skilled builders and master craftsmen, savants and scholars and 
enlightened kings combined to create a very sophisticated and 
advanced civilization whose influence was felt not only in the Indian 
peninsula, but also abroad. Temples of grand designs and majestic 
proportions, embellished with the works of skilled sculptures, are the 
visible manifestations of the spirit and culture, priorities and principles, cherished values and beliefs of the people who lived in those 
days. During the period of the Nayaks up until the final phase of the 
Maratha dynasty, scholars and artists migrated from Andhra and settled in the Agrahāras in the Kaveri delta, contributing to the development of the high classical style. Over generations, pandits preserved 
and transmitted the Vedas using traditional methods.
Sarasvati Mahal Library
The Thanjavur Maharaja Serfoji’s Sarasvati Mahal (TMSSM) Library 
is one of only a few medieval libraries that exist in the world. It is a 
treasure house of knowledge built up by the successive dynasties of 
the Nayaks and Marathas of Thanjavur. It contains very rare and valuable collections of mss, books, maps and paintings on all aspects of 
arts, culture and literature. In its survey of ‘libraries of the world’, the 
Encyclopaedia Britannica mentions it as “the most remarkable library 
in India.” The library was also described by Dr. A. C. Burnell as perhaps the largest and most important in the world. Dr. Bühler said it 
“contains a great many useful, and a number of very rare or nearly

--- Page 182 of 338 ---
p. perumal
166
unique books many of which are quite unknown or procurable only 
with great trouble and expense.”
The origins of the library were probably about the end of the 16th 
century when Thanjavur was under the rule of Telugu Nayaks who 
collected Sanskrit mss written in Telugu characters. In the 18th century, the library was called Sarasvati Bhandar. The Marathas captured 
Thanjavur and conquered the country in 1675 and patronized the culture. Among the Maratha kings, Maharaja Serfoji II (1798-1832) was 
an eminent scholar in many branches of learning. In his youth, he 
studied under Rev. Schwartz, a Danish missionary priest who was his 
father’s friend and guide. In this way, he learned English, French, 
Italian, Latin etc., and also developed interests in the arts, science, and 
literature. With great enthusiasm he took special steps to enrich the 
library. On a pilgrimage to Banares, he employed many pandits to collect, buy and copy a vast number of works from all renowned centres 
of Sanskrit learning in the North and other areas. It is a fitting tribute 
to the great collector Serfoji that the Library is named after him. 
During the Maratha reign the disciples of the Saint Ramdas Swami, 
Guru of Shivaji (1627-1680), established eighteen mutts for the development of religious activities. Most of the heads of these mutts had 
knowledge of Sanskrit and Marathi, and many of the mss written by 
them were added to this library.
Collection of Mss and Books
The library has more than 49,000 mss (titles) in Sanskrit, Tamil, 
Marathi and Telugu, reflecting the history and ancient culture of 
south India. In addition to the existing stacks of mss, this library also 
acquired the private libraries of pandits in the region. Even today, this 
library is still receiving generous donations of mss, and books. This 
library also has more than 65,000 books in Indian and European languages. King Serfoji II personally collected more than 4500 books in 
English, French, German, Danish, Dutch, and Italian. These books 
were printed before 1832 ad; the earliest printed book in this library is 
dated back to 1694 ad.
Sanskrit Ms Collection
The majority of the mss in the library—more than 39,000 titles—are in 
the Sanskrit language. They can be categorized as follows:

--- Page 183 of 338 ---
sanskrit manuscripts in tamilnadu
167
i. Original collection of the kings who ruled Thanjavur.
ii. Collection by their successors till 1912.
iii. Collection from private sources, such as from some families and 
private libraries, from 1918 onwards. 
Some of the important private collections are,
a.  Jambunathabhat Landage Collection (J. L. Collection). In 1921 the 
library received this collection from a descendent of the Jumbunatha family. He was the eldest son of Manambhatta who was an 
expert in Ṛgveda and was in the Maratha kings’ court. This collection contains more than 2000 paper mss.
b.  L. Sethu Ramrao Kagalkar Collection (S. K. Collection). In 1922, 
this 250 year old collection was given to the Thanjavur library by 
the Kagalkar family (who were Sanskrit scholars). The set mostly 
contains mss on Sanskrit grammar. There are also autographed 
copies made by family members and some works composed by 
others. 
c.  Patanga Avadhuta Collection (P. A. Collection). This collection is 
more than 200 years old. Avadhuta was settled at Tiruvidaimarudur in Thanjavur district. In 1921, his descendant Ramayi Ammal 
donated this mss collection, which contains a large number of 
works in Vedānta and Bhakti, to the library. 
d.  Shri Krishnaji Mehta Collection (K. M. Collection). Krishnaji 
Mehta lived in Thanjavur and worked in the Palace administration. He was interested in mantra śāstra. His collection contains 
100 paper and five palm-leaf mss, which were added to the library 
in 1924.
e.  Gopalakrishna Dawalekar Collection (G. K. D. Collection). 
Gopalakrishna Dawalekar hailed from a Maratha brahmin family 
and was an expert in Smārta Prayogas. Hence most of the mss in 
this collection contain Prayoga texts. This was added to this library 
at three different times, in 1925, 1930 and 1934. 
f.  V. B. Nataraja Sastri Collection. (V. B. N. Collection). Ayurve
dacharya V. B. Nataraja Sastri was a reputed vaidya from Tiruchirapalli, as well as a great Sanskrit scholar who had learned 
ancestors. His collection contains 254 bundles of 426 works covering many branches of Sanskrit literature. The mss are about 
Āyurveda medicine and written in Grantha script.

--- Page 184 of 338 ---
p. perumal
168
g.  Tyagaraja Swami Collection (T. S. Collection). Tyagaraja swami 
was head of a Maratha mutt founded by a disciple of Ramdas 
Swami. The collection, which contains more than 1400 mss, was 
added to this library in 1975.
Apart from these major collections, there are more than 200 small collections which were added to the library from 1921 onwards. In 1990 
the library began a special initiative to collect Sanskrit mss in the 
Thanjavur district. Even now the library still appeals to the public to 
donate their mss for preservation.
The mss preserved in the collection are both in palm-leaf and in 
paper form and written in different scripts such as Grantha, Devanā
garī, Telugu, Nandināgarī, Malayalam, Oriya, Bengali and Tigalari 
(Tulu script). Some mss in the Tamil section are written in the 
Maṇipravāla style, that is, in a mixture of Grantha and Tamil scripts. 
The Sanskrit collection of the library contains Vedic mss and literature and copies of the works which were produced directly under the 
patronage of the Telugu and Marathi kings of Thanjavur. They have 
been classified under 18 broad subject headings as: Vedas, Saṁhitās, 
Brāhmaṇas and Upaniṣads, Śrauta sūtras, Kāvya, Nāṭyaśāstra, Kāma
sūtra, Vaidyaka, Jyotiṣa, Āgamas and Tantra, Kośa, Nāṭaka, Alaṁ
kāra, Vyākaraṇa, Dharmaśāstra, Purāṇa, Darśana, Prayogas, Mantras 
and Stotras. The special collection also includes the ancient Saṁhitās 
of Ṛṣi Kāśyapa, Bhāradvāja, Vararuci, Bhela, and Vyāsa, rare commentaries of classical literature; works on Rasaprakriyā; complete 
treatises on medicine such as the Dhanvantarīvilāsa by Tulajarāja; 
Strīdharma Paddhati by Tryaṁbaka Rāya Makhin, exclusive works on 
the duties of women; a rare nāṭya works such as Nttaratnāvali, 
Saṅgīta Muktāvalī by Devendra. It is also worth mentioning a few 
outstanding, unique works such as the rare and short lexicons, the 
grammar of the rare dialect called Bhandira bhāṣā, the play Jambavatīpariṇaya by King Kṛṣṇadevarāya, Vivaraṇadarpaṇa by Raṅgarāja, 
father of the great Appaya Dīkṣita, Kṛṣṇacarita by Agastya Paṇḍit, 
Tattvacintāmaṇi Vyākhyā by Dharmarājādhvarin, a large number of 
short Advaita Prakaraṇas, Sakalādhikāram and Vimānārcanakalpam, 
a work of temple architecture and Viśvakarmīya Śāstra respectively. 
There are also a large number of mss on music and dance, Āyurveda 
medicine, architecture, purāṇas consisting of the legends attached to 
great shrines and sacred villages and towns found in the Kaveri delta. 
Some of the rarest mss include two Sanskrit works by Chidambarakavi 
from the court of Shahaji II. His work Kathātrayī is arranged in a way

--- Page 185 of 338 ---
sanskrit manuscripts in tamilnadu
169
that it can be read as an account of the Rāmāyaṇa, the Mahābhārata 
and the Bhāgavata. Yet another work Niroṣṭya Rāmāyaṇa deals with 
the Sītā Kalyāṇam without ever using labial phonemes like pa, ba, and 
ma. Another mss titled Prapañca Ratnākara is written in microscopic 
Sanskrit letters made up of the words ‘Śiva Śiva’. The oldest palm-leaf 
mss in the collection, entitled Phalavathi, a commentry on the 
Jaiminisūtra, dates back to the 11th century. Another equally important Sanskrit work is known as Bhāmati (No. 7081) a commentary on 
the Śaṅkarabhāṣya written by Vācaspati Miśra, which was copied in 
Banaras in 1468 ad. Among other unique works, are two treatises, 
namely Gajaśāstra and Aśvaśāstra, both with brilliant illustrations, 
depicting the anatomy and characteristics of the elephant and the 
horse. Also Vaidyacintāmaṇi, a Sanskrit treatise with Tamil commentary on Āyurvedic medicines and Saptarishi’s Mohinīvilāsa Kuravanji 
are some of the rare works. The Bhela saṁhitā mss of this library is a 
rare unpublished Āyurvedic mss from 1650 ad. Bhela, one of the six 
disciples of Ātreya Maharṣi, wrote this work that is considered to be 
an Upaveda of the Ṛgveda Saṁhitā. It is made up of 350-year old 
Sanskrit paper mss, which are divided into 85 parts. Each part has an 
illustration, an illuminated gold border and the illustrations are 
depicted in fast colours.
Cataloguing of the Palm-Leaf Mss
Even though the library originated in the 16th century, the earliest 
catalogue available in the library belongs to the period of Raja Serfoji 
II. The catalogues are in both palm-leaf and in paper forms. The palmleaf catalogue was prepared in 1801, following an order from Serfoji, 
by Gaṅgādhara Bhaṭṭalu, alias Sadāśivabhaṭṭalu. The palm-leaf catalogue is written in Telugu script and arranged per subject. The paper 
mss catalogue was prepared for the first time in 1807 ad and then 
again in 1840 by order of the king. After the demise of the last king, 
Shivaji II, the administration of the Palace and the Sarasvati Mahal 
library fell into the hands of the British. The then Thanjavur Collector 
Mr. Forbes ordered the preparation of a catalogue for the library’s 
mss.
In the latter half of the 19th century the library attracted the 
attention of eminent Indologists and great Scholars. In 1868, the gov
ernment of Madras appointed Mr. Pickford, Sanskrit Professor of 
Presidency College, to prepare a catalogue for the Sanskrit mss of this

--- Page 186 of 338 ---
p. perumal
170
library. Unfortunately, due to ill health he was unable to complete the 
work. Then, in 1871, Dr. A.C. Burnell, a Sanskrit scholar and also a 
sessions judge in Thanjavur was appointed to continue the cataloguing. Following his devoted labour the catalogue was finally published 
in 1878-1880 under the title “Classified Index to the Sanskrit Mss in 
the Palace at Tanjore”. It was in three parts with subject headings such 
as Vedic Literature, Modern Sanskrit Literature and Dissertations on 
special topics. In his introduction, Burnell states that “The library, of 
which this catalogue is now made public, was brought to the notice of 
European scholars by H.S.H. Count Noer, Prince Frederic of Schleswig 
Holstein, who brought an account of it to the late professor Gold
stücker. But its full importance was not known till I was deputed, in 
March 1871, to examine it by the then Governor of Madras, Lord 
Napier and Ettrick, whose scholarly and lively interest in the past of 
India will always render his name a cherished souvenir in the minds of 
those who appreciate research. This is now the chief key to the huge 
Sanskrit collection in this library.” 
He adds that “the mss described here are the result of perhaps 300 
years of collecting: firstly, the Nayaks of Tanjore; secondly, after that 
about 1675 nos. by the Maratha Princes. The mss are of different value 
and come from various sources. Some of the palm leaf mss belongs to 
earlier period; but the greater part was collected in the last and recent 
centuries. All the Nāgarī mss belong to the Maratha time and the Raja 
Serfoji collected a large number of these mss at Benares, about fifty 
years ago. The rest are recent Nāgarī copies of mss to south Indian 
characters, and as a rule are badly made; a large staff of copyists were 
formerly entertained.” 
While preparing the catalogue, Dr. Burnell wrote to the Chief 
Secretary, to the Government of Madras on 6th August 1873 as follows: “It may perhaps be asked if the Library is worth the labour spent 
on it. I can answer unhesitatingly that it is. It is now a recognized fact 
that nearly all Sanskrit works of importance exist in different recensions. The Tanjore Library is unrivalled in this respect: it contains several good mss of all the most important ones known as yet, including 
a few that are new … the Tanjore Library, however, contains additional mss of most of the works which I had discovered elsewhere and 
this is a matter of great importance. I believe that this library must, 
sooner or later escheat to the Government. The preparation of this 
catalogue will therefore protect property of enormous value. Sanskrit 
mss have long been very dear and the cost of making proper tran-

--- Page 187 of 338 ---
sanskrit manuscripts in tamilnadu
171
scripts is now very heavy. As far as I can judge, it would not be possible to form a collection like that at Tanjore at a less cost than £ 50, 000 
but many mss are unquestionably unique.”
In 1927 Descriptive Catalogues were prepared by pandits under the 
leadership of P.P. Subramania Sastry (P.P. Sastry). The mss were classified into 18 main headings with subheadings; 19 volumes of 
Descriptive catalogues were prepared and published during 1927- 
1932. Later this library’s pandits brought out four volumes of Descrip
tive Catalogue, a separate Descriptive Catalogue for Jain mss and an 
alphabetical catalogue of Tigalari mss belonging to the library. In 
2006, the library published its 25th volume, an alphabetical index, 
consisting of 4175 paper mss belonging to recent collections and an 
alphabetical list of approximately 7000 palm-leaf mss is also in progress. According to P.P.S. Sastry’s 19th volume of the Descriptive catalogue, the Sanskrit mss of this library, date back to 1430 at the earliest 
and 1902 A.D at the latest. The library publishes the unpublished mss 
in book form and the minor works are published in the form of 
Journal articles. All in all, this library has published nearly 200 books.
The Government of India has initiated the National Mission for 
Manuscripts (NAMAMI) project. This programme will facilitate the 
identification, documentation and preservation of mss, and the rare 
ones are to be digitized. This library is one of the nodal agencies of the 
southern district of Tamil Nadu for this project and more than 10,000 
mss have been documented during the last three years.

CHAPTER nine
Indian exact sciences in Sanskrit manuscripts 
and their colophons
Kim Plofker
Introduction
The exact sciences—particularly jyotiṣa, or mathematical astronomy/
astrology—are a very important part of the Sanskrit scholarly tradition. It has been estimated that perhaps one-tenth of all extant Sanskrit 
manuscripts deal with jyotiṣa or some other aspect of Indian exact sciences, and their contents often reveal useful information about the 
scholarly connections and personal backgrounds of their owners. 
Moreover, the exact sciences in India also supplied the numeric and 
calendric systems that are used to express the dates found in scribal 
post-colophons. Despite these significant contributions, the academic 
study of Sanskrit literature and manuscripts in Indology programs 
hardly ever includes subjects in the mathematical sciences. As a result, 
jyotiṣa items in collections of Sanskrit manuscripts are often neglected 
or sketchily described. This paper attempts to give a preliminary indication of the influence of astronomy, mathematics and astrology on 
the production and description of Sanskrit manuscripts.1
1  The canonical though still incomplete survey of jyotiṣa material in Sanskrit 
manuscripts worldwide .is the late David Pingree’s Census of the Exact Sciences in 
Sanskrit [Pingree 1970-94]; the same author also produced descriptive catalogues of 
many individual collections of jyotiṣa manuscripts, such as [Pingree 1968] and [Pingree 2003]. A survey of South Indian scientiﬁc manuscripts in particular was compiled by the late K. V. Sarma in [Sarma 2002]. For a general introduction to the study 
of Sanskrit manuscripts, see [Murthy 1996]. Historical overviews of Indian mathematics and astronomy have appeared recently in, for example, [Rao 2004] and 
[Plofker 2009].

--- Page 190 of 338 ---
kim plofker
174
Indian Astronomy and the Calendar: Colophon Dates
The paramount contribution of jyotiṣa to Sanskrit literature is the 
complex astronomical legacy of the Indian calendar that allows 
authors and scribes to record dates of composition and copying, as in 
the final line of the ending folio shown below:
Figure 9.1.  Yantrarājakalpa, Benares (Sarasvati Bhavan Library, Sampurnanand 
Sanskrit University) 35245.
The scribe concludes “saṃvatsarāḥ 1877 āśāḍhakṛṣṇa 4 bhārgavāsa
rau”, or “Thursday the fourth tithi (lunar day) in the dark fortnight of 
[month] Āṣāḍha in [Saṃvat] year 1877”.2 This date is an example of 
the sort of detail that has been described by Christopher Minkowski as 
an issue “too obvious to be considered”:3 we take the inclusion of dates 
in colophons or post-colophons for granted as a standard bibliographic feature, without troubling to think about its implications for the 
relationship of scientific systems and scholarship. But in fact, this 
standard feature is rich in technical detail and interpretative complexity, as the following description will show.
Traditional Sanskrit vs. Gregorian Calendars
Comparing the traditional Indian calendars with today’s universal 
Gregorian calendar reveals many important differences. The most 
obvious one is the offset between the arbitrary starting-points of the 
different eras. More significantly, as shown in the table below, they dif2  This dating system is explained in works on traditional Indian calendrics such 
as [Sewell/Dikshit 1896]; see also [S. Pillai 1922-23].
3  Christopher Z. Minkowski, Introductory Lecture, IIAS Workshop on Production, Distribution and Collection of Sanskrit Manuscripts in Ancient South India, 
Leiden, 20 April 2007.

--- Page 191 of 338 ---
indian exact sciences in sanskrit manuscripts
175
fer greatly in their technical sophistication. For the sake of logistical 
convenience, the Gregorian calendar has been simplified until it is 
hardly an astronomical timekeeping system at all any longer. All connections with actual lunar months have been severed, so that the 
Gregorian “month” is merely a schematic unit of approximately 30 
days, completely irrelevant to the phases or positions of the moon. 
The days themselves are divided at the arbitrary point of midnight 
rather than a more astronomically meaningful moment such as sunset 
or sunrise, and the length of the year is approximated by an integer 
number of days (either 365 or 366).
Indian calendars, on the other hand, have preserved their fundamentally astronomical chronometric features throughout their history. In particular, they retained the complicated and challenging 
synchronization of solar and lunar motion, employing an intercalary 
or “leap” month for this purpose rather than a quadrennial leap day. 
The only significant characteristic it shares with the Gregorian system 
is the seven-day week that was inherited by both calendric systems 
from Hellenistic astrology.4 The other timekeeping elements require 
much more computational facility in Indian calendars than in the 
Gregorian.
Gregorian 
Sanskrit
Common Era 
Vikrama, Śaka, etc.
Elapsed years 
Elapsed or current years
Solar reckoning 
Luni-solar reckoning
Year: 365 days 
Year: 12 lunar months
1 “leap” day 
1 “leap” month
Schematic time-units 
Astronomical time-units
Dates: month/day 
Dates: month/tithi
Midnight day-end 
Sunrise day-end
7-day week 
7-day week
As a result, Indian astronomical texts recognize the possibility of 
inconsistent results in date computations, and prescribe solutions for 
dealing with them. A jyotiṣa author will typically acknowledge, for 
4  Greek astrology assigned the names of the seven visible planets (counting the 
sun and moon among them) to the seven days of the ancient Near Eastern week, in 
the now familiar order Sun, Moon, Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, Saturn. This 
seven-day week was assimilated into other cultures’ calendric systems in India, the 
Islamic world, and elsewhere, along with other features of Hellenistic astrology. See 
[Neugebauer 1969, pp. 169–172].

--- Page 192 of 338 ---
kim plofker
176
example, that the computation of a given date from a given epoch or 
starting-point may produce an error of ±1 in the weekday or intercalary months when compared to the current reckoning provided by the 
user’s local calendar. While such a discrepancy in a Gregorian date 
would signal the presence of an outright error, the situation is more 
complicated with Sanskrit calendars. There is no simple standard 
reconstruction of the exact chronological sequence of any Indian calendar reckoning, and thus no easy way to label one of two inconsistent dates “correct” or “incorrect”.
Ambiguities in Date Conversion
Indological researchers working with Sanskrit manuscripts are often 
not aware of this potential for variability in date computations, but it 
deserves to be more widely understood. We can see its effects in many 
attempts to convert Indian to Gregorian dates, which can produce a 
spurious impression of exactitude and certainty if the idiosyncrasies 
of different conversion algorithms are ignored. Consider, for instance, 
the following three examples of post-colophon dates converted to 
Gregorian equivalents, first according to the Indian Ephemeris of 
S. Pillai [S. Pillai 1922-23], and then according to the “Pañcāṅga” software of Yano and Fushimi [Yano/Fushimi 2004], which is based on 
Sewell and Dikshit’s Indian Calendar [Sewell/Dikshit 1896]:
Monday 10 kṛṣṇapakṣa of Phālguna, Saṃ. 1840:
  Monday 15 March 1784 (Pillai, Indian Ephemeris)
  Tuesday 16 March 1784 (Yano & Fushimi, “Pañcāṅga”)
Tuesday 8 kṛṣṇapakṣa of Jyeṣṭha, Saṃ. 1815:
  Tuesday 30 May 1758 (Pillai)
  Thursday 29 June 1758, or Tuesday 30 May pūrṇimānta (Yano & Fushimi)
Sunday 6 kṛṣṇapakṣa of Caitra, Śaka 1653:
  Friday 16 April 1731 Julian (Pillai and Yano & Fushimi)
While both conversion systems agree on the date of the third example, 
the answers they give for the first one differ by a day, and the second 
example involves a possible discrepancy of nearly an entire month 
(depending on whether the calendar used is assumed to be pūrṇimānta, 
reckoning months from the full moon, or āmāvāsya, reckoning from 
new moon—a crucial detail hardly ever explicitly specified in a 
Sanskrit colophon date). These results illustrate the important point 
that there is no entirely reliable and precise mapping from dates

--- Page 193 of 338 ---
indian exact sciences in sanskrit manuscripts
177
appearing in Indian manuscripts to their counterparts in modern 
chronology, a fact too often glossed over by researchers and even 
by manuscript cataloguers.5
“Concrete Numerals” or Bhūtasaṃkhyā Numbers in Dates
Scribes of manuscripts (and of epigraphic inscriptions) sometimes 
borrowed from technical authors a convention for representing 
numerals verbally. This was the bhūtasaṃkhyā or “concrete numeral” 
system, where strings of decimal digits from the least to the most significant are represented by dvandva compounds of words that conventionally stand for particular numbers. The manuscript example 
shown below illustrates this practice with the mix of standard numeral 
and bhūtasaṃkhyā notations in the year-number in its last line: “18 
veda-v[e]dā” [?]. The bhūtasaṃkhyā word veda represents 4, implying 
a date of 1844.
Figure 9.2.  Candrārkī, LDI (L. D. Institute, Ahmadabad) 6759 (4331).
The next example uses both notations separately to express the number 1551, in the third line from the bottom: “ku-viśikha-tithi 1551” or 
“earth-arrow-tithi”. “Earth” signifies 1 and “arrow” 5 (i.e., the five 
arrows of the love-god Kāma or five senses). The word tithi or lunar 
5  Cases such as the one mentioned by Christopher Minkowski in the lecture referenced in note 2 above, where the scribe Tularāma completed the copying of two 
manuscripts within three days and used the same calendar system to date them, are 
a diﬀerent matter. Two dates written by the same scribe using the same local calendar can usually be reliably compared, but the simultaneity of apparently identical 
dates found in diﬀerent sources should not be taken for granted.

--- Page 194 of 338 ---
kim plofker
178
day, here used somewhat confusingly to designate a year-number with 
no reference to actual tithis, stands for 15 because there are fifteen of 
them in a fortnight.
Figure 9.3.  Karaṇakaṇṭhīrava, RORI (Rajasthan Oriental Research Institute) 
(Alwar) 2652.
It remains unclear why manuscript scribes sometimes adopted bhūta
saṃkhyā notation for postcolophon dates. This notation was apparently originally devised by authors of verse treatises to facilitate 
expressing number words in different poetic meters, and to preserve 
their true values through the generations of transmission. But date 
information added by scribes at the end of manuscripts is generally 
not versified, nor is it routinely copied from one manuscript to 
another.
So what is the point of using the poetic flourish of concrete numerals to express it? Perhaps it is an imitation of epigraphic bhūtasaṃkhyā 
notation in inscriptions (which is itself a somewhat puzzling phenomenon, for the same reasons)?
Types of Jyotiṣa Manuscripts: Astronomy
Given the ubiquity of jyotiṣa manuscripts in Sanskrit collections, a 
discussion like this one would be incomplete without a brief descrip-

--- Page 195 of 338 ---
indian exact sciences in sanskrit manuscripts
179
tion of their main categories and characteristics. We begin with the 
works on mathematical astronomy, classified as the siddhānta, karaṇa, 
and koṣṭhaka genres. The first of these is the weightiest and most 
authoritative type of text in the exact sciences: it typically presents in 
hundreds of verses the entire theory and computational methods of 
an astronomical system, based on immense parameters which are 
applicable for any past or future point in time.
The example of a siddhānta folio shown below contains several of 
these impressively large parameter values representing planetary 
motions during the entire lifetime of the universe, ranging up to the 
tens of billions. 
Figure 9.4.  Siddhāntasundara, BORI (Bhandarkar Oriental Research Institute) 
860 of 1887/91.
The karaṇa or handbook genre, illustrated in the following image, is 
simpler in operation and more limited in application. It is a series of 
versified algorithms relying on a given starting-point or epoch date, 
usually occurring within the author’s own lifetime, and reduced versions of astronomical parameters to make computation easier. The 
positions of celestial bodies at the moment of the given epoch are 
listed in tables such as the one shown here, which specifies the positions of the twenty-eight lunar mansions.

--- Page 196 of 338 ---
kim plofker
180
Figure 9.5.  Grahalāghava, Jaipur, 2004. See colour section, Plate X.
The most convenient and most common of all the types of text in 
mathematical astronomy is the koṣṭhaka or table text, a typical example of which is shown below. As the name suggests, this sort of work 
reduces the process of astronomical computation to operating with 
numerical tables worked out for several decades or centuries around 
the time of the text’s composition. The first of the tables on the sample 
folio gives parameter values for the years (in the Śaka era) 1664, 1688, 
1712, 1736, 1760, 1784, 1808, and 1832: that is, at intervals of 24 years 
from 1742 to 1910 ce.
Figure 9.6.  Makaranda, Jaipur, 2004.

--- Page 197 of 338 ---
indian exact sciences in sanskrit manuscripts
181
Astrological Jyotiṣa Texts and Chronology
Even more frequently encountered than table texts in manuscript collections are the innumerable works representing the various branches 
of Sanskrit astrology. These are often invaluable to the manuscript 
researcher owing to their inclusion of horoscopes cast for particular 
dates. The example on the left in the folio below (transcribed in the 
image following it) is a standard simple janmakuṇḍalī or birth horoscope. It divides a square into twelve parts representing the twelve 
signs of the zodiac, numbered from 1 (Aries) to 12 (Pisces), and shows 
in each part a syllabic abbreviation for the name(s) of any planet(s) 
occupying that sign at the moment of birth. In this example, the Sun, 
Venus and Mercury are in Taurus, the Moon, Saturn and the ascending lunar node (Rāhu) are in Cancer, the descending node (Ketu) in 
Capricorn, Jupiter in Aquarius, and Mars in Pisces.
Figure 9.7.  IO (India Office Library) 2541c.

--- Page 198 of 338 ---
kim plofker
182
Figure 9.8.  Janmakuṇḍalī.
This planetary diagram is too crude to use for making detailed 
horoscopic predictions in accordance with an astrological system, but 
it is unexpectedly effective in establishing or verifying dates. This is 
due to the wide variation in the speed of the planets as they progress 
through the sky. The fastest of them, the Moon, stays in the same zodiacal sign on average only a little over two days, whereas the slowest, 
Saturn, lingers in a sign for between two and three years. Consequently, 
any given configuration of planets and signs is not likely to repeat 
itself exactly in a short period of time, and within a given time interval 
it can be pinpointed chronologically with reasonable precision. 
Thus if the age of a text or manuscript containing such a horoscope 
can be estimated within a century or so, the horoscope itself can usually be dated to within one or two days. If a date is given in the manuscript, as in the case of the example shown whose specified date is 
approximately equivalent to 7 May 1535, the date conversion can be 
verified by back-calculating the planetary positions for that time and 
comparing them to the positions in the horoscope. Such recomputed 
positions for 7 May 1535 appear in the following table:6 it is easy to see 
that they agree very well with the crude specifications in the horoscope shown above.
6  These positions were calculated by the late David Pingree in an unpublished 
note now part of the David Pingree Papers, American Philosophical Society, Philadelphia.

  The value of horoscopes as a source of prosopographical data lies in 
the fact that they almost invariably concern particular individuals
(although horoscopes can also be cast for events or institutions), usually an individual of immediate interest to the person who is writing 
the horoscope down. Consequently, even a completely undated manuscript, if it contains a janmakuṇḍalī of this sort, can usually be confidently assigned to a specific period at most a few decades long.
Conclusion: Manuscript Mysteries in the Exact Sciences
The foregoing brief introduction to the applications of jyotiṣa in 
manuscript research should not be taken to imply that the exact 
sciences as manifested Sanskrit manuscripts are productive only of 
enlightenment. Frequently they appear in strange and unexpected 
forms that generate confusion rather than eliminating it. The two 
examples shown below (each a single leaf, apparently written in the 
nineteenth or early twentieth century, acquired in Jaipur in 2004 and 
subsequently donated to a manuscript collection there) illustrate the 
potential for mystery. The first of the two manuscripts shows what 
might be a diagram of a semicircular scale for some observational or 
timekeeping instrument, graded on each side from 1 to 12. The sole 
explanatory information provided is the cryptic utterance “krāntivṛtta 
4 bhāga”, or “declination-circle 4 degree”. Could this have been part 
of a work relating to the instruments of the famed eighteenth-century 
observatory of Jai Singh II in Jaipur? Without additional information 
from sources hitherto unknown, it is hard to see how that question 
could ever be answered.

--- Page 200 of 338 ---
kim plofker
184
Figure 9.9.  Jaipur, 2004.
The second example is even more puzzling and inscrutable: it is simply a table of sequences of numbers with no obviously meaningful 
mathematical pattern, and no obvious relationship to astronomical or 
calendric data. Clearly it represented significant information to the 
scribe who wrote it, but modern researchers may never know why.
Figure 9.10.  Jaipur, 2004.

--- Page 201 of 338 ---
indian exact sciences in sanskrit manuscripts
185
On the whole, however, the exploration of exact sciences is likely to be 
rewarding and enlightening for the student of Sanskrit manuscripts. 
Besides the explicit dates specified in post-colophons and in the epoch 
data of many astronomical texts, they often provide indirect chronological information via horoscopes and calendric techniques. The 
ubiquity of Sanskrit astrology, and the elaborate details of Sanskrit 
calendric computations, meant that even authors and scribes with no 
professional background in jyotiṣa had a far more sophisticated 
awareness of the science of timekeeping than our rigidly simplified 
and abstract modern chronometry generally permits us to acquire. 
Ultimately, this awareness constitutes a vital link in our understanding of the production and history of Sanskrit manuscripts in India.

CHAPTER TEN
Varieties of Grantha Script: the date and 
place of origin of manuscripts
Saraju Rath
1.  Introduction
1.1  The development and, eventually, wide-spread use of grantha1 
script in the southern part of the Indian subcontinent form a constitutive part of the extensive manuscript culture in the Indian world and 
in Asia, especially South and South-East Asia. The beginnings of 
grantha script around one and a half millennium ago are no more 
directly accessible but impressive fragments of its material basis and 
traces of its vibrant life history are still visible. Although the available 
material, in the form of inscriptions and even more in the form of 
manuscripts, is abundant, its detailed examination has hardly begun. 
Early works on Indian scripts such as the one by Burnell (1878) provide the basics only on grantha next to several other scripts of South 
India. Recent publications specifically devoted to grantha2 do not 
intend to be more than introductions and study aids for beginners. In 
this article it is my aim to clarify, in the framework of a synthetic overview, distinctive characteristics of varieties and stages of development 
of the grantha script that have so far not been noted or that have 
remained vague and ambiguous. For this I will not rely on tables of 
hand-drawn characters (as is mostly done in recent publications on 
grantha) but on characters that are directly taken from manuscripts 
from different periods. 
1.2  Texts in the Sanskrit language, transmitted and often also produced in South India, appear in different scripts such as telugu, kan
naḍa, grantha, nandināgarī and malayalam. Among these scripts, 
1  Because I have to refer to a few scripts that have the same names as the language to which they primarily belong, script names in this article will start in small 
case and language names in capital : telugu script—Telugu language. 
2  Grünendahl 2001, Visalakshy 2003.

--- Page 204 of 338 ---
saraju rath
188
grantha occupies a major position as this is the only script specially 
designed to write Sanskrit (and Vedic) texts, including royal records 
and documents in the southern states of the Indian subcontinent. 
Here, grantha is the transregional script employed for texts in the 
Sanskrit language next to other scripts which are preferably used for 
other languages and vernaculars: tamil for Tamil, telugu for Telugu, 
etc. Some of these scripts, for instance telugu, are suitable for writing 
Sanskrit and have actually been employed for that purpose next to the 
writing of the corresponding regional language. Other scripts such as 
tamil, are incapable of distinctively representing all sounds of Sanskrit3 
and are hence in need of grantha for the adequate representation of 
these sounds. Just as Sanskrit as language of several sciences, of literature and of brahmanical and buddhist religious texts is normally part 
of a bilingual situation—speakers in south India thus typically combine knowledge of Sanskrit with an intimate familiarity with Tamil, 
Telugu, etc.—like that the grantha script in south India is normally 
part of a biscriptual situation in which grantha combines with tamil, 
telugu scripts etc.4 It should hence not be a matter of surprise that 
grantha shows regional differences parallel with the distinct nature of 
the other scripts with which it stands in a biscriptual relation. More
over, grantha has gone through a number of relatively clearly identifiable stages of development, from the first half of the first millennium 
ce till early modern times.5 Among the multifaceted changes in style 
and calligraphy of the characters of the different varieties of the script, 
which are the ones that may serve to determine the relative date of a 
grantha manuscript ? 
 
1.3  Since especially South Indian manuscripts normally lack any 
precise statement about the date of their production, indications that 
can be derived from subsequent changes in the style and calligraphy 
of the script-varieties are very much needed for a chronological 
determination of available manuscripts. Even in the very limited 
number of cases their colophon does contain a date, the system of 
dating usually gives the Saṁvatsara year which follows a 60-year cycle6 
3  For example, tamil alphabet ka represents Sanskrit kha, ga and gha. 
4  For the moment we will here not take into account the later use of devanāgarī 
in South India, which will make the situation multi-scriptual. 
5  Its origins are therefore contemporary with those of siddhamātkā and early 
nāgarī in the middle and northern part of the Indian subcontinent (Rath 2006). 
6  See for telugu manuscripts, Sarma’s article on “From my Grandfather’s chest of 
palmleaf Books” in the present volume.

--- Page 205 of 338 ---
varieties of grantha script
189
of Bhaspati (Jupiter), so that a year given as, for instance, virodhakṛt 
saṁvatsara, may correspond to ce 1551 or to 1551 + 60 = 1611, or to 
1671, to 1731 etc. or also to 1551—60 = 1491 etc. In recent decades 
laboratory techniques have been developed to determine the dates of 
an object on the basis of the ratio of a radioactive isotope (C14) in the 
material texture. In practice this technique leaves a considerable margin 
and often remains incapable of adding a significant chronological 
indication beyond the rough estimates that can be made on other 
grounds. 
2.  Varieties of Grantha Script
2.1  In his discussion of the South-Indian alphabets and their devel
opment, A.C. Burnell (1878: 14) gives a chronological chart of the 
southern scripts in order to show the derivation of some of these 
scripts (including grantha) from southern brāhmī, as well as their link 
with other scripts from southern origin. I give here this chronological 
chart with minor modifications.

2.2  As indicated in Chart 2 and Table 1, the varieties we find are, 
i. grantha with telugu-kannaḍa
ii. grantha with vaṭṭeluttu
iii. grantha with tamil
iv. grantha with malayalam (ārya-eluttu)
v. grantha with tulu-malayalam (kol-eluttu)
i.  Stone inscriptions and copper-plates which are found from the 
4th–5th century onwards show an archaic form of grantha characters 
(representing Sanskrit) that has a close affinity to archaic telugu-kan
naḍa7 characters (representing early Telugu and Kannaḍa) of the same 
period. Especially the grantha characters which, in spite of their monumental context, have a cursive form show this affinity. This suggests 
a development of early grantha in connection with early telugukannaḍa writing and in parallel monumental and cursive styles (the 
latter probably on perishable materials now lost). The grantha characters were developed in a full-fledged way to a common variety in 
course of time through a number of developmental phases. The particular early specimens of this writing found in inscriptions and plates 
belonged to the period of the Pallava, the Chalukya and the Kadamba 
rulers, who reigned till the mid 7th century. From then onwards we 
see a gradual speciation till the early southern scripts acquire an individual status and an independent identity.
In addition to the early sources available in Indian epigraphy, 
inscriptions and plates outside India show the archaic cursive and 
monumental styles of early grantha that is close to telugu/kannaḍa. 
K. A. Nilakanta Sastri (1949 [2003]: 79-82) refers to both the monumental and the cursive style of this early grantha in some early inscriptions discovered in Java, e.g. one at Ci-Aruton. Also Diringer refers to 
7  Inscription found in Halmidi, dated ca. 450 ce and Badami rock Inscription 
(5th-6th ad) which was found in Bijapur, Karnataka. Also see Sivaramamurti 1952, 
p. 222.
Also see Epigraphia Indica I, VIII, pp. 159–163, Pikira plates of Pallava king 
Siṁhavarman, dated 5th ad, found in Nelatur, Ongole taluk, Guntur District, 
Andhra Pradesh.

192
this inscription.8 Another relevant specimen is formed by the two thin 
gold plates known as Maunggun plates discovered in Burma.9 
In available manuscripts the archaic monumental and archaic cursive styles of grantha have so far not been found. However, we do find 
Sanskrit manuscripts of a later period written in a round variety of 
grantha where telugu or kannaḍa characters are inserted in between; 
for example, the word gṛhapati where gṛ appears in telugu [or/and 
kannaḍa] and hapati in grantha.
ii.  According to Burnell (1878: 49), tamil script is the direct descendant from vaṭṭeluttu. Both are closely similar, except that in vaṭṭeluttu 
the character slants from down-left to up-right.10 The observations of 
C. Sivaramamurti (1966: 235) are basically in harmony with this: 
“Grantha and Vaṭṭeluttu were used in the extreme south of the 
Peninsula. Vaṭṭeluttu is a form of cursory Tamil written in a peculiar 
slanting way ... ” He further explains, more interestingly, how these 
two scripts nevertheless developed to a large extent independently 
and maintained their distinctive features: “as [vaṭṭeluttu] had an independent development, the letters differ to a certain extent in their general features with greater resemblance in such letters as ā, au, e, ka, pa, 
ra, la and va, but [with less resemblance] in letters like ṭa and ma, and 
in some cases [the resemblance is even less] by their peculiarly different shape as for instance in i, [ṇ]a, ta, na and ya, though the ultimate 
common origin can easily be traced.” Vaṭṭeluttu is thus to be regarded 
as an old and cursive form of southern script which was important 
from the 5th century onwards,11 under Pallava, Cera, Pandya and 
Cola rulers. Grantha characters occurring with vaṭṭeluttu were mostly 
used in inscriptions and later in palm-leaf manuscripts in areas such 
as South Malabar, Coimbatore etc.. In his report on the palace records 
(grandhavaris) in Malabar and Kerala, Dr. Nampoothiry mentions

Fig. 174, p. 383.

the existence of palmleaf manuscripts of the 16th century in vaṭṭeluttu

iii.  The development of characters in grantha-tamil is seen in Pallava, 
Cola and Pandya inscriptions. In his book on Indian epigraphy and 
South Indian scripts, Sivaramamurti (1966: 222-234) shows in detail 
the stages of development through illustrations of the mixed granthatamil variety in inscriptions. In the manuscript tradition, however, 
both the scripts got prominence with an independent, individual status. The following points deserve notice:
a. In a manuscript text in Sanskrit, the text is written in grantha 
which is sometimes mixed with a few letters (the ones most resembling their tamil counterparts) in tamil style, for example, ī, e, o, ra, l 
and l. This happens often if the scribe is from Tamil origin.

b. In this case we also find the Sanskrit text purely in grantha script

c.  In a Sanskrit text with Tamil commentary, the Sanskrit version 
is written purely in grantha and the commentary mainly in tamil 
script and Tamil language. Literature of the maṇipravāla15 type shows 
this variety.

iv.  Among scripts for Dravidian languages, malayalam script is closest 
to grantha as it shows numerous similarities in many individual 
characters. This was well noticed already by Burnell, who referred to 
malayalam script as a catagory of modern grantha16 (western grantha); 
however, the appropriateness of the name which he used for this, tulumalayalam, can be disputed. 17 The grantha-malayalam18 mss studied

by me make me agree with Mahalingam’s view on the influence of the

“Ultimately, however, it became necessary for Malayalam to have a 
script of its own, which was formed from the Grantha script. The new 
script which came into existence towards the end of the 14th century 
ad was called Ārya-eluttu to distinguish it from Tekkaṉ Malayalam, 
the local name for Vaṭṭeluttu in South Kerala and Kōleluttu, another 
variant of Vaṭṭeluttu in North Kerala. Even after the introduction of 
Ārya-eluttu for Malayalam, Vaṭṭeluttu lingered on as it was popular 
among the sections of the population whose dialects were not so heavily Sanskritised as that of the Nambūdiri Brāhmans.”
v.  The tulu-malayalam script is a variety of grantha dating from the 
8th or 9th century ce. It is the variety which Burnell called transitional 
grantha. From about 1300 ce we see the script in the form which it has 
basically maintained till modern times. Currently two varieties of 
tulu-malayalam are in use: Brahmanic, or square, and Jain, or round. 
The development of malayalam script and its varieties (esp. Kōleluttu) 
deserves further research on the basis of mss in mixed scripts.

3.  Geographical Differentiation and Developments 
over Time
3.1  The locations of inscriptions and of collections of manuscripts 
etc. point to distinct areas where the different varieties of grantha 
nantly used in Tulu country; full-fledged malayalam variety came here in use later 
on.

--- Page 212 of 338 ---

were popular and dominant. A map which specifies the geographical

centuries is provided below. 
Figure 10.4.  Map of South India showing the areas of the varieties of grantha.
Map by author. See colour section, Plate XV. 
3.2  Apart from geographical differentiation there are developments 
over time. We find notable changes in some aspects of grantha script 
applying to some or all varieties which can be used in the determination of a date of the manuscript. In addition, each scribe has his own 
style but in the present study I abstract from such variations and focus 
on features that change over time. These notable changes can be summarized under the following headings:
[ A ] Specific characters (vowels and consonants)
[ B ] Conjunct consonants & placements
[ C ] Punctuation marks
[ D ] Numerals: alphabetic, syllabic, or digital

illustrates some major changes over time. The earliest stage is repre-

the period of Rajaraja Cola I. Later stages are represented by characters from palm-leaf manuscripts belonging to different periods (16th–
19th).

3.4  In the comparative chart (table 2) only a small number of characters and conjuncts are discussed in order to illustrate changes in style 
and calligraphy which have occurred from time to time. Among available sources I give examples from manuscripts of the van Manen collection and from the so-called Leiden copper-plates which belong to 
the 11th cent. ad of Chola King Rajaraja Chola I.
Vowel A and Ā
a. Column 1 (copper-plate) gives an ancient vowel A which is simple, 
having a single loop at the left side below, and has few ornamental 
features except for the connected vertical line. An early form of ms. 
variety in column 2 shows double loops, a feature that is not common 
in later periods. The A in a 19th cent. ms. (last column) is simpler in 
form.
b. In long Ā, the ancient version in column one has an extending line at 
the right (of the character) which remains proportional when it comes 
out from the middle in a curving shape (a bit like roman numeral three) 
and encircles it from below till the lower front part of the character 
is reached; the ms. style shows considerable freedom regarding the 
extending line which sometimes starts from the top, sometimes from 
below, and which continues even till the top of the frontside of the 
character. 
Mātrā U and Ū
c.   The mātrās u and ū after consonant in copper-plate are put in two 
vertical lines with the lower ends joined in the case of all characters 
such as su 
, bu 
, du 
 etc. In few cases, earlier mss show a 
partly similar style with the mātrās exclusively put below the characters 
in the middle part such as pu 
, and in other cases they are put at 
the side, for example, sū 
. Mss of the 19th century do not show 
uniformity: this mātrā sometimes starts from the corner below or at the 
side, in pu 
, not from the middle. Even with the same character 
in the same ms. of this period the style of this mātrā can each time 
be different (starting from the corner below, at the side or from the 
middle); this was not the case in earlier centuries.
Diphthongs
d.  The mātrā ai (similarly au not shown here) in inscriptions and 
copper-plates in Chola grantha consists of two spiraling curls one above

--- Page 215 of 338 ---
varieties of grantha script
199
another preceding to the consonant character to which it belongs and 
this style is known as pṛṣṭhamātrā, occasionally also one curl in front of 
the other and both preceding to the consonant; in manuscripts (16th—
18th and 19th cent.) only the latter style is found. 
Visarga
e.  The style of visarga shows some variation in manuscripts between 
the 16th and 18th century, the similarity between the various styles 
being that two dots are never placed separately. The second column 
shows a running style which differs both from what is found in earlier 
and from what is found in later centuries.
Consonants
f.  Among consonants I have taken the first one, ka, as test character, 
and ra as an additional one. A complete overview of all characters is 
to be provided in a forthcoming publication. To be noted is the subtle 
distinction between the ka in the early ms. variety and the later one: the 
right-hand loop is only curved but not joined. 
g.  Two long vertical lines joined both on the top and the bottom 
represents ra in copper-plate and inscription writing. In later writing 
ra is more open and has an addtional line. In the early mss. the special 
features are: (i) the beginning line often joins the second one whereas 
the first line remains always separate in 19th century ms. and in some 
earlier transitional forms; (ii) the topline is lengthened which is mostly 
not the case in 19th cent. ones.
Conjuncts
h.  In case of conjuncts, both characters in full form are put either one 
above the other or side by side: this is what we find both in inscriptions 
and copper-plates and in early mss. However, later mss. show ligatures 
in which the first character is abbreviated and the second one full. Even 
in later mss. we may also find the old system. 
i.  Inscriptions and copper-plates are consistent in the use of a brief 
conjunct form of ya slanting from left-down to right-up which is added 
to consonants; in early mss. a new style comes up which is more vertical 
and more rounded; this is the only form normally found in later mss. 
Duplication in characters
j.  Characters can be doubled in all periods (from early Sanskrit

--- Page 216 of 338 ---
saraju rath
200
inscriptions till recent manuscripts19), especially in conjuncts with 
r and h (Pāṇini’s rule 8.4.46 aco rahābhyāṁ dve) but also in other 
environments.20 For instance, śrī is found written as śśrī (with ś below 
śr). These doubled consonants, as in rppa, rcca, rjja, rṇṇa, are in the 
earlier centuries written one above the other, later on mostly side by 
side (with the repha in between). 
3.5  With regard to numerals [D], the use of specific symbols and systems of representation in grantha script have not yet been sufficiently 
studied and needs a separate treatment. Scholars such as Ojha, 
Gokhale, Pandey, Diringer, Dani, Mahalingam, Shivaganesha Murthy 
do not discuss the problems of the representation of the numerals and 
variations in the system. Bühler’s, Ojha’s, Grünendahl’s, Visalakshy’s 
works contain useful material but they provide only brief charts which 
are quite insufficient for determining the value of the numerals as 
found in the manuscripts.
3.6  The following ms. is a good example of the use of both alphabetic 
numerals (one of the varieties of Akṣarapalli) and digital numerals in 
the margin. The manuscript apparently belongs to a transitional 
period where both types are used.

4.  Indices for Attributing a Date and Region of Origin 
to a Manuscript
4.1  Referring to our previous discussion and adding a few points, we 
give in this section an overview of peculiarities in calligraphy and style 
that can be particularly helpful in dating a manuscript (which I will 
discuss here first, in 4.2) and in localising it (4.3). 
4.2  The characteristics found in inscriptions, for instance copperplate inscriptions (from the 11th) and (rare) early manuscripts till the 
15th century ce are as follows.23
i. 
absence of daṇḍa;
ii. 
absence of kākapada mark (as insertion marker);
iii. absence of other punctuation marks;
iv. 
absence of avagraha mark;
v. 
absence of halantyam;
vi. 
conjunct consonants: one below the other, both in full form;
vii. for diphthongs ai and au, two e mātrās are placed one above the 
other 
; 
viii. archaic forms of grantha is found;
ix. 
only syllabic numerals are used.
The characteristics of manuscripts of the 16th-18th century ce: 
i. 
single daṇḍa, sometimes we find it very small in size;
ii. 
halantyam (consonant without vowel) is shown by placing the 
full characters ma, ta, na, ka, ṭa very small in size at the end;
iii. conjuncts: one below other for specific characters like ppa, cca, 
ccha, lla, ṭṭa, jja, tta; also side by side for combinations such as 
gga, jja, jjha, lla, tta, ṭṭa, both characters appearing in a full form;
iv. 
rare use of the avagraha mark:

4.3  With regard to the determination of the region of origin of a mss. 
or its scribe, I can give here a few simple small hints. 
a.  Sanskrit texts written mainly in grantha characters but mixed 
with tamil, malayalam, tulu, telugu and kannada characters wherever 
possible (on account of phonetic identity or similarity), especially in 
earlier times, show the influence of the scribe’s mother script on his 
writing.
b.  So-called writing mistakes (in the ms where the language is 
Sanskrit and script is grantha or other) often betray properties of the 
scribe’s first language and script: for instance, kaṅkātareṇa for gaṅ
gādhareṇa, patma for padma. Bilingual and biscriptual readers may 
24  More details in Rath (forthcoming, a). 
25  More details in Rath (forthcoming, a).

--- Page 220 of 338 ---
saraju rath
204
automatically correct such reading and understand the sentence 
appropriately, even without experiencing a mistake in writing. While 
working on a manuscript, one therefore needs to have a good knowledge of local languages and scripts and their linguistic representation 
in a particular region.
c.  The principle of pagination style fluctuates in every region. For 
example, the folio-number stands left-side on the front page (recto) of 
each leaf in the dravidian regions26; in other parts of India on the back 
side (verso) known as sāṅkapṛṣṭhā (in eastern region on the right-side 
top, in the north-western region on the left-side middle or top);
d.  South Indian scribes often start their manuscript with śrī and/or 
svasti, where scribes of other parts of India start with om (and just as 
siddham/svasti is used in the beginning of inscriptions).
5. Conclusion
In the southern states of premodern South India, grantha was the specialised script for writing Sanskrit (and Vedic) texts, including royal 
records and documents. Grantha shows regional differences parallel 
with the distinct nature of the other scripts (telugu, etc.) with which it 
was standing in a biscriptual relation; moreover, it shows a number of 
relatively clearly identifiable stages of development, from the first half 
of the first millennium ce till early modern times. In the preceding 
paragraphs we have focused on those among the multifaceted changes 
in style and calligraphy of the characters of the different varieties of 
the script, which may serve to determine the relative date of a grantha 
manuscript, and which have mostly not been analyzed in available 
studies and handbooks. A few major parameters have been reviewed 
in this paper which together can help to narrow down the age of a 
manuscript within a few centuries. If a Saṁvatsara date is given, the 
determination can become more precise and point to a specific year 
(within a 60-year cycle). 
26  See, for instance, Rath 2005: 55.

CHAPTER ELEVEN
From my Grandfather’s Chest 
of Palm Leaf Books
Sreeramula Rajeswara Sarma
0.1 When manuscripts began to be systematically collected in the 
three presidencies of British India in the late eighteenth and nineteenth centuries, the majority of manuscripts so collected were from 
the individual collections of priestly or other Brahmin households. In 
order to understand the manner of production and distribution of 
manuscripts in pre-modern South India, it would be useful to know 
about the nature of such individual collections. 
In this paper, I shall discuss a set of palm leaf manuscripts which I 
saved from my grandfather’s chest of palm leaf books, dwell on the 
manner how they were preserved, speculate how they may have been 
collected and narrate the family tradition regarding these palm leaf 
books. 
0.2  The nature of the subject makes it necessary that I say a few words 
about my family. It was a family of śrotriya or vaidikī Brahmins, who 
followed the Āpastamba Sūtra of the Taittirīya Śākhā of the KṛṣṇaYajurveda. According to a legend perpetuated in the Kanyakāpurāṇa,1 
an ancestor named Bhāskarācārya provided leadership to a community of Vaiśyas in their conflict with the local king several centuries 
ago. Since then the members of the family became the hereditary 
gurus of this community of Vaiśyas and held the title Bhāskarācārya. 
Then about twelve generations ago, an ancestor performed a Rāmayajña and dedicated himself and his future descendants to Śrīrāma. 
Through this dedication, he replaced the then current surname or 
1  Printed often; see also V. Narayana Rao, “Purāṇa as Brahminic Ideology” in: 
Wendy Doniger (ed), Purana Perennis: Reciprocity and Transformation in Hindu 
and Jaina Texts, SUNY Press, New York, 1994, pp. 85-110.

--- Page 224 of 338 ---
sreeramula rajeswara sarma
208
family name Toṭapalli by Śrīrāmula, which is genitive plural of 
Śrīrāma in Telugu, meaning “of / belonging to Śrīrāma.” 2 
The family avidly cultivated this twofold tradition. The first was 
that of Bhāskarācārya, the preceptor-ship of certain clans of Vaiśyas. 
As the Vaiśya clans gradually spread across south India, the Bhās
karācārya travelled all over his “territory” to regularly visit his followers and offer spiritual counsel. In the family parlance, this travel is 
termed saṃcāram. The second tradition was the annual performance 
of the Rāma-yajña, which included the recitation of the complete 
Ārṣa-Rāmāyaṇa, i.e. the Sanskrit Rāmāyaṇa by Vālmīkin. Since this 
was an expensive affair, the Bhāskarācārya utilized his extensive travels to collect contributions from his Vaiśya disciples. 
I often thought that the family must have had highly interesting 
archives, containing the itineraries of the various Bhāskarācāryas, 
documents about the method of performing the Rāma-yajña, and of 
course several manuscript copies of the Kanyakāpurāṇa and of the 
Rāmāyaṇa. Unfortunately, none of these survives save a single copy 
book in which my grandfather recorded the subscriptions he collected 
and the expenses he incurred for what I presume to be his last Rāmayajña. All other documents were lost because the family left the ancestral village and moved to another village. 
A year before I was born in 1937, my father abandoned the ancestral house in the village Achchammapeta (16° 38´ N; 80° 7´ E) in 
Guntur District in what is today the state of Andhra Pradesh and 
moved to south-east along with his mother and wife to a much smaller 
village called Ulichi (15° 33´ N; 80° 9´ E)—situated in the same district, but on the cyclone-prone coast of the Bay of Bengal—to look 
after a childless old couple who were closely related to my grandmother. With this move my father gave up his paternal inheritance 
and gained the inheritance of his mother’s family, or—to speak from 
the perspective of this workshop—he lost one set of palm leaf manuscripts to acquire another. This second set is the subject of this paper. 
In this new village, my father succeeded his grand uncle as purohita; he had half a share of paurohitya in this village. At some point in 
2  Telugu family names (iṃṭiperu, lit. “house name”) are generally derived from 
the village from where the family originated. In Telugu culture, the family name 
constitutes the first element of one’s name. Thus the first initial “S” in my name 
expands to Sreeramula. Today, however, many Telugu people, as they migrate to 
other parts of the world, are placing the family names after the given name, in order 
to conform to the general practice in the US and Europe.

--- Page 225 of 338 ---
grandfather’s palm leaf books
209
the past, the purohitas were given agricultural land for performing 
this essential task. They did not cultivate the land themselves but 
leased it to farmers who paid rent in kind or cash. Besides this modest 
income, the purohita received, whenever he performed a domestic 
ritual in a client’s house, some remuneration in kind: a measure of 
rice, a handful of pulses, some dried coconut, a vegetable or two, a few 
leaves of tāmbūla, two or three betel nuts—barely enough for one 
square meal— and some copper coins. 
My mother used to store some of the non-perishable items so 
received in a large wooden chest which went by the designation of the 
“grandfather’s chest of palm leaf books” (tāta-gāri tāṭāku-pustakālabhoṣāṇam). In my childhood, whenever my mother opened this chest, 
I would try to peep into the chest to see and smell the diverse kinds of 
objects stored there: dried coconut halves, turmeric roots, betel nuts, 
silver trinkets, packets of incense sticks, tablets of camphor, piles of 
old letters, rolls of paper documents, and bundles of palm leaf books 
wrapped in cloth. Sometimes, to amuse me, my father would take out 
a palm leaf book, undo the rope, and show me how the letters were 
incised on the surface of the leaves. Once, as a special treat, he showed 
me a secret drawer in the chest which contained some old coins and 
rolls of palm leaves. Many decades later, when we had to empty the 
house and sell it, I retrieved some palm leaf books and rolls from the 
grandfather’s chest. My mother gave away the empty chest to the temple of Veṇugopāla; now it has the privilege of storing—no more palm 
leaf books, but— God’s festival clothes (Fig. 11.1). 
Figure 11.1.  Grandfather’s Chest is being carried to the temple of Veṇu-gopāla, 
which is inside the walls with the vertical stripes to the left. See colour section, 
Plate XVI.

--- Page 226 of 338 ---
sreeramula rajeswara sarma
210
The palm leaf manuscripts that I saved from the grandfather’s chest 
(Fig. 11.2) have been with me for some time, but they were not of my 
immediate academic interest (which is served mainly by north Indian 
paper manuscripts and museum artifacts). Saraju Rath’s proposal of 
the workshop gave me the opportunity to spend some hours with the 
family manuscripts (and to indulge in excursions into the past). 
Figure 11.2.  Palm leaf manuscripts and rolls retrieved from the grandfather’s chest. 
See colour section, Plate XVII.
1.1  Material 
All the manuscripts are incised on the processed leaves of the Palmyra 
palm (Borassus flabellifer Linn., Sanskrit: tāla / khara-tāla) which 
grows abundantly in our area on the sea coast. The manuscripts have 
very neatly bored holes; I do not know how this was achieved. In one 
manuscript, the corners of the leaves are rounded very nicely; I do not 
know how this was done either. The covers of the manuscripts were 
made up of the leaf stalks (petiole) of what appear to be larger Talipot 
palms (Corypha umbraculifera; Sanskrit, śrītāla); these leaf stalks were 
cut to the exact size, smoothened and varnished. Some covers were 
made of bamboo; some others of wooden planks; in some manuscripts, the palm leaves themselves were made to serve as the covers. 
On these leaves, the letters were incised with an iron stylus (Telugu, 
gaṃṭamu from Sanskrit, loha-kaṇṭaka), as is the custom in South

--- Page 227 of 338 ---
grandfather’s palm leaf books
211
India and all along the eastern coast.3 The manuscripts appear to have 
been used frequently. In one manuscript, a slightly broken edge of a 
leaf was carefully repaired by stitching it with thread. 
1.2  Age of the Manuscripts
The manuscripts are generally dated in the years of the southern style 
Jovian cycle. Since these repeat every 60 years, it is difficult to determine 
the age of a manuscript. Luckily, in one manuscript the Jovian year is 
accompanied by its equivalents in Kali and Śaka eras (Śubhakṛt / Kali 
4943 / Śaka 1764) so that the year can be fixed at ad 1842-43. In another 
case, the date is given in the Christian era also as 23 September 1856. 
I am inclined to think that the remaining documents also belong to this 
period, namely the middle of the nineteenth century of the Christian 
era. Although paper was available at this period—the chest contained 
many government documents with Queen Victoria’s seal embossed 
on them—it had to be purchased against ready cash whereas palm leaf 
was free and plenty. There must have been people in every village who 
processed the palm leaf and made it ready for writing. I cannot say 
whether there were special classes of people whose profession it was to 
process the leaves or whether every literate person (i.e. every Brahmin) 
prepared his own writing material. Perhaps both cases were true. For 
making the covers one took naturally the village carpenter’s help. This 
state of affairs continued up to the beginning of the twentieth century; 
in the first decade my father still used the palm leaf and stylus for his 
first writing lessons. 
1.3  Orthography
These manuscripts are predominantly of Sanskrit texts but written in 
Telugu characters. The Telugu alphabet is capable of reproducing all 
the Sanskrit sounds. Yet there are certain peculiarities in the transcription of Sanskrit words. Since these peculiarities are common to 
all the manuscripts in this collection, it appears that all these manuscripts (with one exception, see 2.4 below) were produced broadly in 
the same geographical area and in the same period. These peculiarities 
are as follows:
3  On the materials for writing, see, inter alia, Sreeramula Rajeswara Sarma, 
“Writing Material in Ancient India,” Aligarh Journal of Oriental Studies, 2 (1985), 
pp. 175-196.

--- Page 228 of 338 ---
sreeramula rajeswara sarma
212
1.  Telugu has long and short e and o, while Sanskrit has only long e 
and o. However, the Telugu scribes rarely pay attention to long e and 
o in Sanskrit, transcribing them often as short. Thus devānām becomes 
dĕvānām. But in actual pronunciation, Telugu Brahmins never 
shorten the Sanskrit e and o. 
2.  But they do indulge in what is known as ya-śruti; i.e. insertion of 
ya in front of vowels i, e, and of v in front of u, o. For example, eka 
becomes yeka; idaṃ becomes yidaṃ and so on. 
3.  As in Middle Indic, the anusvāra is used consistently in the place 
of parasavarṇa, e.g. anta > aṃta, indu > iṃdu, indra > iṃdra. 
4.  But more interestingly, anusvāra is inserted before a nasal consonant when it is in conjunction with another consonant. Examples: 
brahmā > braṃhma; anna > aṃnna; puṇya < puṃnya; yajña 
< yaṃjña.4
5.  The consonant following an anusvāra is reduplicated, e.g. 
santati > saṃtati > saṃttati
 
śṛṅgāra > śṛṃgāra > śṛṃggāra
 
vande > vaṃde > vaṃdde 
So much so, the verse from the Puruṣa-sūkta is transcribed as follows
 
బ్రాం
హ్మణొస్య ముఖమాసీత్ ‍ 
బాహూ రాజంంన్యః కృతః 
వూరౄ తదస్య యద్వైశ్యః 
పద్భ్యాం
గ్ శూద్రొ అజాయత 
 
brāṃhmaṇŏsya mukham āsīt / 
bāhū rājaṃnyaḥ kṛtaḥ /
vūrū tad asya yad vaiśyaḥ / 
padbhyāṃg śūdrŏ ajāyata //
2.0  Description of the Manuscripts
The manuscripts can be divided into three groups: Veda, ritual and 
kāvya. There are three Vedic manuscripts, belonging to the Taittirīya 
branch of the black Yajurveda. Two of the largest manuscripts in the 
4  A similar feature I noticed in Punjabi, or at least in a Punjabi astrolabe where 
an anusvāra is inserted in kanyā (kaṁnyā) and vahni (vaṁnhi).

--- Page 229 of 338 ---
grandfather’s palm leaf books
213
collection contain respectively the second and third aṣṭakas of the 
Taittirīya Brāhmaṇa. It is likely that the first aṣṭaka was also there 
once. 
2.1 The manuscript of the second aṣṭaka contains all the eight pra
pāṭhakas.5 Each prapāṭhaka begins on a new page with an auspicious 
formula like hariḥ oṃ or hariḥ oṃ śrīgurubhyo namaḥ, but at the conclusion, the serial number of the prapāṭhaka is not given, nor is there 
a mention anywhere of the Taittirīya Brāhmaṇa. The final colophon, 
however, states reṃḍḍo āṭha parāyitaṃ. This expression will be discussed below. Fortunately there is the scribe’s colophon at the end 
which states as follows:
Vikāri-nāma-saṃvvatsara-bhādra-bahuḷa-dvādaśi-lu Telidevulapalli 
Tātaṃ-bhoṭla-gāri parāyitaṃ postakaṃ. Dattaṃbhoṭlu vrāsina posta
kaṃ. Dattātreya namaḥ. 
“In the Jovian year named Vikārin, in the dark half of the month 
Bhādrapada, on the 12th day [is completed] the book (postakam) of 
the Parāyitam belonging to Telidevulapalli Tātaṃbhoṭlu. The book is 
written by Dattaṃbhoṭlu. Homage to Dattātreya.” Vikārin is the 33rd 
year in the Jovian cycle and can correspond to 1779-80, or 1839-40, or 
1899-1900. Perhaps 1839-40 would be the right year. 
The scribe Dattaṃbhoṭlu is not the owner of the manuscript; the 
owner is mentioned as Telidevulapalli Tātaṃbhoṭlu followed by an 
honorific gāru. The scribe does not mention his family name probably 
because it is the same as that of the owner. It is possible that Dattaṃ
bhoṭlu is a younger member of the family of Tātaṃbhoṭlu and therefore refers to the latter with the honorific gāru. It may be noted that 
both these names, and many others which will occur in the course of 
this narrative, end in the suffix bhoṭlu, which is derived from Sanskrit 
bhaṭṭa to which a Telugu plural particle is added honoris causa. It is 
therefore certain that Dattaṃbhoṭlu is not a professional scribe from a 
lower caste but a Brahmin of the same family.
2.2  The manuscript of the third aṣṭaka6 was copied by another person, for the writing is much superior to that of the previous manu5  Length 39.5 cm, breadth 3 cm, thickness 5 cm, thickness with covers 7.8 cm; 
covers made of palm leaf stalks.
6  Length 43.5 cm, breadth 3 cm, thickness 5.5 cm, thickness with covers 8 cm. 
Covers are made of palm leaf stalks. There is no pagination; the string got broken
