sreeramula rajeswara sarma
214
script. However, there is no colophon stating who copied it and when. 
On the penultimate leaf is written in a different hand 
Pullaṃbhoṭla postakam / mūḍo aṭhaṃ / panneṃḍu ppaṃnnālu 
saṃppūrṇaṃ //
“Book of Pullaṃbhoṭlu. Third aṭha. Twelve pannās [are] complete.” 
On the final leaf is written, again in another hand, within ornate 
borders,
Yimaṇilli Pullaṃbhoṭla mūḍo aṭha parāyitaṃ saṃppūrṇam. 
“Of Yimaṇilli Pullaṃbhoṭlu , third aṭha parāyitam [is] complete.” 
So clearly this book belongs to Pullaṃbhoṭlu with the family name 
Yimaṇilli. But more important are the expressions reṃḍo aṭha parāya
taṃ in the manuscript of the second aṣṭaka and mūḍo aṭha parāyataṃ 
in the manuscript of the third aṣṭaka. In Telugu reṃḍo means “the 
second” and mūḍo “the third”. Thus the second aṣṭaka of the Taittirīya 
Brāhmaṇa is designated as the “second aṭha parāyitam” and the third 
aṣṭaka as the “third aṭha parāyitaṃ.” Therefore, it is reasonable to 
assume that in the Telugu priestly circles the expression aṭha refers to 
aṣṭaka; there is at least the superficial resemblance between the two 
expressions aṣṭaka and aṭha. Does parāyitaṃ then refer to the 
Taittirīya Brāhmaṇa? 
There is yet a third expression panna/ā. The third prapāṭhaka of the 
second aṣṭaka is described as the third (mūḍo) pannā and the eighth 
prapāṭhaka as the eighth pannā. Again at the conclusion of the third 
aṣṭaka, it is said that twelve pannās are complete. So panna/ā appears 
to be a local term for prapāṭhaka or a sub-section. 
 
2.3  The third manuscript in this collection pertains also to the Tait
tirīya branch.7 It contains the prapāṭhakas 3, 5 and 6 of the Taittirīya 
Āraṇyaka. These three prapāṭhakas are copied on two sets of leaves 
numbered separately from 1 to 33 and again from 1 to 31, with colophons at the end of each set. The third prapāṭhaka which deals with 
the Brahmamedha and includes the famous Puruṣasūkta is not identified in the manuscript as such. It is followed by the fifth prapāṭhaka 
which constitutes the Taittirīyopaniṣad. The three subsections of this 
and the leaves got disorganized. Apparently it was used frequently; the broken edge 
of a leaf was joined by stitching. The script is very clear and easy to decipher. 
7  Length 26.7 cm, breadth 2.8 cm, thickness 3.3 cm. There are no separate covers, 
the outer leaves being treated as the covers.

--- Page 231 of 338 ---
grandfather’s palm leaf books
215
Upaniṣad, called vallīs, are clearly separated with the śānti-pāṭha 
occurring at the beginning and the end of each vallī, but the vallīs are 
not named, nor was the Upaniṣad itself. Within each vallī, the 
anuvākas are not distinguished. This is followed by the sixth 
prapāṭhaka which constitutes the Nārāyaṇopaniṣad, also known as 
the Mahānārāyaṇopaniṣad, or Yājñikopaniṣad. This is identified in 
the margin as Nārāyaṇam. 
I mentioned that the leaves are numbered as two separate sets. At 
the conclusion of the first set, which contains the text of the 
prapāṭhakas 3 and 5, there is a colophon by the scribe which reads 
thus: Iṃdra-kaṃṭhaṃ Raghupatinā yidaṃ likhitaṃ “This was written 
by Indra-kaṃṭhaṃ Raghupati.”
In the second and final colophon, the edge of the leaf is broken 
where the scribe’s name occurs; but it must be the same scribe Indra
kaṃṭhaṃ Raghupati. This colophon reads as follows:
śubhakṛt nāma saṃvvatsara kārtika śu 11 śukravāraṃnnāḍu —
upaniṣattulu 5 paṃnnālu svahastaṃgā vrāsi yītamukkalalo 
telidevulapalli vumāmaheśvaruḍiki dānapūrvaṃgā yi[ccinadi]/ 
umāmaheśvarārpaṇam astū/
“In the year named Śubhakṛt, in the bright half of Kārttika, on 
the 11th day which is a Friday, having written with his own hand 
the five paṃnnās of Upaniṣads, given as dāna to Telidevulapalli 
Umāmaheśvaruḍu in the village Ītamukkala. May this be an 
offering to Maheśvara [accompanied by] Umā.” 
This colophon is interesting for several reasons. First “Telidevulapalli” 
is the family name of my grandmother’s father (see 4.0 below). The 
recipient of the dāna, Telidevulappalli Umāmaheśvara must be a 
member of this family and it explains the presence of this manuscript 
in the “grandfather’s chest.”
The year Śubhakṛt, the 36th year of the Jovian cycle, corresponds to 
ad 1782-3 , 1842-43, or 1902-3. If we accept the middle one, kārtika 
śukla 11 translates to Friday 3 November 1843 when the dāna was 
performed.8 
The scribe describes the contents of the manuscript as upaniṣattulu 
5 paṃnnālu, lit. “five pannās of Upaniṣads”. The manuscript contains 
the third, fifth, and sixth prapāṭhakas of the Taittirīya Āraṇyaka. 
8  For the conversion of dates here and in the following pages, I used the pancanga programme designed by Yano and Fushimi, http://www.cc.kyoto-su.ac.
jp/~yanom/pancanga/index.html

--- Page 232 of 338 ---
sreeramula rajeswara sarma
216
According to tradition the fifth prapāṭhaka constitutes the Taittirīyo
paniṣad and the sixth the Nārāyaṇopaniṣad. But in the scribe’s view, 
the third prapāṭhaka which deals with the Brahmamedha and includes 
the famous Puruṣasūkta is also an Upaniṣad. 
Now we come to the expression panna/ā, which we encountered 
earlier in the sense of prapāṭhaka. The present manuscript contains 
only three prapāṭhakas. How do they become five pannas? I believe 
that the scribe counted the third prapāṭhaka as 1 panna, the three 
vallīs in the fifth prapāṭhaka as 3 pannas, and the sixth prapāṭhaka 
again 1 panna; together these make (1+3+1=) 5 pannas. Charles Philip 
Brown’s A Telugu-English Dictionary, which is available online, gives 
the meanings of pannam in English and Telugu.9 Here the English 
meaning “One Verse in a chapter of the Vedas” is quite off the mark; 
but the meaning given in Telugu is better: vedamulo aṣṭakamuloni 
bhāgamu, “a part of an aṣṭaka in the Vedas”. As I think back to my 
childhood, I remember to have heard of Brahmins who knew so many 
pannas of Veda by heart. Panna then must be a unit of recitation or 
memorization, that is as much as is denoted by the expression 
prapāṭhaka. Brown’s dictionary, however, does not know the expressions aṭha and parāyatam; probably all the words had a limited currency in śrotriya circles. 
But the most important item of the colophon is the purpose for 
which the manuscript was copied, namely for giving it away as dāna. 
Sanskrit texts on Dharmaśāstra, such as the Caturvargacintāmaṇi, 
speak of vidyādāna which consists of copying a manuscript, or getting 
it copied, and then giving it away as dāna to a learned person. Among 
the Jainas, this type of dāna is given a high importance; there are large 
numbers of manuscripts which were caused to be made for this purpose.10 But among the Brahmanic manuscripts, the present one must 
be one of the few which were copied for the sake of dāna. 
9  “pannamu (p. 0712) [ pannamu ] pannamu. [Tel.] n. One verse in a chapter of 
the Vedas. vedamulo astakamuloni bhagamu.”
10  Cf. Hampa Nagarajaih, “The Concept of Shastra-dana in Jainism,” Jain Journal, 33.3 (Jan 1999), pp. 81-85. The colophons of Jaina manuscripts often mention 
that the manuscripts were copied for the purpose of vidyādāna or śāstradāna. For a 
collection of such colophons, see Jaina Pustaka Praśasti Saṁgraha (A Collection of 
Praśastis and Colophons of Ancient Manuscripts preserved in the Jain Bhandars at 
Patan, Cambay, Jaisalmer, and other Places), first part, ed. Jina Vijaya Muni, Bharatitya Vidya Bhavan, Bombay, 1943 (Singhi Jain Series, No. 18). See also 
Jainagrantha-Praśasti-Saṁgraha, part 1, ed. Jugal Kishor Mukhtar, Vira Seva Man-

--- Page 233 of 338 ---
grandfather’s palm leaf books
217
Finally, the texts copied on these manuscripts are generally those 
which the members of the family were expected to know by heart. The 
manuscripts merely served as aids while memorizing the text. Since 
the Vedic accent is learnt directly from the teacher and not from the 
books, the Vedic manuscripts here do not carry accent marks. The 
Vedic anusvāra, however, is denoted by a special symbol. The manuscripts avoid the other frills like the colophons identifying the text, 
author, the chapter number and the like, probably because these are 
never recited in the ritual.
2.4  The next group consists of texts which are connected with the 
profession of the purohita, namely texts on Gṛhya ritual, vratas, pūjās, 
and stotras. Here each manuscript contains several texts, not always 
clearly separated from one another. There are rarely any end colophons11; sometimes the individual texts are identified in the margin. 
The fourth manuscript in this connection deals mainly with the 
Gṛhya and Purāṇic ritual.12 Here the various sections are identified in 
the margin with their titles such as: Cūrṇike, Daśāvatārāla arghyālu, 
Sthālīpāka, Dahanāgnividhiḥ, Vṛṣotsargaḥ, Dūrvāṣṭamī, Śrāvaṇadvādaśī-nirṇayaḥ, Anantavrata-nirṇayaḥ, Narakacaturdaśī, Dīpāvalī, 
Skanda-ṣaṣṭiḥ, Puṇyāha-lakṣaṇam, Agni-praṇayanam, Yajñapuruṣalakṣaṇam, Tāṃbūlacarvaṇānumatiḥ, Vivāhamadhye kanyakā rajas
valā. 
Among these, the first two items are particularly interesting. The 
first one, entitled cūrṇike is a long ornate passage in Sanskrit which 
the householder is to recite when he gives away his daughter in marriage. It begins with 
svasty aṣṭāsītisahasrāṇām dvijagurudevatyānāṃ /
pākaśāsana-hutāśana-daṇḍadhara-niṛruti-varuṇa-samīraṇavaiśravaṇa-śaśiśekharāṇāṃm āyuṣataḥ kamalāsanasya /
dir, Delhi, 1954; part II: Apabhraṁśa Jainagrantha Praśasti-Saṁgraha, ed. Paramanand Jain Shastri, Vira Seva Mandir Society, Delhi, 1963. 
11  Colophons also occur in the middle of the text, such as, at the conclusion of a 
section or at the conclusion of a chapter, besides at the end of the book or manuscripts.

Here we seem to have a relic of an earlier practice where the bride’s

This is followed by what are stated to be offerings (arghya) for the 
ten incarnations of Viṣṇu (daśāvatārāla arghyālu). In this interesting 
version of the Daśāvatāra-pūjā (for the full text, see Appendix I), the 
lunar tithi and the time of the day of the manifestation of each avatāra 
is mentioned. The incarnations are praised accordingly in association 
with the relevant tithi. Thus for instance the fish incarnation is extolled 
in the following words:

gṛhāṇārghyaṃ mayā dattaṃ mahālakṣmyā hare saha //
There is no colophon, nor a date in the manuscript. On the wooden 
cover is incised Śanagavarapu Koṃḍḍayya vrāsukoṃnna … “written 
by Śanagavarapu Koṃḍayya for his own sake ...” The orthography is 
rather peculiar: ka is written as in Kannada script. The first text is 
termed cūrṇike, which is a Kannada form of Sanskrit cūrṇikā. These 
two facts suggest that this manuscript may have been copied in or 
near Karṇāṭaka by Śanagavarapu Koṃḍayya.

2.5  The contents of the next manuscript13 are also of similar nature; 
these are Varalakṣmīvratavidhāna, Navagrahapūjā, Pāṇigrahaṇasaṃskāra-vidhāna and so on. In the Varalakṣmīvratavidhāna, the 
vrata is in Sanskrit and the kathā in Telugu. The rest of the manuscript is in Sanskrit.
2.6  The sixth manuscript of the collection is rather small in size.14 The 
leaves have not only very neatly bored holes, but also round corners. 
The well polished covers are made of some soft timber. However, the 
writing is rather indifferent; there are several hands and several texts, 
not clearly differentiated. These are mainly prayers addressed to different deities, sometimes these are named as kavaca-s in the margin. 
Some of the titles are Tripurasuṃdarī-kavaca, Hanumaṃta-kavaca, 
Subrahmaṇya-kavaca. There are also some ślokas in praise of Venka
ṭeśvara with the refrain veṃkaṭeśaṃ bhaje, some on the river Kāverī 
and yet others on the bridge (setu) to Laṅkā built by Rāma. 
2.7  Then there is a completely Telugu text, albeit with a large sprinkling of Sanskrit terms.15 It is a śatakam with the refrain rāmaprabho. 
There are just 11 leaves, containing some 191 verses; then the manuscript breaks off. Here the first verse reads thus: 
pāhi ramaprabho pāhi rāmaprabho 
pāhi bhadrādri16-vaidehi-rāmaprabhabho /
pāhi māṃ pādapadme hitaṃ mohitaṃ 
dehi bhadrādri-vaidehi-rāmaprabhabho //1//
2.8  The texts described so far pertain to the repertoire of a purohita; 
so do the pañcāṅgas. These are elaborate astronomical tables providing for each day of the year the five aṅgas, namely tithi, vāra, nakṣatra, 
yoga and karaṇa. These pañcāṅgas were used by the purohitas while 
fixing the auspicious moments for performing the various rituals, and 
also for various types of astrological forecasts. There were many 
pañcāṅgas in the grandfather’s chest, but one deserves special mention.

Figure 11.3.  Manuscript of the Varṣaphala. See colour section, Plate XIX.
It is a neatly written manuscript with some marginal decorations (Fig. 
11.3).17 It was compiled for Śubhakṛt, which is the 36th year in Jovian 
cycle; the compiler also records the year in other eras, among others, 
Kali 4943 and Śālivāhana Śaka 1764. With these we can identify the 
year as the one beginning on Monday 11 April 1842 and ending on 
Thursday 30 March 1843. The author is Purāṇam Liṃganna, son of 
Liṃgayya, of Kaśyapa-gotra, and resident of Kalvakūru, which is 80 
km from my village Ulichi. Although it is described as Śubhakṛtnāma-saṃvatsara-pañcāṅgamu, it is not the pañcāṅga proper, but 
varṣaphala, lit. “fruit of the year,” i.e. a forecast of the possibilities for 
the coming year, such as the rain fall, crop yield, well-being or otherwise of different castes, prices of various items and so on (see Appendix 
II for extracts). The possibilities are given in terms of certain number 
of parts, perhaps in a scale of 20 because the parts vary between 4 to 
20. Thus it is stated that the rainfall will be 11 parts, crop yield 19 
parts, happiness 13 parts, sorrow 9 parts, truth 13 parts, untruth also 
13 parts, increase in locusts 8 parts, elimination of locusts 7 parts, …. 
increase in the price of emeralds 4 parts, loss 5 parts, … increase in the 
yield of coconuts 17 parts, of bananas 11 parts, of mangoes 17 parts, 
… etc. etc. 
17  Length 41.2cm, breadth 3.3 cm in the middle, at the ends to 2.7; 6 folia.

grandfather’s palm leaf books

It is customary that on the New Year’s Day (ugādi in Telugu from 
Sanskrit yugādi), which is observed on the first day of the bright half 
of the lunar month Caitra by the Telugus, the purohita reads out this 
varṣaphala to the assembled villagers in the local temple. The text concludes with a phalaśruti: the reward for hearing to the varṣaphala: 
kanyāvanikāñcanadiggajānāṃ gavāṃ sahasraṃ satataṃ 
dvijebhyaḥ /
datvā phalaṃ yal labhate manuṣyaḥ tattatphalāny abdaphalaṃ 
śṛṇotu//
“Whatever merit a man obtains by gifting constantly a thousand 
maidens, [pieces of] land, gold, elephants and cows to the Brahmins, the same merits [he would obtain by hearing] the 
varṣaphala. [Therefore] may you hear [it].” 
2.9  The manuscripts so far pertain to the requisites of śrotriya 
Brahmins who also act as purohitas, dealing as they do with the 
Yajurveda, Gṛhya and other rituals, vratas, pūjās, and stotras. One 
whose intellectual range does not go beyond these is called derisively 
chāndasa. I am pleased that the grandfather’s palm leaf book collection 
did not just cater to the chāndasa demands; it had some kāvya too. 
Actually the best preserved and oft-used manuscript deals with 
kāvya.18 This manuscript contains three separate texts. The first, in 39 
folia, is the second adhyāya of the Kṛṣṇakarṇāmṛta, with a Telugu ṭīkā 
with extracts from the commentary Svarṇacaṣaka by Pasumarti 
Pāpayya Sūri. In contradistinction to the manuscripts which we have 
discussed so far, there is a proper colophon at the end identifying the 
text, the commentary and the adhyāya. The colophon informs further 
that the manuscript was copied by Pālaparti Kṛṣṇamma up to 
Vaiśākha śu 14 in the year Vyaya,19 which can be either Saturday 20 
May 1826, or Monday 17 May 1886. I opt for the former for reasons to 
be explained later (in 4.0 below).

The third text (in 21 leaves) is the first sarga of the Rāmāyaṇa, with 
a Telugu ṭīkā. This text is also accompanied by a regular colophon, 
identifying the text, the author and the section; it also informs that the 
manuscript was copied by Pālaparti Kṛṣṇamma up to Phālguṇa śu 10 
in the year Khara 20 (= Monday 12 March 1832).21 
As mentioned earlier, the two Sanskrit texts, viz. the second 
adhyāya of the Kṛṣṇakarṇāmṛta and the first sarga of the Rāmāyaṇa 
are accompanied by a ṭīkā which provides word by word meaning in 
Telugu. Stylistically the two ṭīkās are identical and must have been 
composed by the same person, probably by the scribe Pālaparti 
Kṛṣṇamma himself. I am not in a position to answer why Kṛṣṇamma 
composed the ṭīkā only on the second adhyāya of the Kṛṣṇakarṇāmṛta 
and only on the first sarga of the Rāmāyaṇa, nor can I say when this 
practice of writing ṭīkās in Telugu on Sanskrit texts commenced. 
However, the kind of Telugu used in the ṭīkās is interesting: here the 
retroflex dental ṭa is replaced at certain places by the retroflex sibilant

3.0.  Palm Leaf Rolls
Now I come to the palm leaf rolls which were stored in the secret 
drawer of the grandfather’s chest. Here I could retrieve six rolls which 
were fully intact. These are single processed palm leaves, which were 
inscribed and then rolled up into small rolls of about three to four cm 
diameter. Three of these rolls are enclosed inside slightly larger rings 
of blank palm leaves which function like envelopes. In these envelopes 
incisions were made at two places and the incised portions were 
pushed into one another so that the leaf does not roll back (Fig. 11.4). 
Figure 11.4.  Inscribed palm leaf roll and the protective envelope. See colour section, 
Plate XX.
22  For example, this is how the ṭīkā on Kṛṣṇakarṇāmṛta 2.1 runs: abhinavana
vanītasnigdhaṁ āpītadugdhaṁ —abhinava / nūtanamainaṣuvaṁṭṭi / navanīta / 
venna cātanu / snigdhaṁ / svacchamainaṣuvaṁṭṭi / dugdhaṁ / pālugalinaṣuvaṁṭ- 
ṭi/ ….

--- Page 240 of 338 ---
sreeramula rajeswara sarma
224
In his South Indian Palaeography, A. C. Burnell makes a brief mention 
of letters written on single palm leaves which were then rolled up and 
tied with a string, but he does not mention legal documents incised on 
single leaves and then preserved inside envelope rolls.23 Nor am I 
aware of any other description of such documents in South India written after Burnell’s time.24 
Therefore, I was eager to know the content of the rolls, but was 
faced with technical problem of unrolling these leaves without damaging them. I filled a bowl with hot water and dropped one palm leaf 
roll into the hot water. It uncoiled itself instantly and also got cleansed 
in the process. Then I dried the leaf between two sheets of newspaper. 
Thus the technical problem of unrolling the palm leaf was solved easily enough, but the problem of deciphering the leaf was rather daunting. 
For, unlike the other palm leaf manuscripts, this one is a legal document and was written by a professional who is usually the village clerk 
(karaṇam) in what appears like a chain writing. While the ideal of 
Telugu script is that each letter should be distinct and separate like a 
pearl, here each letter was linked to the next one in a long chain. One 
has the impression that the scribe incised the entire line without once 
lifting the stylus from the surface of the leaf. I could not decipher 
much from the leaf. I soaked one more roll with the same result. 
23  A. C. Burnell, Elements of South-Indian Palaeography from the fourth to the 
seventh Century ad, being an Introduction to the Study of South-Indian Inscriptions 
and Mss, Trubner & Co., London 1978, 2nd edition “enlarged and improved” [1st 
edn, Mangalore 1874], pp. 89-90: “… in S. India and Ceylon … palm-leaves have 
always been used for this (= letter writing) purpose up to recent times. For this purpose a strip of palm-leaf is cut in the usual form, and smeared with turmeric or some 
similar colour for ornament. The ends are split a little way to secure the whole which 
is folded in a ring, and then fastened by a thread. The earliest complete description 
of such a letter that I know of is in the middle of the 16th century in De Battos’ 
‘Asia’; he says: ‘As outras coussas, que serve ao modo de nossas cartas mesiuas e 
escriptura comum, basta ser a folha escripta e enrolada em si e por chancella atase 
com qualquer linha, ou neruo da mesma palma.’”
24  In a paper entitled “Zwei nepalische Dokumente zur Schuldknechtschaft,” 
Zeitschrift der Deutschen Morgenlӓndischen Gesellschaft,136.2 (1986), pp. 434-449, 
Bernhard Kölver reported about similar palm leaf documents from Nepal. Here only 
a portion of the leaf containing the operative part of the document is rolled up and 
sealed with an official seal. These Nepal documents belong to the second half of the 
eleventh century. See also Naoko Takagi, Yorike Chudo & Reiko Maeda, “Conservation and Digitisation of Rolled Palm Leaf Manuscripts in Nepal,” Asian Arts: the 
on-line Journal for the Study and Exhibition of the Arts of Asia, November 14, 2005 
(www.asianart.com).

I uncoiled yet a third roll; here I could read some but not at all. What 
I gathered from the three rolls is the following.
3.1  Palm Leaf Roll 1
This is rather a small document, with a length of 22.8 cm and a width 
of 2.5 cm (see Fig. 11.5). There are 4 lines of writing on the recto side 
and one and half lines on the verso. I could decipher just a little at the 
beginning and at the end. The beginning states that this is a document 
issued in the year Vibhava on Śrāvaṇa śuddha 1. The document is 
addressed to Telidevulapalli—bhoṭlu, ditto25 Guvvaṃbhoṭlu, and 
ditto Pullaṃbhoṭlu. At the end of the document is the signature of the 
scribe: “This is the handwriting (dastūri) of Mantri Veṃkayya.” It is 
followed by the signature of the person on whose name the document 
is issued. The signature reads “Koṭappa vrālu”. In Telugu legal documents, it is customary to write “vrālu” after the signature. 
Figure 11.5a.  Palm leaf Roll 1, Recto. See colour section, Plate XVIIIa.
Figure 11.5b.  Palm Leaf Roll 1, Verso. See colour section, Plate XVIIIb.
3.2  Palm Leaf Roll 2
The second document is 43.0 cm long and 2.3 cm wide and carries 
3 lines of writing on each side. I could decipher a little more in this 
document, which was issued in the year Pramādin (1819-20 or 
1879-80) on Jyeṣṭha bahula 12. It was addressed to Telidevulapalli 
Rāmakṛṣṇamma, ditto Gurumūrti, ditto Piccayya. Probably these three 
are brothers and joint holders of some agricultural land. The document 
is issued by a farmer named Cuṃcu Koṭappa.26 He took from the three 
25  The Telugu expression is “ā”; I understand this to mean that Guvvaṁbhoṭlu 
also has the same family name “Telidevulapalli.” 
26  Several families still bear this name in my village. These are rich landholders,

--- Page 242 of 338 ---

persons mentioned above a piece of land on lease (kavulu); he owes 
(ruṇam) them the lease money which he cannot pay now; he promises 
to pay it by Mārgiśīrṣa ba 30 of the same year… [which is about 6 
months hence]. The document concludes with the signatures of the 
farmer, the scribe and the witnesses. 
3.3  Palm Leaf Roll 3
The third roll is much larger with a length of 33.2 cm and width of 2.4 
cm. There are 6 lines of writing on the recto and 5 lines on the verso. 
I was able to read much of this document if not the whole and feel 
that the document records a very important event in the history of the 
family. Fortunately it is dated both in the year of the Jovian cycle and 
in the Christian era. 
The document begins by stating that “This is an agreement (kharā
runāmā) caused to be written in the year Nala, on the 11th day of the 
dark half of Bhādrapada, that is, in 1856 September, on the date (tedī) 
23, by the purohitas of the Vulichi village Māmiḷḷapalli Buccaṃbhoṭlu, 
ditto Kanakaṃbhoṭlu, ditto Koṭappa.” Buccaṃbhoṭlu and Kanakaṃ
bhoṭlu are probably brothers. The third one apparently was Kanakaṃ
bhoṭlu’s son; at the end of the document, he signs on his father’s 
behalf. 
The agreement deals with the partition of the agricultural lands 
(mānyaṃ) which were endowed to the family by the village for performing the paurohitya. Therefore it is emphasised that the partition 
is done in accordance with the adjudication made by the village clerk 
(karaṇam) and the senior land-holders (pedda kāpulu). The fields are 
situated in three different locations, to the west of the village, to the 
east, and on the banks of a water reservoir. These are now divided 
between the two brothers, both sharing the liabilities and incomes. 
The document concludes by stating that “This is the agreement 
caused to be written with our approval.” Then follow the signature of 
Buccaṃbhoṭlu, signature of Koṭappa on behalf of his father Kanakaṃ
bhoṭlu; signature of the scribe (vulichi koṃḍalarāyuḍu dastūrī), and 
the signatures of four witnesses. 
I recall from my childhood that we had holdings in these three locations; and at each place there were two equal halves, one belonging to 
us and the other to our immediate neighbours who likewise shared 
the paurohitya of the village. Now I know that the partition was done 
on 23 September 1856.

--- Page 243 of 338 ---
grandfather’s palm leaf books
227
4.0  Production and Collection of Manuscripts
As shown above, these palm leaf manuscripts were produced by individual Brahmins for their own use or for the use of their family members. In one rare instance, the manuscript was produced for the 
purpose of dāna. 
It must be added that the nine manuscripts and six palm leaf rolls 
that I retrieved do not constitute the whole collection of the chest. As 
this chest was known in the family as “the chest of palm leaf books”, I 
assume that it contained mainly documents made of palm leaves; later 
paper documents were also added. In my childhood, there used to be 
many more manuscripts in the chest and the family tried to preserve 
these documents as best as they could, but several were damaged 
through the generally humid climate of the sea coast and the frequent 
cyclones. But the few that remain suggest the nature of the collection 
in this śrotriya family: primarily Sanskrit texts connected with the 
family’s Vedic śākhā and with the family’s profession of paurohitya, 
some poetry of the devotional kind, some Telugu texts of similar 
nature. 
The chest is referred to as “grandfather’s chest”. It was never specified who this grandfather was, nor did I ask this question until now. 
The personal names inscribed in the manuscripts point mainly to two 
families: Telidevulapalli27 and Mamiḷḷapalli.28 The first is the family of 
my grandmother’s father; the second is the family into which my 
grandmother’s father’s sister was married. These two families are thus 
mutually related, and the manuscripts of both families are in this collection. One of these manuscripts contained a clue to the possible 
owner of the chest. In the manuscript containing the Kṛṣṇakarṇāmṛta 
(see 2.9 above), on the verso side of the last folio of this section, there 
is the signature of my grandmother’s father “Telidevulapalli Ātreyulu” 
and the date “vṛṣa-nāma-saṃvatsara-phālguṇa-śuddha 5.” Vṛṣa is the 
15th year of the Jovian cycle and corresponds to ad 1821-22 or 188182. In the case of my grandmother’s father, 1881-82 would be more 
appropriate, and Phālguṇa śuddha 5 would then translate to Wednes
day 22 February 1882.29 Is this the date on which he acquired the 
manuscript or the date when he read the Kṛṣṇakarṇāmṛta? Whatever 
27  See sections 2.1, 2.3, 3.1 and 3.2. 
28  See section 3.3. 
29  Therefore the manuscript in question must have been copied prior to this date, 
namely in 1826, 1829, 1832.

--- Page 244 of 338 ---
sreeramula rajeswara sarma
228
be the case, he appears to be “the” grandfather who owned the chest 
and who collected the manuscripts that were preserved in the chest.30 
The manuscripts preserved in the chest were mainly produced in the 
family, but the grandfather appears to have also collected those produced elsewhere. Three manuscripts clearly came from outside the 
family. The Varṣaphala of 1842-43 (see 2.8 above) was composed and 
scribed by Purāṇaṃ Linganna at a place called Kalvakūru which is 
some 80 km distant from my village. The manuscript containing texts 
on Gṛhya and Purāṇic ritual (see 2.4) was copied still farther away in 
or near Karṇāṭaka. It is not known where Pālaparti Kṛṣṇamma copied 
the Kṛṣṇakarṇāmṛta and other texts during the years from 1826 to 
1832 (see 2.9), but he does not seem to belong to the circle of our relatives. 
5.0  Epilogue 
This account shows that palm leaf manuscripts were actively produced 
and collected till the end of the nineteenth century in my area in the 
coastal Andhra Pradesh. I regret my narrative is rather incoherent, 
partly because I cannot identify the personal names found in the 
manuscripts, partly because of my inability to fully decipher the legal 
documents in the palm leaf rolls. I wish, had this workshop organised 
some ten years earlier; then I could have at least consulted my father’s 
best friend who could read the legal documents and perhaps also 
identify many of the persons mentioned there. My father, of course, 
would have asked: why perform gata-jala-setu-bandhanam? Why 
build a bridge across waters which are no more there? But then isn’t 
that what we Indologists do much of the time? 
Postscript: On a visit to Vadodara in October 2008, I donated the 
palm leaf books and rolls to the Oriental Institute of the Maharaja 
Sayajirao University of Baroda, retaining for myself the two manuscripts of the Varṣaphala and Kṣṇakarṇāmta, and one of the palm 
leaf rolls.
30  His son who lived into the twentieth century did not collect palm leaf manuscripts any more but only printed books, on each of which he signed in English with 
a flourish “This book belongs to …”

Appendix I: Daśāvatāra-arghyālu 
This small text is constituted by four different strands: i. enumeration 
of the incarnations (verse 1), ii. time of the day of descent of each 
avatāra (verse 2), (iii) the lunar tithi of each descent (verses 3-8), and 
(iv) the praise of each avatāra in association of with the tithi (verses 
9-30). These four strands do not agree on the identity of the eighth 
and ninth avatāras. In the first strand, they are [Bala]rāma and 
Buddha; in the second they are Buddha and Kṛṣṇa; in the third and 
fourth Balabhadra (i.e. Balarāma) and Kṛṣṇa. Consequently, the time 
of Balarāma’s descent is missing, so too the tithi of Buddha’s manifestation. Again within each strand there are inconsistencies or at least 
lacunae. In the fourth strand, the praise concludes with the refrain 
gṛhāṇārghyaṃ mayā dattam which is missing in the case of Vāmana. 
Likewise, the tithi of Narasiṃha’s manifestation is missing in this 
strand. Verse 7 correctly places Kṛṣṇa’s birthday on Śrāvaṇa kṛ 8, but 
verse 27 has it on śukla 8. Finally, while verse 2 states that Kalkin will 
manifest himself in the evening (sāyaṃ), verse 29 mentions the time 
as that of sunrise (sūryodayavelāyām). Even so, it is an interesting text. 
I have silently corrected the orthography and numbered the verses; 
metrical and syntactical anomalies are reproduced as they are, but 
these are underlined.

chapter twelve
Rāmasubrahmaṇya’s Manuscripts: 
Intellectual Networks in the Kaveri Delta, 
1693-1922
Dominik Wujastyk
In the last decade of the seventeenth century, in South India, King 
Śāhaji of Thanjavur founded an academic community at the village of 
Tiruviśainallūr, on the banks of the river Kāverī near Kumbakonam. 
In what follows, I report on a visit to the village in 2005, and describe 
the work and manuscript library of Rāmasubba Śāstrin, an active 
scholar from about 1900 whose descendants today energetically maintain some of the traditions founded by King Śāhaji’s academy.
Rāmasubba’s Family
I took the photograph in Fig. 12.1 in 2005. We see a gentleman, 
Mr V. S***, his uncle and the head of the family, Mr R***, and his 
son. In front of them is a pile of written works in Sanskrit that they 
wished to be displayed when I photographed them. On our right are 
printed books, on the left are palm-leaf manuscripts. What cultural 
and historical understandings can we bring to bear on this image?
Tiruviśainallūr Today
The photograph was taken during a visit to a village in the Thanjavur 
district of South India. Tiruviśainallūr is a village close to the betterknown temple city of Kumbakonam, in the fertile valley of the Kaveri 
River, and north of the great temple city of Thanjavur (Fig. 12.2). It is 
a tiny village, scarcely more than a row of houses.

--- Page 252 of 338 ---
dominik wujastyk
236
Figure 12.1.  Rāmasubba’s Descendants, 2005. See colour section, Plate XXI.
Śāhaji and the Foundation of a New College
In or just before 1693, an academic community was founded at this 
village of Tiruviśainallūr,1 near Kumbakonam on the banks of the 
river Kāverī, by King Śāhaji of Thanjavur (regn. 1684-d. 28 Sept 1711, 
see Fig. 12.4).2 To be more precise, he arranged for homes and land in 
the village to be donated tax-free and in perpetuity to a group of 46 
scholars and their descendants.3
Some of these scholars settled in the village itself, while others made 
Thanjavur itself their base. But they formed a single intellectual community, networked not only through their academic work but in many 
cases also through ties of family and origin. In the years to come, the 
scholars of this college would produce a flood of literature on all 
aspects of the arts and sciences, including linguistics, theology, philosophy, law and ethics, drama and medicine. The manuscripts of 
much of this work are today housed in the great Thanjavur library. 
1  A.k.a. Tiruviśalūr, and variously called “Thiruvishaloor” or “Tiruvisalur” by the 
present residents of the village and also in inscriptions (e.g., “Tiruviśalûr” in 
Hultzsch et al. 1895-1913: passim). 
2  For Śāhaji’s date, see Subrahmanyam 2001: 150. The following account draws 
on the seminal article by Kuppuswamy Sastri (1904). 
3  Idem, 128, 179, 130, 134. This type of land grant was called sarvamānyam.

--- Page 253 of 338 ---
rāmasubrahmaṇya’s manuscripts
237
Many of these works have been printed; an even greater number are 
still in manuscript form, unpublished, and are yet to be integrated 
into contemporary scholarship.
Figure 12.2.  The Kaveri delta rice bowl. Image courtesy of Google Earth. 
See colour section, Plate XXIV.
Rāmabhadradīkṣita
One of the first scholars to arrive in Tiruviśainallūr in 1693 was the 
great Rāmabhadra Dīkṣita (fl. 1638-), who moved to this new centre 
from his home village of Kaṇḍaramāṇikyam, a unique village that 
produced several of the most impressive intellectuals of seventeenth 
and eighteenth century South India.4 The Marathi land-grant which 
recorded the college endowment noted that Rāmabhadra was to 
receive four shares of the gift, a higher proportion than other scholars, 
presumably in recognition of his already substantial reputation.5 His 
presence alone bestowed on the new college an academic probity that 
would ensure that it acted as a magnet to fellow scholars as well as 
students seeking education.
Rāmabhadra was a pivotal intellectual figure of the period. Through 
his education, he was in receipt of scholarly influences from some 
4  Kuppuswamy Sastri (1904:126–7) lists the works of eight other leading scholars 
born in Kaṇḍaramāṇikyam. He notes that it was, in 1904, “almost in ruins.” On 
Rāmabhadra’s work, see also Krishnamachariar 1937:§160, who notes that Rāmabha
dra’s brother Rāmacandra (a. of Keralābharaṇa) was a “humorous poet.”
5  Ibid., 128. Kuppuswami Sastri does not give precise reference to this land grant, 
and at the time of writing it is no longer traceable in the TMSSM Library, Thanjavur.

--- Page 254 of 338 ---
dominik wujastyk
238
Figure 12.3.  Tiruviśalūr main street.
of the greatest teachers of the age, including Nīlakaṇṭhadīkṣita of 
Madurai, the pupil of Appayya Dīkṣita.6 Through his students and his 
own writings, he amplified and passed on the learning to a huge number of later scholars. He is just one example of the founding professors 
of Śāhaji’s college.
One of Rāmabhadra’s colleagues in the original foundation by 
Śāhaji was a scholar named Śrīdharaveṅkaṭeśa. Śrīdharaveṅkaṭeśa 
wrote an account of the history of Śāhaji and his Thanjavur court, the 
Śāhendravilāsa, that has long been an important source for historians 
of Early Modern South India.7 However, as we shall see below, it was 
Śrīdharaveṅkaṭeśa’s example of religious tolerance and generosity as 
well as his legacy of powerful songs of religious devotion that have 
kept his memory alive for most South Indians until today.

Rāmasubba’s Family
The gentleman on the left of the picture in Fig. 12.1 is Mr S***, a kind, 
pious and articulate man. We met at the cultural centre that he runs in 
Tiruviśalūr that celebrates the saintly life of Śrīdharaveṅkaṭeśa, who is 
popularly known as Ayyāvaḷ. Ayyāvaḷ is today remembered as a 
smārta brahman who composed Sanskrit devotional hymns (bhajans) 
of great fervour and enduring appeal (Fig. 12.5). In the 1930s, Krishna

--- Page 256 of 338 ---
dominik wujastyk
240
machariar noted that he was “celebrated in South India for his piety 
and devotion”.8 Milton Singer remarked in the 1950s that Ayyāvaḷ’s 
bhajans were an important feature of the religious life of Madras,9 and 
an article in The Hindu newspaper as recently as 2003 described his 
memory as follows:
Ayyaval, an erudite Karnataka smartha Brahmin, having spurned the 
ministerial offer made to him by the then ruler of Mysore, embarked 
upon a kshetradanam (pilgrimage) of the south along with his wife, 
Sundari Ammal, and settled down in the village of Tiruvisainallur. The 
place was then known as Shajirajapuram since it was gifted by Shaji II 
to 45 pandits well versed in the Vedas (AD 1685–AD 1712). In fact, 
the sign board carrying the name of the village as Shajirajapuram was 
seen at the entrance to the village on the Veppathur-Kumbakonam 
road till 1950, and it was an ‘inam’ village up to 1952.10
8  Krishnamachariar 1937:§162.
9  Singer 1963.
10  Krishnamurthy 2003.
Figure 12.5.  Śrīdhara Veṅkaṭeśvara, a.k.a. Ayyāvaḷ (Contemporary devotional 
rendering). See colour section, Plate XXII.

--- Page 257 of 338 ---
rāmasubrahmaṇya’s manuscripts

An annual ten-day festival in November in the village still draws huge 
crowds for a visit to the miraculous well in the compound of Ayyāvaḷ’s 
former home, and for the singing of devotional songs.
Mr S***’s family retained strong traditions of their family history. 
In fact, it was Mr S***’s uncle’s wife, who preferred to remain outside 
the photo frame of Fig. 12.1, who seemed to be the main holder of the 
family memory, and many of the details were recounted by her. In the 
attic of Mr S***’s home there was a large pile of disintegrating documents. Mr S*** and his uncle insisted on giving me several books and 
a manuscript from this collection. The family were adamant, and 
insisted that they could no longer read and understand these documents, and that they would be in better hands with a scholar of 
Sanskrit. Their generosity and trust makes it particularly pleasing to 
be able to report on some of these documents in the present paper.
So what can be discovered about the heritage of this family and its 
place in this village?
Rāmasubba’s Descendants
The ancestor that Mr S*** considered to be the most important member of his family was called Rāmasubba, and the family retained his 
portrait as show in Fig. 12.6. The family tree is, to my best understanding, as given in Fig. 12.7.
As I subsequently learned, Rāmasubba was the familiar name of 
Rāmasubrahmaṇya Śāstrī, a learned scholar, who lived in Tiruviśainal
lūr from about 1840 until his death in 1922. Rāmasubba was well 
known enough that some of his works are mentioned with approval in 
Krishnamachariar’s History of Classical Sanskrit Literature:
Ramasubrahmanya Sastrin was son of Rāmaśankara and grandson of 
Asvatthanārāyaṇa and disciple of Śivarāma. He was an authority on 
Śāstras and lived at Ṭiruvasanallur, Tanjore Dt. He was born in the last 
thirties and died in 1922 AD.11
11  Krishnamachariar 1937:§917.

--- Page 258 of 338 ---
dominik wujastyk
242
Figure 12.6.  Rāmasubba Śāstrī. See colour section, Plate XXIII.
Krishnamachariar noted that Rāmasubba Śāstri was particularly 
respected for his commentaries on the Upaniṣads, and that in his 
Alaṅkāraśāstravilāsa he criticised Vidyānātha’s definition of poetry12, 
and that he wrote the devotional work Bhaktyānandaprakāśa.13 But 
Rāmasubba wrote many other scholarly works on topics including 
chandas, alaṅkāra, dharma, mīmāṃsā and especially vedānta.14
It is interesting to note too that Rāmasubba’s manuscript library 
was known to Eugene Hultzsch, who listed most of the titles in his 
Reports on Sanskrit Manuscripts of Southern India of 1895-1905.15 
Hultzsch noted that,
A large number of works in the list were composed by Râmasubrahmaṇya 
Śâstri or Râmasubba Śâstri, the son of Râmaśaṁkara and grandson of 
Aśvatthanârâyaṇa.16
12  Ibid., citing TC [Kuppuswami Sastri et al. 1916-] II, 2528, 2532, 2541.
13  Ibid., citing TC II, 2520.
14  Hultzsch 1895-1905:v.2, 69, 71.
15  Hultzsch 1895-1905:v.2, xiii, §15, 67–71, et passim.
16  Hultzsch 1895-1905:v.2, xiii.

Figure 12.7.  Rāmasubrahmaṇyaśāstrin’s descendants, described by his family, 
Tiruviśainallūr, 2005.
Hultzsch’s Reports listed sixty-five manuscripts in Rāmasubba’s 
library, and suggested that this was not the whole collection. The list 
was dominated by palm-leaf manuscripts (49 items) mainly on 
Dharmaśāstra, and paper manuscripts (17 items) mainly on Vedānta. 
The manuscripts were predominantly in the Grantha script. Most of 
Rāmasubba’s own compositions were written on paper manuscripts 
in Grantha script.
The Reports included over twenty manuscripts of works authored 
by Rāmasubba himself.17 These were:
1. Matatattvarahasya (Vedānta)
2. Śaivavaiṣṇavavāda (Vedānta)
3. Brahmavidyāmuktāphala (Vedānta)
4. Saguṇanirguṇavādārtha (Vedānta)
5. Bhāṭṭakalpataru (Mīmāṃsā), ibid. c. on Bhāṭṭadīpikā
17  Ibid., index, p.157a: 1104, 1469 (extract, p.139), 1473, 1488, 1530, 1537, 
1542(extr., p.148), 1546 (extr., p.149), 1548, 1550, 1553-1563.

7. Tattvārthavibodhana (Vedānta), c. on Vedāntamuktāvalī by 
Brahmānanda Sarasvatī
8. Bhāṣyatātparyasaṃgraha (Vedānta)
9. Dattāśaucavyavastāpanavāda (Dharma)
10. Brahmasūtratattvavilāsa (Vedānta)
11. Haribhaktirasaprajñā (Vedānta), c. on Bhagavadgītā
12. Bhāratatattvārthavilāsa (Vedānta)
13. Atharvaśiropaniṣadvilāsa (Vedānta)
14. Kaivalyopaniṣadvilāsa (Vedānta)
15. Jagatkāraṇatvavilāsa (Vedānta)
16. Śvetāśvataropaniṣadvilāsa (Vedānta)
17. Raghuvīravṛttastava (Chandas)

19. Alaṅkāraśastrasaṃgraha (Alaṅkāra)

From Hultzsch’s remarks, it appears that he may have met Rāmasubba 
personally.
Amongst the books and manuscripts given to me by the family 
were several publications from around 1913 of original Sanskrit treatises by Rāmasubba himself.18 These Sanskrit treatises are mainly 
Vaiṣṇava theological works. Mrs R*** was able to give some of the 
social background to one of the works, the Viṣṇudveṣakara-Mahā
śaivamatamardanam. She recounted how Rāmasubba participated in 
an intellectual conflict with a scholar who lived in Maṉṉārguḍi (see 
Fig. 12.8). Although Rāmasubba was a devoted Vaiṣṇava, he had no 
serious argument with Śaivas, and had even been into a Śaiva temple 
on some occasions, though he prayed to Rāma. When the scholar 
Mahāmahopādhyāya Rāju Śāstri, alias Tyāgarājamakhin, of Maṉṉār
guḍi, a descendant of Appayadīkṣita, wrote a pro-Śaiva tract, Rāma
subba wrote a counter-blast. A counter-counter-blast came from 
Maṉṉārguḍi, and so forth for about four exchanges, of which the 
Viṣṇudveṣakara-Mahāśaivamatamardanam is one.
The family’s present-day memory is exactly corroborated Hultzsch’s 
firsthand report from a century ago:
[Râmasubba Śâstri] is a Smârta Brâhmaṇa who advocates in his writings the Vaishṇava faith and opposes the Śaiva writings of Mahâma
18  Asvattha Ramasubbhasastrin 1916, Rāmasubrahmaṇyaśāstrin 1912a,b, 1915.

--- Page 261 of 338 ---
rāmasubrahmaṇya’s manuscripts

hôpâdhyâya Râju Śâstri alias Tyâgarâjamakhin, who lives at Maṉṉârguḍi 
near Tanjore and is a descendant of Appayadîkshita. His Chandrikâ
khaṇḍana (No. 1537) is directed against Vyâsarâyayati’s Chandrikâ 
(No. 1533).19

Rāmasubba’s Ancestors
In one of these disputatious works, called the Viṣṇudveṣakaramahāśai
vamatamardanam, Rāmasubba gives the following interesting account 
of his ancestors:20
sāmorpaṭṭūrgrāmaḥ khyātaḥ kāñcyāḥ paścād deśe ramye|
tadgrāmasthā viprā vatsās tv audīcyākhyās teṣāṃ madhye||1||
raghunāthākhyabhūdevas tv āsīc chāstrārthatattvavit|
In a charming place near Kanchi there is a village called 
Sāmorpaṭṭūr.21 The brahmans who live in that village are descendants of Vatsa, called Northerners (audīcya). Amongst them 
there was a brahman called Raghunātha, who knew the true 
meaning of the shastras.
putras tasya catustantrī rāmabhadrākhyapaṇḍitaḥ||2||

20  Rāmasubrahmaṇyaśāstrin 1912b: 68–9.
21  At the time of writing I have been unable to identify this village.

He had a son who was a pandit called Rāmabhadra, who followed the four Tantras.22
tasya putradvayaṃ khyātaṃ tatraiko ’yyākhyabhūsuraḥ|
sa tu vidvān sarvaśāstre tulajīndrapure ’vasat||3||
He was said to have had a pair of sons, one of them a brahman 
called Ayyā. He was wise in all the shastras, and he lived in the 
city of Prince Tulaji.
tasya putratrayaṃ jātaṃ madhyamo ’ṇṇākhyapaṇḍitaḥ|
yasya śiṣyapraśiṣyais tu bhūṣitaṃ maṇḍalaṃ bhuvaḥ||4||
To him were born three sons, the middle one being the pandit 
called Aṇṇā. His pupils and sub-pupils decorated the whole land.
tasya putro harer aṃśaḥ sākṣān nārāyaṇāhvayaḥ|
aśvatthapūrvapadakaḥ prasiddhaḥ pṛthivītale||5||
His son called Nārāyaṇa was like a part of Hari before one’s eyes. 
He was well known on earth with the pre-name Aśvattha.
pituḥ sakāśāt saṃlabdhasarvaśāstrārthatattvavit|
svapitur nikaṭe śāstram advaitaṃ jñātum āgatāt||6||
ahobilākhyaviduṣo mahābhāṣyaṃ gṛhītavān|
He had learned the true meaning of all the shastras from his 
father. From the scholar Ahobila who had come to learn the

250
This family history provides much interesting material, some of it 
clear, some of it tantalizingly unclear. It is valuable to have an account 
by a learned paṇḍita, Rāmasubba, in 1912 of the scholarly expertise 
and intellectual networking of his grandfather Aśvattha in the first 
half of the nineteenth century.
The reference to Ayya as a resident in Tulajī’s City (v. 3) connects 
the family with eighteenth-century Thanjavur (cf. Fig. 12.4). If the 
reference is to Tulajī the first (r. 1730-d. 1735), then we may speculate 
that either Ayya or his father Rāmabhadra may have lived in Thanjavur 
in the 1730s.32 The family tree of Rāmasubba’s ancestors is shown in 
Fig. 12.9.
Several of the philosophical works known by Aśvattha Nārāyaṇa 
were still represented in the libraries of Rāmasubba and his brother 
Kodaṇḍa, as catalogued by Hultzsch in 1905.33 For example, Kodaṇḍa 
owned copies of works by Śaṅkarācārya34, Gadādhara35, Svayaṃpra
kāśayati36, and Khaṇḍadeva37 while Rāmasubba owned manuscripts of 
works by Doḍḍācārya38, and Vanamālimiśra.39 Aśvattha’s works were 
also read elsewhere in the region, for example in the village of Tiruvi
ḍaimarudūr.40
Some of these specific links are also confirmed by the patterns of 
manuscript ownership and scholarship documented in the chapter by 
Kenneth Zysk in the present volume. Zysk’s tables 2 & 3 show, for 
example, that nine manuscripts on Nyāya owned by Aśvattha 
Nārāyaṇa were present in the library collection at Kanchipuram. This 
proves that Aśvattha’s manuscripts from Tiruviśainallūr were studied 
in Kanchi, one of the great centres of scholarship in South India from 
medieval times.
This literary and scholarly persistence demonstrates the family’s 
lasting intellectual engagement in serious scholarship and its participation in a philosophical discussion over centuries that stretched from 
Tiruviśainallūr to Thanjavur and Kanchipuram.
32  This rules out the possibility of this Rāmabhadra being the seventeenth-century Rāmabhadra Dīkṣita.
33  Hultzsch (1895-1905: v. 2) lists Kodaṇḍa’s library in § 14 (pp. 64–7) and 
Rāmasubba’s in § 15 (pp. 67–71).
34  #1456: Śārīrakamīmāṃsābhāṣya, #1480: Vivekacūḍāmaṇisaṃgraha and #1481: 
Jagadutpattiprakaraṇa.
35  #1465: Hetvābhāsasāmānyalakṣaṇa
36  #1484: c. on Dakṣiṇāmūrtyaṣṭaka.
37  #1490: Bhaṭṭadīpikā.
38  #1532: Caṇḍamāruta.
39  #1540: Nyāyaratnākara; #1541: Nyāyāmṛtasaugandhya.
40  #1287 c. on (Gādādhara’s?) Vādārtha.

--- Page 267 of 338 ---
rāmasubrahmaṇya’s manuscripts
251
Furthermore, both Kodaṇḍa and Rāmasubba owned manuscripts 
of works written by members of Śahaji’s original endowment in the 
village, colleagues and contemporaries of Ayyāvaḷ. Thus, Kodaṇḍa 
owned a work by Bhāskaradīkṣita,41 one of the original recipients of 
Śāhaji’s grant, and Rāmasubba owned a work by Mahādeva[vāja
peyin],42 another of the original recipients, as well as Mahādeva’s second son Vāsudevadīkṣita (fl. 1729-1736)43. The presence of these 
manuscripts in the brothers’ libraries demonstrates that although evidence is lacking to connect this family’s lineage directly to the recipients of Śāhaji’s endowment, the family nevertheless engaged with the 
work of those original scholars and their successors. Whether or not 
Rāmasubba’s ancestor was one of the founding scholars of King 
Śāhaji’s college, dates point to his arrival at Tiruviśainallūr at the time 
of this foundation. This family tree connects Rāmasubba and his family into a wide network of scholarship in the Kāverī delta that can still 
be closely documented over several centuries.
Today, the village of Tiruviśainallūr is perhaps a 90 minute drive 
from Thanjavur on good roads. It is no longer a place of intellectual 
work, but thanks to Ayyaval’s spiritual legacy, it is an active centre of 
devotion and pilgrimage.
King Śāhaji’s gift to 46 leading scholars in 1693 is still remembered 
well by the Brahman families in the village, and several of the houses 
of the original scholars are still identified by the inhabitants. The villagers tell enthusiastically of how great intellectuals used to meet and 
debate abstruse topics together, and to sing devotional hymns, and 
how their ancestors were actors in a great intellectual and religious 
drama. The documentary evidence abundantly supports this community memory, as well as the extensive networks of Sanskrit scholarship 
and intellectual exchange that resulted from Śāhaji’s endowment and 
that were still alive and active in the early decades of the twentieth 
century.

CHAPTER THIRTEEN
The use of manuscript catalogues as sources 
of regional intellectual history in India’s 
early modern period
Kenneth G. Zysk
Catalogues of Sanskrit manuscripts have been principal sources of 
valuable textual information and major tools for students and scholars of Indology. They provide basic information about a particular 
text for study, including the locations where that text is available and 
any special features of the individual manuscripts. The catalogues’ 
purpose has served scholars well throughout the decades; and new 
government-sponsored endeavours such as the National Mission for 
Manuscripts will facilitate the study of these important historical documents. A perusal of the printed catalogues quickly reveals a good 
deal of variation in the information they provide, despite more recent 
efforts at standardising them. One of the most important differences is 
the classification of literature. It is not uncommon to find a text classified under one genre in one catalogue and under a different genre in 
another catalogue. Standard categories of manuscript-description 
may exist, but a standard list of literary genres has yet to be established. Despite this shortcoming, this paper aims to explore a select 
number of Sanskrit manuscript-catalogues in an effort to show that 
they can provide a good deal more information than the basic description and location of a literary artefact. 
A crucial piece of information connected to any manuscript as with 
any archaeological find, is its provenance, for it provides valuable historical data about the item, including its owner and its place of discovery or “find-spot”. By means of this information, owners and users of 
different types of literature can be identified. From that a picture of 
the intellectual life for a particular region can begin to emerge, leading 
eventually to a more comprehensive understanding of India’s intellectual history in the early modern period and to the identification of 
potential centres of learning in different subjects.

--- Page 270 of 338 ---
kenneth g. zysk
254
Unfortunately, the recording of a manuscript’s provenance and 
find-spot was not a standard practice by all surveyors, collectors, and 
cataloguers of Sanskrit manuscripts. Moreover, more recent catalogues, which constitute the majority of such catalogues, have simply 
ignored a manuscript’s provenance. Fortunately, this important information has been provided in several of the early reports and catalogues, which will be the source materials for this study. Even though 
provenance is given for a certain manuscript, the extraction of data 
and their presentation in a useful form require a methodology that 
involves literally undoing the work of the early cataloguers and reporters so that the original owner and his location stand in direct relationship to the piece of literature. It requires a two step process. First, each 
manuscript report is examined and its owner, location, subject-classification recorded. Secondly, the titles and their subjects are grouped 
rather under their original owners with their locations, if such data are 
provided than under the title of the work. This information, then, provides the raw data for an analysis that reveals a fairly accurate picture 
of the intellectuals and their principal centres of location in various 
parts of India, at a distinct point in time, i.e., when most of the early 
surveying and cataloguing took place in the nineteenth and early 
twentieth centuries. Since most of the intellectual life was centred in 
the family, it is not unreasonable to assume that the individual’s intellectual interests reflect a family tradition and in some cases that its 
members may indeed have travelled from one region to another, such 
as the numerous Mahārāṣṭrian Brahmins that have relocated in Kāśī 
over the years. Moreover, if a certain intellectual specialised in a particular intellectual tradition or traditions, based on the relative proportion of manuscripts in his collection, a further determination 
about his special intellectual interests may be presumed. These data 
can then be used with other information from various sources in order 
to contextualise the intellectual life of scholarly Brahmins in the wider 
frame of Indian history.
The paper begins with a brief history of the manuscript collection, 
survey, and cataloguing in India and abroad, for this provides the necessary context for understanding the principal source materials used 
in this study. This will be followed by a description of the different 
types of manuscript catalogues that are available for examination. 
Finally, I have chosen a couple of examples which show how catalogues can be used as sources of intellectual history: one comes from 
the north and the other from the south of India.

--- Page 271 of 338 ---
use of manuscript catalogues
255
Brief Survey of Manuscript Cataloguing in India
Interest in and collection of Sanskrit manuscripts began with different 
individuals attached chiefly to the colonial government in India, but 
also included others who travelled to India in search of early Sanskrit 
literary remains. Among the early collectors whose collections ended 
up in various European libraries were Sir Robert Chambers, Sir 
William Jones, Robert Orme, Major Colin Mackenzie, Henry Thomas 
Colebrooke, John Leyden, Georg Bühler, Martin Haug, Sir Aurel 
Stein, Albrecht Weber and Rasmus Rask. The catalogues of these early 
collections were very much individual efforts by men who had knowledge of the relevant languages. For the most part, their catalogues are 
comprehensive and detailed, reflective not only of the linguistic acumen of the early civil servants, missionaries, and other scholars 
engaged in the study of Indian languages, but also of the “insider’s 
knowledge” of the native savants commissioned to help them and on 
whom the western scholars greatly depended to complete their catalogues. Many of the manuscripts from these individual collectors are 
listed in the catalogues of manuscripts housed in various university 
and institutional libraries in Europe and in the India Office which is 
now part of the British Library. 
One such catalogue is the three volume Catalogue Raisonné of 
Oriental Manuscripts by William Taylor, a missionary attached to 
Fort St. George in Madras. The catalogue entails a detailed description 
of all the manuscripts kept in the library of Fort Saint George as well 
as part of the so-called Mackenzie collection1. Most of the texts were 
in the form of palm-leaf manuscripts composed in languages such as 
Sanskrit, Telugu, Tamil, Kannada (Canarese), Malayalam and Oriya, 
written in the respective scripts and included Grantha and Devanāgarī. 
Moreover, many of them were in the form of multiple-text manuscript, containing several individual texts. They may or may not have 
had a common subject-matter and were often in different languages. 
The detail by which each text was described necessitated that Taylor 
read each and every leaf of every manuscript. Such an undertaking 
would require more a labour of love than a librarian’s chore. However, 
when set against the backdrop of his outright disdain for Indians and 
1  On the importance of Mackenzie, his collection of epigraphic materials, and 
especially his collaborators in the development of Indian epigraphy in the nineteenth 
century (see Wagoner, 2003).

--- Page 272 of 338 ---
kenneth g. zysk
256
their literature,2 serious doubts arise about Taylor’s involvement in 
the cataloguing work. We shall never know how much Taylor relied 
on the native paṇḍits’ linguistic knowledge and cultural familiarity for 
he failed to acknowledge how much they had helped him other than 
the fact that a sum of 250 rupees per month was allotted for “a special 
staff of paṇḍits and copyists” (Taylor, Vol. 1, 1857, xvi). 
The use of native paṇḍits and scholars was a common part of the 
early cataloguing efforts. However, as in the case of Taylor, just as 
common, was the lack of acknowledgement of their enormous help. 
This was a practice that was carried on for most of the early period of 
discovery and cataloguing of Indian manuscripts.3 
In 1868 the Indian Government began to take an active role in 
securing and cataloguing Sanskrit manuscripts. This new enterprise 
was prompted, it seems, from the growing demand from European 
scholars of Indian languages and literatures for better and more comprehensive tools with which to pursue their scholarship.
Acting on the suggestion of Paṇḍit Radhakrishna of Lahore, who 
urged the Government to compile a catalogue of all Sanskrit manu2  His views on Indians and their literature cannot be more clearly expressed than 
in his description of Indian poetry:
From the prevalence of poetry in Hindu composition, the simplicity of truth 
is almost always disguised. The painful result is that the Hindu mind has 
become familiarized with lying. Truth is insipid. Evidence loses its force. A 
brilliant comparison is deemed proof. It must not be forgotten that the phrenological construction of the Hindu skull is of the lower order of the Celts, 
and very inferior to the broad Saxon. Besides it would seem that there is more 
development of the cerebellum, and greater sensuality, than in the AngloSaxon. The poetry of the Hindus runs rampant on sexualities….The Hindu 
mind cannot go beyond itself…it is probably defective as to higher degrees of 
intellectual power (Taylor Vol. 1, 1857, Intro, iv).
What is perhaps even more interesting in this quotation is Taylor’s complete acceptance of the pseudo-science of phrenology, which was popular during the time.
3  Cf. Peter Peterson’s unrestrained remarks on the subject from his first report on 
search for Sanskrit manuscripts in the Bombay Circle:
I have before me now a Report, which is to my mind chiefly remarkable from 
the fact that, neither on the covers, nor anywhere within the covers, does the 
European scholar, whose name appears on the title-page,…, make any reference at all to the other native collaborateur without whose special knowledge 
of Māgadhī, and the Jain literature, that part of the Report, I make bold to say, 
could not have been written. Such a proceeding—and it does not, I regret to 
think, stand alone—may tend to confirm that relative estimate of native and 
European learning; but it is at the expense, I submit, of something more valuable than even a character for learning. (Peterson, 1883: 3-4n)

--- Page 273 of 338 ---

257
scripts in Indian and European libraries, Mr Whitley Stokes, the 
Secretary to the Council of the Governor-General for making Laws 
and Regulations drew up a four-part scheme for collecting and cataloguing Indian manuscripts, which would: 1. “print uniformly all 
procurable unprinted lists of Sanskrit MSS. in Indian libraries;” 
2. “institute searches for MSS.;” 3. “proceed to copy those [MSS.] 
which are unique or otherwise desirable, but which the possessors 
refuse to part with;” and 4. “increase the grant made to the Asiatic 
Society at Calcutta for the publication of the Bibliotheca Indica,” and 
“for the publication of Sanskrit works hitherto unprinted.” The proposal was approved by the Government of India by order numbers 
4338-48, followed by order numbers 4349-55, dated, Simla, 3 
November 1868. A sum of 24,000 Rupees per annum was allotted to 
the initiative for the whole of India (Gough 1878, 1-13; Belvalkar 1916, 
xii-xiv; Gode, 1969, 50n1; Janert, 1965, 11).
 A significant part of Stokes’ plan maintained that the actual catalogue of the manuscripts was to be done in Europe by European scholars and the catalogue should be produced in England, because he said 
that he knew of “no native scholar possessed of the requisite learning, 
accuracy and persistent energy,” and “no European scholar in India 
possessed of the requisite time, or who might not be more usefully 
employed in making original researches” (Gough 1878, 2; Belvalkar 
1916, xiv). The persistent vilification of the Indian intellect, so obviously apparent in Stokes’ public statement, prompted Peter Peterson, 
Professor of Sanskrit at Elphinstone College, Bombay, to formulate 
the following rebuttal in his first report on the search for Sanskrit 
manuscripts in the Bombay circle from 1883:
I will only add here, that my experience at Jeypore, and throughout 
this tour, confirmed me in the belief that while there is much which 
the native scholar may learn from the European scholar, there is more, 
in that which is of common interest to the two, that the European can 
learn from the native. No one who is not himself a student of Sanskrit 
letters can fully realise how dense and far-spreading the cloud is that, 
to our eyes, still hangs over the history of the literature. But no candid 
mind can engage in the study of that history in India without recognising that much, which remains for the European to discover, had never 
been hid from the native learned community.4 
4  Peterson, 1883: 3. For an illuminating discussion of the paṇḍits in colonial Bengal, see Hatcher (1996, 2005).

--- Page 274 of 338 ---
kenneth g. zysk
258
In the months that followed, Mr Stokes’ scheme was put into action in 
the following regions: Bengal, Bombay, Madras and Mysore, NorthWest Provinces, Punjab, Oudh, Central Provinces, and Rajputana. 
Teams of Indian scholars and assistants, headed by a professor of 
Sanskrit in the particular region, undertook expeditions in search of 
manuscripts. They were principally interested in acquiring by purchase or by copy important and rare treatises written in Sanskrit but 
also in other Indian languages. While in the field they gathered information on each manuscript resulting in a preliminary catalogue of the 
collections. This primary information, along with the manuscript 
itself, if obtained, was then to be dispatched to England and eventually 
to the other parts of Europe, where European scholars would work on 
different texts according to their specialisation and interest in order to 
complete the descriptive work for a catalogue in English, modelled on 
Aufrecht’s catalogue of Oxford’s Bodleian collection (Belvalkar 1916, 
xiv, xxvii-xxix).
For the next three decades expeditions in search of Sanskrit manuscripts went on at a feverish pitch all over the subcontinent, the results 
of which were published in the form of “reports”.5 Collections were 
identified and their contents described, and thousands of manuscripts, 
along with their preliminary descriptions, purchased. 
Reporting only on interesting, rare and important manuscripts was 
the purpose that united almost all the efforts of early manuscript surveyors, with few notable exceptions including Bābu Rājendralāl Mitra 
and Mahāmahopādhyāya Haraprasāda Śāstrī (1871-1911), Gustav 
Oppert (1880-85), and Eugen Hultzsch (1895-1905),6 whose lists were 
comprehensive and included the provenance of each manuscript. In 
the case of Mitra and Śāstrī, there were also detailed descriptions, 
which included beginning and ending lines or verses. 
Even though there was great willingness to loan manuscripts to 
qualified European scholars, the Europeans were less than enthusiastic about completing the work begun in India.7 Consequently, much 
of the work of producing the catalogues of manuscripts fell back on

India from 1807-1940, see PK Gode (1969: 56-70).

259
the native scholars who were merely assistants under Stokes’ plan. 
Their efforts have resulted in catalogues of manuscripts acquired and 
deposited in the Government’s oriental manuscript collections in 
most of the regions of India and of collections in smaller institutions, 
temples and the like. These catalogues vary in quality and in the 
amount of information they provide: some were merely hand-lists, 
others were presented in tabular form, but the better ones followed 
more or less the model of a descriptive catalogue, like that of the 
Oxford catalogue.8
Over time, the interest in acquiring and cataloguing Sanskrit manu
scripts began to assume less and less importance by the Government 
of India, which had to confront such global problems as two world 
wars and economic depression, and, on the local front, India’s fight 
for independence. The money eventually dried up for the cultural programmes. Even though manuscripts continued to be collected, albeit 
at a greatly reduced rate, the lack of funds resulted in bundles of 
manuscripts left to deteriorate in harsh climate conditions on the subcontinent. The situation of manuscript preservation and cataloguing 
remained pretty much stagnant until the Sanskrit Commission, 
appointed by the Government of India in October 1956, brought the 
deplorable state of Indian manuscripts to the attention of the Ministry 
for Education in a published report of 1958.9 With respect to manuscripts the Committee found the state of manuscript work to be less 
than adequate and advocated that action should be undertaken to collect, preserve, and catalogue manuscripts in two spheres, the central 
and state governments. In terms of cataloguing of manuscripts it 
xlii). On the origins of the Bhandarkar Oriental Research Institute and its collection 
of Sanskrit manuscripts, see Johnson 1988, 1990, and 1993).

and European catalogues: in India, different collections were catalogued by a team 
organised by one or two principal editors, while in Europe, there was normally more 
than one editor, and so the succeeding editor did not have the knowledge of his 
predecessor and therefore had to start afresh. Furthermore, inconsistencies in the 
early catalogues were due to the social, economic and political conditions in each 
part of the world at the time and by the current state of Indological knowledge 
(9-10).
