noticed that there was “no uniform method followed” and insisted 
that “[p]roper care should, therefore, be taken to see that the catalogue is informative as well as accurate.”10 Furthermore, it recommended that future catalogues should follow the classical method of 
the descriptive catalogue, but recognised that some have the simpler 
and easier tabular form of cataloguing with more detailed information 
on particular texts reserved for an appendix.11 Acting on the Sanskrit 
Committee’s report and the report of the Manuscript Committee, 
appointed by the University Grants Commission in 1959,12 a tabular 
form for cataloguing was adopted by the Ministry of Scientific 
Research and Cultural Affairs with the following eleven categories : 
1. serial number, with “E” indicating it will have extra information in 
an appendix, 2. accession number or collection number, 3. title either 
Romanized or in Devanāgarī-script, with an asterisk (∗) indicating the 
text has been published, 4. author, 5. name of commentator, 6. material (paper, palm-leaf, etc.), 7. script according to a prescribed list 
of abbreviations, 8a. the size in centimetres, 8b. number of folia, 
8c. number of lines on a page, 8d. number of akṣaras in a line, 9. extent 
(complete or incomplete), 10. condition and date, 11. additional particulars. This tabular form of cataloguing Indian manuscripts has been 
used for almost all the Sanskrit catalogues in India since the early 
1960s. As can be noticed, unfortunately, there was no category for 
provenance.
The Types of Sanskrit Manuscript Catalogues
As indicated in the Sanskrit Commission Report of 1958, catalogues 
of Sanskrit manuscripts generally fall into two distinct types: a tabular 
and a descriptive form of catalogue. The former was formally adopted 
by the Ministry of Scientific Research and Cultural Affairs and provides the essential information under the different headings mentioned above in tabular form. The catalogues varied only slightly one 
from the other, such as in the number of items included in the list or 
the order in which the manuscripts were presented. Sometimes entries 
10  Government of India, 1958: 180.
11  Ibid.
12  Raghavan, 1963: 97-108; Deshmukh, 1996: 2-7. The members of this committee were Dr. V. Raghavan, Dr. S. Nurul Hasan, Mr. K.D. Bhargava, and Dr. S. Bhattacharya.

--- Page 277 of 338 ---
use of manuscript catalogues
261
simply follow the order of the Sanskrit alphabetical order; while at 
other times, they are organised according to subject-matter. They may 
or may not contain a separate section which provides a fuller description of certain manuscripts deemed by the editor or compiler to be 
important, and an index of titles and authors. It is unfortunate that 
the multi-volume catalogue of the Sanskrit manuscripts at the 
Sampurnanand Sanskrit University in Varanasi, the largest single collection in the world, falls into this category of catalogues and lacks 
sections with fuller descriptions of certain manuscripts.
The descriptive catalogues likewise tend to follow a distinct model 
that provides not only the essential information, but also contain 
extracts from the manuscripts themselves. This gives the means for 
better identification of the text in question; and they are often organised according to subject-matter. Each manuscript is dealt with in 
roughly the same manner. A work’s title is given usually in Devanāgarī 
or the local script and in roman script, along with its author or authors. 
This is followed by information about the manuscript’s material, 
script, size, extent, number of folia, number of lines, number of 
akṣaras or syllables per line, completeness, date, condition, and occasionally its owner and scribe, if available. This may be followed by a 
brief statement about the content and subject-matter of the manuscript, but always there is given extracts that usually include the first 
and last couple of verses or lines of the manuscript, and if available, its 
colophon and post-colophon which contains information about 
when, by whom, and occasionally for whom and where the manuscript was copied.
In addition to the two types of manuscript catalogues, there are 
sometimes published or unpublished hand-lists of manuscripts and 
“reports” and “notices” of manuscripts based on searches that took 
place in different regions of India in the latter part of the nineteenth 
and early part of the twentieth centuries. The “reports” tend to vary in 
content and presentation. Some are travel-narratives in search of 
manuscripts with extracts from important and “rare” works or academic discussions about works and authors discovered while on tour, 
while others are narratives with a simple description of acquired 
manuscripts usually in tabular form and detailed listings of collections 
and their locations. The “notices” generally provide a full descrip- 
tion of each manuscript along with quotations from the text and enumeration of its content, and, therefore, are invaluable for scholars of 
Sanskrit.

--- Page 278 of 338 ---
kenneth g. zysk
262
Because most of the catalogues lack the criteria of find-spot and 
ownership of each manuscript, they are useless for identifying individual scholars, their locations, and their intellectual interests. We are, 
however, not completely at a loss when it comes to obtaining this vital 
information from the existing catalogues. Several of the sources examined do contain the desired information. Among these sources, one 
catalogue, two reports and one notice give names of owners and their 
locations for parts of north and south India. A regional rather than a 
comprehensive picture of Indian intellectual life during the period 
can, therefore, begin to emerge.13 The catalogue and notice come from 
north India and the two reports from south India. One north Indian 
source is Notices of Sanskrit Mss. First and Second Series, by Rājendralāl 
Mitra and Mahāmahopādhyāya Haraprasād Śāstrī. The first series 
consists of volumes 1-11 (nos. 1-26), of which volumes 1-9 were compiled and edited by Mitra and the rest by Śāstrī and describes 4365 
manuscripts. The second series of volumes 1-4 (nos. 27-34), compiled 
and edited by Śāstrī, and describes 1413 manuscripts.14 The other 
north Indian source is A Descriptive Catalogue of Manuscripts in 
Mithilā, 4 volumes, edited by Kashiprasad Jayaswal and Anantaprasad 
Banerji-Śāstrī. The south Indian sources are Lists of Sanskrit manuscripts in private libraries of southern India, 3 volumes by Gustav 
Oppert and Reports on Sanskrit manuscripts [deposited in private 
libraries] in southern India, 3 volumes by Eugen Hultzsch. In addition 
to these, wherever possible, the catalogues of collections from different Europeans stationed in India and now housed in European libraries contain information about owners and their locations. There is 
also evidence that the provenance of a manuscript was often recorded 
in the preliminary list of a manuscript’s information during the early 
expeditions in search of manuscripts; however, this important bit of 
data rarely, if ever, made it into print.15
13  Some catalogues provide part of the information, while others lack it altogether. Some provide the “find-spot” but not the owner: R.S. Panchamukhi, et al., 
eds. 1953-70. A descriptive catalogue of manuscripts in the Kannada Research Institute. 9 volumes. Dharwar: Kannada Research Institute. This catalogue includes both 
Sanskrit and Kannada manuscripts. 
14  The entire series has been reprinted in 15 volumes by Sharada Prakashan in 
Delhi in 1990-91.
15  See in particular the different extracts from reports in Gough 1878.

--- Page 279 of 338 ---
use of manuscript catalogues
263
Examples of Useful Sources 
Of the different sources that provide the provenance of each manuscript, I have chosen one from the north and one from the south to 
serve as examples for this study of the regional intellectual life in the 
early modern period. The north Indian example comes from Mithilā, 
which from the eleventh century was a major centre for Brahmanic 
learning in all subjects, but especially in logic (Mookerji 1951 [2003]: 
596-98). In south India, the example comes from modern-day 
Tamilnādu and parts of Andhra Pradesh, where from the eleventh 
century grants were given for the establishment of Brahmanic institutions of learning (Mookerji 1951 [2003]: 367-72).
Each of the samples relies on one or more collections of catalogued 
manuscripts, which, however, are arranged in different ways. In 
Mithilā the manuscripts were catalogued according to subject-matter, 
while in the south according to location. Therefore, the former provides owners and locations of manuscripts only on the basis of subject-matter, while the latter gives the content of their complete 
collections, covering a variety of subjects and genres of Sanskrit literature. The one is specific and the other is general. 
The methods for extracting and studying a manuscript’s provenance are essentially the same for both and require the reconstitution 
of the contents of the owners’ original libraries. The results obtained 
from each catalogue, however, are different. Mithilā’s manuscripts 
give information on a restricted subject, while Tamilnādu’s manuscripts provide a more comprehensive understanding of the owners’ 
overall intellectual interests. Both sets of data are informative about 
local intellectual life in their respective regions. 
1.  North India: The Manuscripts of Mithilā
Originally planned in eleven volumes, A Descriptive Catalogue of 
Manuscripts in Mithilā only reached four volumes and was published 
by The Bihar and Orissa Research Society, Patna from 1927 to 1940. 
Volume one covers the Smti manuscripts (1927); volume two, the literature, prosody and rhetoric manuscripts, (1933); volume three, the 
Jyotiḥśāstra manuscripts (1937); and volume four, the Vedic manuscripts (1940).

--- Page 280 of 338 ---
kenneth g. zysk
264
Had it been completed, this collection would have been especially 
interesting because Mithilā was a place renowned for scholarship and 
intellectual life. Mithilā is the antique name for the region that was 
known as Tirhut under the Mughals. From 1875, under the British, 
the same area was divided into three Districts: Champāran to the west 
and Darbhangā to the east of Muzaffarpur. Darbhangā was the headquarters of Mithilā’s Brahmanism and, therefore, crucial for understanding the distribution of Sanskrit manuscripts (Imperial Gazetteer 
of India, Vol. XI, 153-54).
The catalogues of manuscripts represent the results of the search 
for rare Sanskrit and Prakrit manuscripts in Bihār and Orissa, under 
the auspices of the Government of Bihār and Orissa and the President 
of the Bihār and Orissa Research Society. Publication was underwritten by the Mahārājādhirāja of Darbhangā.16 Among the cataloguing 
data included for each manuscript is found the ‘place of deposit’ or 
provenance, which includes the name of the owner and the town 
where he resided. Therefore, by a process of data-extraction and analysis, one can theoretically reconstruct the original owners of different 
types of manuscripts and the extent of their holdings in a particular 
intellectual subject. However, since only part of the collected manuscripts has been catalogued, the full range of an owner’s collection is 
not known. A controlled analysis of the provenance of manuscripts in 
a particular branch of learning can, therefore, provide a fairly accurate 
picture of specialists in that field and where in the region of greater 
Mithilā they were located.
For the example from Mithilā, I have selected the catalogue of Smti 
manuscripts, which represents the traditional subject-matter of Dhar
maśāstra or Hindu Law. Historically, Dharmaśāstra has held a long 
and distinguished history in Mithilā, since it is “the only surviving 
centre in the whole of northern India where the whole of Smti may be 
studied in all its successive stages from earliest times to present day.”17 
The catalogue of Smti-manuscripts will, therefore, allow us to identify the owners and their locations of this intellectual tradition in the 
region and thus provide a more detailed picture of the intellectual life 
surrounding Hindu Law in Mithilā in the early modern period.
16  Jayaswal and Śāstrī 1927, i-ii.
17  Jayaswal and Śāstrī 1927, xiv.

--- Page 281 of 338 ---
use of manuscript catalogues
265
A number of 455 manuscripts is identified in the catalogue, but the 
actual number is over twice this amount, because the editor simply 
lists works with the same name under one title. The classification of 
Smti includes literature from the Dharmaśāstras as well as literature 
which is normally found under a category called Karmakāṇḍa, which 
deals with ritual procedures for Brahmanic religious observances. 
Most of the manuscripts are written on paper in Maithilī script, and 
many of the manuscripts have direct bearing on the legal and religious 
issues pertaining to the areas from which they derived and are written 
by local paṇḍits.
A total of 116 paṇḍits from the Darbhangā area of Bihār contributed 1026 individual manuscripts to the catalogue (see table-118). The 
Rāj Library in Darbhangā with 187 manuscripts was the largest single 
collection. The Chitradhar Library in Tabhaka, Dalsiṅgh Sarai, with 
thirteen manuscripts is a smaller collection. However, since they represent repositories rather than individual owners’ collections, they are 
excluded from the survey, leaving a total number of Smti-manuscripts 
in individual collections at 826. Only owners with ten or more manuscripts in their collections are included, because fewer than ten can 
hardly be seen as constituting an intellectual concentration. Twentysix individuals fit into the criterion of ten or more manuscripts. 
Together they yield 515 manuscripts which represent about 62% of 
the Smti-manuscripts from individual collections. Although the 
majority of owners bear the name Miśra or Jhā, it is impossible to 
know from this source alone whether any of the Miśras or Jhās is 
related. Such information could be obtained from census reports or 
other data from the region. However, the owners’ distribution based 
on location is as follows: seven in Jhañjhārpur; four in Maṇīgāchī; 
three in Madhubanī; two each in Andhrāṭhārhī, Dalsiṅgh Sarai, Sakrī, 
and Salampur (Ghatāho); and one each in Mādhavpur, Tamuriā, 
Pandauli, Deodhā, and Benīpati (see map - I).
18  In compiling this list of names and places, it was noticed that the spellings 
were not consistent, so I have made an effort to standardise them throughout.

--- Page 282 of 338 ---
kenneth g. zysk
266
Map-I shows the locations in Darbhangā, Bihār, North India as follows: 
1. Jhañjhārpur 2. Madhubanī 3. Darbhangā 4. Mādhavpur 5. Andhrāṭhārhī 
6. Salampur (Ghatāho). 
Map after Imperial Gazetteer of India, 1909 ed. 
Courtesy: Chicago Univ. collection. See colour section, Plate XXVI.
It may be pointed out that the pattern of locations of the collections 
with fewer than ten Smti-manuscripts conforms to that of the collections with more than ten manuscripts shown in Map-I. 
The information about owners and their locations provided in the 
catalogue of Smti-manuscripts from Mithilā, therefore, permits us 
accurately to identify the principal preservers of the intellectual tradition of Dharmaśāstra along with their locations in the region known 
as Darbhangā. In this way, we are able to add specific names and 
places to the intellectual life of Dharmaśāstra in Mithilā during the 
early modern period, and, therefore, contribute important information to the intellectual history of the region. 
2.  South India: The Manuscripts of Tamilnādu
The southern Indian example relies on two reports of manuscripts 
in private libraries in Tamilnādu and part of modern-day Andhra 
Pradesh: Gustav Oppert’s Lists of Sanskrit manuscripts in private

--- Page 283 of 338 ---
use of manuscript catalogues
267
libraries of South India, vols. 1 and 2, and Eugen Hultzsch’s Reports 
of Sanskrit Manuscripts in Southern India, no.1 of 3. Both cataloguers 
acknowledge the use of native authorities to visit the different col
lections and to gather information on each manuscript. In the case 
of Hultzsch’s reports, extracts of manuscripts were also made and are 
found in the appendices. The final edited compilation, which included 
the identification of each manuscript’s subject-matter, was undertaken 
by Oppert and Hultzsch. In general, the latter covers collections 
omitted by the former, so that both can be considered as forming one 
single listing of the private manuscripts in the region. In a couple of 
instances in Oppert’s catalogue, a collection is mentioned twice with 
no explanation. Hultzsch noticed the same occurrence, where Oppert 
had already mentioned a collection by the same owner, but the titles 
of manuscripts were different. He suggested that it could be the result 
of showing parts of a single collection at different times (note 2, p. iii). 
Oppert’s two-volume catalogue a total of 18,797 manuscripts (vol. 
1: 8,376, vol. 2: 10,421) from the following districts: North and South 
Arcot, Bellary, Chingleput, Coimbatore, Kadapa, Karnul, Krishna, 
Madura, Malabar, Mysore, Salem, Tanjore, Tinnevelly, Trichinopoly, 
and Vizagapatam. Hultzsch’s first two numbers catalogue about 1,600 
manuscripts from Nellore, Tiruvailyāru, Tiruviḍaimarudūr, and Tira
visanallūr. His last number is a catalogue of a single collection of about 
700 manuscripts, belonging to Jambunāthabhaṭṭa, a Marāṭhā Brahmin 
in Tanjore, who was the eldest son of Mannambhaṭṭa. It contains 
largely Devanāgarī copies of north Indian manuscripts and is very 
rich in Vedic literature. 
Unlike the catalogue from Mithilā, these two catalogues present the 
information in tabular form by means of owner and location, both in 
terms of district and town, along with other relevant information, 
including title, author, subject, script, material, extent, average lines 
per page, and pertinent remarks. For the purpose of illustration, I 
have selected only a part of the districts covered in both catalogues.
The methodology used to obtain data from these catalogues is very 
similar to that employed in the case of the Smti-catalogue from 
Mithilā with an additional step aimed at identifying the primary and 
secondary literary interests of a given collection. This step was unnecessary with the manuscripts from Mithilā because the cataloguing 
already took into consideration the primary interest in Dharmaśāstra. 
The first step necessitates the extraction of information which yields a

--- Page 284 of 338 ---
kenneth g. zysk
268
list of owners, districts, total number of manuscripts each owner possessed, and number of manuscripts in each subject (see table-2). This 
is done in order to give a clear view of the range and number of manuscripts in any given collection. For the sake of consistency the number 
of at least ten (10) manuscripts in a single collection is used, because it 
is deemed that a collection with fewer manuscripts would not permit 
an accurate assessment of both primary and secondary intellectual 
focus, except where a single subject constitutes the entire collection. 
These “acceptable” collections are indicated in bold typeface in table 2. 
In listing the manuscripts from each collection, only subjects with the 
highest number of manuscripts are mentioned, and subjects with only 
one manuscript are cited only in collections with less than ten manuscripts. In a few cases, individual collections appear to belong to family members from the same gotra, based on the similarities of their 
names. Only collections with thirty or more manuscripts are included, 
regardless of the number of manuscripts owned by individual family 
members.
After identifying the “acceptable” collections for this study, the second step involves a further refinement of the information to determine the principal owners, their locations, and their primary and 
secondary intellectual interests. This information is found in table 2, 
where it is organised according to owner, district, town, and subjectmatter. An arbitrary number of at least 25% of the total collection was 
deemed sufficient for a primary specialisation and at least 20% for a 
secondary specialisation. In certain cases, the percentages did not 
reach the designated minimums; however, the overall size of the collection was sufficient to offer a primary and/or secondary specialisation. These data are provided in table 3.
The information found in table 3 identifies the owners, their towns, 
and their primary and occasionally secondary subjects of specialisation in the three South Indian districts of Chingleput, Coimbatore, 
and Nellore (see map-II for Chingleput and Nellore, and map-III for 
Coimbatore).

As in the case of the owners from Mithilā, a kindred relationship 
between them cannot be determined from these data, but the fol
lowing names bear closer examination. In Chingleput, there is 
Narasiṁhācāryār from Ammaṇapālam and Elanakar; Varadācāryār 
and Vijagarācāryār from Ammaṇapālam and Kañcīpuram; Rāmānujā
cāryār from Elanakar, Kañcīpuram, Marutvānpāḍi, and Taḍakamaṅg
lam; Tātācāryār and Rāghavācāryār from Kañcīpuram and Uttara- 
mallūr; Raṅgācāryār from Kañcīpuram, Pillapakkam, and Velamur; 
and Śāstriyār from Kañcīpuram and Kārūr in Coimbatore; and in 
Coimbatore, there is Śrauti from Kāṇiyūr and Bhavāni; Rāmās
vāmiśāstrī from Kojuman and Kumāraliṅgam; and Aiyar from 
Pallaḍam.
In terms of subjects covered in different regions of Tamilnādu, 
Chingleput is dominated by philosophy, principally Vedānta and 
Nyāya. Second comes Kāvya, followed by Dharma, Veda, and Stotra.

--- Page 287 of 338 ---
use of manuscript catalogues
271
The town of Kañcīpuram has the largest number of specialists in 
Philosophy, Kāvya, Dharma, and one in Vedāṅga. The other towns 
have considerably fewer specialists and reflect the specialisations of 
Kañcīpuram. Since Kañcīpuram has been a major religious centre for 
Hindus for centuries, it is natural that it would also be a central place 
for Brahmanic learning. It can also be noticed that it would appear 
that its influence, especially in the subjects of philosophy and literature, spread throughout the district to most of the other towns of 
Chingleput. 
There are relatively few secondary intellectual interests among the 
collections from Chingleput. Most come from Kañcīpuram and reflect 
the overall dominance on philosophy, i.e., Vedānta and Nyāya. Others 
include Dharma and Veda.
Coimbatore is dominated by owners who had interest in literature, 
which includes both Kāvya and Itihāsa/Purāṇa. This is followed by 
philosophy (i.e., Vedānta and Nyāya). The town of Bhavāni has the 
most number of specialists, who, from their family names, may belong 
to the same gotra. Aside from these major subjects in the district, 
Kaḍattūr has a paṇḍit who specialised in Vyākaraṇa, Bhavāni has one 
in Veda, and Kārūr one in Dharma. 
The secondary interests mirror the primary ones and also exhibit a 
good deal of diversity, including Veda, Dharma and Vyākaraṇa.
Finally, the district of Nellore has the fewest number of collections 
that fit the minimum criterion of thirty manuscripts. Overall, 
Philosophy, principally Vedānta, predominates. This could reflect the 
influence from the neighbouring Chingleput district. After Philosophy, 
comes Dharma, Kāvya, and Śrauta. In terms of secondary interests, 
two have Vyākaraṇa, and one has Dharma, one Śrauta, and one 
Vedāṅga.
The districts thus far examined in Tamilnādu and Andhra Pradesh 
indicate that in the early modern period individual manuscript collections were dominated by philosophy, primarily Vedānta and Nyāya, 
and literature, with secondary interests in a variety of subjects including Dharma, Veda and Vedic ritual, as well as grammar. In general 
these primary and secondary subjects correspond rather closely to the 
Brahmanic literature mentioned in the eleventh century inscriptions, 
which tell about grants given for the establishment of centres for 
Brahmanic learning (Mookerji, 1951 [2003]: 367-72). A closer and 
more detailed examination of the inscriptions could reveal connections between owners of manuscripts, their locations, and their special

--- Page 288 of 338 ---
kenneth g. zysk
272
interests from which one might well point to continuity in Indian 
intellectual history from the eleventh through the nineteenth centuries for parts of Tamilnādu and modern-day Andhra Pradesh. 
Discussion and Conclusions
In this study, I have attempted to show how catalogues of Sanskrit 
manuscripts can provide information useful for understanding Indian 
intellectual history. I have focussed on the datum of provenance which 
gives the manuscripts’ owner and their location. This information 
could then be used to reconstruct a specific area’s intellectual history 
in the early modern period when most of the surveys of collections of 
manuscripts took place in India. Moreover, since the specialisation in 
an intellectual tradition is inextricably connected to the intellectual’s 
family, the individual and his location may well reflect a longer intellectual history for the area.19
Several difficulties had to be addressed before a successful result 
could be achieved. Since relatively few catalogues and reports contained the manuscript’s provenance, the areas examined were necessarily very circumscribed, resulting in incomplete coverage. Not all 
the sources that contained the datum of provenance were arranged in 
the same manner. Some listed the manuscripts by subject, some by 
location, and some simply by the order in which the manuscript was 
received and catalogued. In all cases, however, the methodology 
required the reconstruction of the owners’ original collections by 
looking at every entry and extracting its owner and location. These 
data provided the basic information for further analysis. 
In this paper, I have not examined all of the available reports and 
catalogues that provide a manuscript’s provenance, but have limited 
myself to two such sources, one from north India and one from south 
India. The former catalogued the manuscripts by subject and the latter 
by location.
 From the north, the first volume of the catalogue of Mithilā manuscripts was used, because it provided the ‘find spot’ for each of the 
Smti-manuscripts listed in the catalogue. First, a complete list of 
owners, their locations, and the quantity of manuscripts in their col19  In a way, this study complements and builds upon David Pingree’s groundbreaking study of the Anūp Library in Bīkāner as an important intellectual centre 
through the use of its catalogue of Sanskrit manuscripts (Pingree, 1997).

--- Page 289 of 338 ---
use of manuscript catalogues
273
lections was made. Since the entire extent of an owner’s collection is 
not known, it was impossible to determine the percentage of the total 
collection represented by the Smti-manuscripts, and thereby to know 
if Dharmaśāstra reflected a primary or secondary concentration. 
Therefore, an arbitrary number of a total of ten manuscripts20 served 
as the basis for establishing an intellectual’s interest. The results of the 
survey provided a list of owners of collections of Smti-manuscripts, 
who were also the principal maintainers of the intellectual tradition of 
Dharmaśāstra in Mithilā. The names and places contribute to a better 
understanding of the intellectual life of the region during the early 
modern period. 
From the south, two reports of collections of manuscripts in 
Tamilnādu and parts of modern-day Andhra Pradesh were used, with 
a focus on districts of Chingleput, Coimbatore, and Nellore. Unlike 
the Mithilā catalogue, these reports listed manuscripts by district, city 
and owner, and include the subject-matter of each manuscript. With 
this information, a more comprehensive understanding of an owner’s 
collection could be determined. First, the collections were grouped 
according to district, owner, and town, and the number and subjects 
of manuscripts in each collection. Secondly, this list was reduced using 
as criterion ten or more manuscripts in a particular subject. Moreover, 
because the sources provided an owner’s complete collection, a primary and secondary interest could be determined on the basis of relative percentages: 25% for primary and 20% for secondary interests. 
Small collections with manuscripts in only one subject would also be 
included. Secondary specialisation mirrored that of the primary.
From these data a complete overview of the preservers, their locations, and their primary and secondary intellectual interests could be 
obtained for the three South Indian districts in the early modern 
period. Moreover, the primary intellectual traditions reflected in their 
collections correspond to those which are mentioned in the South 
Indian inscriptions from the eleventh century, reflecting perhaps continuity over time. 
20  The number ten was chosen on the basis of my personal visual inspection of 
the manuscript collections, where it appeared that ten or more manuscripts in a 
particular subject reflected the owner’s interest, if not specialisation, in that subjectmatter. Other suggestions on a more accurate establishment of an owner’s con
centration based on the number of manuscript in a particular subject are most 
welcomed.

--- Page 290 of 338 ---
kenneth g. zysk
274
The two sources used as examples in this study, therefore, provided 
information about each manuscript’s provenance. One focussed on a 
particular tradition of Sanskrit learning, and the other on complete 
collections, their locations, and the different systems of Sanskrit 
knowledge they contained. The first yielded specific information about 
the locations of the single intellectual tradition, while the second gave 
more extensive knowledge about the distribution of different traditional form of Sanskrit literature in a particular region. Both are useful, but the results are slightly different in each case.
Although this study used only two examples, it offered at least two 
useful results. One shows how catalogues of Sanskrit manuscripts can 
be used to understand better India’s general intellectual history; and 
two, it points specifically to certain trends in intellectual history of a 
special region or area of India during the early modern period if not 
before. Therefore, based on the information provided in this paper, a 
more comprehensive project can be undertaken in which all of the 
catalogues and reports are surveyed using a methodology similar to 
that described in this essay in order to increase our understanding of 
both the regional and general intellectual history of India.
